# 1:2: 21 questions

## 1:2/q01 What does ḥamd mean and how does it relate to praise and thanks?  [ٱلْحَمْدُ · meaning]
turns on: Whether praise and thanks are synonymous, whether ḥamd requires a chosen good, and whether its object or its means is broader.
- 1:2/q01/p1 Ḥamd praises qualities as well as gifts; shukr answers a gift. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): Al-hamd is complete praise, with the definite article covering the whole class of praise; it is broader than thanks, which responds to a favor, while praise may simply describe qualities. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): All beautiful praise belongs to God, who deserves worship as the source of original blessings and the creator, nurturer, and reformer of creation; the verse teaches gratitude and praise. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Praise, commendation, and gratitude are related: praise contrasts with blame and commendation with satire, while gratitude contrasts with ingratitude. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Praise need not respond to a favor, whereas gratitude is specifically for a favor; praise can nevertheless substitute for gratitude, as its use with shukran shows. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Praise and gratitude overlap, but praise may be offered initially whereas gratitude is only in response to a blessing. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن قتيبة): Ibn Qutayba defines praise as commending a person's inherent virtues, while gratitude is praise for a favor or kindness received. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن قتيبة): Ibn Qutayba notes that ḥamd can stand for shukr, as in praising someone for a kindness, just as one thanks him for courage. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-hamdu lillah is better than ash-shukru lillah because it praises God for every benefit to the worlds, even one not received by the speaker; shukr is personal gratitude. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (المخالف): One reported distinction says hamd thanks God for warding off calamity, while shukr thanks Him for gifts; an objection asks why the lesser benefit is named first. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi offers three answers: thanking for the lesser implies gratitude for the greater; warded-off harm is unbounded while gifts are finite; and preventing harm matters more than adding benefit. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-hamdu lillah also means praise and thanks are God’s due and property, because His abundant favors make Him inherently deserving of them. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because full gratitude is beyond human capacity, the verse states that all praise belongs to God rather than commanding people to praise Him. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The ability, motive, and freedom from obstacles needed for gratitude are themselves gifts, so thanking them would require endless further thanks. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الواسطي): Al-Wāsiṭī calls gratitude shirk when a person imagines that his thanks can equal God’s blessings. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The report’s expanded wording contrasts one immeasurable blessing with the servant’s boundless thanks in saying al-ḥamdu lillāh. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (داود): David asks how he can thank God when even the ability to thank depends on God’s gift; God says recognizing that incapacity is gratitude according to one’s capacity. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise thanks God for past blessings and brings future increase, as promised in the Qur’an; it closes barriers and opens the way to knowledge of God, hence the name al-Fātiḥa. | mentions 14:7 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Scholars disagree whether gratitude is owed by reason or only by revealed law; the latter cite the rule that punishment awaits a messenger and the removal of excuses by messengers. | mentions 17:15, 4:165 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī argues that gratitude is due at all times, even before revelation: the verse makes praise God’s right without qualification and grounds it in His nurturing and mercy. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A second group calls praise bad manners: the tiny thanks cannot match immeasurable blessings, recalling blessings distracts from knowing the giver, and praise for benefits seems self-interested. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Contemplating creatures reveals more mercy, favor, and beneficence than evil, because good is essential and evil incidental; therefore praise and thanks predominate. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise corresponds to bowing: gratitude looks both to God and His gift, a middle state between turning away and absorption, as bowing lies between standing and prostration; abundant gifts also weigh the back down. QURTUBI, d. 671 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya counters that adding shukran narrows the praise to gratitude for a particular blessing, which shows the words are not identical. QURTUBI, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Another view makes praise broader because it includes both gratitude and commendation, can stand in place of gratitude, and is not itself replaced by gratitude. QURTUBI, d. 671 (شقيق بن إبراهيم): Shafīq ibn Ibrāhīm says gratitude has three conditions: recognizing the giver, being content with what was given, and not disobeying God while one retains strength. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAtiyya says “praise to God, in thanks” restricts praise to a blessing and therefore argues against equating praise and gratitude. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Others say praise is broader: it includes gratitude and commendation, can stand in place of gratitude, but gratitude cannot replace praise. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Other explanations say God praised Himself because servants cannot fully praise Him, or because abundant blessings exceed their capacity; divine praise removes the burden of gratitude. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (شقيق بن إبراهيم): Shafiq says gratitude requires recognizing the giver, accepting what is given, and not disobeying while one has strength. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (النبي): The Prophet is quoted saying praise is the head of gratitude and that whoever does not praise God has not thanked Him. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Blame is the opposite of praise, and ingratitude the opposite of shukr. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report calls praise the head of gratitude and says one who does not praise God has not thanked Him. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative as a subject, though it can be read accusative with an implied verb as an explanatory verbal noun, like ‘thanks’ or ‘ingratitude’; nominative wording conveys a settled, enduring meaning. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): A report calls praise the head of gratitude and says a servant has not thanked God without praising Him; verbal mention spreads notice of a favor more than inward belief or ambiguous bodily acts. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Three positions equate praise and thanks, make ḥamd broader, or distinguish thanks for deeds from praise for attributes; Abū Ḥayyān prefers ḥamd as broader. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الراغب): Al-Raghib says hamd can concern chosen and innate qualities, while shukr answers a favor; he also explains mahmud as praised, Muhammad as much-praised, and Ahmad as surpassing others in praise. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir questions Ibn Jarir's equation: praise is verbal commendation for necessary or bestowed qualities, while thanks applies only to bestowed qualities and may be inward, spoken, or enacted. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir resolves the scope dispute by saying praise covers more kinds of objects but is speech-only, whereas thanks uses more kinds of means but concerns bestowed qualities alone. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الجوهري): Al-Jawhari defines praise as the opposite of blame, says taḥmīd is more emphatic than ḥamd, and regards ḥamd as broader than thanks. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الجوهري): Al-Jawhari defines thanks as praising a benefactor for what he bestowed and says using the preposition li- is more eloquent. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says al-ḥamdu lillāh is a word of thanks; when a servant says it, God says that His servant has thanked Him. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas explains praise as thanks, humble submission, and acknowledgment of God's favors, guidance, and origination. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir explains praise as exclusive thanks to God for countless blessings, including faculties for worship, worldly provision, and guidance toward lasting bliss despite no prior entitlement. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha's many titles converge on مراقبة: it is the Mother of the Book, foundation, oft-repeated, treasure, healing, sufficing, complete, protecting, incantation, praise, thanks, supplication and prayer. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha is a treasure, cure, sufficiency, fulfillment, protection and incantation; it gathers praise, gratitude and supplication, with prayer as their greatest assembly. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The root and forms of al-hamd suggest reaching an end, extension, encompassing and circularity; these imply lowering the head and can lead to contentment and gratitude. DURR, d. 911 (الله): The report glosses al-Hamd as gratitude and praise to God. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet calls al-hamd the head of gratitude: a servant has not thanked God unless he praises Him. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): Anas reports that tawhid is the price of Paradise and praise repays gratitude for every blessing. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): In a report from Kaʿb ibn ʿUjra, the Prophet promises gratitude if an Ansar force returns safe with booty; when reminded, he says he has fulfilled it with thanks and abundant praise. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A weak report from the Companion al-Hakam ibn Umayr says that saying Lord of the worlds is gratitude to God, bringing an increase. DURR, d. 911 (سفيان الثوري): Sufyan al-Thawri says al-hamdulillah is both remembrance and gratitude, a combination found in no other expression. DURR, d. 911 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): Ibn ʿAmr assigns distinct functions to remembrances: glorification is creation's prayer, al-hamd gratitude, tawhid sincerity, takbir fills heaven and earth, and the declaration of God's powerlessness-to-act-without-help brings God's command to submit. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas calls al-hamd the word of gratitude; when a servant says it, God says that His servant has thanked Him. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas explains al-hamd as gratitude, humble submission to God, and acknowledgment of His blessings, guidance, creation, and other favors. DURR, d. 911 (أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي): Abu ʿAbd al-Rahman al-Jubbaʾi says prayer, fasting, and every good deed done for God count as gratitude, with al-hamd the best gratitude. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): A prophetic report calls al-hamd the head of gratitude: a servant has not thanked God until praising Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): When a returned camel prompted the Prophet to fulfill a vow of gratitude, he said “al-hamd lillah,” which the Companions accepted as thanks. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas defines praise as gratitude, praise of God, and acknowledgment of His blessings, guidance, and originating bounty. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي): Abu Abd al-Rahman al-Jubba'i says prayer, fasting, and every good deed for God are gratitude, with al-hamd the best form. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report about an Ansar expedition says the Prophet pledged thanks if they returned safe with spoils, then said he had fulfilled that pledge and praised God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (سفيان الثوري): Sufyan al-Thawri says al-hamd is both remembrance and gratitude, a combination found in no other expression. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): Abd Allah ibn Amr assigns distinct meanings to the formulas: tasbih is the creatures' prayer, al-hamd is gratitude, tahlil is sincerity, takbir fills heaven and earth, and “no power” expresses surrender. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses praise to God as thanks to God. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Other names include the Prayer, Healing, Cure, Foundation, Sufficient, Complete, Praise, Supplication, Thanks, and Invocation surahs because their meanings occur in it. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Other definitions reserve shukr for apparent blessings or, alternatively, for inward blessings. ALUSI, d. 1270 (صاحب التجريد): The author of al-Tajrīd adds a fourth form of gratitude: thanking God by God, since no one but God can give Him His due thanks. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): People generally recognize three modes of shukr; it overlaps with ḥamd in some respects, but ḥamd is its strongest branch because it openly discloses the blessing. ALUSI, d. 1270 (ابن عمر): A report from Ibn ʿUmar calls ḥamd the head of gratitude and says a servant has not thanked God unless he praises Him; al-Ālūsī notes its chain is interrupted. ALUSI, d. 1270 (أنس): Al-Ālūsī cites a supporting report from Anas in which God tells Abraham that praise is the key to gratitude and gratitude ascends to the Lord of the Throne. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Calling ḥamd the head of gratitude suits its status as the most visible and principal form; this also explains why the Qurʾān opens with praise and begins the precious sūra with what is most precious. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Hamd is more elevated as worship than shukr because it need not be prompted by a benefit received, unlike gratitude for a gift. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): An additional rationale is that shukr responds to finite gifts, while ḥamd can praise even for the unbounded benefit of averting harm, which is more urgent than gaining benefit. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): In the common account, ḥamd has a limited means but a general object, whereas shukr has a general means but is directed specifically toward blessings. ALUSI, d. 1270 (داود): In a report, David asks how to thank God when gratitude itself is among His gifts; God answers that recognizing inability to thank Him is itself gratitude. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Hamd and gratitude overlap in part, but hamd is stronger because it openly discloses blessing while ingratitude conceals it; verbal praise is clearer than hidden belief or deeds that may be habitual. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar transmits the saying that praise is gratitude’s head and that no servant thanks God without praising Him; al-Ālūsī notes its interrupted chain but says a supporting report strengthens it. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Other rationales say praise is a fitting beginning for the Lord and that praise exceeds thanks because it need not arise from a benefit received by the speaker. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Praise can concern withholding as well as giving and is thus unbounded, while gratitude answers a finite gift; averting harm is more urgent than gaining benefit, favoring praise first. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Praise has a narrower occasion but a broader object than gratitude; its inclusion may signal a sacred allusion, and tasbīḥ is omitted because praise already includes both negative and positive perfections. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الإمام): An argument for rational gratitude before revelation says the verse establishes God's praiseworthiness through creation, mercy, and future judgment; al-Alusi replies that this proof itself is scriptural, not rational. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Alusi says deriving the duty of gratitude from God's own revealed declaration only proves it through revelation, so it cannot establish a pre-revelation rational duty. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Resûl-i Ekrem): The Prophet calls hamd the head of gratitude and says that whoever fails to praise God has not thanked Him. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Hamd springs from justified hope or experienced enjoyment and leans toward the future, whereas shukr celebrates an accomplished gift and its received fulfillment. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Gratitude is a distinctive mark of people of truth, yet only a few servants attain it. | mentions 34:13 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The basmala names God and His attributes while leaving humanity implicit; saying it establishes the divine relation that lets al-Fatiha bring humans forth with gratitude into speech. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Al-Fatiha begins in gratitude, places humans in the worlds under a hidden divine relation, renews the manifestation of mercy, and trains consciousness through truth and instruction until people recognize God and themselves. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Sibawayhi distinguishes supplicatory accusative sources from declarative ones such as hamdan and shukran, whose omitted verbs mean 'I praise' or 'I thank'. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Dhu al-Rumma's phrase 'we brought into being thanks for its Creator' confirms that Arabs described praise or thanks as something they create, treating it as performative expression. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The sura also teaches gratitude by crowning supplication with praise of the giver of intellect and guidance; opening important speech with praise is therefore Qur'anic practice. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayh explains conventional verbal nouns such as “beware” or “thanks” as accusatives governed by verbs suppressed from use, and distinguishes supplicatory formulas from other expressions. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): For hamdan or shukran, an understood verb such as “I praise” is omitted because the source itself stands in its place, as in the established supplicatory formulas. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (ذي الرمة): Dhu al-Rumma's line about a flame says that it produced gratitude to its Creator, showing Arabs could describe praise or thanks as something brought into being. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (علي): Ali explains that God teaches servants general gratitude because His blessings cannot all be counted or known in detail; the author connects this to praise taught on the servant's behalf. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Every effect and its perfections depend on their cause; since all causes end in God, all genuine beauty, praise, and thanks ultimately belong to Him. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Thanks is a verbal or practical response to intentional kindness and a moral duty toward benefactors, human or divine; hamd is spoken praise for all willful goodness, not only gifts. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (النبي): The Prophet says that whoever does not praise people has not praised God, recognizing gratitude for people's real contributions. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (بعضهم): Hamd applies whether a favor reaches the speaker or someone else, whereas shukr is limited to a favor received by the speaker. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): One thanks a person only for a benefit received, but may praise that person's benefit to oneself or others. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (لسان العرب): Hamd is more general than shukr: one praises intrinsic qualities and gifts, but does not thank someone for an intrinsic quality. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Hamd is praise, not necessarily for a blessing and possibly for deeds; shukr responds only to blessings and is enacted through gratitude. STUDYQURAN (author): Al-ḥamd praises God for His qualities and thanks Him for worldly favors and next-world reward. STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet says that praising God, Lord of the worlds, constitutes thanks and brings increased bounty. STUDYQURAN (author): Thanks responds to what has already been received, while praise is more universal because it extols qualities prior to any gift. STUDYQURAN (author): The definite form al-ḥamd indicates that all praise and gratitude belong to God. MUQATIL: Muqātil glosses the praise as gratitude to God. TAB: Al-Ṭabarī defines praise as thanks for faculties, provision and guidance; Ibn ʿAbbās adds submission and acknowledgment, a prophetic report promises increase, and Kaʿb calls it praise. Al-Ṭabarī argues praise and thanks overlap in Arabic, while the article embraces all praise. JISHUMI: It treats praise and gratitude as near synonyms but distinguishes blessing from other goods and heart from tongue. BAGHAWI: The statement instructs praise; ḥamd exceeds shukr in object, while lām marks entitlement; an alternative distinguishes verbal and bodily thanks. BAGHAWI-FULL: The text explains report and instruction, ḥamd's breadth, verbal versus bodily gratitude and the entitlement lām. IBNATIYYA: Ibn ʿAṭiyya takes praise as broader than gratitude, rejects al-Ṭabarī's synonymy, and treats 'in gratitude' as restriction to a blessing. [173 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(الطبري,بعض الناس); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن قتيبة); RAZI-FULL(المخالف,الواسطي,داود); QURTUBI(ابن عطية,بعض العلماء,شقيق بن إبراهيم); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية,بعض العلماء,شقيق بن إبراهيم); BAYDAWI(النبي); BAYDAWI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); NASAFI; NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(الراغب); IBNKATHIR(ابن جرير,ابن عباس,الجوهري); BIQAI-FULL; DURR(أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي,ابن عباس,الله,رسول الله,سفيان الثوري,عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص); DURR-FULL(أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي,ابن عباس,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,سفيان الثوري,عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص); ITQAN(ابن عباس); BURSEVI; ALUSI(أنس,ابن عمر,بعضهم,داود,صاحب التجريد); ALUSI-FULL(ابن عمر,الإمام); ELMALILI(Resûl-i Ekrem); IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL(ذي الرمة,سيبويه); TABATABAI(علي); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(النبي,بعضهم,لسان العرب); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; STUDYQURAN(Prophet Muhammad); MUQATIL; TAB(ابن عباس,الحكم بن عمير,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,قيل,كعب الأحبار); TAB-FULL(الأسود بن سريع,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,عبد الله بن عباس,كعب الأحبار); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس,الضحاك,علي,كعب); THALABI(ابن عباس,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,قوم); MAWARDI; MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو بكر,بعض العلماء); WAHIDI-QT; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; DAMGHANI; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI; BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA(الطبري); ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; IBNATIYYA(بعض الناس)+; RAZI-FULL+]
- 1:2/q01/p2 Ḥamd praises beautiful attributes or acts. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (بعضهم): Some read Rabb in the accusative as a form of praise. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (القراء السبعة وجمهور الناس): The seven canonical readers and most people pronounce the dal of al-hamdu with nominative case. TABRISI, d. 548 (الأخفش وأبو علي الفارسي): They parse al-ḥamdu as a nominative subject with an omitted predication such as is due or is established for God; the phrase lillah supplies its meaning. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The nominative reading is stronger and more laudatory than an accusative meaning I praise, because it makes praise due to God from all creation, not only the speaker. TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن جني): Ibn Jinni says kasra on ḥamd and ḍamma on lillah signal a close subject-predicate bond, making the two parts sound like one word. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn may be accusative as praise or as an implied command to remember the Lord; a nominative praise reading is also possible, as poetry shows. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Readers agree on ḍamma in al-ḥamdu and kasra in lillāh; three noncanonical vowel combinations are also transmitted. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (بعض المفسرين): One of six Qur’anic senses is every living being, as in the opening praise of God. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Al-ḥamd is nominative as the subject and lillāh its predicate: praise is established and belongs to God. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن السميفع): Ibn al-Sumayfa reads al-ḥamda with fatḥa on dāl and kasra on lām. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أبو نهيك): Abū Nuhayk reads both dāl and lām in al-ḥamd lillāh with kasra. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (قيل): Another grammatical account takes the wording as a declarative form whose intended force is a command: say, “Praise belongs to God.” RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi sketches the sūra’s sequence: seek refuge from sins, begin good works in God’s name, praise and recognize his lordship and mercy, heed judgment, worship and seek help, ask guidance, and avoid ignorance and error. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Praise is praise for a blessing, and praise for a blessing requires knowing it; God’s blessings cannot be counted, as the Qur’an says. | mentions 14:34, 16:18 RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi links the statement that God subjected what is in the heavens and earth for people to the vast created benefits contained in praise. | mentions 45:13 RAZI, d. 606 (author): Because all these innumerable blessings and their wisdom fall under praise to God, the phrase “Praise belongs to God” encompasses about a million questions, more or less. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Many names indicate the honor of the named; Fātiḥat al-Kitāb is so called because it opens codices, teaching, recitation, and prayer, or because praise opens speech, or because it was the first sura sent down. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): It is called Sūrat al-Ḥamd because its opening word is praise. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A second reason for calling it the Mother of the Book is that all revelation comprises praise, worshipful service, and requests for unveiling; the sura includes each in that order. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A third reason is that all knowledge concerns either divine lordship or human servitude: the opening praises God’s mastery, while the remainder shows the servant’s dependence on His aid and guidance. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A fourth reason is that the sura gathers theology, religious duties, and spiritual purification: praise and lordship establish divine existence and attributes, worship presents duties, and guidance seeks unveiling. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The phrase al-ḥamdu lillāh raises questions about God’s existence and right to praise; Rabb al-ʿālamīn answers the first, while al-Raḥmān, al-Raḥīm, and Mālik yawm al-dīn answer the second. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise is due to the one who possesses everything in the heavens. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Deserving praise shows God acts by choice, not by necessity: fire and ice are not praised for natural effects, while changing effects prove a voluntary, powerful agent. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Anʿām’s praise for creating the heavens, earth, darkness, and light is one subset of Rabb al-ʿālamīn; dependence on God for preservation entails dependence for origination. | mentions 6:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Kahf’s praise for the revealed Book concerns spiritual nurture through knowledge and unveiling, one kind of the Fātiḥa’s universal nurturing. | mentions 18:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The world's evident order and its fitness for human interests show that its maker knows; praise thus implies God's existence, transcendence from place and indwelling, and utmost power, knowledge, and wisdom. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since beneficial ease and safety predominate, the agent is merciful and beneficent and deserves praise; these familiar facts make the reason for praising God apparent to every rational person. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Recognizing the true cause detaches the heart from apparent means: every benefit comes from God and every harm is attributed to Him, so praise and requests belong to God alone. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The rightly guided caliphs consistently recited al-Fatiha; reports in both Sahihs say the Prophet, Abu Bakr, and Umar began recitation with al-Hamd, so their practice should be followed. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): In the divided-prayer report, God calls the basmala glorification, al-Hamd praise, the two mercy names extolling, the Master of Judgment entrustment, and the remaining response a sharing and a request granted. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise is praise for blessing, and praising a blessing presupposes knowing it; God’s blessings cannot be counted, as the cited verse says. | mentions 14:34 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Considering divine wisdom in the throne, heavens, luminaries, elements, minerals, plants, and animals adds vast further questions; most of creation is made for human benefit, and al-hamd lillah thus encompasses roughly a million issues. | mentions 45:13 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The formula says al-hamdu lillah rather than ahmadu Allah so it declares God worthy of praise before any creature praises Him, from eternity to eternity. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The declarative al-ḥamdu lillāh is preferable to ahmadu Allāh: it establishes praise as God’s right instead of reporting one speaker’s act. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The lām in lillāh can signify fitting اختصاص, ownership, or power and dominion; each makes all praise belong to God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ḥamdu lillāh has eight letters corresponding to Paradise’s eight gates; saying it with a pure heart merits those gates. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): One grammatical view makes definite al-ḥamd refer to a prior known instance, or otherwise to all praise to avoid ambiguity. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A second grammatical view says the definite noun denotes only the essence or nature of praise, not universal scope. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): On either grammatical analysis, the phrase denies that anyone besides God is independently entitled to praise. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A giver who seeks reward, praise, repayment, or release from stinginess seeks compensation and is not the true benefactor; God gives from intrinsic fullness. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Every blessing is a possible existent brought into being by God, directly or through means; therefore praise for blessing belongs to God. | mentions 16:53 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Human conceptions of perfection never reach God’s greater perfection, so people cannot praise Him as He deserves. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Worldly blessings are finite, but the praise phrase is infinite; what remains after accounting for a finite blessing is endless, warranting eternal reward. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise precedes tasbīḥ because it includes transcendence and adds God’s beneficence, making Him beyond mere completeness. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (Prophet): A report says that when a blessed servant says al-ḥamdu lillāh, God tells the angels that the servant returned something priceless for an unvalued gift. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ḥamdu lillāh encompasses every praise uttered or possible from all creatures, prophets, angels, saints, scholars, and future inhabitants of Paradise. | mentions 10:10 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A report says Adam’s first words were al-ḥamdu lillāh after he sneezed; the people of Paradise end with the same praise, so the world begins and ends with it. | mentions 10:10 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī rejects supplying an unspoken command ‘say’: the phrase is already a complete assertion that praise belongs to God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The assertion makes God intrinsically deserving of praise through His essence and acts, whether people praise Him or not; what is essential exceeds what depends on another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Stating that praise is due, rather than directly commanding it, lets the obedient praise God while leaving the disobedient with a lesser offense than rejecting an order. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Jabriyya argue that if humans created faith, the human would deserve more praise for this noblest act than God; therefore God creates faith. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Jabriyya argue that Muslims praise God for the blessing of faith; if faith were a human act, praising God for it would wrongly credit Him with another’s act. | mentions 3:188 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Jabriyya argue that obligatory acts only avert blame and cannot earn praise, while voluntary grace would make God gain praise and thus be perfected by another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): They further argue that if God is praised intrinsically, no act causes that entitlement, so no duty, compensation, or reward is owed; if it is not intrinsic, God would depend on another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الجبرية): The Jabriyya infer that faith too comes from God: all praise belongs to Him, praise follows blessing, and faith is the greatest blessing. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الجبرية): They argue that God’s self-praise, though blameworthy among people, shows divine acts cannot be judged by human standards and challenges Muʿtazilī principles. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (المعتزلة): The Muʿtazila say absolute praise is deserved by one whose acts contain no evil, rulings no injustice, and judgments no oppression; they reject the Jabriyya picture of God creating disbelief then punishing it. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (المعتزلة): They argue that if a servant deserves God’s praise, the servant must be capable of action, contrary to determinism; if God praises Himself, that is also invalid, so the phrase only works on their doctrine. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): True praise is any act expressing reverence for a benefactor: inward belief in perfection, spoken words of praise, or bodily deeds that express it; saying al-ḥamdu lillāh merely reports praise. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): One group says God cannot command praise: if it repays a blessing, the command makes generosity transactional; if not, it is needless toil imposed on another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The same group says praise is useless to a God complete in Himself and that punishing people eternally for omitting a useless act would not befit the generous Lord. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Created nurturers seek their own profit, such as reward or praise, while God nurtures for the creatures’ benefit rather than His own. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The sequence assigns praise to God, then names Him Lord of the worlds: Allah signifies necessary perfection, while Lord of the worlds shows that all else flows from His generosity, making Him beyond completeness. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The seven Fatiha verses remove seven vices: praise dispels desire, Lord of the worlds greed, Judgment Day anger, worship and aid pride and vanity, guidance caprice, the favored path unbelief and doubt, and the final phrase innovation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الله تعالى): In a divine report transmitted by the Prophet, God responds to each Fatiha phrase: the opening remembers Him, praise praises Him, the mercy names magnify Him, Judgment Day glorifies Him or is entrusted to Him, worship belongs to Him, aid relies on Him, and guidance is the servant's request. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The report that God says “my servant praised Me” shows that praise is higher than remembrance. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise frames creation: the angels speak of it before Adam is created, and the inhabitants of Paradise end their invocation with it. | mentions 2:30, 10:10 QURTUBI, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report from Abu Hurayra and Abu Saʿid says that when a servant praises God, God affirms that the servant has spoken truly and that praise belongs to Him. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Malik al-Ashʿari reports from the Prophet that purification is half of faith, praise fills the balance, and glorification with prai se fills or fills the space between heaven and earth; Qurtubi cites Muslim. QURTUBI, d. 671 (طائفة): One group considers praise of God better than the declaration of unity because praise includes both tawhid and praise, while the declaration includes tawhid alone. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Muslims agree that God is praised for all blessings, including faith; since faith belongs to the created worlds, Qurtubi infers that faith is God’s act and creation, contrary to the Qadari claim that humans create it. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): In Arabic, al-ḥamd is complete praise; the definite article encompasses every kind of praise, all of which God deserves because He has the most beautiful names and highest attributes. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): The cited verse uses the word aḥmadī as a plural of ḥamd, supporting the linguistic discussion of the word’s forms. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Ḥamd is the opposite of blame; ḥamīd and maḥmūd describe one praised, and taḥmīd is more emphatic than ḥamd. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): The poet’s use of muḥammad for a person rich in praiseworthy qualities explains the name Muhammad; another verse says the Throne’s Lord is Maḥmūd and the Prophet is Muhammad. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports that God is pleased when a servant eats or drinks and praises Him for it; Qurtubi cites this from Muslim. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Maḥmada means the opposite of blame; aḥmada can mean one’s affairs turned praiseworthy or that one found a place pleasing, while a ḥamada is someone who overpraises things and ḥamadat al-nār is the sound of its flames. QURTUBI, d. 671 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās calls praise the word of every grateful person and illustrates its use by Adam, Noah, Abraham, David and Solomon, Muhammad, and the people of Paradise. | mentions 23:28, 14:39, 27:15, 17:111, 35:34, 10:10 QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Ḥamd can also mean satisfaction: one says I tested him and praised him to mean I was pleased with him; Qurtubi links this usage to maqāman maḥmūdan and a prophetic phrase about approving washing the urethra. | mentions 17:79 QURTUBI, d. 671 (الحسن): Al-Hasan says that no blessing exists for which praise of God is not better than the blessing itself. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): God praises Himself and opens His Book with praise, while people are forbidden to claim purity for themselves; Qurtubi cites the command not to praise yourselves and the report to throw dust in flatterers’ faces. | mentions 53:32 QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): God’s self-praise precedes any creature’s praise and is without cause, while created praise is mixed with ulterior causes. QURTUBI, d. 671 (علماؤنا): Qurtubi’s scholars say an imperfect creature should not praise himself to attract benefits or repel harm. QURTUBI, d. 671 (قيل): One explanation says God praised Himself eternally because His servants cannot fulfill the duty of praising Him; even the Messenger declared that he could not enumerate praise to God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet’s confession that he cannot enumerate praise to God illustrates the limits of human praise. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): A poet says that when people praise God rightly, He is as they praise Him and greater than their praise can express. QURTUBI, d. 671 (قيل): Another explanation says God praised Himself eternally because He knew His blessings would overwhelm servants’ ability to repay them, thereby easing the burden of indebtedness and making the blessing more enjoyable. QURTUBI, d. 671 (سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج): The seven readers and most people read the dāl in al-ḥamdu with nominative ḍamma; Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna and Ruʾba ibn al-ʿAjjāj are reported to read it accusative, with an implied verb. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports from the Prophet that when God gives a servant a blessing and the servant praises Him, the praise is better than what was received. QURTUBI, d. 671 (سيبويه): Sībawayh says nominative al-ḥamdu lillāh conveys that praise from the speaker and all creatures belongs to God, while the accusative reports only the speaker’s own praise. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الطبري): Al-Ṭabarī says God’s praise of Himself implicitly commands His servants to praise Him, as though the wording said, Say praise belongs to God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الحسن بن أبي الحسن وزيد بن علي): Al-Ḥasan ibn Abī al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn ʿAlī read the dāl of al-ḥamd with kasra by assimilation of the first vowel to the second. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report in Nawādir al-uṣūl from Anas says that if someone possessed the whole world and then praised God, the praise would be better than it. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Abu ʿAbd Allah explains that the person first receives the world and then the enduring word of praise, which is better because the world perishes while the word remains among the righteous deeds. | mentions 18:46 QURTUBI, d. 671 (الجنيد): A man praised God before al-Junayd but stopped before saying Lord of the worlds; al-Junayd urged him to complete the verse, explaining that created things vanish when paired with the Eternal. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb may be read in the nominative by syntactic disconnection, meaning He is Lord of the worlds, or in the accusative as a word of praise. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Another wording says that what the servant gave was more than what he received; this frames praise as the servant’s gift and the world as God’s gift, though both ultimately come from God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn ʿUmar reports that angels could not record a servant’s praise fitting God’s majesty, so God told them to write it as the servant had said until He rewards him. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas calls praise the expression of every grateful person, citing Adam’s praise at a sneeze and praise by Noah, Abraham, David and Solomon, the Prophet, and Paradise’s people. | mentions 23:28, 14:39, 27:15, 17:111, 35:34, 10:10 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Malik al-Ashʿari reports that purification is half of faith, praise fills the balance, and glorification with praise fills the space between heaven and earth. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (طائفة من العلماء): One group prefers alhamdulillah because it combines praise with the monotheism expressed by “There is no god but God.” QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Muslims agree God is praised for all blessings; since faith is a blessing and belongs among all created things, faith is God’s act and creation, against the Qadariyya. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Hamd means complete praise; the definite article encompasses every kind of praise, all of which God deserves for His beautiful names and exalted attributes. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): A poet uses hamd in a small-number plural form; hamd is the opposite of blame, tahmid is more emphatic, and Muhammad names one rich in praiseworthy qualities. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet admitted that he could not enumerate praise of God, illustrating the creature’s inability to fulfill God’s due praise. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Hamd can mean satisfaction, as in saying one was pleased with someone or in the “praised station”; a Prophetic expression likewise means “I approve for you.” | mentions 17:79 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (جعفر الصادق): Jaʿfar al-Sadiq offers an esoteric reading of the letters of hamd: unity, dominion, and permanence; knowing these attributes is the reality of praising God. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): God praises Himself and opens His Book with praise, while scripture and the Prophet forbid human self-praise; human praise is compromised by motives, unlike God’s eternal self-praise. | mentions 53:32 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (علماؤنا): The scholars say an imperfect creature should not praise himself to attract benefits or ward off harms. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (القراء السبعة): The seven readers and most people read alhamdu with the dāl in the nominative; Sufyan ibn ʿUyayna and Ruʾba report an accusative reading with an implied verb. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (سيبويه): Sibawayh says the nominative attributes praise to the speaker and all creation, while the accusative attributes it to the speaker alone. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الطبري): Al-Tabari says God’s praise of Himself implicitly commands people to praise Him; Arabic can omit the understood imperative, as in the cited poem. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Hurayra and Abu Saʿid report that when a servant says “Praise belongs to God,” God affirms the servant and says the praise belongs to Him. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports that God is pleased when a servant praises Him after eating a meal or drinking. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الحسن): Hasan says that no blessing is better than saying “Praise belongs to God.” QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report from Anas says that if someone owned the whole world and then said alhamdulillah, the praise would be better than it. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو عبد الله): Abu ʿAbd Allah explains that the enduring word is better because the world perishes, while praise remains among the righteous deeds; both world and praise ultimately come from God. | mentions 18:46 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): A variant says the gift was greater than what was received; this treats praise as the servant’s gift and the world as God’s, though both are from God, who enriches with the world and honors with praise in the afterlife. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn ʿUmar reports that angels could not record a servant’s praise “as befits” God’s majesty, so God told them to write it as spoken and reward him when they meet. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): If the article is exhaustive, all praise belongs to God because He bestows every good directly or indirectly; this implies He is living, powerful, willing, and knowing. | mentions 16:53 BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): The accusative rabb al-ʿālamīn is analyzed as praise, a vocative, or an implied verb indicated by “praise.” BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Al-ḥamdu is nominative as subject with “to God” as predicate, though an accusative reading is also reported. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): The nominative conveys universal and permanent praise belonging to God rather than newly arising praise. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Ḥamd is a verbal noun normally governed by an implicit, rarely used verb; its article may denote the genus or point to the familiar idea of praise. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): An accusative reading treats رب العالمين as praise, direct address, or an implied action indicated by الحمد. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The word is nominative as a subject with لله as predicate, though an accusative reading exists; nominative signals praise as universal and enduring rather than newly arising. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The definite article can mark the genus or all praise: in reality every good comes from God, directly or through an intermediary, as 16:53 says. | mentions 16:53 BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The entitlement to praise implies that God is living, powerful, willing, and knowing. NASAFI, d. 710 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): Abu Hurayra reports God’s dialogue dividing the Fatiha between Himself and the servant: praise, glorification, and exaltation belong to God; the worshipper’s request for guidance and the response to it belong to the servant, while worship and aid are shared. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Hamd is describing someone with praise and excellence, giving preference to the praiseworthy quality. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): For Nasafi, the definite article in al-hamd is comprehensive, against the Mu‘tazila; its attachment to Allah, the name of the divine essence, gathers all perfections and reflects the doctrine of created acts. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The report begins God's response when the servant says “Praise belongs to God,” which indicates the basmala is not part of al-Fatiha; if it is outside al-Fatiha, consensus excludes it from other surahs too. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Al-hamd is beautiful praise given preferentially; syntactically it is a nominative subject with an implied predicate such as “due” or “established,” while a reading with an implicit verb makes it an accusative verbal noun. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The shift from accusative to nominative indicates that praise is settled and enduring. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The definite article in al-hamd is exhaustive according to the author's school, against the Mu'tazila; joining it to Allah's proper name gathers every perfection and rests on the doctrine of created acts. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): Praise in action and in the tongue of each thing’s state is the appearance of its perfections and attainment of its ends, a fitting opening tribute to its Lord. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): All beings praise and glorify God through their particular properties, orientation toward ends, and actualization of potential; their dependence on Him alone denies partners and deficiency and manifests His majesty and beauty. The Qur’an says every thing glorifies Him. | mentions 17:44 QASHANI, d. 736 (author): All praise belongs to God eternally according to His due, at beginning, end, and everything between; He is both praiser and praised, worshipper and worshipped, in detail and in unity. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الجمهور): The majority read al-ḥamdu with ḍamma on the dāl. Ibrāhīm ibn Abī ʿAbla assimilates its mīm to the following lām; al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn ʿAlī assimilate the dāl's kasra to the lām's, a stranger assimilation. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (هارون العتكي ورؤبة وسفيان بن عيينة): Hārūn al-ʿAtakī, Ruʾba, and Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna read al-ḥamda in the accusative. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The article in al-ḥamd may be anaphoric, defining the familiar praise; essential, defining the nature; or generic, encompassing all praises. A verbal noun is ordinarily not pluralized. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (ابن الأعرابي): Ibn al-Aʿrābī records the plural aḥmad for ḥamd, apparently treating its different kinds as distinct, and cites a poem using aḥmadī. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (القراء السبعة): The nominative reading is stronger and agreed upon by the seven canonical readers: it states that praise is established and enduringly belongs to God, including praise by God and others. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): With nominative al-ḥamdu the lām marks entitlement; with accusative al-ḥamda a verb such as “I praise God” must be supplied, conveying renewed action. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): For the accusative reading, Abū Ḥayyān prefers an omitted verb directly indicated by the wording, such as “I praise God,” over “I say” or “adhere to praise.” ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (زيد بن علي وطائفة): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and a group read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative as praise; the reading weakens if the following adjectives remain genitive. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects parsing accusative rabb as an implied verb of praise or as apposition: one rests on imagined syntax, the other is interrupted by raḥmān raḥīm. | mentions 1:3 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Ḥamd is verbal praise for beauty, whether based on a blessing or not, spoken with the tongue; it is the opposite of blame. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الجمهور): The majority pronounce the dāl of al-ḥamdu with ḍamma. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (هارون العتكي، ورؤبة، وسفيان بن عيينة): Hārūn al-ʿAtakī, Ruʾba, and Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna read al-ḥamda in the accusative. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects deriving the accusative rabb from a prior implied verb such as We praise God, since this analogy to ellipsis in coordination is not productive. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The definite article on ḥamd may indicate a known instance, the abstract quality, or the entire genus of praise; the last implies exhaustive praise. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (ابن الأعرابي): Ibn al-Aʿrābī records the plural aḥmad for ḥamd, as if accounting for its varied kinds, and Abū Ḥayyān quotes a poetic example. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The nominative reading is semantically stronger and unanimously adopted by the Seven because it states that praise is established for God. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The accusative reading needs an implied governor such as I praise God or I praised God; it presents praise as newly occurring and names its agent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (زيد بن علي وطائفة): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and a group read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative as praise, though lowering the following epithets weakens the construction. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): God's lordship and mercy make Him worthy of praise; lordship as mastery or ownership is followed fittingly by mercy, which sustains servants' hope after failure. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The definite article in al-ḥamd intensifies praise by making it comprehensive. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Some rhetoricians call “Praise be to God” a statement whose intended force is command, like “no doubt in it” meaning “do not doubt.” | mentions 2:2 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The lām in “to God” restricts all praise to Him, and the annexation “Master of the Day of Judgment” underscores His sole dominion when other dominions vanish. | mentions 40:16 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The accusative reading of “al-ḥamd” involves ellipsis; grammarians differ over whether its omitted governing word repeats the noun or is another word. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The article in al-hamd is analyzed as universal, generic, or referring to known praise; al-Zamakhshari chooses the generic reading. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari rejects the universal reading; the author infers that praise here is performed by the servant, who cannot produce every praise by himself and others. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Hamd is fundamentally a verbal noun and therefore normally has no dual or plural; Ibn al-A'rabi nevertheless reports an af'al plural with poetic evidence. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading raises al-hamd as subject with a deleted nominal predicate, the preferred estimate, though a verb can also be supplied. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): A rare accusative al-hamda is parsed either as a verbal noun replacing I praise, or as an imperative-like source meaning say praise; al-Tabari favors the command interpretation, but the author prefers declarative force. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Raising al-hamd is stronger and more expressive of stable praise, while accusative signals renewal; Abraham's salām is better than the angels' salāman by the verse's instruction to answer more beautifully. | mentions 11:69, 4:86 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The kasra in a variant al-hamdi may be vowel harmony with the following lām, a Tamim and some Ghatafan dialect feature; another possibility is hidden nominative or accusative inflection. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading makes rabb genitive as an adjective or appositive to Allah; accusative allows an implied I praise, a detached praise, or vocative, with vocative weakest because it separates adjective from noun; nominative is detached predicate huwa rabb. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): When a known noun is described by praise, blame, or compassion, its adjective may follow or detach with an implicit verb or subject; no explicit governor is allowed. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (القراء السبعة): The seven canonical readers vocalize the dāl of al-ḥamdu with ḍamma, making al-ḥamdu a subject and li-llāhi its predicate. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (علي بن أبي طالب): When Umar asks what al-ḥamdu lillāh means after learning other formulas of glorification, Ali answers that it is a phrase God approves for Himself. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (كعب الأحبار): Ka'b al-Ahbar says al-ḥamdu lillāh is God's praise. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak calls al-ḥamdu lillāh the cloak of the Most Merciful and notes that a hadith has come with a similar meaning. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج): Sufyan ibn Uyaynah and Ru'bah ibn al-Ajjaj read al-ḥamda with fatḥa as an implied verbal object. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report attributed to the Prophet says that whoever says al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn has thanked God, who then increases him. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Companion al-Aswad ibn Sari asks to recite praises of his Lord, and the Prophet replies that his Lord loves praise. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says the best remembrance is lā ilāha illā Allāh and the best supplication is al-ḥamdu lillāh; al-Tirmidhi grades this report ḥasan gharīb. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports that when a servant praises God for a blessing, the reward for that praise is better than the blessing received. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (القرطبي): Al-Qurtubi transmits a report that praising God would be better than possessing the whole world; he explains that the enduring reward for praise exceeds transient worldly enjoyment. | mentions 18:46 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn Umar reports that angels cannot calculate the full reward for a servant's praise fitting God's majesty, so God tells them to record it as said until He rewards him. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (طائفة): A group holds that al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn is better than lā ilāha illā Allāh because it combines praise with monotheism. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): The definite article in al-ḥamdu encompasses every kind and form of praise as belonging to God, as expressed in a transmitted prayer. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir says the phrase is God's praise of Himself and implicitly commands His servants to say it too. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas calls al-ḥamdu lillāh the expression used by every grateful person. BURHAN, d. 794 (author): The Fātiḥa has the usual order الحمد لله; in al-Jāthiya فلله الحمد answers the implied question of who deserves praise, like the question about today's dominion and its answer. | mentions 40:16 BURHAN, d. 794 (author): The successive praises intensify the servant's attention: God's exclusive praise, universal lordship, gifts of mercy, and command of judgment prepare the worshipper to approach and seek help. BURHAN, d. 794 (قيل): One explanation says praise uses third person because it ranks below worship, while direct address elevates the worshipper's encounter; similarly, blessing is explicitly attributed to God but anger is phrased obliquely as a matter of etiquette. | mentions 6:101 BURHAN, d. 794 (author): These three senses can coincide in praise of God: He deserves praise, has authority over it, and is the one to whom it belongs. NASHR, d. 833 (author): A pause on an incomplete fragment such as “bi-smi,” “al-ḥamdu,” or “rabbi” leaves the utterance unfinished and without an intelligible meaning. NASHR, d. 833 (إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة): Ibrahim b. Abi Ablah reads the first lām in al-ḥamdulillāh with ḍamma. NASHR, d. 833 (الحسن البصري): Al-Hasan reads the dāl of al-ḥamdulillāh with kasra; Ibn al-Jazari says both this and the preceding form are linguistically distant, explained as vowel-following. NASHR, d. 833 (زيد بن علي بن الحسين، رؤبة بن العجاج، هارون بن موسى العتكي، الحسن): These readers pronounce the dāl of al-ḥamdu with fatḥa; the accusative is treated as a verbal noun with its commonly understood verb omitted. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): Because the basmala is a kind of praise, it is naturally followed by the comprehensive name al-ḥamd; this implicitly calls people to praise God as the one deserving every praise. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): People should make this form of praise open their affairs, since it awakens hope and fear of God and leads to steadfastness on guidance. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): Al-ḥamd lillāh establishes that God deserves every praise by His essence and all perfections; Rabb adds that He deserves praise as Lord, Owner, and Benefactor. BIQAI, d. 885 (أبو الحسن الحرالي): Al-Ḥarālī defines ḥamd as complete praise encompassing every action and attribute: all are from God and all are praise untouched by blame. BIQAI, d. 885 (أبو الحسن الحرالي): Al-Ḥarālī says that when praise is understood to pervade everything, the definite form al-ḥamd signals its completion; al- marks the endpoint and perfection of what it qualifies. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha's purpose is to establish God's exclusive right to all praise and perfection, sovereignty over both worlds, worship and aid, and to ask for the path of the successful rather than the doomed; its center is مراقبة. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Since the basmala is a kind of praise, it naturally leads to comprehensive al-hamd: begin affairs by praising God as the one deserving every praise, with hope and awe guiding the way. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Al-hamd lillah establishes that God deserves praise for His essence and perfection; Rabb al-alamin adds His lordship, sovereignty and beneficence, while the worlds point to creation's beginning and the inference to resurrection. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali defines al-hamd as complete praise covering all actions and attributes, all from God and free of blame; its definite form signals praise reaching its full extent. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Lordship does not yield wellbeing without mercy, so Rahman and Rahim follow al-hamd and encourage people to persist in praising God. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali explains the sound report that God divided prayer between Himself and His servant: the first three verses are God's praise, which He accepts from the reciting servant as praise and glorification. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (أبو هريرة): In the authentic report in Muslim and the four Sunan, God says the Fatiha is divided between Himself and His servant: He answers praise, glorification and worship, and grants the servant's final requests. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali maps al-Fatiha's verses to the seven letters: praise, lawful and forbidden, command and prohibition, clear and ambiguous, then the corresponding petitions; its opening gathers all seven. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Because praise is the seventh inclusive letter, the Fatiha begins with it and gathers the seven universals diffused through the Qur'an, as the Qur'an gathers earlier scriptures. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): The first verse contains praise's seventh letter; the second contains the lawful and forbidden letters that Rahmaniyyah establishes for worldly life, allowing enjoyment and removing either prohibition's burden or sin. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): The fifth verse's request returns to praise, the sixth to the grace of lawful and forbidden provisions, and the seventh to the sovereign's command; together these complete all seven letters. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says the best remembrance is there is no god but God, while the best supplication is al-hamdulillah; al-Tirmidhi grades the report hasan. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): In a hasan report, the Prophet says praise after a blessing is better than the blessing received. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says al-hamd is better than any blessing bestowed on a servant. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): Al-Hasan transmits that every blessing praised by the servant is surpassed by praise of God, whatever the blessing may be. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says that if someone possessed the entire world and then said al-hamdulillah, the praise would be better than it all. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says purification is half of faith and al-hamd fills the balance; other phrases fill the space between heaven and earth, while prayer, charity, patience, and the Quran serve as signs or testimony. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A man of Banu Sulaym reports that the Prophet said al-hamd fills the balance, while other remembrances fill the space between heaven and earth; purification and fasting are each half of a measure. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says glorification is half the balance and al-hamd fills it; there is no barrier between God and the declaration of divine unity. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): When al-Aswad ibn Sariʿ offers the Prophet praises he composed for God, the Prophet replies that his Lord loves praise; al-Hakim authenticates the report. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says nothing is more beloved to God than praise, so God praised Himself by saying al-hamdulillah. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): In a weakly chained report, the Prophet treats saying al-hamdulillah after his stolen camel returned as fulfilling his vow to thank God. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet contrasts divine forbearance with satanic haste and says nothing is more beloved to God than praise. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A report from Anas says tawhid is the price of Paradise and al-hamdulillah the price of every blessing; people share Paradise according to their deeds. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says every important matter begun without praise of God is deficient. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas describes an exchange at sneezing: the sneezer says al-hamdulillah, an angel answers Lord of the worlds, and after the sneezer repeats it the angel asks God to show mercy. DURR, d. 911 (علي بن أبي طالب): Ali says that whoever says the full praise at every sneeze heard, in any circumstance, will never suffer toothache or earache. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says whoever anticipates a sneezer with praise will be spared abdominal illness. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): Ali reports that the Prophet vowed to thank God properly if his family expedition returned safely; on their return he said al-hamdulillah for God's abundant favors and counted that as fulfilling the vow. DURR, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad tells how he vowed to praise God with approved praises if his mule returned, then said al-hamdulillah alone because it encompasses all praise. DURR, d. 911 (منصور بن إبراهيم): Mansur ibn Ibrahim transmits the saying that al-hamdulillah is the expression whose reward is multiplied most. DURR, d. 911 (علي): When Umar asks what al-hamd is, Ali answers that it is a phrase God chose for Himself and loves people to say. DURR, d. 911 (كعب): Kaʿb defines al-hamd as praise of God. DURR, d. 911 (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak metaphorically calls al-hamd the mantle of the All-Merciful. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (مجاهد): Mujāhid says the revelation of al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabb al-ʿālamīn greatly distressed Iblīs, who groaned and snorted; those who groan or snort are accursed. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (رجاء الغنوي): Rajāʾ al-Ghanawī attributes to the Prophet a healing practice using God's own praise of Himself, naming al-ḥamdu lillāh and al-Ikhlāṣ; whoever is not healed by the Qur'an is not healed by God. | mentions 112:1 DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الله تعالى): When the servant says “al-hamd lillah,” God says the servant has thanked and praised Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الحكم بن عمير): In a weak report, saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” counts as thanking God, after which He increases the speaker. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says that when the servant says al-hamd, God says the servant has thanked Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي): Ali answers Umar that al-hamd is a phrase God approved for Himself and loves to be spoken. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (كعب): Kab says “al-hamd lillah” is praise of God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak calls al-hamd the cloak of the Merciful. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says the best remembrance is “la ilaha illa Allah” and the best supplication is “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A sound report says the praise offered for any divine blessing is better than the blessing received; related reports extend this to every blessing and all the world. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says purification is half of faith, al-hamd fills the balance, and glorification fills the space between heaven and earth, among other deeds' spiritual benefits. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Another report says al-hamd fills the balance, while tasbih is half of it and takbir fills the space between heaven and earth. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn Umar reports that al-hamd fills the balance and there is no barrier between “la ilaha illa Allah” and God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الأسود بن سريع): Al-Aswad ibn Sari asked to recite praises he had composed, and the Prophet replied that God loves praise. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says nothing is more beloved to God than praise, so God praised Himself with “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أنس): Anas reports that deliberation is from God, haste from Satan, God is most ready to excuse, and praise is most beloved to Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أنس): A report says monotheism is the price of Paradise and al-hamd repays each blessing, while people receive Paradise according to their deeds. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو هريرة): Abu Hurayra reports that every significant matter begun without praise of God is deficient. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): When someone sneezes and says al-hamd, an angel responds “Lord of the worlds”; when the sneezer repeats it, the angel says “may God have mercy on you.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي بن أبي طالب): A report attributed to Ali says that saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” at each heard sneeze prevents toothache and earache. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): After an expedition returned safely, the Prophet fulfilled his pledge to thank God by saying “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): When Jaafar ibn Muhammad's mule returned, he thanked God with only “al-hamd lillah,” saying this gathered all praise. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (منصور بن إبراهيم): Mansur ibn Ibrahim reports the saying that al-hamd is the most multiplied speech. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أم سلمة): Aisha reports that the Prophet counted the basmala as verse one, al-hamd as two, al-Rahman al-Rahim as three, Malik yawm al-din as four, then indicated five for the next verse. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): A good stop may be made, but starting with the words that follow is not sound; the opening praise is the example. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Al-ḥamdu li-llāh is read with nominative case as a subject, accusative as a verbal noun, or genitive by vowel assimilation. ITQAN, d. 911 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari says the Fatiha encompasses praise, worship through commands and prohibitions, and promise and warning; every Qur’anic verse falls under one of these topics. ITQAN, d. 911 (الإمام فخر الدين): Fakhr al-Din maps the Qur’an’s four aims—God, resurrection, prophethood, and divine decree—onto the Fatiha: praise, Judgment Day, worship and reliance, and guidance through the surah’s end; this supports its name Umm al-Qur’an. | mentions 1:2, 1:4, 1:6, 1:7 ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن أبي جمرة): Explaining the scale of commentary, Ibn Abi Jamra says the opening requires explaining praise, the divine name and transcendence, and the worlds, their nature, types, number, and extent. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The opening praise and names of the Merciful indicate God's divinity. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): One quality can be both what a person is praised by and what they are praised for, as when someone is seen giving generously or praying; the two roles arise under different aspects. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Praising God for essential attributes raises a problem if ḥamd requires voluntary acts, since those attributes may be identical with or additional to the divine essence. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): One solution treats praise of divine attributes as praise for what is effectively voluntary because God’s essence suffices for those attributes. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): One analysis treats the accusative as detachment from the preceding construction, supplying a verb of praise or mention; another says the prior الحمد licenses the construction. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī concludes that lexical ḥamd is praise for voluntary acts; praise of essential attributes is either figurative through their voluntary effects or conventional praise, both unobjectionable. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الصدر): The praiser must exalt the praised one through praise both outwardly and inwardly. ALUSI, d. 1270 (المحققون): The discerning scholars do not require the praiser to believe the praised quality actually exists; it suffices that the description not accompany an assertion of contempt. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الجنيد): Junayd was asked about the spiritual end and answered that it is returning to the beginning, an apt pattern for the sūra’s opening praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī rejects the replies that a praiser must believe the literal description or must intend a metaphorical description; denial of a statement’s content does not prevent its rhetorical use. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The fifth element is an expression that manifests the praised one’s praiseworthiness; speech is conventionally singled out because the tongue is the usual instrument of speaking. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): A person without a tongue can still praise through lip sounds or speech produced in another organ, as witnessed in a person lacking the whole tongue. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The Qurʾānic statement that every thing glorifies God with praise shows that praise is not confined to utterance by a human tongue. | mentions 17:44 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الأكثر): Most scholars explain God’s self-praise as declaring His entitlement to praise, commanding praise, quoting the words of servants, or figuratively displaying His perfections; al-Sayyid favors the last account. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawwānī considers figurative divine praise unnecessary because the people of truth affirm that God truly speaks; al-Ālūsī reads the tongue restriction as relative or customary. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The lexical reality of ḥamd is manifesting perfection; spoken praise is merely its most obvious and familiar form, while manifestation through action is even fuller. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): In its familiar lexical sense, ḥamd is verbal praise of beauty, whether the praised excellence is an inherent virtue or a bestowed favor. ALUSI, d. 1270 (النواس بن سمعان): Al-Ṭabarānī reports from al-Nawwās b. Samʿān that the Prophet vowed to thank God if his she-camel returned; when it did, he said al-ḥamdu lillāh, and the Companions expected further prayer or fasting. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The act of ḥamd involves five elements: what is praised by, what is praised for, a praiser, a praised one, and an expression indicating the praised quality. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Although tasbīḥ commonly precedes taḥmīd, the sūra omits it because praise includes declaring God free of defects while the reverse is not always true; tasbīḥ elsewhere serves a different purpose. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Tasbīḥ signals that God is free of defects, while ḥamd also points to His positive beneficence and the knowledge, power, and sufficiency that make it possible. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Alternatively, every tasbīḥ is a kind of praise, but ḥamd is broader because it includes both negative and positive attributes while tasbīḥ addresses negative attributes. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الإمام الرازي): Al-Rāzī allows the praised quality to be positive or privative, transitive or intransitive, and voluntary or involuntary; al-Dawwānī and others endorse this account. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī takes the article in al-ḥamdu as generic: it points to the familiar notion of praise, as in Labīd’s verse describing the camels. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī calls the exhaustive interpretation of al-ḥamdu a mistaken assumption held by many. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī paraphrases the phrase as we praise God with a praise, then treats إياك نعبد as an answer explaining how that praise is performed. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers an exhaustive reading here: the sūra’s encompassing attributes of lordship, mercy, and sovereignty support all praise belonging to God, and the shift to إياك نعبد marks a new address rather than an explanation. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Zamakhsharī’s statement in 64:1 that sovereignty and praise belong exclusively to God supports a comprehensive reading, though the present context gives further reason to include every instance of praise. | mentions 64:1 ALUSI, d. 1270 (أبو العباس المرسي): Al-Mursī says the article is covenantal because God praised Himself eternally on behalf of creatures unable to encompass His praise, supported by the prophetic supplication that He is as He praised Himself. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufis understand the praise as belonging to God Himself, like sovereignty and creation-command: He is both praiser and praised, and all things are His manifestations. | mentions 7:54 ALUSI, d. 1270 (العلامة في شرح التهذيب): A view reported and adopted in Sharḥ al-Tahdhīb requires the quality praised and the ground for praise to be voluntary. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الماتريدي): Al-Māturīdī says God praised Himself in order to teach creatures, and this is fitting because He inherently deserves praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The transmitted and standard reading has al-ḥamdu in the nominative as a subject with lillāhi as its predicate. ALUSI, d. 1270 (هارون بن موسى): Hārūn b. Mūsā reads al-ḥamda in the accusative, a construction common among Arabs for a definite verbal noun with an implied verb such as نحمد. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The fact that people do not praise stature or a bright face, or that a pearl goes unpraised, cannot by itself prove whether the restriction lies in the praised quality or the ground for praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The nominative reading is strongest because the nominal sentence, in this context, conveys settled and enduring praise rather than a renewed act of praising. ALUSI, d. 1270 (معظم العلماء): Most scholars consider الحمد لله informative: otherwise God’s possession of the praised qualities would begin only when the speaker utters the praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (ابن الهمام): Ibn al-Humām argues that ḥamd is performative because it manifests qualities rather than establishing them, so praise need not be informative. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī sides with the majority: the sentence informs that God deserves praise, while a believe r who says it intending reverent description is rightly called a praiser. | mentions 28:70 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The servants’ contingent praise relates to God without inhering in Him, just as knowledge relates to its objects without becoming part of them. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some take the verbal noun in a passive sense, meaning that God is the one praised; because its form allows several construals, early commentators counted forty-two possibilities. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): A particularly unusual parsing makes the lām causal, so that praise is established for God’s sake. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Naming God by His essential name avoids suggesting that He deserves praise only through one particular attribute; entitlement by essence still means entitlement for divine excellence. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The thing praised for is the cause of the praise: its excellence becomes manifest through the praised person’s possession of it; most scholars include both acts and enduring qualities. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا): A Sufi account divides praise from God into praise of His essence, unity, manifestations, qualities, effects, and acts, while creaturely praise varies with knowledge and capacity. | mentions 6:91, 20:110, 37:180, 53:42 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): When the distinction between Creator and creation is considered together, all praise comes from God and returns to Him; there is no praiser or praised one apart from Him. | mentions 53:42 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Praise precedes the divine name because the speaker is about to perform the act of praise; in 30:18 the order is reversed for a different purpose. | mentions 30:18 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الإمام الرازي): Al-Rāzī appears to restrict the ground of praise to an act performed voluntarily by the praised person. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Because the book opens with the basmala, a kind of praise, God follows it with comprehensive praise at the highest degree of perfection. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Hamd conventionally means verbal praise for what is beautiful, whether a virtue or benefaction; its realization involves a praiseworthy attribute, its ground, a praiser, the praised, and an expression of that quality. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الإمام الرازي): Al-Rāzī and other authorities allow the praised attribute to be positive or negative, transitive or not, and voluntary or involuntary; al-Dūrānī, Ṣadr al-Afāḍil, and Mullā Khusraw also affirm this. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (العلامة في شرح التهذيب): An authority cited in Sharḥ al-Tahdhīb requires the praised attribute to be voluntary and adopts that condition; al-Ālūsī notes the parallel question about what praise is for. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The praised ground is what the praise answers to and displays as the subject’s perfection; one thing can be both attribute and ground under different aspects, while the public view includes actions and qualities.
