ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الإمام): The Imam appears to prefer a voluntary act as the ground of praise and to require that it arise from the praised person’s choice. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Several answers address praise for God’s essential attributes: treat them as voluntary because the divine essence suffices for them, define choice as willing-agent attribution, or say they occur by choice in the sense that God acts if He wills. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Some therefore do not require the praised ground to be voluntary, since it is what occasions praise; the verse about the Praised Station is cited as an example. | mentions 17:79 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī’s preferred linguistic account restricts hamd to voluntary acts; praise for essential attributes is either linguistic by reference to their voluntary effects or conventional, while the cited examples use praise metaphorically for approval. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The third element is the praiser, who must exalt the praised in outward expression and inwardly; the next segment debates whether inward belief must match the praise. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The tongue restriction is either relative to heart and limbs or merely usual; hamd originally means displaying perfection, with speech its most familiar form and action potentially fuller. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawānī explains that praise without belief matching its content would become mockery, taking belief to mean the act of inward exaltation rather than propositional assent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī answers that hamd can be performative, so correspondence to belief is inapplicable; ordinary speakers also use iʿtiqād to mean regard or esteem for someone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He rejects two replies—that the speaker believes the attributed quality, or that the praise is figurative—because the first contradicts plain experience and the second often contradicts the facts. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The fourth element is the praised object; the fifth is an expression showing that it possesses praiseworthiness, conventionally assigned to the tongue. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Tongue is the usual instrument, not an absolute condition: lip sounds without a tongue, or speech produced through another organ, still count as praise; the Qur’an says everything glorifies God’s praise. | mentions 17:44 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الأكثر): Most authorities interpret God praising Himself as reporting His entitlement to praise, commanding praise, speaking through servants, or figuratively displaying His perfect attributes; al-Sayyid favors the last explanation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Calling the state more eloquent than speech is a rhetorical claim: worshipful actions may be indistinguishable from ordinary habits, whereas verbal praise explicitly names God’s glory. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): One argument says Muʿtazilīs assign praise for human deeds to humans because they create their acts; the reply is that even good deeds depend on God’s power and enabling, so praise ultimately returns to Him. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī rejects the claim that genus is the obvious common reading: in rhetorical settings a definite generic article commonly conveys comprehensiveness, especially when exclusive praise is at issue. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Praise is like the head because speech makes meaning clearest, and the head is the most evident, elevated, and sustaining member; this may explain why God names praise first. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Every tasbīḥ counts as praise, not vice versa: glorification negates deficiencies, whereas praise also affirms positive qualities, so its comprehensiveness suits the Qur’an; priority changes with context. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The definite article in al-ḥamdu is taken here to indicate the familiar genus of praise, illustrated by a line of Labīd about the wild asses and their she-asses. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī says treating genus as perfection and treating comprehensive praise as exhaustive both require interpretation; making everything else negligible adds an unnecessary step. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): One account answers how the speakers enact praise by joining verbal praise to bodily and inward worship and seeking aid. | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A grammatical rationale treats al-ḥamd as a verbal noun standing in for its verb, so it denotes the underlying reality and can be explained by the worship that follows. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A further account makes worship the explanation of praise by combining verbal praise with bodily and inward devotion; al-Ālūsī says this does not require choosing genus over comprehensive scope. | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The sounder reading makes the first clause praise of God in the third person and the next a speaker’s self-report; the missing conjunction marks a change of state, not an explanation. | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The following divine attributes imply that God deserves praise for them; since their scope is comprehensive, the praise should likewise be comprehensive, with God encompassing all names and worlds and mercy extending universally. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): In al-Taghābun, al-Zamakhsharī says the fronted phrases “His is the dominion” and “His is praise” signal their exclusivity; praise of others recognizes that God’s favor reached them through those people. | mentions 64:1 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Even on the Muʿtazilī account, people’s praise does not displace God’s: He enables their acts and bestows the conditions, while their own agency merits a distinct, lesser praise. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Some read al-ḥamdu as true, complete praise alone belonging to God rather than all instances; al-Ālūsī says this meaning can be apt but rejects using it to evade comprehensive scope here. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He rejects limiting the expression to “true complete praise” as an ad hoc escape from comprehensiveness, even when supported by a leading commentator, because rhetorical discourse often speaks through persuasive address. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The claim that exclusive genus praise is more comprehensive than exclusive individuals is reversible: if every individual is exclusively God’s, no instance remains outside the exclusive genus. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو العباس المرسي): Al-Fākihī reports that Abū al-ʿAbbās al-Mursī told Ibn al-Naḥḥās the article is referential: God praised Himself eternally on creatures’ behalf because they could not praise Him adequately. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (النبي ﷺ): Al-Mursī appeals to the Prophet’s authentic supplication that people cannot enumerate God’s praise and that He is as He praised Himself. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufi masters say “praise belongs to God” is like “might belongs to God”: God is both praiser and praised, and all things are His affairs, as the verse says creation and command belong to Him. | mentions 7:54 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الماتريدي): Al-Māturīdī says God praised Himself to teach creatures; this is fitting because He deserves praise by His own essence and perfection. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The standard transmitted reading has al-ḥamdu nominative as the subject; Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and Zayd b. ʿAlī assimilate the vowel of dāl to the following lām, while Ibrāhīm b. Abla and Bedouins do the reverse. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (هارون بن موسى): Hārūn b. Mūsā reads al-ḥamda in the accusative, like Arabs who use definite verbal nouns with an omitted verb; the implied “we praise” suits worship and seeking help better than royal “we.” | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers the nominative nominal sentence because, in context, it expresses established and continuing praise, whereas a verbal sentence ordinarily presents renewed action; permanence also depends on context, not form alone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī opens a further question about whether “Praise belongs to God” is declarative or performative; most scholars call it declarative, following the apparent form. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الأكثر): The question is how a contingent human act of praise can be for God without inhering in Him; relation to an object does not entail inherence, as knowledge relates to its objects. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): A mystical account divides praise into praise originating from God and from creation: God’s praise concerns His essence, manifestations, attributes, and effects, while creatures praise His essence, names, actions, secrets, and wisdom according to their understanding. | mentions 6:91, 20:110, 37:180, 53:42 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): When all praise is considered together, it begins and ends in God: there is no praiser or praised one besides Him, and human understanding cannot encompass Him. | mentions 53:42 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Praise precedes the name “God” because the speaker is about to utter its meaning; the contextual need can outweigh the name’s greater intrinsic importance, unlike another verse whose arrangement has another purpose. | mentions 28:70 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن الهمام): Another interpretation parses al-ḥamd as a passive verbal noun meaning being praised; its form permits other readings, and some even take the lām as causal. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Naming God by His essence avoids suggesting that entitlement to praise belongs only to one attribute; essential entitlement means no particular attribute is specified, not that praise lacks a beautiful ground. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الملا خسرو): Entitlement by essence means entitlement without specifying a particular attribute; it does not mean God deserves praise apart from beautiful qualities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Mystically, praise originating from God ranges across His hidden essence, absolute existence, manifestations, attributes, effects, and knowledge in general or detail. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Mystical praise arising from creatures may concern God's essence, attributes, names, actions, mysteries, subtle workmanship, or hidden wisdom, according to each person's knowledge and understanding. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The question how servants’ created praise is “for” God is answered by distinguishing attachment from inherence, like knowledge attaching to its objects; another parses the noun as passive “being praised,” while a rare view makes the lām causal. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Mentioning the divine proper name avoids implying that entitlement to praise belongs only to one attribute; the entitlement belongs to the essence without isolating it from its attributes. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (زيد بن علي): A reading attributed to Zayd ibn ʿAli puts Rabb al-ʿalamin in the accusative; al-Alusi finds the first grammatical explanation, an implied praise or mention verb, most apparent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): One analysis treats the accusative as an adjective cut off from its antecedent, supplying an implied verb such as I praise or I mention; an objection about the following adjective is answered by taking Rahman as apposition. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو حيان): Abu Hayyan rejects deriving the accusative from the earlier praise merely by attraction, arguing that this mechanism belongs to coordination; al-Alusi disputes that objection using grammatical authorities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Another analysis supplies the earlier word al-ḥamd as the governing verb; objections concern the definite verbal noun's government and an intervening predicate, both answered by grammarians. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The descriptions close by pointing to resurrection, just as Lord of the worlds opened with origination; they explain the prior praise and prepare for the prayer that follows. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Fatiha’s successive clauses unfold the opening praise and return to alhamdu lillah; the surah is the root and comprehensive representative of the other Qur’anic surahs. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The basmala is the crown, the Qur’an a perfect body, Fatiha its head, and alhamdu lillah its face; mercy, guidance, worship, and divine unity express its spiritual life. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Resûl-i Ekrem): The Prophet says that one who does not praise people does not praise God, making hamd generally commendable. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Verbal expressions of reverence count as hamd; announcing a blessing is a moral virtue that praises its free agent and rightly rejoices in the happiness received. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The lām in lillah denotes entitlement, particularity, and ownership; the whole genus and every form of hamd belongs to God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Alhamdu lillah informs believers, implies reverent praise, and uses a timeless nominal sentence; the four following clauses explain it, making Fatiha one sentence in substance. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Human happiness depends on recognizing and tasting a blessing, not merely possessing it; the recipient’s expressed hamd lets the giver witness that the gift reached its fitting recipient. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Giving stands above receiving, so the station of the praised giver is the highest rank; the joy of this rank culminates in further hamd and increased gifts. | mentions 14:7 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Hamd advances through four ranks: praising, being praised, uniting both, then receiving praise while praising. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The promised station of praise belongs especially to the Seal of the Prophets and is the station of the great intercession, where he receives the banner of praise. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Through the Prophet’s intercession, the community gathered beneath the banner of praise shares in divine hamd, and the people of Paradise conclude their plea with this verse. | mentions 10:10 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Hamd’s fullest nature speaks through the Prophet’s Ahmediyyah and Muhammadiyyah; its ultimate reality belongs at its beginning and end to God alone. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The definite article in alhamd can express a known instance, totality, kind, or essence; because no particular hamd is presupposed, it here gathers the entire genus and all its ranks. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): He renders the verse as praise belonging to the Lord of the worlds. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): A sacred hadith says al-Fatiha is divided equally between God and servant, with the servant entitled to what is asked; its opening praises and glorifies God, its middle is shared, and its ending is the servant's supplication. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Nothing lies outside the basmala's attraction, so it relates generally to al-Fatiha as one recites, writes, praises, or begins the Quran in its name. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): After invoking the Merciful God, the worshipper turns to praise Him and name His unrestricted lordship over all worlds. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Praising God wells up in a believer's heart at His mention: existence itself and ceaseless blessings call forth praise. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Praise begins and ends the Islamic worldview; the Qur'an says God alone is deity and that praise belongs to Him in this world and the next. | mentions 28:70 QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله [ ص ]): Ibn Umar reports that the Prophet described a servant's expansive praise, which two angels could not record; God ordered them to write it as spoken and promised reward when the servant meets Him. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله): Abu Hurayra reports in Sahih Muslim that God calls al-Fatiha a prayer divided between Himself and His servant: praise, mercy, and glorification are the Lord's; worship and aid are shared; guidance and the servant's request belong to the servant, who receives what he asks. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A fourth rule for a preface is to begin with praise of God. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Arabic dignitary address begins with praise before a request; Umayya ibn Abi al-Salt says that praise of the addressee can itself satisfy the petitioner. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (أبو هريرة عن النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report from Abu Hurayra, attributed to the Prophet, says an important matter not begun with praise of God is cut off; Ibn Ashur gives no grading. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Muslims consequently called valuable speech lacking opening praise abtar; Ziyad ibn Abi Sufyan's Basran sermon was nicknamed al-Batra for this omission. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Hamd is praise of a beautiful quality, whether an intentional action such as generosity or aiding the distressed, or another quality such as courage. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Thanā is the broader category of favorable mention and is not the opposite of hamd; some anomalously use it for mention regardless of praise or blame. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Nithā, with initial n, applies to praise or blame and is more commonly used for blame. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): To allow praise of God's essential attributes under the majority's definition, scholars offer three replies: the attributes count as voluntary by their perfection, by their effects, or because God acts freely even when the praised attribute is not voluntary. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Hamd is the strongest language available to people for praising God, though it cannot fully convey realities beyond ordinary human experience. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative as the subject in every transmitted reading, and li-llah is its predicate, related to the general existential predicate. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayhi explains that praise expressions originally use an omitted verb in the absolute accusative, as in 'we praise God with praise'; the verbal noun replaces the verb. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The cited grammar says a definite source such as al-hamdu can be raised as a subject because it is declarative and definite; the sense of the omitted verb remains. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari also describes hamd's original accusative with an omitted verb and its shift to nominative as a means of indicating stability. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (سيبويه): A reading with al-hamda in the accusative and definite article is a Tamimi dialect form attested by Sibawayhi; its implied verb limits praise to the speaker or intended group. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Raising hamd announces praise from the speaker and all creation, whereas the accusative announces only the speaker's praise. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Praise is foregrounded because revelation of the Quran is the immediate great blessing being acknowledged; its contextual importance can take precedence over God's name's intrinsic importance. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The article in al-hamd marks a known genus: its referent is recognizable to speaker and hearer, making the expression emphatic rather than singling out one instance. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari rejects treating the article as grammatically exhaustive; Ibn Ashur agrees that it marks genus, while the construction's combined effect entails all praise. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The genus article with the possessive l am implies all praise belongs to God: if any instance belonged to another, exclusive possession of the genus would fail; this is hyperbolic restriction. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): One group treats the sentence as a plain report that praise belongs to God, objecting that this leaves the speaker not actually praising Him. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Defenders of a purely declarative analysis answer that reporting praise recognizes God's beautiful attributes, that the speaker is included in the general report, or that inherited practice entails following it. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A response says literal report may serve its implied praise, as saying 'long sword-strap' to imply tall stature. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Another group says the wording remains a report but its intended purpose is performative praise, like declarative wording for grief in 3:36; report form permits permanence, universality, exclusivity, and emphasis. | mentions 3:36 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (فريق ثان): A contrasting position calls the sentence a pure performative, shifted from reporting into praise like contracts and verbs of praise or blame. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur's preferred account is a report used to perform praise: the intention is performative, while report form supplies duration, universality, exclusivity, and emphasis unavailable to direct performative wording. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Allah is the proper name of the necessarily existent essence, worthy of every praise. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Rabb is an attribute of Allah: after praise is attached to His essence to show inherent entitlement, the attribute adds entitlement through His qualities; this is clearer than saying 'praise belongs to Lord of the worlds.' | mentions 83:6 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The four descriptions—Lord of the worlds, Merciful, Compassionate, King of Judgment Day—show each divine name's meaning merits praise and distinguishes God from alleged deities. | mentions 1:3, 1:4 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The surah begins with praise so the second half of the Qur'an opens with الحمد لله, echoing the opening of the first half in al-Fātiḥa and the approximate start of the fourth quarter in Q 35:1. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He also supports the sura's comprehensiveness by citing the sound report that al-Ikhlas counts as a third of the Qur'an because all its wording praises God. | mentions 112:1 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A second explanation is that the sura gathers the Qur'an's three main aims: praise, commands and prohibitions, and promise and warning. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The opening praise gathers God's praised attributes, declares His uniqueness in divinity, and affirms resurrection and recompense. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu lillah implies all attributes of perfection that entitle God to exclusive praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report in Sahih al-Bukhari from Abu Sa'id ibn al-Mu'alla says the Prophet identified al-Hamdu lillah as the seven oft-repeated verses and the great Qur'an given to him. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو هريرة): The first proof is Malik's report from Abu Hurayra: God's division of prayer begins with al-Hamdu lillah, without mentioning the basmala; prayer here means recitation in prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبي بن كعب): In the second proof, Ubayy ibn Ka'b recites the celebrated sura from al-Hamdu lillah to its end when asked how he opens prayer, without reciting the basmala. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports in Muslim and other collections that the Prophet, Abu Bakr, and Umar began recitation with al-Hamdu lillah and did not mention the basmala at either end. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عائشة): Aisha reports that the Prophet opened prayer with the takbir and began recitation with al-Hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عبد الله بن مغفل): Ibn Mughaffal says he prayed with the Prophet and the first three caliphs but never heard them say the basmala, and directs worshippers to recite al-Hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-Fatiha models four rules for introductions: keep them brief, foreshadow the subject, use concise comprehensive wording, and begin with praise of God. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): God placed praise before the supplication, following Arabic eloquence in which requests to great people begin with commendation. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أمية ابن أبي الصلت): Umayya ibn Abi al-Salt's praise of Abd Allah ibn Jud'an says the addressee's modesty and praise can make a request unnecessary. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Opening weighty speech with praise became Qur'anic practice; Muslims call an important speech without such an opening “cut off,” following a hadith from Abu Hurayra. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ziyad ibn Abi Sufyan's Basra sermon was nicknamed al-Batra because it did not begin with praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Hamd is praise for a chosen beautiful quality, whether an action such as generosity or another quality such as courage; thana' is its broader genus. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn al-Qatta' and others wrongly infer from a hadith that thana' can praise or blame; the negative usage is verbal symmetry and hints that one should praise a Muslim or stay silent. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (السيد الشريف): Al-Sayyid al-Sharif takes al-Zamakhshari to mean that both words remain restricted to chosen beautiful qualities, supported by his discussion of praise for beauty and noble character at 49:7. | mentions 49:7 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur notes that reliable scholars of rhetoric restrict praise to core virtues such as eloquence, courage, justice, and chastity, even where people praise attractive appearance. | mentions 49:7 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayh says some such source expressions can be made nominative topics, as in al-hamdu lillah; the definite form favors this declarative use. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur cites Sibawayh to explain the Arabic stages of this verbal noun construction and the rationale for the nominative al-hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A difficulty for defining hamd by voluntary qualities is that it would exclude God's essential attributes such as knowledge and power; proponents offer several answers. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الجمهور): A third answer reads “voluntary” as describing the praised agent, not the particular attribute for which praise is given. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative in all transmitted readings; its predicate is lillah, while an underlying analysis treats hamd as an accusative verbal noun replacing an omitted verb meaning “we praise God.” IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Raising hamd creates a nominal sentence for permanence, generic al for comprehensive praise, and fronting for emphasis; these effects are unavailable in the ordinary accusative construction. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Accusative hamd can take al in the Tamim reading, but an implicit “I praise” limits praise to the speaker and “we praise” to believers or monotheists; generic hamd instead includes every praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayh's distinction is that nominative hamd reports praise from the speaker and all creatures, while accusative hamd reports the speaker's praise alone. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur says the nominative definite wording is most eloquent, conveys enduring and comprehensive praise, and cites al-Kashshaf's comparison with Abraham's greeting at 51:25. | mentions 51:25 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Praise precedes God's name because this occasion is the beginning of Qur'anic revelation, the great blessing that brings success in both worlds and evokes the Revealer's beautiful attributes. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Situational importance can warrant foregrounding praise despite God's name being inherently more important; rhetoric follows the occasion and highlights what might otherwise go unnoticed. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Hamd can be fronted deliberately even though it is the grammatical subject, since Arabic eloquent usage offers both al-hamdu lillah and lillah al-hamd. | mentions 45:36 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The al in al-hamd is generic: because the verbal noun stands in place of an action, the definite form presents the kind of praise as familiar and makes it prominent, rather than identifying one instance. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Although the generic article is not itself exhaustive, its combination with the lām of divine specification implies that every instance of praise belongs to God; the restriction is rhetorical and emphatic. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): To decide whether al-hamdu lillah is report or performance, Ibn Ashur first explains that Arabic can turn statements into speech acts, as with sale formulas and words of praise or hope. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): One camp treats the sentence as a plain report: reporting that God has praise acknowledges His beautiful qualities, and some say the speaker is included among those praising Him. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Other report-only explanations say the speaker's own praise follows conventionally from the statement or that its literal content serves an intended implication, though this can make the speaker's praise secondary. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A second view treats the line as an informative sentence deliberately used to perform praise, as a report can enact sorrow in “I bore her as a female” at 3:36. | mentions 3:36 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A separate view says the sentence is a pure performative transferred from reporting, like formulas for contracts and praise; the original report sense remains possible with contextual support. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur's conclusion is that al-hamdu lillah reports praise while functioning as its performance; declarative form adds permanence, comprehensiveness, exclusivity, and emphasis. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He says these effects cannot be achieved throug h a purely performative wording such as hamdan lillah or ahmadu Allah hamdan. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Allah is the proper name of the necessarily existent divine essence, the one deserving every praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verse puts الحمد before لله because praise for revelation and Islam frames the opening recitation; contextual importance can precede God's intrinsic priority, while praising Him still attends to His affairs. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): رب العالمين apposes God's name: praise first recognizes His intrinsic worth, then this description adds His descriptive worthiness for praise. | mentions 83:6 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The four descriptions that follow signal that each attribute also merits praise and distinguish God from the idols, objects, and elements other peoples worship. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Praise is description of God’s beautiful acts; only God’s sincere servants, whom He draws near without intermediary, can properly describe Him, as 37:159 says. | mentions 1:2, 37:159 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God directly relates praise from selected prophets, Muhammad, and the purified inhabitants of Paradise; other creatures' praise is paired with glorification because they cannot encompass divine perfection. | mentions 23:28, 14:39, 17:111, 27:15, 10:10 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Creatures cannot comprehend God's perfection; their praise would limit Him to their understanding, so they must first declare Him transcendent of what they delimit. | mentions 42:5, 17:44, 20:110, 16:74 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God makes the praise and description of His sincerely purified servants His own. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (النبي): A report transmitted by both communities from the Prophet says no one can enumerate praise of God as fully as He praises Himself. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The opening الحمد لله is taught etiquette: a servant could not rightly utter this praise without God first teaching it and speaking on the servant's behalf. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (الصادق): When al-Sadiq's father recovered a lost mule, he praised God only by saying الحمد لله and explained that every kind of praise is contained in that phrase. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God creates and acts with knowledge and choice, not under compulsion; therefore every being is His voluntary beautiful act, and all genuine praise belongs to Him. | mentions 39:4, 20:111, 20:8, 7:180 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Since God is beautiful in names and acts and all praised beauty comes from Him, every true act of praise is rightfully His. | mentions 20:8, 7:180 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The shift to direct address at the worship verse shows that the sura is speech placed on the servant's tongue, teaching how to praise and behave in servitude. | mentions 1:5 ABUDAWUD (الله تعالى): In a hadith qudsi transmitted by Abu Hurayra, God divides al-Fatiha between Himself and the servant: praise and glorification receive divine responses, worship is mutual, and the guidance petition belongs to the servant. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): The hymn of praise begins the sura’s actual text and gives it the alternative name al-Ḥamd. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Praise can be given for beauty or good whether voluntary or not, whereas hamd is for good and beauty arising from will and choice. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Hamd in its complete sense belongs only to God: human good is never fully independent of divine will, while unchosen gifts and qualities are directly the Creator’s work. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Hamd is praise of beauty, whether a quality or gift, with love and reverence. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Hamd covers praise of intrinsic qualities and beneficence, and is directed only to a living, rational being. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The impersonal formula al-hamd lillah does not bind praise to a particular speaker, unlike I or we praise God, and presents God as universally praised. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Saying I praise God reports the speaker's act but does not establish that its object deserves praise; al-hamd lillah states God's entitlement without a specific speaker. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The verb form is time-bound to the speaker's present or future, while al-hamd lillah leaves praise ongoing without a temporal limit. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): The formula means God was praised before any praiser or thanker and remains praised from eternity to eternity. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): A nominal sentence conveys established, lasting praise more strongly than a verbal sentence that suggests occurrence and renewal. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah declares praise to be God's due by His very essence and abundant favors, unlike a report that one person praises Him. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Hamd is an inward recognition of a benefactor's favor and right to reverence; clai ming I praise God can be false if the heart lacks that recognition, while the formula states a fact. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The nominative reading al-hamdu makes a nominal sentence and is stronger and more enduring than the accusative, which requires an implied verb. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (البحر المحيط): The seven readers agree on the nominative, which establishes praise; the accusative requires an implied agent and makes praise actor-specific and renewed. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (البيضاوي): The nominative is chosen to convey praise's universality and permanence rather than a renewed event. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Even if the accusative is read as an imperative, commanding praise does not establish that the praised one deserves it; the nominative indicates deserved, established praise. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamdu lillah is a nominal and definite expression, unlike hamdan lillah; al can indicate known praise or all praise, while indefiniteness lacks this scope. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (بعضهم): Some take al as referring to the familiar praise among people, while others read it as encompassing every instance and kind of praise. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The author prefers both senses together: familiar praise belongs to God in a way that encompasses every kind and instance. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Most scholars treat al-hamd lillah as a report that praise is established for God; others call it performative praise, a transferred report, or a report that entails performance, and the author accepts both report and exaltation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah is better than inna al-hamd lillah because believers do not doubt the claim, and the unmarked formula can report praise while enacting exaltation; inna makes it mere report. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah is better than lillah al-hamd here: fronting lillah suggests exclusivity or rebuttal of doubt, neither of which fits this address to believers. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): People may rightly praise others for favors; the hadith recognizes others' merit, while 3:188 condemns seeking praise for deeds not done. | mentions 3:188 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-hamd may appear to apply to God or another, so naming God clarifies it; worship can only be for God, so fronting You in 1:5 prevents an implication of worshipping another. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Word order varies with context: al-Jathiya foregrounds God as deserving praise because it confronts disbelievers' false beliefs and denials of His lordship. | mentions 45:10, 45:23, 45:24 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya answers denials of God's creative and beneficent lordship by showing Him as sole creator and provider, so He alone deserves praise; al-Fatiha addresses believers who seek steadfast guidance. | mentions 45:5, 45:12, 45:13, 45:22, 45:26 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya repeatedly uses restrictive constructions, matching its context of disbelievers who reject God and setting up its exclusive praise formula. | mentions 45:9, 45:10, 45:11, 45:24 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The many restrictive statements throughout al-Jathiya culminate in exclusive praise and sovereignty for God, so its context calls for fronting Him as the one deserving praise. | mentions 45:12, 45:15, 45:17, 45:24, 45:25, 45:26, 45:27, 45:28, 45:30, 45:32, 45:33, 45:34, 45:35, 45:36, 45:37 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Using God's proper name rather than an attribute avoids implying that He deserves praise only for one quality; the name points to His essence and entitlement across all attributes. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (روح المعاني): The proper name makes God's essence, rather than one particular quality, the ground of His right to praise. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): An owner, master, and beneficent educator has a stronger claim to praise, and the Lord can direct His subjects as He wills. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya mentions God's true sovereignty because it condemns arrogant mockers, while al-Fatiha has no such context and mentions only praise. | mentions 45:7, 45:8, 45:9, 45:31, 45:33, 45:35, 45:37 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Other opening praises name God as creator, originator, owner of all things, or sender of scripture; each fits its surah and the openings complement one another. | mentions 6:1, 35:1, 34:1, 18:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): The next four opening praise formulas describe particular aspects contained in al-Fatiha's general Rabb al-alamin. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Saba's statement that all in heaven and earth belongs to God is one aspect of al-Fatiha's general praise of the Lord of the worlds. | mentions 34:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God is praiseworthy in His essence, lordship, rightly placed mercy, and ownership of Judgment Day; the sequence spans all times from eternity through the endless afterlife. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The praise covers time from eternity to eternity, echoed by praise in the first and last life and at the end of judgment; this fullness makes al-Fatiha worthy to be the Mother of the Book. | mentions 28:70, 39:75, 10:10 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (التفسير القيم): The names following praise show God is praiseworthy in divinity, lordship, mercy, and kingship. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Invoking the congregation’s mercy, the worshipper praises, worships, seeks help, asks guidance, follows the righteous, and flees the rejected as a group. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Unrestricted praise and the enduring nominal descriptions of the angry and astray all suggest timeless, lasting states. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Praise of God implies that a Creator exists, knows, has power, and deserves praise and glorification. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Razi summarizes praise as God’s existence and choice, lordship as unity, mercy as compassion in both worlds, and Judgment Day as wisdom and mercy. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Because hamd is a state of the heart, someone saying أحمد الله without reverence may lie, whereas الحمد لله remains true whether or not the speaker is attentive. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Even if the accusative is construed as a command to praise, a command does not establish that its object deserves praise; the declarative nominative is preferable. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (بعضهم): Some take the definite article in الحمد as referring to the known praise among its hearers. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (بعضهم): Others take the article as generic and exhaustive, covering every kind and instance of praise. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The author favors the exhaustive sense, supported by the Prophetic prayer اللهم لك الحمد كله: all known praise belongs to God without exception. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (أكثر النحاة والمفسرين): Most grammarians and exegetes treat الحمد لله as a report that praise belongs to God. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The phrase can function as either report or invocation depending on context, letting the speaker affirm God's praise while expressing appreciation and reverence. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Fronting لله in لله الحمد normally marks restriction, but al-Fatiha's context is not one of exclusive praise: worldly praise can also be directed to people, unlike exclusive worship. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The context contrast explains the order: al-Fatiha has believers confessing worship and asking for help, while al-Jathiya answers unbelievers' claims about life and death by foregrounding God's praise. | mentions 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Jathiya repeatedly discusses God's lordship over the heavens, earth, creation, and worlds; its closing praise gathers these themes, unlike al-Fatiha's focus on the worlds and their accountable groups. | mentions 45:4, 45:27, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Using God's proper name means He deserves praise for His essence, not merely for one attribute such as power or creation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Rabb al-alamin is the apt continuation after praise: Rabb means owner, master, educator, benefactor, and caretaker, and encompasses God's attributes. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The sequence praises God for His essence, lordship, properly placed mercy, and ownership and kingship on the day of recompense. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The opening attributes span all time: praise before creation, at creation, through continuing mercy, and through the unending recompense of the afterlife, as 28:70 says. | mentions 28:70 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Hamd is heartfelt praise of a living, rational being's beautiful qualities or benefactions, with love and reverence. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Praise is broader than hamd: it may precede a good deed and may describe an inanimate object, while hamd requires actual merit and follows benefaction. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet warns against excessive flatterers, supporting the distinction between unearned praise and merited hamd. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The wording الحمد لله states that God merits praise for His qualities and deeds, whereas أحمد الله reports only the speaker's own act of praising. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Unlike a first-person verb, الحمد لله is not limited to a speaker or to the finite time in which that person can praise; its praise is unceasing. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (الرازي): Al-Razi says أحمد الله declares the speaker able to praise, while الحمد لله says God was praised before any created praiser and remains praised eternally. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The nominal الحمد لله conveys stable, lasting praise, unlike the verbal sentence أحمد الله, which suggests occurrence and renewal. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): الحمد لله also declares praise to be God's rightful property because of His abundant favors; a personal act of praising does not assert this entitlement. STUDYQURAN (author): God praises Himself in the opening so humans can praise Him in divine speech, which He knows is more adequate than their own words. STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet acknowledges that no one can enumerate praise due to God, who is as He praised Himself. STUDYQURAN (author): Muslims use the basmalah to consecrate actions at their beginning and al-ḥamd to thank God upon completion; etiquette answers any inquiry about one’s condition with praise. STUDYQURAN (author): As Sūrat al-Ḥamd and Sūrat al-Ṣalāh, the Fātiḥah begins each prayer cycle for Sunnis and many Shiites. STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet identifies the Fātiḥah as the greatest sūrah, calling it Praise be to God and the seven oft-repeated, and linking it to 15:87. | mentions 15:87 STUDYQURAN (God): In a hadith qudsī, God responds to the servant’s praise, magnification, and glorification during the prayer, which is divided between them. - 1:2/q01/p3 Madḥ can praise qualities or objects beyond the scope of ḥamd. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Madh is speech intentionally reporting the greatness of the person praised. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Madḥ is broader than hamd: one may praise a living or inanimate thing such as a pearl, but cannot give it hamd. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Madḥ can precede or follow a benefaction, whereas hamd is only after beneficence. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Madḥ can be condemned, as in the warning to throw dust in flatterers’ faces, whereas hamd is generally commanded. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Madḥ denotes a general kind of excellence, while hamd denotes the specific virtue of benefaction; this completes the distinction that madḥ is broader. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Saying al-hamdu lillah rather than al-madhu lillah acknowledges God as a freely choosing agent, not a necessary emanation as philosophers claim. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The terms madhma and ahmada describe blame versus praise, finding something agreeable, and becoming worthy of praise; hamda can mean one who overpraises, while hamadat al-nar is its crackling. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Ḥamd praises chosen goodness, whether a blessing or otherwise; madh praises goodness without qualification, so one praises knowledge and generosity but only commends beauty. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Shukr is broader than ḥamd and madh in one respect and narrower in another; praise is a branch of gratitude and better signals blessings than hidden belief or ambiguous bodily acts, so it is gratitude's chief element. NASAFI, d. 710 (قيل): Another distinction makes madh praise inherent perfections, shukr praise for beneficence, and hamd broad enough to cover both. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Hamd and madḥ may both mean praising what is beautiful, whether a favor or an inherent quality; shukr is specifically for a favor and uses heart, tongue, and limbs, whereas hamd is praise by tongue alone. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): An alternative distinction makes madḥ praise inherent perfections, such as power and knowledge, and shukr praise for benefactions; hamd covers both. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Ḥamd is verbal praise of beauty, for a blessing or otherwise, and its opposite is blame; it is not a reversal of madḥ, since madḥ can apply to inanimate things. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (ابن الأنباري): Ibn al-Anbārī treats ḥamd as an inversion of madḥ; Abū Ḥayyān rejects this because a jewel may be praised but not thanked. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (ابن الأنباري): Ibn al-Anbārī says ḥamd is a metathesized form of madḥ; Abū Ḥayyān rejects this despite their parallel inflection. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Tha'lab's derivation of hamd as a reversal of madh is unsound: both forms are equally common, and hamd applies to God where madh does not; a possible prohibition is acknowledged. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الراغب): Al-Raghib defines hamd as praise for excellence, narrower than m adh but broader than shukr: every hamd is madh and every shukr is hamd, but not conversely. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir distinguishes praise from eulogy: eulogy can concern living or dead people and even objects, precede or follow a favor, and address necessary or bestowed qualities. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): One distinction is that ḥamd applies to voluntary actions of knowing beings, while madḥ also applies to involuntary qualities and nonrational things. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Ḥamd presupposes knowledge rather than conjecture and concerns complete qualities; madḥ may rest on conjecture or a pleasing but imperfect trait. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Ḥamd carries greater reverence and grandeur than madḥ, applies more particularly to rational beings and eminent persons, and is more often used of God. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Ḥamd reports another’s virtues with love and reverence, so i t combines information with an act of esteem; madḥ is bare information. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Ḥamd is generally commanded, as in the saying that whoever does not thank people does not thank God, whereas madḥ is not universally praised and flatterers are rebuked. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): One distinction says hamd concerns vol untary acts of rational beings, while madḥ may concern voluntary or involuntary qualities and rational or non-rational subjects. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Other distinctions reserve hamd for known perfection, certainty, and greater reverence, while madḥ may praise merely supposed or imperfect qualities and report beauty without love. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Hamd is also said to be both report and performative through love and reverence, unlike madḥ as bare report; one report says whoever does not praise people does not praise God, while another condemns flatterers. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī holds that madḥ can praise involuntary qualities, unlike hamd; that distinction explains why God says al-hamdu lillāh, since God is a freely acting agent. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Medh can praise living or inanimate things and can precede or follow a favor; hamd is directed to a choosing benefactor after a favor, though the benefit need not reach the praiser. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Shukr responds in speech, action, or heart to a blessing actually received; spoken gratitude is also hamd and medh, while inward or bodily thanks need not be either. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Every hamd is medh but not every medh is hamd; hamd is broader than gratitude in one respect and narrower in another, and unlike empty flattery both hamd and shukr affirm truth. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (الجمهور): The majority distinguish madh from hamd by making madh broader: it can praise voluntary or nonvoluntary qualities. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الجمهور): The majority hold that madh is broader than hamd because it can concern voluntary or involuntary qualities. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Hamd praises voluntary excellence, while madh is broader and can praise non-voluntary qualities; the definite article here yields the same result whether generic or comprehensive. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Madḥ is broader than hamd because one may praise beautiful pearls or jewels, but cannot give them hamd. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (بعضهم): One distinction says hamd follows a real benefaction or praiseworthy quality, while madḥ may precede any good deed or be undeserved. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (النبي): The Prophet warns against excessive flatterers, supporting the distinction between deserved hamd and potentially misplaced madḥ. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah implies that God is living, possesses perfect qualities, and does beautiful deeds and favors; madḥ lillah would not convey all this. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Hamd includes love, exaltation, and reverence beyond madḥ, making hamd the more fitting choice. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Hamd is preferable to both madḥ and shukr because it covers God's qualities and favors to the speaker and all creation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The verse's wording is preferable to the alternatives madḥ, shukr, I/we praise, an imperative, accusative praise, an indefinite praise, inna, fronted lillah, or a divine attribute. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Hamd is preferable to madḥ and shukr because it covers both God's intrinsic qualities and His benefactions, whereas shukr applies only to favors and madḥ need not reflect real merit. THALABI: Ibn al-Anbārī derives ḥamd by inversion from madḥ. [42 notes: TABRISI; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI; NASAFI(قيل); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN(ابن الأنباري); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن الأنباري); SAMIN-DURR(الراغب); IBNKATHIR; ALUSI; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR(الجمهور); IBNASHUR-FULL(الجمهور); TABATABAI; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي,النبي,بعضهم); THALABI(ابن الأنباري); ALUSI-FULL+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+]
- 1:2/q01/p4 Ḥamd and shukr can be used interchangeably. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (الطبري): Al-Tabari treats praise and thanks as synonyms; the author rejects this as unsatisfactory. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (بعض الناس): Some distinguish thanks as praise for God's acts and favors, and praise as praise of his attributes; the author finds this more accurate than synonymy. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): Al-Tabari's evidence “praise to God, in thanks” actually limits praise to praise for a favor and therefore argues against synonymy. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الطبري والمبرد): Al-Ṭabarī and al-Mubarrad hold that ḥamd and shukr have the same meaning; Qurtubi says this view is not sound. QURTUBI, d. 671 (ابن عطاء): Al-Sulami transmits from Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq and Ibn ʿAṭāʾ the synonymy view; Ibn ʿAṭāʾ explains praise as gratitude for God enabling people to learn and praise Him. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الطبري): Al-Ṭabarī argues the terms are synonymous because Arabic permits the phrase al-ḥamdu lillāhi shukran, praise to God in gratitude. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو جعفر الطبري): Al-Tabari and al-Mubarrad treat hamd and shukr as synonymous; al-Sulami also attributes this position to Jaʿfar al-Sadiq and Ibn ʿAtaʾ, who says praise is thanks for God teaching it. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Some consider ḥamd and madh synonymous or close companions. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Ḥamd is praise for chosen excellence, including a blessing, while madḥ is praise for any excellence; one praises knowledge but merely commends beauty. [BAYDAWI-FULL:1: 2/r2] BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 · author | reports | Some regard ḥamd and madḥ as equivalent; shukr is gratitude for a blessing expressed in speech, action, and belief. NASAFI, d. 710 (قيل): One view treats hamd and madh as near-synonyms: both are praise and notice of beauty, whether for a blessing, courage, or noble lineage. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Views say ḥamd and shukr are synonymous, ḥamd is broader, or shukr praises God's acts while ḥamd praises His attributes; Abū Ḥayyān prefers ḥamd as broader. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Views range from treating hamd and shukr as synonymous, to making hamd absolutely broader, to distinguishing praise of attributes from thanks for acts. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir reports a distinction that praise names God's beautiful attributes while thanks praises His favors, then argues Arabic speakers use both words interchangeably. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (جعفر الصادق وابن عطاء): The Sufi authorities Ja'far al-Sadiq and Ibn Ata are reported to hold that praise and thanks are equivalent. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī appears to treat ḥamd and madḥ as synonymous; al-Ālūsī rejects that position and maintains that madḥ can praise what is involuntary. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawānī objects that treating God’s praise as figurative conflicts with the orthodox affirmation of real divine speech, since saying and speech are equivalent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī’s Kashshāf and Fāʾiq suggest hamd and madḥ are synonymous, defining hamd as praise and description with beauty; al-Ālūsī judges this likely but rejects it. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Hamd is beautiful verbal praise that honors an agent for a voluntary benefaction or its source, goodness; it combines aspects of praise and gratitude and lacks a single Turkish equivalent. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari calls hamd and madh equivalent; a proposed reading takes his word 'brothers' to mean derivational relatives like jazb and jadhb. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur rejects the derivational-sibling reading: the commentators' synonym reading fits al-Zamakhshari's gloss in al-Fa'iq and his treating blame as hamd's opposite. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari calls hamd and madh “siblings”; Ibn Ashur rejects the interpretation that he means only a remote derivational relationship and reads him as calling them synonymous. [21 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(الطبري,بعض الناس); QURTUBI(ابن عطاء,الطبري,الطبري والمبرد); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو جعفر الطبري); BAYDAWI; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI(قيل); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR(ابن جرير,جعفر الصادق وابن عطاء); ALUSI(الزمخشري); ALUSI-FULL(الدواني,الزمخشري); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR(صاحب الكشاف); IBNASHUR-FULL(الزمخشري); ABUHAYYAN+; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية)-]
- 1:2/q01/p5 Ḥamd is spoken praise, while shukr also uses heart and deeds. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Gratitude is acknowledging a blessing with reverence, rooted in the heart and also expressed by the tongue to deny a charge of ingratitude. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Hamd applies whether a benefaction reaches the speaker or someone else, while shukr is limited to a benefit received by the thankful person. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Shukr responds to a blessing through speech, action, and belief; a poem names hand, tongue, and hidden heart as the three responses. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Unlike hamd, shukr is only for a blessing and can be expressed by heart, tongue, and limbs; the cited poet names hand, tongue, and the veiled heart as its three forms. NASAFI, d. 710 (النبي): A Prophetic report calls praise the head of gratitude and says a servant has not thanked God without praising Him; praise leads because the tongue makes gratitude more public than the hidden heart or ambiguous bodily acts. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The cited poet says favors elicited three forms of gratitude: hand, tongue, and the veiled heart. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Hamd praises beauty whether or not a favor was bestowed and is expressed only by the tongue; shukr is for a received favor and may be by heart, tongue, or limbs. | mentions 34:13 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): A poem listing hand, tongue, and hidden conscience as three gifts illustrates that gratitude includes bodily action as well as speech. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some distinguish shukr from ḥamd by reserving shukr for bodily action and ḥamd for speech. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الراغب): Al-Rāghib points to a definition of shukr as verbal expression for a blessing, while the familiar account includes grateful speech, service by the limbs, and belief and love in the heart. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Definitions distinguish gratitude as bodily deed versus hamd as speech, outward versus inward blessings, or tongue-only thanks for blessings; al-Rāghib’s account limits it to blessing. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الطيبي): Al-Ṭībī calls gratitude by speech, deeds, and heart the technical usage of legal theorists, who mean the obligatory worship of the benefactor; al-Ālūsī rejects the claim that linguistic gratitude is only verbal. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Scripture applies gratitude to actions as well as speech: the Qur’an commands David’s household to act in gratitude. | mentions 34:13 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (النواس بن سمعان): Al-Ṭabarānī reports that after the Prophet’s she-camel was stolen, he vowed to thank God if it returned; when it did, he said alhamdulillah, and the Companions awaited fasting or prayer, showing they understood gratitude as action. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (صاحب التجريد): Some add a fourth form, God thanking God, since only God can thank Him fully; a cited poem contrasts gratitude to people by heart, word, and deed with gratitude to God through His own thanking. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Specifically, basmala and al-Fatiha are closely linked by cadence, wording, summary and explanation; al-Fatiha's gratitude answers the basmala's attraction of mercy. [16 notes: TABRISI; RAZI-FULL; BAYDAWI; NASAFI(النبي); NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI(الراغب,بعضهم); ALUSI-FULL(الطيبي,النواس بن سمعان,صاحب التجريد); ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q01/p6 Shukr has broader means of expression. — QURTUBI, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some scholars say gratitude is broader because it can be expressed by tongue, limbs, and heart, while praise is verbal alone. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Qurtubi’s preferred distinction is that praise commends attributes without requiring prior kindness, whereas gratitude praises a benefactor for a favor; praise is broader because it includes commendation as well as thanks. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some say gratitude is broader because it uses tongue, limbs, and heart, whereas praise is only verbal. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Hamd praises the praised one’s attributes without prior favor, while shukr praises a benefactor for a favor; thus hamd is broader and fits the verse’s scope. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Gratitude is broader than praise and commendation in one respect but narrower in another; praise is its chief expression because it displays blessings and makes gratitude audible. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Qurʾān 34:13 uses shukr for action, supporting the broader usage of gratitude beyond speech. | mentions 34:13 [6 notes: QURTUBI(بعض العلماء); QURTUBI-FULL(بعض العلماء); BAYDAWI-FULL; ALUSI; QURTUBI-FULL+; QURTUBI+]
- 1:2/q01/p7 Ḥamd and madḥ share the same sense. — KASHSHAF: Al-Zamakhsharī equates ḥamd and madḥ, while gratitude is for gifts by heart, tongue and limbs; verbal praise reveals gifts and is called gratitude's head. KASHSHAF: Al-Zamakhsharī equates ḥamd and madḥ, while gratitude is for gifts by heart, tongue and limbs; verbal praise reveals gifts and is called gratitude's head. KASHSHAF: Al-Zamakhsharī equates ḥamd and madḥ, while gratitude is for gifts by heart, tongue and limbs; verbal praise reveals gifts and is called gratitude's head. KASHSHAF: Al-Zamakhsharī equates ḥamd and madḥ, while gratitude is for gifts by heart, tongue and limbs; verbal praise reveals gifts and is called gratitude's head. KASHSHAF: Al-Zamakhsharī equates ḥamd and madḥ, while gratitude is for gifts by heart, tongue and limbs; verbal praise reveals gifts and is called gratitude's head. KASHSHAF: Al-Zamakhsharī equates ḥamd and madḥ, while gratitude is for gifts by heart, tongue and limbs; verbal praise reveals gifts and is called gratitude's head. KASHSHAF-FULL: The full commentary links ḥamd and madḥ, gratitude's broader means, a weak head-of-gratitude report, and the nominative's enduring force. KASHSHAF-FULL: The full commentary links ḥamd and madḥ, gratitude's broader means, a weak head-of-gratitude report, and the nominative's enduring force. Al-Bayḍāwī reports that some equate ḥamd and madḥ, while shukr answers a blessing through speech, action, and belief.
