## 1:2/q02 What scope does the article in al-ḥamd give?  [ٱلْحَمْدُ · grammar]
turns on: Whether the article directly denotes all praise, the genus, praise's essence, or a known praise.
- 1:2/q02/p1 The article encompasses all praise. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): Al-hamd is complete praise, with the definite article covering the whole class of praise; it is broader than thanks, which responds to a favor, while praise may simply describe qualities. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ʿālamīn includes everything besides God, which depends on Him for existence and continuance; every indivisible particle and individual accident proves the wise, powerful God. | mentions 17:44 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The lām in lillāh can signify fitting اختصاص, ownership, or power and dominion; each makes all praise belong to God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because full gratitude is beyond human capacity, the verse states that all praise belongs to God rather than commanding people to praise Him. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ḥamdu lillāh encompasses every praise uttered or possible from all creatures, prophets, angels, saints, scholars, and future inhabitants of Paradise. | mentions 10:10 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الجبرية): The Jabriyya infer that faith too comes from God: all praise belongs to Him, praise follows blessing, and faith is the greatest blessing. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): In Arabic, al-ḥamd is complete praise; the definite article encompasses every kind of praise, all of which God deserves because He has the most beautiful names and highest attributes. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): With the definite article, Rabb is specific to God; without it, the term can apply to God or a servant, but God alone owns owners and owned things, creates and provides, and cannot lose His dominion as human owners can. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Hamd means complete praise; the definite article encompasses every kind of praise, all of which God deserves for His beautiful names and exalted attributes. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): With the definite article, Rabb belongs exclusively to God; without it, people may be called a household’s lord. God alone creates and provides, owns both owners and owned things, and cannot lose His dominion. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): If the article is exhaustive, all praise belongs to God because He bestows every good directly or indirectly; this implies He is living, powerful, willing, and knowing. | mentions 16:53 BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The definite article can mark the genus or all praise: in reality every good comes from God, directly or through an intermediary, as 16:53 says. | mentions 16:53 NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The definite article in al-hamd is exhaustive according to the author's school, against the Mu'tazila; joining it to Allah's proper name gathers every perfection and rests on the doctrine of created acts. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): All praise belongs to God eternally according to His due, at beginning, end, and everything between; He is both praiser and praised, worshipper and worshipped, in detail and in unity. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The article in al-ʿālamīn is exhaustive; ʿālam is an irregular collective and its sound masculine plural is even more irregular because it violates some conditions of that pattern. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The definite article in al-ḥamd intensifies praise by making it comprehensive. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The lām in “to God” restricts all praise to Him, and the annexation “Master of the Day of Judgment” underscores His sole dominion when other dominions vanish. | mentions 40:16 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari rejects the universal reading; the author infers that praise here is performed by the servant, who cannot produce every praise by himself and others. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): The definite article in al-ḥamdu encompasses every kind and form of praise as belonging to God, as expressed in a transmitted prayer. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): Because the basmala is a kind of praise, it is naturally followed by the comprehensive name al-ḥamd; this implicitly calls people to praise God as the one deserving every praise. BIQAI, d. 885 (أبو الحسن الحرالي): Al-Ḥarālī says that when praise is understood to pervade everything, the definite form al-ḥamd signals its completion; al- marks the endpoint and perfection of what it qualifies. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha's purpose is to establish God's exclusive right to all praise and perfection, sovereignty over both worlds, worship and aid, and to ask for the path of the successful rather than the doomed; its center is مراقبة. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Since the basmala is a kind of praise, it naturally leads to comprehensive al-hamd: begin affairs by praising God as the one deserving every praise, with hope and awe guiding the way. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali defines al-hamd as complete praise covering all actions and attributes, all from God and free of blame; its definite form signals praise reaching its full extent. DURR, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad tells how he vowed to praise God with approved praises if his mule returned, then said al-hamdulillah alone because it encompasses all praise. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): When Jaafar ibn Muhammad's mule returned, he thanked God with only “al-hamd lillah,” saying this gathered all praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الطيبي): Al-Ṭībī answers that singular exhaustive scope could cover individuals yet fail to make the multiplicity of kinds explicit, so the plural is clearer here. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Because contingent beings need a cause both to begin and to persist, continual divine preservation is itself tarbiya; keeping even indefinitely many possible obstacles absent sustains a thing. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī calls the exhaustive interpretation of al-ḥamdu a mistaken assumption held by many. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī rejects arguments that the generic article means only the perfect kind or that a verbal noun cannot convey exhaustive scope; context can make either reading exhaustive. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers an exhaustive reading here: the sūra’s encompassing attributes of lordship, mercy, and sovereignty support all praise belonging to God, and the shift to إياك نعبد marks a new address rather than an explanation. ALUSI, d. 1270 (هارون بن موسى): Hārūn b. Mūsā reads al-ḥamda in the accusative, a construction common among Arabs for a definite verbal noun with an implied verb such as نحمد. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): When the distinction between Creator and creation is considered together, all praise comes from God and returns to Him; there is no praiser or praised one apart from Him. | mentions 53:42 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The definite plural makes the scope of divine lordship explicit across every created kind; singular al-ʿālam might be mistaken for the familiar perceptible world alone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī calls comprehensive scope an imagined error and takes the article as genus; the ensuing debate addresses whether that reading protects the Muʿtazilī account of human acts. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Another defense treats genus in rhetorical context as the complete instance; a critic replies that comprehensive scope can likewise treat everything else as negligible, so neither reading avoids the theological difficulty unaided. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A third argument derives genus from verbal nouns’ basic, non-comprehensive meaning; the reply is that surrounding indications can still make a verbal noun comprehensive. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī rejects the claim that genus is the obvious common reading: in rhetorical settings a definite generic article commonly conveys comprehensiveness, especially when exclusive praise is at issue. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī says treating genus as perfection and treating comprehensive praise as exhaustive both require interpretation; making everything else negligible adds an unnecessary step. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A further account makes worship the explanation of praise by combining verbal praise with bodily and inward devotion; al-Ālūsī says this does not require choosing genus over comprehensive scope. | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The claim that exclusive genus praise is more comprehensive than exclusive individuals is reversible: if every individual is exclusively God’s, no instance remains outside the exclusive genus. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (هارون بن موسى): Hārūn b. Mūsā reads al-ḥamda in the accusative, like Arabs who use definite verbal nouns with an omitted verb; the implied “we praise” suits worship and seeking help better than royal “we.” | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): When all praise is considered together, it begins and ends in God: there is no praiser or praised one besides Him, and human understanding cannot encompass Him. | mentions 53:42 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The plural al-ʿālamīn guarantees inclusion of every created kind: a singular definite noun could be heard as the familiar perceptible world, whereas the plural distributes lordship across all named kinds and their individuals. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The claim that a definite singular is more comprehensive does not undermine this wording; the plural avoids the familiar-world reading and includes all created genera. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Another analysis supplies the earlier word al-ḥamd as the governing verb; objections concern the definite verbal noun's government and an intervening predicate, both answered by grammarians. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sound rational plural and the definite article extend the meaning across all realms and foreground rational beings; understanding the Lord of the worlds requires conceiving rational agents. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The Quran has an exhaustive, subtle order among surahs, stories, verses, words, and letters; analyzing it involves innumerable sciences, beginning with grammar and rhetoric. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The cited grammar says a definite source such as al-hamdu can be raised as a subject because it is declarative and definite; the sense of the omitted verb remains. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (سيبويه): A reading with al-hamda in the accusative and definite article is a Tamimi dialect form attested by Sibawayhi; its implied verb limits praise to the speaker or intended group. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Although anomalous, the accusative reading documents an Arabic stage in this familiar construction, showing that some speakers retained its absolute-object origin even with the article. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The nominative is more eloquent than the accusative, and definite accusative is more expressive than indefinite accusative; raising marks permanence and stability. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari rejects treating the article as grammatically exhaustive; Ibn Ashur agrees that it marks genus, while the construction's combined effect entails all praise. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The genus article with the possessive l am implies all praise belongs to God: if any instance belonged to another, exclusive possession of the genus would fail; this is hyperbolic restriction. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The lam in li-llah may mean figurative exclusive possession or strengthen the verbal noun's relation as a weak, derived construction; exclusivity is also conveyed by the two definite parts. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Arabs do not appear to have used definite singular al-ilah for an idol: they used a possessive construction, another title, or the plural alihah. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Calling Allah with ya while retaining the definite article shows that speakers treat the article as part of the name, unlike ordinary nouns entered by al. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (الجوهري عن سيبويه): Another proposed derivation takes Allah from laah, a verbal noun meaning to be hidden; the article was added to suggest the original sense, as in fadl and majd used as names. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A second proposal derives the name from wilah, 'overwhelmed and bewildered'; initial waw became hamza after kasra, then the article was added and hamza dropped. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A further etymology borrows Syriac laaha as a divine name, Arabized by removing its final alif and adding the article. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): In this rhetorical context the definite article is exhaustive: there is no external or mental antecedent and no suitable literal or generic reading, so all genera are included. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The plural prevents an antecedent or generic reading and explicitly indicates exhaustive scope; Ibn Ashur rejects the Sakaki school's claim that singular is more comprehensive, referring to 2:31 for discussion. | mentions 2:31 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayh says some such source expressions can be made nominative topics, as in al-hamdu lillah; the definite form favors this declarative use. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Raising hamd creates a nominal sentence for permanence, generic al for comprehensive praise, and fronting for emphasis; these effects are unavailable in the ordinary accusative construction. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Accusative hamd can take al in the Tamim reading, but an implicit “I praise” limits praise to the speaker and “we praise” to believers or monotheists; generic hamd instead includes every praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur says the nominative definite wording is most eloquent, conveys enduring and comprehensive praise, and cites al-Kashshaf's comparison with Abraham's greeting at 51:25. | mentions 51:25 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Although the generic article is not itself exhaustive, its combination with the lām of divine specification implies that every instance of praise belongs to God; the restriction is rhetorical and emphatic. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The lām in lillah can indicate rhetorical specification or strengthen the relation of the verbal-noun subject to its predicate; specification remains conveyed by the definiteness of both parts. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur finds no evidence that Arabs used the definite singular al-ilah for an idol; they used a possessive phrase, often rabb, or a plural, and a conquest report speaks of idols in the Kaaba. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The divine name became a unique proper form whose article is treated as part of the word, as the vocative يا الله shows; its formation has no parallel among ordinary names. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الجوهري عن سيبويه): One proposed origin is لاه, a verbal noun meaning that God is hidden, with the definite article added to suggest the original sense. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The article in العالمين is exhaustive because the context supplies neither an external nor mental referent; it therefore covers every relevant species and, by implication, its individuals. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The plural العالمين makes exhaustive scope explicit and removes a possible generic or familiar reading; Ibn Ashur rejects the claim among al-Sakkaki's followers that an exhaustive singular is broader than an exhaustive plural. | mentions 2:31 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Hamd praises voluntary excellence, while madh is broader and can praise non-voluntary qualities; the definite article here yields the same result whether generic or comprehensive. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Since God is beautiful in names and acts and all praised beauty comes from Him, every true act of praise is rightfully His. | mentions 20:8, 7:180 ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): With the definite article, rabb is used in the Qur'an exclusively for God, sole sustainer of all creation and ultimate authority. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The verse's wording is preferable to the alternatives madḥ, shukr, I/we praise, an imperative, accusative praise, an indefinite praise, inna, fronted lillah, or a divine attribute. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (بعضهم): Others take the article as generic and exhaustive, covering every kind and instance of praise. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Arabic particles can shift a phrase between invocation and report, as رحمه الله and رزقك الله differ from their versions introduced by قد. STUDYQURAN (author): The definite form al-ḥamd indicates that all praise and gratitude belong to God. Al-Ālūsī: genus, generic instance, and comprehensiveness are possible in principle, but this context calls for all praise because every perfection ultimately comes from God. [81 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL; RAZI-FULL(الجبرية); QURTUBI; QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; QASHANI; ABUHAYYAN; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(الزمخشري); IBNKATHIR; BIQAI(أبو الحسن الحرالي); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR(جعفر بن محمد); DURR-FULL(جعفر بن محمد); ALUSI(الزمخشري,الطيبي,هارون بن موسى); ALUSI-FULL(الزمخشري,بعضهم,هارون بن موسى); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR(الجوهري عن سيبويه,سيبويه,صاحب الكشاف); IBNASHUR-FULL(الجوهري عن سيبويه,سيبويه); TABATABAI; ASAD-NOTES; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT(بعضهم); STUDYQURAN; ALUSI-FULL+; ALUSI+; ALUSI-FULL-]
- 1:2/q02/p2 The article points to known praise. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): One grammatical view makes definite al-ḥamd refer to a prior known instance, or otherwise to all praise to avoid ambiguity. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The article in al-ḥamd may be anaphoric, defining the familiar praise; essential, defining the nature; or generic, encompassing all praises. A verbal noun is ordinarily not pluralized. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The definite article on ḥamd may indicate a known instance, the abstract quality, or the entire genus of praise; the last implies exhaustive praise. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The article in al-hamd is analyzed as universal, generic, or referring to known praise; al-Zamakhshari chooses the generic reading. ALUSI, d. 1270 (أبو العباس المرسي): Al-Mursī says the article is covenantal because God praised Himself eternally on behalf of creatures unable to encompass His praise, supported by the prophetic supplication that He is as He praised Himself. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو العباس المرسي): Al-Fākihī reports that Abū al-ʿAbbās al-Mursī told Ibn al-Naḥḥās the article is referential: God praised Himself eternally on creatures’ behalf because they could not praise Him adequately. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The definite article in alhamd can express a known instance, totality, kind, or essence; because no particular hamd is presupposed, it here gathers the entire genus and all its ranks. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The article in al-hamd marks a known genus: its referent is recognizable to speaker and hearer, making the expression emphatic rather than singling out one instance. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): An alam is a genus among existing things; the word may derive from knowledge or sign because each genus is distinguishable and serves as a sign by which it is known. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (التفتزاني): Al-Taftazani says alam names knowledgeable beings or each genus through which the Creator is known; singular alam normally needs a qualifying genitive, while 'the entire cosmos' is later theological terminology. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamdu lillah is a nominal and definite expression, unlike hamdan lillah; al can indicate known praise or all praise, while indefiniteness lacks this scope. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (بعضهم): Some take the definite article in الحمد as referring to the known praise among its hearers. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The author favors the exhaustive sense, supported by the Prophetic prayer اللهم لك الحمد كله: all known praise belongs to God without exception. [13 notes: RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI(أبو العباس المرسي); ALUSI-FULL(أبو العباس المرسي); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR(التفتزاني); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT(بعضهم); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+]
- 1:2/q02/p3 The article denotes the genus of praise. — BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Ḥamd is a verbal noun normally governed by an implicit, rarely used verb; its article may denote the genus or point to the familiar idea of praise. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): A prepositional phrase or adverb functioning as clause predicate, relative clause, adjective, or circumstantial phrase has an omitted governor; a generic kā'in cannot be overt except in a poetic anomaly. | mentions 27:40 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī takes the article in al-ḥamdu as generic: it points to the familiar notion of praise, as in Labīd’s verse describing the camels. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Some tie the generic reading to the Muʿtazilī account that human actions belong to their agents; al-Ālūsī replies that good acts still depend on God’s enabling and empowering. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The suggestion that ʿālamīn is a unique collective noun on the pattern of sound plurals does not settle its form; the author distinguishes ordinary plurals, collective generics, and collective nouns. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He prefers genus because its exclusivity follows directly from the wording and logically entails exclusivity of every instance, making the proof stronger than separately asserting total scope. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He challenges the claim that genus exclusivity is immediately apparent: its inference is too subtle to be the obvious reading, and the manuscript ends while he disputes the argument’s rhetorical strength. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The definite article in al-ḥamdu is taken here to indicate the familiar genus of praise, illustrated by a line of Labīd about the wild asses and their she-asses. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The lām in lillah denotes entitlement, particularity, and ownership; the whole genus and every form of hamd belongs to God. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The nominative makes a nominal sentence for permanence, the generic article broadens it, and fronting highlights it; the accusative construction cannot convey all three effects. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The name derives from al-ilah, definite form of ilah, a generic word for deity; ilah is linked to worship or states entailing humble reverence and follows a passive fa'al pattern like kitab. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Hamd is praise for a chosen beautiful quality, whether an action such as generosity or another quality such as courage; thana' is its broader genus. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The al in al-hamd is generic: because the verbal noun stands in place of an action, the definite form presents the kind of praise as familiar and makes it prominent, rather than identifying one instance. KASHSHAF: Al-Zamakhsharī rejects exhaustive totality. [14 notes: BAYDAWI; SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL; KASHSHAF; ALUSI-FULL+; IBNASHUR+]
- 1:2/q02/p4 The article denotes praise's essence. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A second grammatical view says the definite noun denotes only the essence or nature of praise, not universal scope. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): For Nasafi, the definite article in al-hamd is comprehensive, against the Mu‘tazila; its attachment to Allah, the name of the divine essence, gathers all perfections and reflects the doctrine of created acts. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): Al-ḥamd lillāh establishes that God deserves every praise by His essence and all perfections; Rabb adds that He deserves praise as Lord, Owner, and Benefactor. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Allah is the proper name of the necessarily existent essence, worthy of every praise. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Exhausting all genera entails their individuals, since lordship over abstract essences makes no sense; the plural with the definite article explicitly signals comprehensive scope. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Allah is the proper name of the necessarily existent divine essence, the one deserving every praise. [6 notes: RAZI-FULL; NASAFI; BIQAI; IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 1:2/q03 How is al-ḥamd read?  [ٱلْحَمْدُ · readings]
turns on: How Arabic dialects treat case vowels and assimilation between the two frequently joined words.
- 1:2/q03/p1 Al-ḥamd is read nominative. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): The same implied-command reading continues with “Say, You alone”; the author notes that Arabic often omits words clear from context. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (القراء السبعة وجمهور الناس): The seven canonical readers and most people pronounce the dal of al-hamdu with nominative case. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج): Sufyan ibn ʿUyayna and Ruʾba reportedly read the dal with fatḥ, implying an omitted verb. TABRISI, d. 548 (الأخفش وأبو علي الفارسي): They parse al-ḥamdu as a nominative subject with an omitted predication such as is due or is established for God; the phrase lillah supplies its meaning. TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن جني): Ibn Jinni says kasra on ḥamd and ḍamma on lillah signal a close subject-predicate bond, making the two parts sound like one word. TABRISI, d. 548 (سيبويه): The lām normally has fatḥa because it cannot begin with sukun; lām of possession and emphasis are distinguished, and attached pronouns restore its original opening vowel. TABRISI, d. 548 (سيبويه): The kasra of lām in the jussive verb li-yafʿal distinguishes it from emphatic lām. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Another account says the plural nūn is opened because kasra after waw or yā is heavy, like in sawfa and ayna. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Readers agree on ḍamma in al-ḥamdu and kasra in lillāh; three noncanonical vowel combinations are also transmitted. TABRISI, d. 548 (زيد بن علي): Zayd ibn Ali's fatḥa on the bā of rabb is understood as demonstrating that the form is possible, not as a recitation. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Al-ḥamd is nominative as the subject and lillāh its predicate: praise is established and belongs to God. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أبو رزين العقيلي والربيع بن خيثم وأبو عمران الجوني): These readers pronounce the bāʾ of rabb in the nominative. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): In the mystical reading, guidance brings light to the heart, the blessed become polished mirrors reflecting sanctity, and the final plea seeks protection from desire and doubt. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi reads Lord of the worlds as an argument for the ancient God's existence; the created lights, darkness, and angels are kinds within that all-encompassing world-order. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Most scholars require recitation in prayer, while al-Asamm and al-Hasan b. Salih deny that recitation is obligatory. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The command to perform the dawn recitation in Quran 17:78 is a command to read and therefore makes recitation obligatory. | mentions 17:78 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو الدرداء عن النبي): A man asked the Prophet whether prayer includes recitation; when the man concluded that it was obligatory, the Prophet approved him. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن مسعود عن النبي): In another report, the Prophet answers a question about reciting in prayer by asking whether there can be prayer without recitation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الأصم): Al-Asamm argues that the Prophet described prayer as things seen, whereas recitation is not visible and must therefore fall outside prayer; Razi answers that seeing with two objects means knowing. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If the hadith must be figurative, invalidity is nearer than mere incompleteness because a nonexistent act resembles an invalid act more than a valid but imperfect one; presumption, prohibition, and caution reinforce this reading. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Although belief in obligation may itself leave theoretical doubt, actual recitation creates no danger while omission creates fear of failing the duty; therefore action is the safer course. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi reports disagreement over the minimum recitation for Hanafis: Abu Hanifa accepts one verse, even isolated letters, while Abu Yusuf and Muhammad require three short verses or one long verse. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī rejects supplying an unspoken command ‘say’: the phrase is already a complete assertion that praise belongs to God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Allah is nobler than Lord, although supplicants commonly call God ‘Lord’; al-Rāzī refers readers to his earlier discussion of the divine names. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The rightly guided caliphs also consistently recited it, and the Prophet commanded Muslims to follow their sunna. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Muslims everywhere recite it in prayer, and the Qur'an commands following the path of believers. | mentions 4:115 QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Given that usage, the report’s wording may be read as aʿḍalat al-malakayn or ʿaḍḍalat al-malakayn without a preposition. QURTUBI, d. 671 (جعفر الصادق): Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq gives a symbolic reading of the letters: ḥāʾ signifies unity, mīm dominion, and dāl permanence; knowing these is knowing God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (غير سيبويه): Another explanation takes the nominative as an appeal for pardon and as exaltation and glorification, so it functions as supplication rather than a plain report. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (جعفر الصادق): Jaʿfar al-Sadiq offers an esoteric reading of the letters of hamd: unity, dominion, and permanence; knowing these attributes is the reality of praising God. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Other grammarians take the phrase as an appeal for pardon and mercy, magnification, or a request rather than a plain report; anot her reading teaches people to say it. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Readings assimilate dāl and lām in opposite directions, treating the letters as one word because they are used together. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): The nominative conveys universal and permanent praise belonging to God rather than newly arising praise. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Two recitations assimilate the dāl and lām in either direction, treating them as one word because the terms are used together. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): A report calls praise the head of gratitude and says a servant has not thanked God without praising Him; verbal mention spreads notice of a favor more than inward belief or ambiguous bodily acts. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (القراء السبعة): The nominative reading is stronger and agreed upon by the seven canonical readers: it states that praise is established and enduringly belongs to God, including praise by God and others. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān prefers that al-ʿālamīn denote morally responsible beings, supported by the verse about signs for the knowledgeable; Ḥafṣ's kasra on the lām clarifies the sense. | mentions 30:22 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الجمهور): The majority pronounce the dāl of al-ḥamdu with ḍamma. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The nominative reading is semantically stronger and unanimously adopted by the Seven because it states that praise is established for God. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Although al-ʿālamīn is an irregular plural, Abū Ḥayyān favors the responsible creatures because a cited verse and Ḥafṣ's lām-kasra reading support that scope. | mentions 29:44 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو رزين العقيلي والربيع بن خيثم وأبو عمران الجوني): Abū Razīn, al-Rabīʿ ibn Khuthaym, and Abū ʿImrān al-Jūnī read both epithets in the nominative as detached clauses. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The article in al-hamd is analyzed as universal, generic, or referring to known praise; al-Zamakhshari chooses the generic reading. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari rejects the universal reading; the author infers that praise here is performed by the servant, who cannot produce every praise by himself and others. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading raises al-hamd as subject with a deleted nominal predicate, the preferred estimate, though a verb can also be supplied. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): A variant with damma on the lām of li-llah follows the preceding dāl; al-Zamakhshari prefers harmony from inflection to building, citing a Qays dialect and Qur'anic murdifin. | mentions 8:9 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): “Qur'an of dawn” in 17:78 means the dawn prayer, as the sound reports say angels of night and day witness it; scholars agree prayer requires recitation. | mentions 17:78 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (القراء السبعة): The seven canonical readers vocalize the dāl of al-ḥamdu with ḍamma, making al-ḥamdu a subject and li-llāhi its predicate. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Companion al-Aswad ibn Sari asks to recite praises of his Lord, and the Prophet replies that his Lord loves praise. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الله تعالى): In a divine report, God divides the prayer between Himself and His servant, responds to each recited section, calls 1:5 shared between them, and grants the servant's request. NASHR, d. 833 (author): Maki deems these noncanonical-imam readings permissible when reliably transmitted, since each has a sound Arabic analysis and agrees with the codex spelling; Ibn al-Jazari says others also share some forms. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali explains the sound report that God divided prayer between Himself and His servant: the first three verses are God's praise, which He accepts from the reciting servant as praise and glorification. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The passage names al-Baqara 2:281 as the last revealed verse and says its recitation position between the debt verse and the verse before it shows revelation and recitation order can differ. | mentions 2:281, 75:17 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Recitation order can differ from revelation chronology; the verse allowing the Prophet to marry certain women was placed before a later restriction. | mentions 33:50, 33:52 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): He offers two readings of the merciful names: universal mercy permits created benefits while specific mercy protects from harm; he says the nearer reading is general lordly beneficence and a special divine mercy directed to felicity. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الأسود بن سريع): Al-Aswad ibn Sari asked to recite praises he had composed, and the Prophet replied that God loves praise. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أنس): Anas reports that deliberation is from God, haste from Satan, God is most ready to excuse, and praise is most beloved to Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عائشة): During a drought prayer, the Prophet invoked God as Lord of the worlds, the Merciful, and Master of Judgment, asking for rain that would sustain them; Abu Dawud calls the report rare with a good chain, and says it supports the Medinan no-alif reading. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): It is called the seven oft-repeated because it has seven verses, each can stand for seven Qur'anic portions, and reciting them is said to earn the whole Qur'an's reward or close seven gates of Hell. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some restrict the worlds to rational beings among angels, humans, and jinn; al-Suyūṭī associates this reading with ʿilm, while universal scope fits ʿalāma, sign. ALUSI, d. 1270 (جعفر الصادق): Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq is reported to identify the worlds as humans; al-Ālūsī says the broader reading suits the context, while human beings are the focal recipients of law and prophecy. | mentions 25:1, 26:165 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī’s own Sufi reading is that souls invoke God by the first name through which they acknowledged Him in the primordial covenant. | mentions 7:172 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): Ibn ʿArabī’s account says the universal spirit sought its source without knowing that the sought reality was already within it, then recognized God in the station of lordship. | mentions 50:16 ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Other proposed governors are the expressed الحمد, a vocative, or a past-tense reading of rabb; al-Ālūsī objects to the first for its syntax and to the latter parses as awkward. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawwānī considers figurative divine praise unnecessary because the people of truth affirm that God truly speaks; al-Ālūsī reads the tongue restriction as relative or customary. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Some tie the generic reading to the Muʿtazilī account that human actions belong to their agents; al-Ālūsī replies that good acts still depend on God’s enabling and empowering. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī rejects arguments that the generic article means only the perfect kind or that a verbal noun cannot convey exhaustive scope; context can make either reading exhaustive. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers an exhaustive reading here: the sūra’s encompassing attributes of lordship, mercy, and sovereignty support all praise belonging to God, and the shift to إياك نعبد marks a new address rather than an explanation. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Zamakhsharī’s statement in 64:1 that sovereignty and praise belong exclusively to God supports a comprehensive reading, though the present context gives further reason to include every instance of praise. | mentions 64:1 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The transmitted and standard reading has al-ḥamdu in the nominative as a subject with lillāhi as its predicate. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الحسن البصري وزيد بن علي): Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and Zayd b. ʿAlī read with the dāl assimilated toward the following lām; Ibrāhīm b. Abla and Bedouins assimilate the reverse way. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some prefer Ibrāhīm’s reading because following the first sound is easier than the reverse and because the dāl’s inflection is stronger than the lām’s fixed vowel. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Others prefer al-Ḥasan’s reading because the second sound more commonly follows the first and the uninflected sound should follow the inflected one. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī calls Ibrāhīm’s reading أشف, a word that can indicate either greater clarity or greater obscurity. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The nominative reading is strongest because the nominal sentence, in this context, conveys settled and enduring praise rather than a renewed act of praising. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Grammarians may treat an underlying verbal form as the source construction for verbal nouns, but here the nominative form answers the rhetorical need for an established state. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Words often become conventionally narrow because one instance is common or other instances are unknown; this can obscure Qur’anic and Prophetic meanings that preserve original lexical breadth. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī calls comprehensive scope an imagined error and takes the article as genus; the ensuing debate addresses whether that reading protects the Muʿtazilī account of human acts. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Another defense treats genus in rhetorical context as the complete instance; a critic replies that comprehensive scope can likewise treat everything else as negligible, so neither reading avoids the theological difficulty unaided. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī rejects the claim that genus is the obvious common reading: in rhetorical settings a definite generic article commonly conveys comprehensiveness, especially when exclusive praise is at issue. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He challenges the claim that genus exclusivity is immediately apparent: its inference is too subtle to be the obvious reading, and the manuscript ends while he disputes the argument’s rhetorical strength. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Praise has a narrower occasion but a broader object than gratitude; its inclusion may signal a sacred allusion, and tasbīḥ is omitted because praise already includes both negative and positive perfections. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The sounder reading makes the first clause praise of God in the third person and the next a speaker’s self-report; the missing conjunction marks a change of state, not an explanation. | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Some read al-ḥamdu as true, complete praise alone belonging to God rather than all instances; al-Ālūsī says this meaning can be apt but rejects using it to evade comprehensive scope here. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The standard transmitted reading has al-ḥamdu nominative as the subject; Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and Zayd b. ʿAlī assimilate the vowel of dāl to the following lām, while Ibrāhīm b. Abla and Bedouins do the reverse. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): The assimilation readings are anomalous; al-Zamakhsharī calls Ibrāhīm’s reading “more lucid,” while competing arguments favor each reading based on direction of assimilation and the relative strength of inflection and construction. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers the nominative nominal sentence because, in context, it expresses established and continuing praise, whereas a verbal sentence ordinarily presents renewed action; permanence also depends on context, not form alone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن الهمام): Another interpretation parses al-ḥamd as a passive verbal noun meaning being praised; its form permits other readings, and some even take the lām as causal. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The claim that a definite singular is more comprehensive does not undermine this wording; the plural avoids the familiar-world reading and includes all created genera. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Humanity is a concentrated summary of created things, and reflection on the self reveals the Creator's greatness; the apt reading here remains universal. | mentions 51:20, 51:21 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Other proposed parses take Rabb as a vocative or a past-tense verb, but al-Alusi finds the vocative unclear and the verbal reading syntactically remote. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Signs of God’s unity may be immediate or detailed, sensory or imagined, and can be read through perception, memory, and reason; all require conscious attention. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Judging the self, consciousness, and external objects as truly corresponding already presupposes an encompassing necessary truth that guarantees their relations; this tacit attribution to God grounds our judgments. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله [ ص ]): The Prophet says prayer is invalid without reciting the Opening of the Book; the report is in both Sahih collections. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari calls hamd and madh equivalent; a proposed reading takes his word 'brothers' to mean derivational relatives like jazb and jadhb. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur rejects the derivational-sibling reading: the commentators' synonym reading fits al-Zamakhshari's gloss in al-Fa'iq and his treating blame as hamd's opposite. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (السيد الشريف): Al-Sayyid al-Sharif, apparently also Sa'd al-Din, reads the synonyms as retaining the voluntary-quality restriction, citing al-Zamakhshari on 49:7. | mentions 49:7 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (عبد الحكيم السلكوتي): Abd al-Hakim al-Salkuti takes synonymy to cancel the voluntary-quality restriction for both words, presenting this as a possible or transmitted reading rather than a preference. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative as the subject in every transmitted reading, and li-llah is its predicate, related to the general existential predicate. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A second proposal derives the name from wilah, 'overwhelmed and bewildered'; initial waw became hamza after kasra, then the article was added and hamza dropped. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari says Arabs pronounce Allah's lam emphatically except where the preceding sound requires kasra, treating inherited consensus as evidence against speculative explanations. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): In this rhetorical context the definite article is exhaustive: there is no external or mental antecedent and no suitable literal or generic reading, so all genera are included. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The plural prevents an antecedent or generic reading and explicitly indicates exhaustive scope; Ibn Ashur rejects the Sakaki school's claim that singular is more comprehensive, referring to 2:31 for discussion. | mentions 2:31 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو سعيد الخدري): A report in Sahih al-Bukhari says Abu Sa'id al-Khudri recited Umm al-Qur'an over a person suffering from a sting as a healing prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Taken explicitly and by implication, the sura gives a general outline of the Qur'an's aims and prompts readers to seek their fuller detail as able. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The sura has seven verses by agreement among reciters and commentators, with a few dissenting counts. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Mathani is a plural of muthanna, with alternative vocalized or lightened forms; al-Jurjani notes that each can also be feminine. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو هريرة): The first proof is Malik's report from Abu Hurayra: God's division of prayer begins with al-Hamdu lillah, without mentioning the basmala; prayer here means recitation in prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبي بن كعب): In the second proof, Ubayy ibn Ka'b recites the celebrated sura from al-Hamdu lillah to its end when asked how he opens prayer, without reciting the basmala. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports in Muslim and other collections that the Prophet, Abu Bakr, and Umar began recitation with al-Hamdu lillah and did not mention the basmala at either end. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عائشة): Aisha reports that the Prophet opened prayer with the takbir and began recitation with al-Hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عبد الله بن مغفل): Ibn Mughaffal says he prayed with the Prophet and the first three caliphs but never heard them say the basmala, and directs worshippers to recite al-Hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The decisive sixth proof is Medina's continuous practice: across generations, its mosque did not recite the basmala aloud in congregational prayers, making the practice mutawatir evidence that it is not part of the sura. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari calls hamd and madh “siblings”; Ibn Ashur rejects the interpretation that he means only a remote derivational relationship and reads him as calling them synonymous. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayh says some such source expressions can be made nominative topics, as in al-hamdu lillah; the definite form favors this declarative use. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Not every source phrase permits the nominative pattern; Sibawayh says expressions such as al-saqyu laka or al-raʿyu laka are not licensed. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur cites Sibawayh to explain the Arabic stages of this verbal noun construction and the rationale for the nominative al-hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الجمهور): A third answer reads “voluntary” as describing the praised agent, not the particular attribute for which praise is given. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur says the nominative definite wording is most eloquent, conveys enduring and comprehensive praise, and cites al-Kashshaf's comparison with Abraham's greeting at 51:25. | mentions 51:25 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He explains the indirect reading as analogous to saying “long sword-belt” to convey tall stature: a literal expression can serve an intended implication. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Kashshaf says all Arabs pronounce the divine lām emphatically, and their agreement shows the practice was inherited. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verse puts الحمد before لله because praise for revelation and Islam frames the opening recitation; contextual importance can precede God's intrinsic priority, while praising Him still attends to His affairs. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The plural العالمين makes exhaustive scope explicit and removes a possible generic or familiar reading; Ibn Ashur rejects the claim among al-Sakkaki's followers that an exhaustive singular is broader than an exhaustive plural. | mentions 2:31 CORPUSCORANICUM (author): The doxology was already used in the early Meccan period; in middle Meccan texts it is joined to the epithet rabb al-ʿālamīn, itself attested early. | mentions 52:48, 37:182, 39:75, 83:6, 81:27, 81:29, 69:43, 68:52, 56:80 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الكشاف): The nominative shift signals a settled meaning; the raised salam in Abraham's reply indicates a more enduring and complete greeting than theirs. | mentions 51:25 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (البيضاوي): The nominative is chosen to convey praise's universality and permanence rather than a renewed event. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (بعضهم): Some take al as referring to the familiar praise among people, while others read it as encompassing every instance and kind of praise. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): الحمد لله can carry both report and invocation, but إن الحمد لله forces a performative reading and loses that breadth. STUDYQURAN (author): Thanks responds to what has already been received, while praise is more universal because it extols qualities prior to any gift. FARRA: Al-Farrāʾ says reciters agree on the nominative. TAB: Al-Ṭabarī claims agreement and rejects deliberate accusative recitation as a distortion. TAB-FULL: Al-Ṭabarī defends the inherited nominative against deliberate accusative recitation. NAHHAS: Al-Naḥḥās compares Basran, Kisāʾī and Farrāʾ parses, accusative dialect readings and their scope; he prefers nominative as universal, while discussing further vowel forms and Basran objections. NAHHAS: Al-Naḥḥās compares Basran, Kisāʾī and Farrāʾ parses, accusative dialect readings and their scope; he prefers nominative as universal, while discussing further vowel forms and Basran objections. [141 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(القراء السبعة وجمهور الناس,سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج); TABRISI(ابن جني,الأخفش وأبو علي الفارسي,زيد بن علي,سيبويه); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو رزين العقيلي والربيع بن خيثم وأبو عمران الجوني); RAZI-FULL(أبو الدرداء عن النبي,ابن مسعود عن النبي,الأصم); QURTUBI(جعفر الصادق,غير سيبويه); QURTUBI-FULL(جعفر الصادق); BAYDAWI; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو رزين العقيلي والربيع بن خيثم وأبو عمران الجوني,الجمهور); SAMIN-DURR(الزمخشري); IBNKATHIR(القراء السبعة,الله تعالى,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); NASHR; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(أنس,الأسود بن سريع,عائشة); BURSEVI; ALUSI(الحسن البصري وزيد بن علي,الدواني,الزمخشري,الشيخ الأكبر,بعضهم,جعفر الصادق); ALUSI-FULL(ابن الهمام,الزمخشري,بعضهم); ELMALILI; QUTB-ZILAL(رسول الله [ ص ]); IBNASHUR(السيد الشريف,صاحب الكشاف,عبد الحكيم السلكوتي); IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو سعيد الخدري,أبو هريرة,أبي بن كعب,أنس بن مالك,الجمهور,الزمخشري,سيبويه,صاحب الكشاف,عائشة,عبد الله بن مغفل); CORPUSCORANICUM; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(البيضاوي,الكشاف,بعضهم); STUDYQURAN; FARRA(القراء); TAB; TAB-FULL; NAHHAS(ابن عيينة ورؤبة ابن العجاج,البصريون,الفراء,الكسائي,علي بن سليمان); ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; ABUHAYYAN(القراء السبعة)+; ALUSI-FULL+; ALUSI+; NAHHAS+; RAZI-FULL+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT+]
- 1:2/q03/p2 Al-ḥamd is read accusative. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (بعضهم): Some read Rabb in the accusative as a form of praise. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (بعضهم): Others take accusative Rabb as direct address, parallel to the implied address in “You alone.” TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The nominative reading is stronger and more laudatory than an accusative meaning I praise, because it makes praise due to God from all creation, not only the speaker. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn may be accusative as praise or as an implied command to remember the Lord; a nominative praise reading is also possible, as poetry shows. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الجمهور): Most readers put rabb in the genitive; Abū al-Ghāliya, Ibn al-Sumayfa, and ʿĪsā ibn ʿUmar read it in the accusative; several others use nominative. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Many names indicate the honor of the named; Fātiḥat al-Kitāb is so called because it opens codices, teaching, recitation, and prayer, or because praise opens speech, or because it was the first sura sent down. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet's lifelong practice of reciting al-Fatiha obligates imitation, supported by Quranic commands to follow him. | mentions 7:158, 24:63, 3:31 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The rightly guided caliphs consistently recited al-Fatiha; reports in both Sahihs say the Prophet, Abu Bakr, and Umar began recitation with al-Hamd, so their practice should be followed. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Muslims everywhere recite al-Fatiha in prayer; even if some regard it as recommended rather than obligatory, abandoning the unanimous practice leaves the believers' path and incurs Quran 4:115's warning. | mentions 4:115 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet identifies al-Fatiha as a surah unmatched in the Torah, Gospel, or Psalms; the Companions' answer about what they recite in prayer indicates a known universal practice. [RAZI-FULL:v1p158#2/ r2] RAZI-FULL, d. 606 · author | holds | Razi argues that Quran 73:20 requires al-Fatiha: the options are al-Fatiha, another surah, or a choice; the latter two fail because another is not universally required and al-Fatiha is agreed better and more complete. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Fatiha is the recitation made accessible to all because every Muslim has it memorized, whereas other surahs may not be memorized by each person. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (رفاعة بن مالك عن النبي): A man who asks the Prophet to teach him prayer is told to face the qibla, say takbir, and recite al-Fatiha; Razi treats this imperative as obligation and recitation as part of prayer. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If prayers with and without al-Fatiha were both valid, habitual recitation would make other surahs abandoned; because the community agrees al-Fatiha is preferable, Razi concludes omission is invalid. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Just as bowing and prostration cannot be replaced, al-Fatiha cannot be replaced by another reading, with caution as the common rationale. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since the Prophet recited al-Fatiha throughout his life, replacing it with another recitation is innovation and abandonment of the required prophetic example. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): Abu Huraira reports that the Prophet ordered him to announce that there is no prayer without recitation, even if by al-Fatiha. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أم سلمة عن النبي): Umm Salama reports that the Prophet recited al-Fatiha while counting the basmala as its first verse, followed by the rest of its verses. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi supports obligatory Fatiha recitation because the Prophet consistently recited it and commands require following him. | mentions 6:153 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The command to recite what is easy from the Qur'an is obligatory; because reciting other passages is not, the obligatory recitation must be the Fatiha. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Reciting the Fatiha is the safer course, following the prophetic instruction to leave what causes doubt for what does not. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet's constant practice makes abandoning his command forbidden, and Muslims agree that the Fatiha is better and more complete than another recitation. | mentions 24:63 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since God names the Fatiha “prayer,” prayer without its recitation does not occur; Razi takes this to establish that reciting it is a pillar, as his school holds. QURTUBI, d. 671 (سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج): The seven readers and most people read the dāl in al-ḥamdu with nominative ḍamma; Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna and Ruʾba ibn al-ʿAjjāj are reported to read it accusative, with an implied verb. QURTUBI, d. 671 (سيبويه): Sībawayh says nominative al-ḥamdu lillāh conveys that praise from the speaker and all creatures belongs to God, while the accusative reports only the speaker’s own praise. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb may be read in the nominative by syntactic disconnection, meaning He is Lord of the worlds, or in the accusative as a word of praise. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (القراء السبعة): The seven readers and most people read alhamdu with the dāl in the nominative; Sufyan ibn ʿUyayna and Ruʾba report an accusative reading with an implied verb. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (سيبويه): Sibawayh says the nominative attributes praise to the speaker and all creation, while the accusative attributes it to the speaker alone. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): The accusative rabb al-ʿālamīn is analyzed as praise, a vocative, or an implied verb indicated by “praise.” BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Al-ḥamdu is nominative as subject with “to God” as predicate, though an accusative reading is also reported. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): An accusative reading treats رب العالمين as praise, direct address, or an implied action indicated by الحمد. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The word is nominative as a subject with لله as predicate, though an accusative reading exists; nominative signals praise as universal and enduring rather than newly arising. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative as a subject, though it can be read accusative with an implied verb as an explanatory verbal noun, like ‘thanks’ or ‘ingratitude’; nominative wording conveys a settled, enduring meaning. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Al-hamd is beautiful praise given preferentially; syntactically it is a nominative subject with an implied predicate such as “due” or “established,” while a reading with an implicit verb makes it an accusative verbal noun. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The shift from accusative to nominative indicates that praise is settled and enduring. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (هارون العتكي ورؤبة وسفيان بن عيينة): Hārūn al-ʿAtakī, Ruʾba, and Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna read al-ḥamda in the accusative. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): With nominative al-ḥamdu the lām marks entitlement; with accusative al-ḥamda a verb such as “I praise God” must be supplied, conveying renewed action. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): For the accusative reading, Abū Ḥayyān prefers an omitted verb directly indicated by the wording, such as “I praise God,” over “I say” or “adhere to praise.” ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): In the accusative reading the lām is explanatory, not a strengthening of transitivity, since the verbal noun cannot govern this phrase as its object. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (زيد بن علي وطائفة): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and a group read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative as praise; the reading weakens if the following adjectives remain genitive. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الأهوازي): Al-Ahwāzī reports Zayd ibn ʿAlī reading rabb, raḥmān, and raḥīm all in the accusative, avoiding the mismatch in case. | mentions 1:3 ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects parsing accusative rabb as an implied verb of praise or as apposition: one rests on imagined syntax, the other is interrupted by raḥmān raḥīm. | mentions 1:3 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (هارون العتكي، ورؤبة، وسفيان بن عيينة): Hārūn al-ʿAtakī, Ruʾba, and Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna read al-ḥamda in the accusative. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects deriving the accusative rabb from a prior implied verb such as We praise God, since this analogy to ellipsis in coordination is not productive. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The accusative reading needs an implied governor such as I praise God or I praised God; it presents praise as newly occurring and names its agent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Among proposed governors for the accusative, Abū Ḥayyān prefers the explicit-sense verb ahmadu over a command or other unexpressed verb. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): With the accusative, lillāh is an explanatory lām; it cannot be governed by the verbal noun, as the Arabic construction saqyan li-Zayd shows. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (زيد بن علي وطائفة): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and a group read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative as praise, though lowering the following epithets weakens the construction. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الأهوازي): Al-Ahwāzī records Zayd ibn ʿAlī reading rabb, raḥmān, and raḥīm all in the accusative, which removes the syntactic weakness. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو العالية وابن السميفع وعيسى بن عمرو): Abū al-ʿĀliya, Ibn al-Sumayfaʿ, and ʿĪsā ibn ʿAmr read raḥmān and raḥīm in the accusative, as detached epithets. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The accusative reading of “al-ḥamd” involves ellipsis; grammarians differ over whether its omitted governing word repeats the noun or is another word. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): A rare accusative al-hamda is parsed either as a verbal noun replacing I praise, or as an imperative-like source meaning say praise; al-Tabari favors the command interpretation, but the author prefers declarative force. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The accusative may alternatively be a direct object of recite or read, but the verbal-noun analysis is better because the wording itself supports it. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Raising al-hamd is stronger and more expressive of stable praise, while accusative signals renewal; Abraham's salām is better than the angels' salāman by the verse's instruction to answer more beautifully. | mentions 11:69, 4:86 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): On the accusative reading, li-llah is attached to an omitted explanatory verb such as I mean; source nouns do not normally govern this lām phrase, though it may strengthen a derivative verb. | mentions 47:8 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading makes rabb genitive as an adjective or appositive to Allah; accusative allows an implied I praise, a detached praise, or vocative, with vocative weakest because it separates adjective from noun; nominative is detached predicate huwa rabb. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): In the divine hadith, “prayer” means recitation, as in 17:110; because the Fatiha details the division, its recitation is a major pillar of prayer. | mentions 17:110 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج): Sufyan ibn Uyaynah and Ru'bah ibn al-Ajjaj read al-ḥamda with fatḥa as an implied verbal object. BURHAN, d. 794 (ابن حبان): Ibn Hibban reads the report comparing al-Fatiha with the Torah and Gospel as a difference in reward granted by God’s favor, not as intrinsic superiority within the Quran. NASHR, d. 833 (زيد بن علي بن الحسين، رؤبة بن العجاج، هارون بن موسى العتكي، الحسن): These readers pronounce the dāl of al-ḥamdu with fatḥa; the accusative is treated as a verbal noun with its commonly understood verb omitted. NASHR, d. 833 (الحسن): Al-Hasan also reads the lām in Allāh with fatḥa, matching the preceding accusative; the source calls this a dialect of some Qays speakers. NASHR, d. 833 (أبو زيد سعيد بن أوس الأنصاري): Abu Zayd reports rabb al-ʿālamīn in both nominative and accusative; successive adjectives may differ, with some governed by an implied verb and others by an implied subject, but not revert to genitive. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Al-ḥamdu li-llāh is read with nominative case as a subject, accusative as a verbal noun, or genitive by vowel assimilation. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn is read genitive as an adjective, nominative with an implicit subject, or accusative with an implicit verb or as a vocative. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The surah is Fatiha because it opens the codex, instruction, Qur'an recitation, prayer, speech, revelation, the aims of this life, and the gates of Paradise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (زيد بن علي): Zayd b. ʿAlī is reported to read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative; the author’s preferred parse is an accusative of detachment with an implied verb such as أمدح. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): One analysis treats the accusative as detachment from the preceding construction, supplying a verb of praise or mention; another says the prior الحمد licenses the construction. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī considers accusative detachment the strongest analysis, especially if al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm is read in the accusative as a substitute rather than an adjective. ALUSI, d. 1270 (هارون بن موسى): Hārūn b. Mūsā reads al-ḥamda in the accusative, a construction common among Arabs for a definite verbal noun with an implied verb such as نحمد. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The implied verb for the accusative is in the first-person plural, matching naʿbudu and nastaʿīn and the humility of worship, rather than a majestic first-person singular. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (هارون بن موسى): Hārūn b. Mūsā reads al-ḥamda in the accusative, like Arabs who use definite verbal nouns with an omitted verb; the implied “we praise” suits worship and seeking help better than royal “we.” | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Some grammarians say verbal nouns are originally accusative because events require predication; the underlying verbal relation is normally expressed by a verb, making the noun-plus-verb construction seem redundant. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (زيد بن علي): A reading attributed to Zayd ibn ʿAli puts Rabb al-ʿalamin in the accusative; al-Alusi finds the first grammatical explanation, an implied praise or mention verb, most apparent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): One analysis treats the accusative as an adjective cut off from its antecedent, supplying an implied verb such as I praise or I mention; an objection about the following adjective is answered by taking Rahman as apposition. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو حيان): Abu Hayyan rejects deriving the accusative from the earlier praise merely by attraction, arguing that this mechanism belongs to coordination; al-Alusi disputes that objection using grammatical authorities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Alusi considers an accusative absolute construction the strongest and nearly compelled by the apparent wording. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The relation between the basmala and Fatiha unfolds the primordial covenant between the eternal Lord and c reation, inscribing a relation already present in human nature. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Fatiha proceeds as instruction; the command to read in the name of the Lord is a key contextual sign of this teaching purpose. | mentions 96:1 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Nothing lies outside the basmala's attraction, so it relates generally to al-Fatiha as one recites, writes, praises, or begins the Quran in its name. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Al-Fatiha is Meccan and has seven verses; Muslims recite it at least seventeen times daily, more with voluntary prayers, and may repeat it without limit. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayhi explains that praise expressions originally use an omitted verb in the absolute accusative, as in 'we praise God with praise'; the verbal noun replaces the verb. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Sibawayhi distinguishes supplicatory accusative sources from declarative ones such as hamdan and shukran, whose omitted verbs mean 'I praise' or 'I thank'. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari also describes hamd's original accusative with an omitted verb and its shift to nominative as a means of indicating stability. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The nominative makes a nominal sentence for permanence, the generic article broadens it, and fronting highlights it; the accusative construction cannot convey all three effects. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (سيبويه): A reading with al-hamda in the accusative and definite article is a Tamimi dialect form attested by Sibawayhi; its implied verb limits praise to the speaker or intended group. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Raising hamd announces praise from the speaker and all creation, whereas the accusative announces only the speaker's praise. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Although anomalous, the accusative reading documents an Arabic stage in this familiar construction, showing that some speakers retained its absolute-object origin even with the article. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The nominative is more eloquent than the accusative, and definite accusative is more expressive than indefinite accusative; raising marks permanence and stability. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari compares the nominative 'peace' in 51:25 with the earlier accusative greeting: Abraham answers with a more enduring, better greeting. | mentions 51:25 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He reads the sura's annexation to Fatihat al-Kitab as descriptive: it means a sura characterized as the Book's opening, with no separate title needed. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): On this reading, mathani in al-Fatiha resembles the repeated narratives and themes in the Qur'an called mathani in al-Zumar. | mentions 39:23 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayh explains conventional verbal nouns such as “beware” or “thanks” as accusatives governed by verbs suppressed from use, and distinguishes supplicatory formulas from other expressions. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative in all transmitted readings; its predicate is lillah, while an underlying analysis treats hamd as an accusative verbal noun replacing an omitted verb meaning “we praise God.” IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Raising hamd creates a nominal sentence for permanence, generic al for comprehensive praise, and fronting for emphasis; these effects are unavailable in the ordinary accusative construction. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Accusative hamd can take al in the Tamim reading, but an implicit “I praise” limits praise to the speaker and “we praise” to believers or monotheists; generic hamd instead includes every praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayh's distinction is that nominative hamd reports praise from the speaker and all creatures, while accusative hamd reports the speaker's praise alone. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The nominative reading al-hamdu makes a nominal sentence and is stronger and more enduring than the accusative, which requires an implied verb. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (البحر المحيط): The seven readers agree on the nominative, which establishes praise; the accusative requires an implied agent and makes praise actor-specific and renewed. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Even if the accusative is read as an imperative, commanding praise does not establish that the praised one deserves it; the nominative indicates deserved, established praise. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The verse's wording is preferable to the alternatives madḥ, shukr, I/we praise, an imperative, accusative praise, an indefinite praise, inna, fronted lillah, or a divine attribute. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The nominative reading الحمدُ لله makes a stable nominal sentence; the accusative requires an implied verb and yields a verbal sentence or command, so the nominative is stronger. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Even if the accusative is construed as a command to praise, a command does not establish that its object deserves praise; the declarative nominative is preferable. STUDYQURAN (author): Shiite law permits reciting the Fātiḥah in the third and fourth cycles or an alternative formula praising God. STUDYQURAN (author): Muslims recite the Fātiḥah at diverse life events; funeral services are called fātiḥah in some lands as an opening from one life to another. [103 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(بعضهم); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الجمهور); RAZI-FULL(أبو هريرة عن النبي,أم سلمة عن النبي,رفاعة بن مالك عن النبي); QURTUBI(سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج,سيبويه); QURTUBI-FULL(القراء السبعة,سيبويه); BAYDAWI; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN(الأهوازي,زيد بن علي وطائفة,هارون العتكي ورؤبة وسفيان بن عيينة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو العالية وابن السميفع وعيسى بن عمرو,الأهوازي,زيد بن علي وطائفة,هارون العتكي، ورؤبة، وسفيان بن عيينة); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR(سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج); BURHAN(ابن حبان); NASHR(أبو زيد سعيد بن أوس الأنصاري,الحسن,زيد بن علي بن الحسين، رؤبة بن العجاج، هارون بن موسى العتكي، الحسن); ITQAN; BURSEVI; ALUSI(بعضهم,زيد بن علي,هارون بن موسى); ALUSI-FULL(أبو حيان,بعضهم,زيد بن علي,هارون بن موسى); ELMALILI; QUTB-ZILAL; IBNASHUR(سيبويه,صاحب الكشاف); IBNASHUR-FULL(سيبويه); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(البحر المحيط); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; STUDYQURAN; ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; ABUHAYYAN+; ALUSI-FULL+; ALUSI+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+; SAMIN-DURR+; TABRISI+]
- 1:2/q03/p3 The dāl of al-ḥamd is read with kasra. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (الحسن بن أبي الحسن وزيد بن علي): Al-Hasan and Zayd ibn ʿAli reportedly read the dal with kasra by assimilating its vowel to the following word. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن السميفع): Ibn al-Sumayfa reads al-ḥamda with fatḥa on dāl and kasra on lām. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الحسن بن أبي الحسن وزيد بن علي): Al-Ḥasan ibn Abī al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn ʿAlī read the dāl of al-ḥamd with kasra by assimilation of the first vowel to the second. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الحسن بن أبي الحسن): Hasan al-Basri and Zayd ibn ʿAli read the dāl with kasra by vowel assimilation. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الجمهور): The majority read al-ḥamdu with ḍamma on the dāl. Ibrāhīm ibn Abī ʿAbla assimilates its mīm to the following lām; al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn ʿAlī assimilate the dāl's kasra to the lām's, a stranger assimilation. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الحسن وزيد بن علي): Al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn ʿAlī assimilate the dāl's kasra to the lām's kasra; the author calls this stranger because a case vowel follows a non-case vowel. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الحسن وزيد بن علي): Al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn ʿAlī harmonize the dāl's kasra with the lām's kasra, a stranger kind of agreement between inflectional and non-inflectional vowels. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The kasra in a variant al-hamdi may be vowel harmony with the following lām, a Tamim and some Ghatafan dialect feature; another possibility is hidden nominative or accusative inflection. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الحسن وزيد بن علي): Al-Hasan and Zayd ibn Ali read the dāl with kasra by assimilating the first vowel to the second. NASHR, d. 833 (الحسن البصري): Al-Hasan reads the dāl of al-ḥamdulillāh with kasra; Ibn al-Jazari says both this and the preceding form are linguistically distant, explained as vowel-following. NAHHAS: Al-Ḥasan and Ibrāhīm readings and Tamīm/Rabīʿa vowel harmony are discussed. NAHHAS: Al-Ḥasan and Ibrāhīm readings and Tamīm/Rabīʿa vowel harmony are discussed. NAHHAS: Al-Ḥasan and Ibrāhīm readings and Tamīm/Rabīʿa vowel harmony are discussed. KASHSHAF-FULL: Al-Ḥasan assimilates it to lām. [14 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(الحسن بن أبي الحسن وزيد بن علي); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن السميفع); QURTUBI(الحسن بن أبي الحسن وزيد بن علي); QURTUBI-FULL(الحسن بن أبي الحسن); ABUHAYYAN(الجمهور,الحسن وزيد بن علي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن وزيد بن علي); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR(الحسن وزيد بن علي); NASHR(الحسن البصري); NAHHAS(إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة,الحسن); KASHSHAF-FULL(الحسن البصري)]
- 1:2/q03/p4 The lām follows the dāl in ḍamma. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (ابن أبي عبلة): Ibn Abi ʿAbla reportedly reads both dal and lam with ḍamma, assimilating the second to the first. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الجمهور): Most readers pronounce the lām of lillāh with kasra; Ibn ʿAbla uses ḍamma, a dialect of some Rabīʿa according to al-Farrāʾ. QURTUBI, d. 671 (ابن أبي عبلة): Ibn Abī ʿAbla reads the dāl and lām with ḍamma by vowel assimilation for phonetic harmony, a common pattern illustrated by other words and a poetic line. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن أبي عبلة): Ibn Abi ʿAbla reads the dāl and lām with ḍamma for vowel harmony, a common Arabic tendency also illustrated by other readings and poetic examples. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): A poem’s “wayl ummiha” derives from “waylun li-ummiha”: the first lām was dropped and the hamza’s ḍamma shifted after kasra proved heavy. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن أبي عبلة): Ibn Abi Abla reads both dāl and lām with ḍamma by assimilating the second vowel to the first; Ibn Kathir calls the reading anomalous. NASHR, d. 833 (إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة): Ibrahim b. Abi Ablah reads the first lām in al-ḥamdulillāh with ḍamma. KASHSHAF-FULL: Ibrāhīm's reading is preferred because of the strong inflectional vowel. KASHSHAF-FULL: Ibrāhīm's reading is preferred because of the strong inflectional vowel. - 1:2/q03/p5 Arabic speech also attests other vowel combinations in al-ḥamdu lillāh. — FARRA: Al-Farrāʾ records Bedouin vowels, accusative verbal-noun construction, vowel harmony from frequent use, and Abū Tharwān's contracted vocative poetry; 47:4 and 12:79 illustrate omitted verbs. IBNATIYYA: The seven read nominative; reports give accusative, kasra and double-ḍamma. IBNATIYYA: The seven read nominative; reports give accusative, kasra and double-ḍamma.
