[26 notes: FARRA(أبو ثروان,بعضهم); THALABI(إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة الشامي,الحسن البصري,قراء العامة,هارون بن موسى الأعور ورؤبة بن العجاج); JISHUMI(الفراء,زيد بن علي); KASHSHAF(إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة,الحسن البصري); IBNATIYYA(ابن أبي عبلة,الحسن بن الحسن وزيد بن علي,سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج); IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن أبي عبلة,الحسن بن أبي الحسن وزيد بن علي,القراء السبعة وجمهور الناس,سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج); KASHSHAF+]
- 1:2/q03/p6 Both connected vowels are read with kasra. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أبو نهيك): Abū Nuhayk reads both dāl and lām in al-ḥamd lillāh with kasra. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو نهيك)]
- 1:2/q03/p7 The divine name is read with imāla. — NASHR, d. 833 (قتيبة عن الكسائي): Qutayba transmits imāla of the alif in lillāh from al-Kisa'i, explained by the following kasra. NASHR, d. 833 (قتيبة عن الكسائي، كردم عن نافع، ورش): The author adds unlisted transmissions: Qutayba from al-Kisa'i has imāla in al-ʿālamīn and al-raḥmān; Kurram from Nafi and al-Ahwazi from Warsh lengthen vowels in mālik, naʿbudu, and iyyāka. [2 notes: NASHR(قتيبة عن الكسائي,قتيبة عن الكسائي، كردم عن نافع، ورش)]

## 1:2/q04 How is al-ḥamdu lillāh parsed and what does it do?  [ٱلْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ · grammar]
turns on: Whether the wording is a nominal report, an omitted verbal noun, or an instruction or performance.
- 1:2/q04/p1 The declarative expression instructs the servant to praise God. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): The same implied-command reading continues with “Say, You alone”; the author notes that Arabic often omits words clear from context. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn may be accusative as praise or as an implied command to remember the Lord; a nominative praise reading is also possible, as poetry shows. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (قيل): Another grammatical account takes the wording as a declarative form whose intended force is a command: say, “Praise belongs to God.” RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The command to perform the dawn recitation in Quran 17:78 is a command to read and therefore makes recitation obligatory. | mentions 17:78 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet's lifelong practice of reciting al-Fatiha obligates imitation, supported by Quranic commands to follow him. | mentions 7:158, 24:63, 3:31 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because the established form of the Prophet's prayer included al-Fatiha, the command to establish prayer requires it; the command recurs over a hundred times in the Quran. | mentions 2:43 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (رفاعة بن مالك عن النبي): A man who asks the Prophet to teach him prayer is told to face the qibla, say takbir, and recite al-Fatiha; Razi treats this imperative as obligation and recitation as part of prayer. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Madḥ can be condemned, as in the warning to throw dust in flatterers’ faces, whereas hamd is generally commanded. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because full gratitude is beyond human capacity, the verse states that all praise belongs to God rather than commanding people to praise Him. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī rejects supplying an unspoken command ‘say’: the phrase is already a complete assertion that praise belongs to God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Stating that praise is due, rather than directly commanding it, lets the obedient praise God while leaving the disobedient with a lesser offense than rejecting an order. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): One group says God cannot command praise: if it repays a blessing, the command makes generosity transactional; if not, it is needless toil imposed on another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (القدرية): The Qadariyya argue that God would not truly be Lord if He created a disbeliever’s unbelief and then punished him, or commanded faith while preventing it. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi supports obligatory Fatiha recitation because the Prophet consistently recited it and commands require following him. | mentions 6:153 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The rightly guided caliphs also consistently recited it, and the Prophet commanded Muslims to follow their sunna. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Muslims everywhere recite it in prayer, and the Qur'an commands following the path of believers. | mentions 4:115 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The command to recite what is easy from the Qur'an is obligatory; because reciting other passages is not, the obligatory recitation must be the Fatiha. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet's constant practice makes abandoning his command forbidden, and Muslims agree that the Fatiha is better and more complete than another recitation. | mentions 24:63 QURTUBI, d. 671 (طائفة): Another group considers la ilaha illa Allah better because it repels unbelief and associating partners, and people fight on its basis; they cite the Prophet’s command to fight until people say it. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): God praises Himself and opens His Book with praise, while people are forbidden to claim purity for themselves; Qurtubi cites the command not to praise yourselves and the report to throw dust in flatterers’ faces. | mentions 53:32 QURTUBI, d. 671 (الطبري): Al-Ṭabarī says God’s praise of Himself implicitly commands His servants to praise Him, as though the wording said, Say praise belongs to God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Qurtubi treats the implied command to say as a common Arabic ellipsis whose context supplies the omitted words, and says the same construction applies to the later address, as though it said, Say, You alone we worship. | mentions 1:5 QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb also means master: Qurtubi cites the command to remember Joseph with his master and the report that a slave woman gives birth to her mistress. | mentions 12:42 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الطبري): Al-Tabari says God’s praise of Himself implicitly commands people to praise Him; Arabic can omit the understood imperative, as in the cited poem. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): For the accusative reading, Abū Ḥayyān prefers an omitted verb directly indicated by the wording, such as “I praise God,” over “I say” or “adhere to praise.” ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Among proposed governors for the accusative, Abū Ḥayyān prefers the explicit-sense verb ahmadu over a command or other unexpressed verb. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Some rhetoricians call “Praise be to God” a statement whose intended force is command, like “no doubt in it” meaning “do not doubt.” | mentions 2:2 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): A rare accusative al-hamda is parsed either as a verbal noun replacing I praise, or as an imperative-like source meaning say praise; al-Tabari favors the command interpretation, but the author prefers declarative force. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir says the phrase is God's praise of Himself and implicitly commands His servants to say it too. BURHAN, d. 794 (author): The successive praises intensify the servant's attention: God's exclusive praise, universal lordship, gifts of mercy, and command of judgment prepare the worshipper to approach and seek help. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali maps al-Fatiha's verses to the seven letters: praise, lawful and forbidden, command and prohibition, clear and ambiguous, then the corresponding petitions; its opening gathers all seven. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): The fifth verse's request returns to praise, the sixth to the grace of lawful and forbidden provisions, and the seventh to the sovereign's command; together these complete all seven letters. DURR, d. 911 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): Ibn ʿAmr assigns distinct functions to remembrances: glorification is creation's prayer, al-hamd gratitude, tawhid sincerity, takbir fills heaven and earth, and the declaration of God's powerlessness-to-act-without-help brings God's command to submit. ITQAN, d. 911 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari says the Fatiha encompasses praise, worship through commands and prohibitions, and promise and warning; every Qur’anic verse falls under one of these topics. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن العربي): Ibn al-Arabi quotes a teacher saying the Fatiha contains a thousand commands, prohibitions, rulings, and reports; Ibn Umar spent eight years teaching it because of its jurisprudential depth. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الأكثر): Most scholars explain God’s self-praise as declaring His entitlement to praise, commanding praise, quoting the words of servants, or figuratively displaying His perfections; al-Sayyid favors the last account. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Ḥamd is generally commanded, as in the saying that whoever does not thank people does not thank God, whereas madḥ is not universally praised and flatterers are rebuked. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufis understand the praise as belonging to God Himself, like sovereignty and creation-command: He is both praiser and praised, and all things are His manifestations. | mentions 7:54 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Hamd is also said to be both report and performative through love and reverence, unlike madḥ as bare report; one report says whoever does not praise people does not praise God, while another condemns flatterers. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الأكثر): Most authorities interpret God praising Himself as reporting His entitlement to praise, commanding praise, speaking through servants, or figuratively displaying His perfect attributes; al-Sayyid favors the last explanation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Scripture applies gratitude to actions as well as speech: the Qur’an commands David’s household to act in gratitude. | mentions 34:13 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rhetorical address is a divinely used mode even when a proof is available, because it affects hearts and benefits ordinary hearers; al-Ālūsī points to the command to invite with wisdom and good exhortation. | mentions 16:125 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufi masters say “praise belongs to God” is like “might belongs to God”: God is both praiser and praised, and all things are His affairs, as the verse says creation and command belong to Him. | mentions 7:54 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Fatiha proceeds as instruction; the command to read in the name of the Lord is a key contextual sign of this teaching purpose. | mentions 96:1 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (bazı müfessirîn): Some exegetes posit an implicit command meaning say this at the start of Fatiha. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (muhakkıkīn): The discerning exegetes hold that the teaching is clear from the style itself, without supplying an omitted command. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Resûl-i Ekrem): The Prophet commands that dust be thrown in the faces of professional eulogists, illustrating the moral danger of medh that can become flattery. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Rab entails complete power to nurture, govern, guide, command, reward, and discipline, and also means owner; without a qualifying relation it is reserved for God. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A good opening hints at its subject, preparing receptive hearers and allowing advanced hearers to assess or complete the argument; this displays the speaker's command of it. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Another group says the wording remains a report but its intended purpose is performative praise, like declarative wording for grief in 3:36; report form permits permanence, universality, exclusivity, and emphasis. | mentions 3:36 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A second explanation is that the sura gathers the Qur'an's three main aims: praise, commands and prohibitions, and promise and warning. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A prologue should display rhetorical aptness by signaling the purpose, preparing listeners either to receive its details or to assess and complete them, and showing the speaker's command of the subject. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A separate view says the sentence is a pure performative transferred from reporting, like formulas for contracts and praise; the original report sense remains possible with contextual support. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Even if the accusative is read as an imperative, commanding praise does not establish that the praised one deserves it; the nominative indicates deserved, established praise. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The verse's wording is preferable to the alternatives madḥ, shukr, I/we praise, an imperative, accusative praise, an indefinite praise, inna, fronted lillah, or a divine attribute. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The nominative reading الحمدُ لله makes a stable nominal sentence; the accusative requires an implied verb and yields a verbal sentence or command, so the nominative is stronger. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Even if the accusative is construed as a command to praise, a command does not establish that its object deserves praise; the declarative nominative is preferable. TAB: Al-Ṭabarī says God praises Himself and omits an understood command to say the words, as shown by the servant's address in 1:5. TAB: Al-Ṭabarī says God praises Himself and omits an understood command to say the words, as shown by the servant's address in 1:5. TAB-FULL: God speaks and teaches worshippers to say His praise before 1:5. TAB-FULL: Arabic ellipsis and Ibn ʿAbbās's report of Gabriel's command support supplying 'say'. TAB-FULL: Arabic ellipsis and Ibn ʿAbbās's report of Gabriel's command support supplying 'say'. THALABI: Al-Thaʿlabī says self-praise favors creation and functions as 'say'; Ibn ʿAbbās glosses praise as gratitude. THALABI: Al-Thaʿlabī says self-praise favors creation and functions as 'say'; Ibn ʿAbbās glosses praise as gratitude. JISHUMI: The verse chooses ḥamd for its broader scope; the report-like clause has command force, with disputed implied verbs. JISHUMI: The verse chooses ḥamd for its broader scope; the report-like clause has command force, with disputed implied verbs. JISHUMI: The verse chooses ḥamd for its broader scope; the report-like clause has command force, with disputed implied verbs. IBNATIYYA: Al-Ṭabarī's implied 'say' uses ordinary Arabic ellipsis and a poem. IBNATIYYA: Al-Ṭabarī's implied 'say' uses ordinary Arabic ellipsis and a poem. IBNATIYYA-FULL: The implied command 'say praise' continues in the next segment. [70 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(قيل); RAZI-FULL(القدرية,رفاعة بن مالك عن النبي); QURTUBI(الطبري,طائفة); QURTUBI-FULL(الطبري); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR(ابن جرير); BURHAN; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR(عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص); ITQAN(ابن العربي,الزمخشري); ALUSI(الأكثر,بعض ساداتنا الصوفية); ALUSI-FULL(الأكثر,بعض ساداتنا الصوفية); ELMALILI(Resûl-i Ekrem,bazı müfessirîn); IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; TAB; TAB-FULL(عبد الله بن عباس); THALABI(ابن عباس); JISHUMI; IBNATIYYA(الطبري); ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; ABUHAYYAN+; ELMALILI(muhakkıkīn)+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+]
- 1:2/q04/p2 Al-ḥamd is the subject and lillāh its predicate. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): The poet demonstrates ellipsis: when asked whom they had dug for, the answer “a vizier” means a vizier is the person buried. TABRISI, d. 548 (الأخفش وأبو علي الفارسي): They parse al-ḥamdu as a nominative subject with an omitted predication such as is due or is established for God; the phrase lillah supplies its meaning. TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن جني): Ibn Jinni says kasra on ḥamd and ḍamma on lillah signal a close subject-predicate bond, making the two parts sound like one word. TABRISI, d. 548 (غيره من النحويين): Other grammarians hold that the same syntactic governor that governs the described noun also governs its adjective. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Al-ḥamd is nominative as the subject and lillāh its predicate: praise is established and belongs to God. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi links the statement that God subjected what is in the heavens and earth for people to the vast created benefits contained in praise. | mentions 45:13 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The declarative al-ḥamdu lillāh is preferable to ahmadu Allāh: it establishes praise as God’s right instead of reporting one speaker’s act. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Ḥamd is a verbal noun normally governed by an implicit, rarely used verb; its article may denote the genus or point to the familiar idea of praise. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): The lām in lillah attaches to an omitted predicate, understood as ‘due’ or ‘established.’ NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Rabb means owner; the cited exchange has Safwan tell Abu Sufyan that being owned by a Qurayshi is preferable to being owned by a Hawazini. Rabb can also be a verbal noun used intensively as an adjective. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Rabb means owner or master; the source cites Safwan telling Abu Sufyan that being ruled by a Quraysh man is preferable to being ruled by a Hawazin man, and allows “rabb” as an intensive verbal noun. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The article in al-ḥamd may be anaphoric, defining the familiar praise; essential, defining the nature; or generic, encompassing all praises. A verbal noun is ordinarily not pluralized. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Rabb may be a verbal noun used adjectivally or an active participle with deleted alif; as a title it is exclusive to God, while others require a possessive complement. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Rabb is a verbal noun used adjectivally in one analysis, or an active participle with its alif omitted, like barr from bārr. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Hamd is fundamentally a verbal noun and therefore normally has no dual or plural; Ibn al-A'rabi nevertheless reports an af'al plural with poetic evidence. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading raises al-hamd as subject with a deleted nominal predicate, the preferred estimate, though a verb can also be supplied. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): A prepositional phrase or adverb functioning as clause predicate, relative clause, adjective, or circumstantial phrase has an omitted governor; a generic kā'in cannot be overt except in a poetic anomaly. | mentions 27:40 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The omitted governor can generally be a noun or verb, but relative clauses require a verb and the fa-idha and amma constructions require a noun; grammarians differ over the default elsewhere. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The lām is regularly extra with a preposed complement of a verb or with a derivative governor, and elsewhere only irregularly. | mentions 12:43, 11:107 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Others treat rabb as the verbal noun rabba yarubbu, meaning owned or governed, functioning as an agent noun like a just or fasting man. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): When a known noun is described by praise, blame, or compassion, its adjective may follow or detach with an implicit verb or subject; no explicit governor is allowed. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (القراء السبعة): The seven canonical readers vocalize the dāl of al-ḥamdu with ḍamma, making al-ḥamdu a subject and li-llāhi its predicate. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Other proposed governors are the expressed الحمد, a vocative, or a past-tense reading of rabb; al-Ālūsī objects to the first for its syntax and to the latter parses as awkward. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī rejects arguments that the generic article means only the perfect kind or that a verbal noun cannot convey exhaustive scope; context can make either reading exhaustive. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The transmitted and standard reading has al-ḥamdu in the nominative as a subject with lillāhi as its predicate. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Grammarians may treat an underlying verbal form as the source construction for verbal nouns, but here the nominative form answers the rhetorical need for an established state. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some take the verbal noun in a passive sense, meaning that God is the one praised; because its form allows several construals, early commentators counted forty-two possibilities. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Rabb is explained as an intensive verbal noun, a form of exaggerated agent noun, or—according to Abū Ḥayyān—an agent noun with a deleted alif; one grammatical account is disputed. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The praised ground is what the praise answers to and displays as the subject’s perfection; one thing can be both attribute and ground under different aspects, while the public view includes actions and qualities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): One distinction says hamd concerns vol untary acts of rational beings, while madḥ may concern voluntary or involuntary qualities and rational or non-rational subjects. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A third argument derives genus from verbal nouns’ basic, non-comprehensive meaning; the reply is that surrounding indications can still make a verbal noun comprehensive. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A grammatical rationale treats al-ḥamd as a verbal noun standing in for its verb, so it denotes the underlying reality and can be explained by the worship that follows. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The standard transmitted reading has al-ḥamdu nominative as the subject; Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and Zayd b. ʿAlī assimilate the vowel of dāl to the following lām, while Ibrāhīm b. Abla and Bedouins do the reverse. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن الهمام): Another interpretation parses al-ḥamd as a passive verbal noun meaning being praised; its form permits other readings, and some even take the lām as causal. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Another analysis supplies the earlier word al-ḥamd as the governing verb; objections concern the definite verbal noun's government and an intervening predicate, both answered by grammarians. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Rab is a verbal noun for nurturing, applied emphatically to the nurturer; rububiyyah names this relation. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The hidden humanity of the basmala becomes a speaking subject in al-Fatiha, while God is present as interlocutor; the surah captures the full relation of life and existence. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative as the subject in every transmitted reading, and li-llah is its predicate, related to the general existential predicate. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The subject is deliberately fronted because another familiar formula could place the predicate first; the choice itself signals emphasis. | mentions 45:36 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The lam in li-llah may mean figurative exclusive possession or strengthen the verbal noun's relation as a weak, derived construction; exclusivity is also conveyed by the two definite parts. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Defenders of a purely declarative analysis answer that reporting praise recognizes God's beautiful attributes, that the speaker is included in the general report, or that inherited practice entails following it. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (الجوهري عن سيبويه): Another proposed derivation takes Allah from laah, a verbal noun meaning to be hidden; the article was added to suggest the original sense, as in fadl and majd used as names. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Rabb may be a verbal noun or adjective on fa'l from rabba-yarubbu, 'to nurture and govern,' or from 'to own'; a verbal noun is intensive. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur prefers 'nurturer and governor' over 'owner' because it fits God's management of creation toward perfection and avoids redundancy with 'King of Judgment Day'; the latter can specify the afterlife. | mentions 1:4 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-Fatiha models four rules for introductions: keep them brief, foreshadow the subject, use concise comprehensive wording, and begin with praise of God. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A short introduction avoids tiring listeners while they await the main subject; the sura's brevity also explains placing it before longer chapters. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayh says some such source expressions can be made nominative topics, as in al-hamdu lillah; the definite form favors this declarative use. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur cites Sibawayh to explain the Arabic stages of this verbal noun construction and the rationale for the nominative al-hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Hamd can be fronted deliberately even though it is the grammatical subject, since Arabic eloquent usage offers both al-hamdu lillah and lillah al-hamd. | mentions 45:36 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The al in al-hamd is generic: because the verbal noun stands in place of an action, the definite form presents the kind of praise as familiar and makes it prominent, rather than identifying one instance. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The lām in lillah can indicate rhetorical specification or strengthen the relation of the verbal-noun subject to its predicate; specification remains conveyed by the definiteness of both parts. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur's conclusion is that al-hamdu lillah reports praise while functioning as its performance; declarative form adds permanence, comprehensiveness, exclusivity, and emphasis. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الجوهري عن سيبويه): One proposed origin is لاه, a verbal noun meaning that God is hidden, with the definite article added to suggest the original sense. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Kashshaf limits رب here to master or king, allowing either a verbal noun or adjective, because the word most commonly carries those meanings. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): رب may be a verbal noun or an adjective from upbringing, meaning one who nurtures and directs; tarbiya gradually brings something to its perfection. It may also derive from ownership, with an intensive or rare adjectival form. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): An owner, master, and beneficent educator has a stronger claim to praise, and the Lord can direct His subjects as He wills. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya details God's lordship over humans and other living beings through creation, subjection, sustenance, and resurrection. | mentions 45:4, 45:12, 45:13, 45:26 AKHFASH: Al-Akhfash discusses hamzat qaṭʿ, nominative inception, competing accounts of what raises the subject, accusative verbal noun, and indeclinable kasra, with Qurʾanic examples. AKHFASH: Al-Akhfash discusses hamzat qaṭʿ, nominative inception, competing accounts of what raises the subject, accusative verbal noun, and indeclinable kasra, with Qurʾanic examples. KASHSHAF: The author parses nominative and accusative, says nominative denotes lasting praise, cites Abraham's greeting in 11:69, and connects praise with worship in 1:5. - 1:2/q04/p3 An accusative verbal noun has an omitted governing verb. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج): Sufyan ibn ʿUyayna and Ruʾba reportedly read the dal with fatḥ, implying an omitted verb. QURTUBI, d. 671 (سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج): The seven readers and most people read the dāl in al-ḥamdu with nominative ḍamma; Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna and Ruʾba ibn al-ʿAjjāj are reported to read it accusative, with an implied verb. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (القراء السبعة): The seven readers and most people read alhamdu with the dāl in the nominative; Sufyan ibn ʿUyayna and Ruʾba report an accusative reading with an implied verb. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): The accusative rabb al-ʿālamīn is analyzed as praise, a vocative, or an implied verb indicated by “praise.” BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Al-ḥamdu is nominative as subject with “to God” as predicate, though an accusative reading is also reported. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The word is nominative as a subject with لله as predicate, though an accusative reading exists; nominative signals praise as universal and enduring rather than newly arising. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative as a subject, though it can be read accusative with an implied verb as an explanatory verbal noun, like ‘thanks’ or ‘ingratitude’; nominative wording conveys a settled, enduring meaning. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Al-hamd is beautiful praise given preferentially; syntactically it is a nominative subject with an implied predicate such as “due” or “established,” while a reading with an implicit verb makes it an accusative verbal noun. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): In the accusative reading the lām is explanatory, not a strengthening of transitivity, since the verbal noun cannot govern this phrase as its object. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects parsing accusative rabb as an implied verb of praise or as apposition: one rests on imagined syntax, the other is interrupted by raḥmān raḥīm. | mentions 1:3 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects deriving the accusative rabb from a prior implied verb such as We praise God, since this analogy to ellipsis in coordination is not productive. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The accusative reading needs an implied governor such as I praise God or I praised God; it presents praise as newly occurring and names its agent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): With the accusative, lillāh is an explanatory lām; it cannot be governed by the verbal noun, as the Arabic construction saqyan li-Zayd shows. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The accusative reading of “al-ḥamd” involves ellipsis; grammarians differ over whether its omitted governing word repeats the noun or is another word. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading makes rabb genitive as an adjective or appositive to Allah; accusative allows an implied I praise, a detached praise, or vocative, with vocative weakest because it separates adjective from noun; nominative is detached predicate huwa rabb. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج): Sufyan ibn Uyaynah and Ru'bah ibn al-Ajjaj read al-ḥamda with fatḥa as an implied verbal object. NASHR, d. 833 (زيد بن علي بن الحسين، رؤبة بن العجاج، هارون بن موسى العتكي، الحسن): These readers pronounce the dāl of al-ḥamdu with fatḥa; the accusative is treated as a verbal noun with its commonly understood verb omitted. NASHR, d. 833 (أبو زيد سعيد بن أوس الأنصاري): Abu Zayd reports rabb al-ʿālamīn in both nominative and accusative; successive adjectives may differ, with some governed by an implied verb and others by an implied subject, but not revert to genitive. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Al-ḥamdu li-llāh is read with nominative case as a subject, accusative as a verbal noun, or genitive by vowel assimilation. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn is read genitive as an adjective, nominative with an implicit subject, or accusative with an implicit verb or as a vocative. ALUSI, d. 1270 (زيد بن علي): Zayd b. ʿAlī is reported to read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative; the author’s preferred parse is an accusative of detachment with an implied verb such as أمدح. ALUSI, d. 1270 (هارون بن موسى): Hārūn b. Mūsā reads al-ḥamda in the accusative, a construction common among Arabs for a definite verbal noun with an implied verb such as نحمد. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The implied verb for the accusative is in the first-person plural, matching naʿbudu and nastaʿīn and the humility of worship, rather than a majestic first-person singular. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (هارون بن موسى): Hārūn b. Mūsā reads al-ḥamda in the accusative, like Arabs who use definite verbal nouns with an omitted verb; the implied “we praise” suits worship and seeking help better than royal “we.” | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Some grammarians say verbal nouns are originally accusative because events require predication; the underlying verbal relation is normally expressed by a verb, making the noun-plus-verb construction seem redundant. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): One analysis treats the accusative as an adjective cut off from its antecedent, supplying an implied verb such as I praise or I mention; an objection about the following adjective is answered by taking Rahman as apposition. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayhi explains that praise expressions originally use an omitted verb in the absolute accusative, as in 'we praise God with praise'; the verbal noun replaces the verb. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Sibawayhi distinguishes supplicatory accusative sources from declarative ones such as hamdan and shukran, whose omitted verbs mean 'I praise' or 'I thank'. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The cited grammar says a definite source such as al-hamdu can be raised as a subject because it is declarative and definite; the sense of the omitted verb remains. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari also describes hamd's original accusative with an omitted verb and its shift to nominative as a means of indicating stability. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (سيبويه): A reading with al-hamda in the accusative and definite article is a Tamimi dialect form attested by Sibawayhi; its implied verb limits praise to the speaker or intended group. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayh explains conventional verbal nouns such as “beware” or “thanks” as accusatives governed by verbs suppressed from use, and distinguishes supplicatory formulas from other expressions. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative in all transmitted readings; its predicate is lillah, while an underlying analysis treats hamd as an accusative verbal noun replacing an omitted verb meaning “we praise God.” SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The nominative reading al-hamdu makes a nominal sentence and is stronger and more enduring than the accusative, which requires an implied verb. [34 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج); QURTUBI(سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج); QURTUBI-FULL(القراء السبعة); BAYDAWI; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR(سفيان بن عيينة ورؤبة بن العجاج); NASHR(أبو زيد سعيد بن أوس الأنصاري,زيد بن علي بن الحسين، رؤبة بن العجاج، هارون بن موسى العتكي، الحسن); ITQAN; ALUSI(زيد بن علي,هارون بن موسى); ALUSI-FULL(بعضهم,هارون بن موسى); IBNASHUR(سيبويه,صاحب الكشاف); IBNASHUR-FULL(سيبويه); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+]
- 1:2/q04/p4 The expression reports praise while performing it. — ALUSI, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawwānī explains al-Sharīf’s requirement of inward and outward exaltation as intending the performative creation of esteem, not belief in a proposition. ALUSI, d. 1270 (ابن الهمام): Ibn al-Humām argues that ḥamd is performative because it manifests qualities rather than establishing them, so praise need not be informative. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī answers that hamd can be performative, so correspondence to belief is inapplicable; ordinary speakers also use iʿtiqād to mean regard or esteem for someone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī opens a further question about whether “Praise belongs to God” is declarative or performative; most scholars call it declarative, following the apparent form. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (فريق ثان): A contrasting position calls the sentence a pure performative, shifted from reporting into praise like contracts and verbs of praise or blame. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur's preferred account is a report used to perform praise: the intention is performative, while report form supplies duration, universality, exclusivity, and emphasis unavailable to direct performative wording. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Dhu al-Rumma's phrase 'we brought into being thanks for its Creator' confirms that Arabs described praise or thanks as something they create, treating it as performative expression. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He says these effects cannot be achieved throug h a purely performative wording such as hamdan lillah or ahmadu Allah hamdan. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Most scholars treat al-hamd lillah as a report that praise is established for God; others call it performative praise, a transferred report, or a report that entails performance, and the author accepts both report and exaltation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (قسم): Another group treats the expression as performative because it embodies love. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (قسم): A further view classifies it as a report that also contains a performative act. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): الحمد لله can carry both report and invocation, but إن الحمد لله forces a performative reading and loses that breadth. [12 notes: ALUSI(ابن الهمام,الدواني); ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR(فريق ثان); IBNASHUR-FULL; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT(قسم); IBNASHUR+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+]
- 1:2/q04/p5 The definite article has a connected-speech hamza and special interrogative forms. — AKHFASH: Al-Akhfash explains added alif-lām, hamzat waṣl and lengthening with an interrogative; he cites 10:59, 27:59, 10:91, 37:153 and 34:8. AKHFASH: Al-Akhfash explains added alif-lām, hamzat waṣl and lengthening with an interrogative; he cites 10:59, 27:59, 10:91, 37:153 and 34:8. ## 1:2/q05 What is reported about the value and use of praise?  [ٱلْحَمْدُ · reports]
turns on: Reports differ in the specific reward or use assigned to the praise.
- 1:2/q05/p1 Praise is beloved and richly rewarded. — IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (author): In ordinary usage, calling someone the best of the worlds can mean the people of his own era; lines by Labīd and al-Ḥuṭayʾa illustrate this usage. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Known bodily benefits are only a drop in the ocean of the unknown; understanding divine wisdom in creating the human being involves ten thousand questions or more. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Anʿām’s praise for creating the heavens, earth, darkness, and light is one subset of Rabb al-ʿālamīn; dependence on God for preservation entails dependence for origination. | mentions 6:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet identifies al-Fatiha as a surah unmatched in the Torah, Gospel, or Psalms; the Companions' answer about what they recite in prayer indicates a known universal practice. [RAZI-FULL:v1p158#2/ r2] RAZI-FULL, d. 606 · author | holds | Razi argues that Quran 73:20 requires al-Fatiha: the options are al-Fatiha, another surah, or a choice; the latter two fail because another is not universally required and al-Fatiha is agreed better and more complete. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A giver who seeks reward, praise, repayment, or release from stinginess seeks compensation and is not the true benefactor; God gives from intrinsic fullness. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Worldly blessings are finite, but the praise phrase is infinite; what remains after accounting for a finite blessing is endless, warranting eternal reward. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The phrase should be reserved for the best blessings: religious benefits outrank worldly ones, inner acts outrank bodily acts, and seeing a worldly benefit as the giver’s gift is nobler than treating it merely as a benefit. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): They further argue that if God is praised intrinsically, no act causes that entitlement, so no duty, compensation, or reward is owed; if it is not intrinsic, God would depend on another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (المعتزلة): The Muʿtazila say absolute praise is deserved by one whose acts contain no evil, rulings no injustice, and judgments no oppression; they reject the Jabriyya picture of God creating disbelief then punishing it. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Created nurturers seek their own profit, such as reward or praise, while God nurtures for the creatures’ benefit rather than His own. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet's constant practice makes abandoning his command forbidden, and Muslims agree that the Fatiha is better and more complete than another recitation. | mentions 24:63 QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Abu ʿAbd Allah explains that the person first receives the world and then the enduring word of praise, which is better because the world perishes while the word remains among the righteous deeds. | mentions 18:46 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAtiyya chooses the monotheistic formula, citing the report that it is the best utterance of the Prophet and earlier prophets. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports that God is pleased when a servant praises Him after eating a meal or drinking. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو عبد الله): Abu ʿAbd Allah explains that the enduring word is better because the world perishes, while praise remains among the righteous deeds; both world and praise ultimately come from God. | mentions 18:46 BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): If the article is exhaustive, all praise belongs to God because He bestows every good directly or indirectly; this implies He is living, powerful, willing, and knowing. | mentions 16:53 BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Readings assimilate dāl and lām in opposite directions, treating the letters as one word because they are used together. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Shukr is broader than ḥamd and madh in one respect and narrower in another; praise is a branch of gratitude and better signals blessings than hidden belief or ambiguous bodily acts, so it is gratitude's chief element. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Two recitations assimilate the dāl and lām in either direction, treating them as one word because the terms are used together. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): God is owner at the final return and recompense; only the worshipped one to whom sovereignty returns can reward enduring blessing for perishable life, as servants shed acts and attributes and receive true existence after annihilation. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (ابن الأعرابي): Ibn al-Aʿrābī records the plural aḥmad for ḥamd, apparently treating its different kinds as distinct, and cites a poem using aḥmadī. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The accusative may alternatively be a direct object of recite or read, but the verbal-noun analysis is better because the wording itself supports it. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Hamd praises beauty whether or not a favor was bestowed and is expressed only by the tongue; shukr is for a received favor and may be by heart, tongue, or limbs. | mentions 34:13 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Views range from treating hamd and shukr as synonymous, to making hamd absolutely broader, to distinguishing praise of attributes from thanks for acts. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir questions Ibn Jarir's equation: praise is verbal commendation for necessary or bestowed qualities, while thanks applies only to bestowed qualities and may be inward, spoken, or enacted. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir resolves the scope dispute by saying praise covers more kinds of objects but is speech-only, whereas thanks uses more kinds of means but concerns bestowed qualities alone. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الجوهري): Al-Jawhari defines thanks as praising a benefactor for what he bestowed and says using the preposition li- is more eloquent. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir distinguishes praise from eulogy: eulogy can concern living or dead people and even objects, precede or follow a favor, and address necessary or bestowed qualities. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Companion al-Aswad ibn Sari asks to recite praises of his Lord, and the Prophet replies that his Lord loves praise. BURHAN, d. 794 (ابن حبان): Ibn Hibban reads the report comparing al-Fatiha with the Torah and Gospel as a difference in reward granted by God’s favor, not as intrinsic superiority within the Quran. BURHAN, d. 794 (ابن حبان): Ibn Hibban says calling al-Fatiha the greatest surah means greatest in reward, not that one part of the Quran is intrinsically superior. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): When al-Aswad ibn Sariʿ offers the Prophet praises he composed for God, the Prophet replies that his Lord loves praise; al-Hakim authenticates the report. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says nothing is more beloved to God than praise, so God praised Himself by saying al-hamdulillah. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet contrasts divine forbearance with satanic haste and says nothing is more beloved to God than praise. DURR, d. 911 (منصور بن إبراهيم): Mansur ibn Ibrahim transmits the saying that al-hamdulillah is the expression whose reward is multiplied most. DURR, d. 911 (أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي): Abu ʿAbd al-Rahman al-Jubbaʾi says prayer, fasting, and every good deed done for God count as gratitude, with al-hamd the best gratitude. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي): Abu Abd al-Rahman al-Jubba'i says prayer, fasting, and every good deed for God are gratitude, with al-hamd the best form. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الأسود بن سريع): Al-Aswad ibn Sari asked to recite praises he had composed, and the Prophet replied that God loves praise. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says nothing is more beloved to God than praise, so God praised Himself with “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أنس): Anas reports that deliberation is from God, haste from Satan, God is most ready to excuse, and praise is most beloved to Him. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): It is called the seven oft-repeated because it has seven verses, each can stand for seven Qur'anic portions, and reciting them is said to earn the whole Qur'an's reward or close seven gates of Hell. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): In its familiar lexical sense, ḥamd is verbal praise of beauty, whether the praised excellence is an inherent virtue or a bestowed favor. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Rabb is also used for creator, master, king, benefactor, reformer, worshipped one, and companion; tarbiya is the primary sense, with the others best treated as figurative extensions. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawānī objects that treating God’s praise as figurative conflicts with the orthodox affirmation of real divine speech, since saying and speech are equivalent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī says treating genus as perfection and treating comprehensive praise as exhaustive both require interpretation; making everything else negligible adds an unnecessary step. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (السعد): Al-Ālūsī says treating You alone we worship as an explanation rests on the missing conjunction, but that reverses the proper relation between the opening and its continuation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Even on the Muʿtazilī account, people’s praise does not displace God’s: He enables their acts and bestows the conditions, while their own agency merits a distinct, lesser praise. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He rejects “owner” here because “Owner of the Day of Judgment” would repeat the meaning, with no evident reason for placing al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm between them; nurturing better fits the context and signals divine beneficence, power, and wisdom. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Some say al-ʿālamīn is a plural restricted to rational beings; replies derive it from rational ʿālam after restriction, from grammatical predominance, or by treating knowledgeable signs as rational. | mentions 41:11, 12:4 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Rab entails complete power to nurture, govern, guide, command, reward, and discipline, and also means owner; without a qualifying relation it is reserved for God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Some misuse evolutionary language by treating nature’s law as an uncaused absolute, displacing divine nurturing and making humanity the absolute summit of existence. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur rejects the derivational-sibling reading: the commentators' synonym reading fits al-Zamakhshari's gloss in al-Fa'iq and his treating blame as hamd's opposite. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari compares the nominative 'peace' in 51:25 with the earlier accusative greeting: Abraham answers with a more enduring, better greeting. | mentions 51:25 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari rejects treating the article as grammatically exhaustive; Ibn Ashur agrees that it marks genus, while the construction's combined effect entails all praise. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Dhu al-Rumma's phrase 'we brought into being thanks for its Creator' confirms that Arabs described praise or thanks as something they create, treating it as performative expression. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari says Arabs pronounce Allah's lam emphatically except where the preceding sound requires kasra, treating inherited consensus as evidence against speculative explanations. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A poet's use of لاه and fixed expressions such as لله أبوك support treating لاه as a shortened form of Allah. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (النبي): The Prophet transmits God's division of al-Fatiha between Himself and the servant: each phrase receives divine acknowledgement and a tailored promise of blessing, mercy, ease of judgment, reward, help, and fulfillment of the servant's requests. STUDYQURAN (author): Al-ḥamd praises God for His qualities and thanks Him for worldly favors and next-world reward. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Anas reports thirty good deeds and another report compares twenty for tawḥīd with thirty for praise; Ibn ʿAṭiyya distinguishes reward from doctrinal superiority. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Anas reports thirty good deeds and another report compares twenty for tawḥīd with thirty for praise; Ibn ʿAṭiyya distinguishes reward from doctrinal superiority. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Anas reports thirty good deeds and another report compares twenty for tawḥīd with thirty for praise; Ibn ʿAṭiyya distinguishes reward from doctrinal superiority. [62 notes: IBNJAWZI-NUZHA; RAZI; RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة); QURTUBI; QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عبد الله,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); BAYDAWI; BAYDAWI-FULL; QASHANI; ABUHAYYAN(ابن الأعرابي); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR(الجوهري,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); BURHAN(ابن حبان); DURR(أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي,رسول الله,منصور بن إبراهيم); DURR-FULL(أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي,أنس,الأسود بن سريع,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); BURSEVI; ALUSI; ALUSI-FULL(الدواني,السعد); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR(صاحب الكشاف); IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI(النبي); STUDYQURAN; IBNATIYYA-FULL(أنس بن مالك,رسول الله); ALUSI+; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية)+; SAMIN-DURR+]
- 1:2/q05/p2 Angels cannot measure the reward of fitting praise. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): One view limits the worlds to rational beings: angels, jinn, and humans. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās first defines the worlds as all rational beings among humans, jinn, and angels. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن عباس، واختاره ابن قتيبة): A fourth view includes jinn, humans, and angels; it is also transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās and chosen by Ibn Qutayba. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن عباس): A fifth interpretation, also transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās, says the worlds here are the angels. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi reads Lord of the worlds as an argument for the ancient God's existence; the created lights, darkness, and angels are kinds within that all-encompassing world-order. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (Prophet): A report says that when a blessed servant says al-ḥamdu lillāh, God tells the angels that the servant returned something priceless for an unvalued gift. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ḥamdu lillāh encompasses every praise uttered or possible from all creatures, prophets, angels, saints, scholars, and future inhabitants of Paradise. | mentions 10:10 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise frames creation: the angels speak of it before Adam is created, and the inhabitants of Paradise end their invocation with it. | mentions 2:30, 10:10 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Before beginning the ascent, the worshipper purifies body and clothing for the holy presence and chooses which of opposing companions—angel or devil, reason or desire, truth or falsehood, and so forth—to follow, since settled companionship is hard to break. | mentions 9:119 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The spiritual ascent rises through human spirits, the spirits of the heavens, angels, throne-bearers, and pure spirits detached from bodies, whose worship and love of God exceed human comprehension. | mentions 39:75, 69:17, 21:20, 12:76 QURTUBI, d. 671 (الفراء وأبو عبيدة): Al-Farrāʾ and Abū ʿUbayda restrict the term to rational beings: humans, jinn, angels, and devils, excluding animals because the plural form is for rational beings; al-Aʿshā’s verse is cited. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn ʿUmar reports that angels could not record a servant’s praise fitting God’s majesty, so God told them to write it as the servant had said until He rewards him. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The expressions for a matter becoming difficult or blocked explain the report’s wording about the two angels; either verb form may be used without the extra yāʾ. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn ʿUmar reports that angels could not record a servant’s praise “as befits” God’s majesty, so God told them to write it as spoken and reward him when they meet. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Another view restricts al-ʿālamīn to rational beings—angels and the human and jinn races—with others included secondarily. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): An alternative restricts the term to knowledgeable beings—angels, humans, and jinn—with other creatures included only secondarily. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (أبو عبيدة والفراء): Abū ʿUbayda and al-Farrāʾ include humans, jinn, angels, and devils among al-ʿālamīn. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (ابن عباس): Another report from Ibn ʿAbbās specifies humans, jinn, and angels. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās is cited for two scopes of al-ʿālamīn: every living being, or humans, jinn, and angels. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو عبيدة والفراء): Abū ʿUbayda and al-Farrāʾ include humans, jinn, angels, and devils in al-ʿālamīn. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Raising al-hamd is stronger and more expressive of stable praise, while accusative signals renewal; Abraham's salām is better than the angels' salāman by the verse's instruction to answer more beautifully. | mentions 11:69, 4:86 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الراغب): Al-Raghib transmits Ibn Abbas's explanation that al-alamin means angels, jinn, and humans, and says the word was formed as an instrument of knowing the Creator. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): “Qur'an of dawn” in 17:78 means the dawn prayer, as the sound reports say angels of night and day witness it; scholars agree prayer requires recitation. | mentions 17:78 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn Umar reports that angels cannot calculate the full reward for a servant's praise fitting God's majesty, so God tells them to record it as said until He rewards him. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الفراء وأبو عبيدة): Al-Farra and Abu Ubayda restrict the term worlds to rational beings—humans, jinn, angels, and devils—and exclude animals. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliyah says humans and jinn are worlds and reports uncertainty between fourteen and eighteen thousand other angelic worlds on earth, in groups worshipping God. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): A surah's name summarizes its purpose, as a title signals the details within; Adam demonstrated this naming principle when he named things before the angels. DURR, d. 911 (الله): The Lord of the worlds is Lord of humans, jinn, angels, devils, all creation, and everything. DURR, d. 911 (أبو العالية): Abu al-ʿAliya counts humans and jinn as two worlds and says there are another eighteen thousand worlds of angels; each of earth's four corners has 3,500 worlds created to worship God. DURR, d. 911 (أبي بن كعب): Ubayy ibn Kaʿb identifies the worlds as angels, numbering eighteen thousand; four regions each have four or five hundred angels, each with innumerable helpers. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الله تعالى): The report explains “Lord of the worlds” as lord of humans, jinn, angels, devils, all creation, and everything. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliya says humans and jinn are two worlds and there are eighteen thousand other worlds of angels, with 3,500 worshipping worlds at each of the earth's four corners. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبي كعب): Ubayy ibn Kab identifies the worlds as angels, numbering eighteen thousand; he describes groups in four regions with countless attendants. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some restrict the worlds to rational beings among angels, humans, and jinn; al-Suyūṭī associates this reading with ʿilm, while universal scope fits ʿalāma, sign. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some commentators restrict the worlds to beings with knowledge: angels and the two accountable species, humans and jinn. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A Sufi anthropology maps the five divine names to the human's angelic intellect, devilish, predatory, animal, and dense bodily dimensions, with cited verses illustrating the correspondences. | mentions 13:28, 23:97, 25:26, 5:4, 55:31 QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله [ ص ]): Ibn Umar reports that the Prophet described a servant's expansive praise, which two angels could not record; God ordered them to write it as spoken and promised reward when the servant meets Him. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The visible domain is called the realm of dominion and witness, while immaterial existence is the unseen and angelic realm; God alone owns the latter, though worldly ownership can appear to belong to others. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Fatir's origination differs from Anam's creation by measure, yet both are aspects of Rabb al-alamin; Anam then mentions darkness and light, while Fatir mentions angels as messengers. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Fatiha contains faith and good works, affirming God, the Last Day, angels, messengers, and scriptures, and joins lordship's unity to the unity of worship; it is therefore the Mother of the Book. STUDYQURAN (author): Some take the worlds as four communities: humans, jinn, angels, and satans. [41 notes: TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(ابن عباس); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس,ابن عباس، واختاره ابن قتيبة); RAZI-FULL(Prophet); QURTUBI(الفراء وأبو عبيدة,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); BAYDAWI; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN(أبو عبيدة والفراء,ابن عباس); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو عبيدة والفراء,ابن عباس); SAMIN-DURR(الراغب); IBNKATHIR(أبو العالية,الفراء وأبو عبيدة,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); BIQAI-FULL; DURR(أبو العالية,أبي بن كعب,الله); DURR-FULL(أبو العالية,أبي كعب,الله تعالى); ALUSI(بعضهم); ALUSI-FULL(بعضهم); QUTB-ZILAL(رسول الله [ ص ]); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; STUDYQURAN; SAMIN-DURR+]
- 1:2/q05/p3 Praise is worth more than a received blessing. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-hamdu lillah is better than ash-shukru lillah because it praises God for every benefit to the worlds, even one not received by the speaker; shukr is personal gratitude. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Existence is better than nonexistence, and every possible existent receives its existence from God; hence every creature receives His blessing and mercy. QURTUBI, d. 671 (طائفة): One group considers praise of God better than the declaration of unity because praise includes both tawhid and praise, while the declaration includes tawhid alone. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الحسن): Al-Hasan says that no blessing exists for which praise of God is not better than the blessing itself. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports from the Prophet that when God gives a servant a blessing and the servant praises Him, the praise is better than what was received. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report in Nawādir al-uṣūl from Anas says that if someone possessed the whole world and then praised God, the praise would be better than it. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الحسن): Hasan says that no blessing is better than saying “Praise belongs to God.” QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report from Anas says that if someone owned the whole world and then said alhamdulillah, the praise would be better than it. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports that when a servant praises God for a blessing, the reward for that praise is better than the blessing received. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (القرطبي): Al-Qurtubi transmits a report that praising God would be better than possessing the whole world; he explains that the enduring reward for praise exceeds transient worldly enjoyment. | mentions 18:46 DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): In a hasan report, the Prophet says praise after a blessing is better than the blessing received. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says al-hamd is better than any blessing bestowed on a servant. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says that if someone possessed the entire world and then said al-hamdulillah, the praise would be better than it all. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A sound report says the praise offered for any divine blessing is better than the blessing received; related reports extend this to every blessing and all the world. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (هارون بن موسى): Hārūn b. Mūsā reads al-ḥamda in the accusative, like Arabs who use definite verbal nouns with an omitted verb; the implied “we praise” suits worship and seeking help better than royal “we.” | mentions 1:5 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah is better than inna al-hamd lillah because believers do not doubt the claim, and the unmarked formula can report praise while enacting exaltation; inna makes it mere report. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah is better than lillah al-hamd here: fronting lillah suggests exclusivity or rebuttal of doubt, neither of which fits this address to believers. [17 notes: RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI(الحسن,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,طائفة); QURTUBI-FULL(الحسن,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); IBNKATHIR(القرطبي,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); DURR(رسول الله); DURR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); ALUSI-FULL(هارون بن موسى); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+]
- 1:2/q05/p4 The praise fulfills a vow of gratitude. — TABRISI, d. 548 (النبي): The Prophet uses rabb to mean owner, asking a man whether he owns sheep or camels. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): In a weakly chained report, the Prophet treats saying al-hamdulillah after his stolen camel returned as fulfilling his vow to thank God. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): Ali reports that the Prophet vowed to thank God properly if his family expedition returned safely; on their return he said al-hamdulillah for God's abundant favors and counted that as fulfilling the vow. DURR, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad tells how he vowed to praise God with approved praises if his mule returned, then said al-hamdulillah alone because it encompasses all praise. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): When a returned camel prompted the Prophet to fulfill a vow of gratitude, he said “al-hamd lillah,” which the Companions accepted as thanks. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): After an expedition returned safely, the Prophet fulfilled his pledge to thank God by saying “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report about an Ansar expedition says the Prophet pledged thanks if they returned safe with spoils, then said he had fulfilled that pledge and praised God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): When Jaafar ibn Muhammad's mule returned, he thanked God with only “al-hamd lillah,” saying this gathered all praise. ITQAN, d. 911 (علي): Ali said, as transmitted by Ibn Abi Jamra, that he could load seventy camels with commentary on the Umm al-Qur’an; the source explains the remark by showing how much each phrase requires. ALUSI, d. 1270 (النواس بن سمعان): Al-Ṭabarānī reports from al-Nawwās b. Samʿān that the Prophet vowed to thank God if his she-camel returned; when it did, he said al-ḥamdu lillāh, and the Companions expected further prayer or fasting. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī takes the article in al-ḥamdu as generic: it points to the familiar notion of praise, as in Labīd’s verse describing the camels. ALUSI, d. 1270 (لبيد): Labīd’s line, cited as linguistic evidence, uses al-ʿirāk for the camels pressing together at the watering place. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (النواس بن سمعان): Al-Ṭabarānī reports that after the Prophet’s she-camel was stolen, he vowed to thank God if it returned; when it did, he said alhamdulillah, and the Companions awaited fasting or prayer, showing they understood gratitude as action. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (الصادق): When al-Sadiq's father recovered a lost mule, he praised God only by saying الحمد لله and explained that every kind of praise is contained in that phrase. [14 notes: TABRISI(النبي); DURR(جعفر بن محمد,رسول الله); DURR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,جعفر بن محمد); ITQAN(علي); ALUSI(النواس بن سمعان,لبيد); ALUSI-FULL(النواس بن سمعان); TABATABAI(الصادق)]
- 1:2/q05/p5 Praise brings an increase. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise thanks God for past blessings and brings future increase, as promised in the Qur’an; it closes barriers and opens the way to knowledge of God, hence the name al-Fātiḥa. | mentions 14:7 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report attributed to the Prophet says that whoever says al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn has thanked God, who then increases him. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A weak report from the Companion al-Hakam ibn Umayr says that saying Lord of the worlds is gratitude to God, bringing an increase. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الحكم بن عمير): In a weak report, saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” counts as thanking God, after which He increases the speaker. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Giving stands above receiving, so the station of the praised giver is the highest rank; the joy of this rank culminates in further hamd and increased gifts. | mentions 14:7 STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet says that praising God, Lord of the worlds, constitutes thanks and brings increased bounty. [6 notes: RAZI-FULL; IBNKATHIR(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); DURR(رسول الله); DURR-FULL(الحكم بن عمير); ELMALILI; STUDYQURAN(Prophet Muhammad)]
- 1:2/q05/p6 Praise fills the balance. — QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Malik al-Ashʿari reports from the Prophet that purification is half of faith, praise fills the balance, and glorification with prai se fills or fills the space between heaven and earth; Qurtubi cites Muslim. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Malik al-Ashʿari reports that purification is half of faith, praise fills the balance, and glorification with praise fills the space between heaven and earth. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says purification is half of faith and al-hamd fills the balance; other phrases fill the space between heaven and earth, while prayer, charity, patience, and the Quran serve as signs or testimony. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A man of Banu Sulaym reports that the Prophet said al-hamd fills the balance, while other remembrances fill the space between heaven and earth; purification and fasting are each half of a measure. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says glorification is half the balance and al-hamd fills it; there is no barrier between God and the declaration of divine unity. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says purification is half of faith, al-hamd fills the balance, and glorification fills the space between heaven and earth, among other deeds' spiritual benefits. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Another report says al-hamd fills the balance, while tasbih is half of it and takbir fills the space between heaven and earth. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn Umar reports that al-hamd fills the balance and there is no barrier between “la ilaha illa Allah” and God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Quranic rhetoric includes composition, context, implication, modes of expression, logic, wisdom, and the verbal and semantic beauties that secure fluency, force, balance, and inimitability. [9 notes: QURTUBI(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); DURR(رسول الله); DURR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q05/p7 The praise is said at a sneeze. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A report says Adam’s first words were al-ḥamdu lillāh after he sneezed; the people of Paradise end with the same praise, so the world begins and ends with it. | mentions 10:10 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas calls praise the expression of every grateful person, citing Adam’s praise at a sneeze and praise by Noah, Abraham, David and Solomon, the Prophet, and Paradise’s people. | mentions 23:28, 14:39, 27:15, 17:111, 35:34, 10:10 DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas describes an exchange at sneezing: the sneezer says al-hamdulillah, an angel answers Lord of the worlds, and after the sneezer repeats it the angel asks God to show mercy. DURR, d. 911 (علي بن أبي طالب): Ali says that whoever says the full praise at every sneeze heard, in any circumstance, will never suffer toothache or earache. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says whoever anticipates a sneezer with praise will be spared abdominal illness. DURR, d. 911 (موسى بن طلحة): Musa ibn Talha reports God's instruction to Solomon to remember Him if anyone sneezes, even beyond seven seas. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): When someone sneezes and says al-hamd, an angel responds “Lord of the worlds”; when the sneezer repeats it, the angel says “may God have mercy on you.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي بن أبي طالب): A report attributed to Ali says that saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” at each heard sneeze prevents toothache and earache. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (واثلة بن الأسقع): Wathila reports that praising God when greeting a sneezer prevents abdominal illness; another report says God told Solomon to remember Him when someone sneezes e ven seven seas away. [9 notes: RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عباس); DURR(ابن عباس,رسول الله,علي بن أبي طالب,موسى بن طلحة); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,علي بن أبي طالب,واثلة بن الأسقع)]
- 1:2/q05/p8 Praise and the declaration of oneness have distinct reported merits. — QURTUBI, d. 671 (طائفة): Another group considers la ilaha illa Allah better because it repels unbelief and associating partners, and people fight on its basis; they cite the Prophet’s command to fight until people say it. QURTUBI, d. 671 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAtiyya chooses la ilaha illa Allah as superior, citing the report that it is the best thing the Prophet and earlier prophets said. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says the best remembrance is lā ilāha illā Allāh and the best supplication is al-ḥamdu lillāh; al-Tirmidhi grades this report ḥasan gharīb. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (طائفة): A group holds that al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn is better than lā ilāha illā Allāh because it combines praise with monotheism. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (آخرون): Others hold that lā ilāha illā Allāh is superior because it distinguishes faith from unbelief and is the formula people must profess; reports also call it the best remembrance. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says the best remembrance is there is no god but God, while the best supplication is al-hamdulillah; al-Tirmidhi grades the report hasan. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says the best remembrance is “la ilaha illa Allah” and the best supplication is “al-hamd lillah.” [7 notes: QURTUBI(ابن عطية,طائفة); IBNKATHIR(آخرون,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,طائفة); DURR(رسول الله); DURR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]

## 1:2/q06 What does the lām in lillāh express?  [لِلَّهِ · grammar]
turns on: Whether the lām marks God's right, possession, explanation, or cause.
- 1:2/q06/p1 The lām marks God's entitlement to praise. — TABRISI, d. 548 (الأخفش وأبو علي الفارسي): They parse al-ḥamdu as a nominative subject with an omitted predication such as is due or is established for God; the phrase lillah supplies its meaning. TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن جني): Ibn Jinni says kasra on ḥamd and ḍamma on lillah signal a close subject-predicate bond, making the two parts sound like one word. TABRISI, d. 548 (سيبويه): The kasra of lām in the jussive verb li-yafʿal distinguishes it from emphatic lām. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (بعض المفسرين): Some derive ʿālam from ʿalāma, a sign pointing to its Creator; that derivation supports taking it for all created existence. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الجمهور): Most readers pronounce the lām of lillāh with kasra; Ibn ʿAbla uses ḍamma, a dialect of some Rabīʿa according to al-Farrāʾ. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن السميفع): Ibn al-Sumayfa reads al-ḥamda with fatḥa on dāl and kasra on lām. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أبو نهيك): Abū Nuhayk reads both dāl and lām in al-ḥamd lillāh with kasra. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أهل النظر): The derivation of ʿālam from ʿalāma, “sign,” supports the speculative theologians' view: the world is named as a sign pointing to its Creator. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Considering divine wisdom in the throne, heavens, luminaries, elements, minerals, plants, and animals adds vast further questions; most of creation is made for human benefit, and al-hamd lillah thus encompasses roughly a million issues. | mentions 45:13 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Saying al-hamdu lillah rather than al-madhu lillah acknowledges God as a freely choosing agent, not a necessary emanation as philosophers claim. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The formula says al-hamdu lillah rather than ahmadu Allah so it declares God worthy of praise before any creature praises Him, from eternity to eternity. QURTUBI, d. 671 (ابن أبي عبلة): Ibn Abī ʿAbla reads the dāl and lām with ḍamma by vowel assimilation for phonetic harmony, a common pattern illustrated by other words and a poetic line. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن أبي عبلة): Ibn Abi ʿAbla reads the dāl and lām with ḍamma for vowel harmony, a common Arabic tendency also illustrated by other readings and poetic examples. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): A poem’s “wayl ummiha” derives from “waylun li-ummiha”: the first lām was dropped and the hamza’s ḍamma shifted after kasra proved heavy. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report from Anas says that if someone owned the whole world and then said alhamdulillah, the praise would be better than it. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The entitlement to praise implies that God is living, powerful, willing, and knowing. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): The lām in lillah attaches to an omitted predicate, understood as ‘due’ or ‘established.’ ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الجمهور): The majority read al-ḥamdu with ḍamma on the dāl. Ibrāhīm ibn Abī ʿAbla assimilates its mīm to the following lām; al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn ʿAlī assimilate the dāl's kasra to the lām's, a stranger assimilation. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): With nominative al-ḥamdu the lām marks entitlement; with accusative al-ḥamda a verb such as “I praise God” must be supplied, conveying renewed action. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān prefers that al-ʿālamīn denote morally responsible beings, supported by the verse about signs for the knowledgeable; Ḥafṣ's kasra on the lām clarifies the sense. | mentions 30:22 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة): Ibrāhīm ibn Abī ʿAbla harmonizes the mīm's vowel with the following lām's ḍamma. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الحسن وزيد بن علي): Al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn ʿAlī harmonize the dāl's kasra with the lām's kasra, a stranger kind of agreement between inflectional and non-inflectional vowels. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The lām in “to God” restricts all praise to Him, and the annexation “Master of the Day of Judgment” underscores His sole dominion when other dominions vanish. | mentions 40:16 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Raising al-hamd is stronger and more expressive of stable praise, while accusative signals renewal; Abraham's salām is better than the angels' salāman by the verse's instruction to answer more beautifully. | mentions 11:69, 4:86 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The kasra in a variant al-hamdi may be vowel harmony with the following lām, a Tamim and some Ghatafan dialect feature; another possibility is hidden nominative or accusative inflection. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): A variant with damma on the lām of li-llah follows the preceding dāl; al-Zamakhshari prefers harmony from inflection to building, citing a Qays dialect and Qur'anic murdifin. | mentions 8:9 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The lām is regularly extra with a preposed complement of a verb or with a derivative governor, and elsewhere only irregularly. | mentions 12:43, 11:107 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): For raddafa lakum, some explain the lām by semantic inclusion and others regard it as extra. | mentions 27:72 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن أبي عبلة): Ibn Abi Abla reads both dāl and lām with ḍamma by assimilating the second vowel to the first; Ibn Kathir calls the reading anomalous. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir explains praise as exclusive thanks to God for countless blessings, including faculties for worship, worldly provision, and guidance toward lasting bliss despite no prior entitlement. NASHR, d. 833 (إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة): Ibrahim b. Abi Ablah reads the first lām in al-ḥamdulillāh with ḍamma. NASHR, d. 833 (الحسن): Al-Hasan also reads the lām in Allāh with fatḥa, matching the preceding accusative; the source calls this a dialect of some Qays speakers. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Al-hamd lillah establishes that God deserves praise for His essence and perfection; Rabb al-alamin adds His lordship, sovereignty and beneficence, while the worlds point to creation's beginning and the inference to resurrection. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says the best remembrance is there is no god but God, while the best supplication is al-hamdulillah; al-Tirmidhi grades the report hasan. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says nothing is more beloved to God than praise, so God praised Himself by saying al-hamdulillah. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): In a weakly chained report, the Prophet treats saying al-hamdulillah after his stolen camel returned as fulfilling his vow to thank God. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A report from Anas says tawhid is the price of Paradise and al-hamdulillah the price of every blessing; people share Paradise according to their deeds. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas describes an exchange at sneezing: the sneezer says al-hamdulillah, an angel answers Lord of the worlds, and after the sneezer repeats it the angel asks God to show mercy. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): Ali reports that the Prophet vowed to thank God properly if his family expedition returned safely; on their return he said al-hamdulillah for God's abundant favors and counted that as fulfilling the vow. DURR, d. 911 (منصور بن إبراهيم): Mansur ibn Ibrahim transmits the saying that al-hamdulillah is the expression whose reward is multiplied most. DURR, d. 911 (سفيان الثوري): Sufyan al-Thawri says al-hamdulillah is both remembrance and gratitude, a combination found in no other expression. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الله تعالى): When the servant says “al-hamd lillah,” God says the servant has thanked and praised Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): When a returned camel prompted the Prophet to fulfill a vow of gratitude, he said “al-hamd lillah,” which the Companions accepted as thanks. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الحكم بن عمير): In a weak report, saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” counts as thanking God, after which He increases the speaker. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (كعب): Kab says “al-hamd lillah” is praise of God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says the best remembrance is “la ilaha illa Allah” and the best supplication is “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says nothing is more beloved to God than praise, so God praised Himself with “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي بن أبي طالب): A report attributed to Ali says that saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” at each heard sneeze prevents toothache and earache. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): After an expedition returned safely, the Prophet fulfilled his pledge to thank God by saying “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): When Jaafar ibn Muhammad's mule returned, he thanked God with only “al-hamd lillah,” saying this gathered all praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some restrict the worlds to rational beings among angels, humans, and jinn; al-Suyūṭī associates this reading with ʿilm, while universal scope fits ʿalāma, sign. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الأكثر): Most scholars explain God’s self-praise as declaring His entitlement to praise, commanding praise, quoting the words of servants, or figuratively displaying His perfections; al-Sayyid favors the last account. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الحسن البصري وزيد بن علي): Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and Zayd b. ʿAlī read with the dāl assimilated toward the following lām; Ibrāhīm b. Abla and Bedouins assimilate the reverse way.
