ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Naming God by His essential name avoids suggesting that He deserves praise only through one particular attribute; entitlement by essence still means entitlement for divine excellence. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الأكثر): Most authorities interpret God praising Himself as reporting His entitlement to praise, commanding praise, speaking through servants, or figuratively displaying His perfect attributes; al-Sayyid favors the last explanation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (النواس بن سمعان): Al-Ṭabarānī reports that after the Prophet’s she-camel was stolen, he vowed to thank God if it returned; when it did, he said alhamdulillah, and the Companions awaited fasting or prayer, showing they understood gratitude as action. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The standard transmitted reading has al-ḥamdu nominative as the subject; Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and Zayd b. ʿAlī assimilate the vowel of dāl to the following lām, while Ibrāhīm b. Abla and Bedouins do the reverse. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Naming God by His essence avoids suggesting that entitlement to praise belongs only to one attribute; essential entitlement means no particular attribute is specified, not that praise lacks a beautiful ground. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الملا خسرو): Entitlement by essence means entitlement without specifying a particular attribute; it does not mean God deserves praise apart from beautiful qualities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Mentioning the divine proper name avoids implying that entitlement to praise belongs only to one attribute; the entitlement belongs to the essence without isolating it from its attributes. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Fatiha’s successive clauses unfold the opening praise and return to alhamdu lillah; the surah is the root and comprehensive representative of the other Qur’anic surahs. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The basmala is the crown, the Qur’an a perfect body, Fatiha its head, and alhamdu lillah its face; mercy, guidance, worship, and divine unity express its spiritual life. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Alhamdu lillah informs believers, implies reverent praise, and uses a timeless nominal sentence; the four following clauses explain it, making Fatiha one sentence in substance. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative as the subject in every transmitted reading, and li-llah is its predicate, related to the general existential predicate. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Rabb is an attribute of Allah: after praise is attached to His essence to show inherent entitlement, the attribute adds entitlement through His qualities; this is clearer than saying 'praise belongs to Lord of the worlds.' | mentions 83:6 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu lillah implies all attributes of perfection that entitle God to exclusive praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report in Sahih al-Bukhari from Abu Sa'id ibn al-Mu'alla says the Prophet identified al-Hamdu lillah as the seven oft-repeated verses and the great Qur'an given to him. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو هريرة): The first proof is Malik's report from Abu Hurayra: God's division of prayer begins with al-Hamdu lillah, without mentioning the basmala; prayer here means recitation in prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبي بن كعب): In the second proof, Ubayy ibn Ka'b recites the celebrated sura from al-Hamdu lillah to its end when asked how he opens prayer, without reciting the basmala. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports in Muslim and other collections that the Prophet, Abu Bakr, and Umar began recitation with al-Hamdu lillah and did not mention the basmala at either end. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عائشة): Aisha reports that the Prophet opened prayer with the takbir and began recitation with al-Hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عبد الله بن مغفل): Ibn Mughaffal says he prayed with the Prophet and the first three caliphs but never heard them say the basmala, and directs worshippers to recite al-Hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (البيضاوي): Al-Baydawi explains pairing God's proper name with al-Rahman and al-Rahim as invoking His entitlement to assistance and mercy: al-Rahman signals the gifts involved in righteous action, with al-Rahim explained later. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayh says some such source expressions can be made nominative topics, as in al-hamdu lillah; the definite form favors this declarative use. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur cites Sibawayh to explain the Arabic stages of this verbal noun construction and the rationale for the nominative al-hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative in all transmitted readings; its predicate is lillah, while an underlying analysis treats hamd as an accusative verbal noun replacing an omitted verb meaning “we praise God.” IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Hamd can be fronted deliberately even though it is the grammatical subject, since Arabic eloquent usage offers both al-hamdu lillah and lillah al-hamd. | mentions 45:36 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Although the generic article is not itself exhaustive, its combination with the lām of divine specification implies that every instance of praise belongs to God; the restriction is rhetorical and emphatic. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The lām in lillah can indicate rhetorical specification or strengthen the relation of the verbal-noun subject to its predicate; specification remains conveyed by the definiteness of both parts. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): To decide whether al-hamdu lillah is report or performance, Ibn Ashur first explains that Arabic can turn statements into speech acts, as with sale formulas and words of praise or hope. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur's conclusion is that al-hamdu lillah reports praise while functioning as its performance; declarative form adds permanence, comprehensiveness, exclusivity, and emphasis. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He says these effects cannot be achieved throug h a purely performative wording such as hamdan lillah or ahmadu Allah hamdan. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Kashshaf says all Arabs pronounce the divine lām emphatically, and their agreement shows the practice was inherited. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn corresponds to Jewish liturgical ribbon ʿōlām, an eschatological title spanning this world and the next; a Syriac parallel itself lacks that connotation. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): The form may be a cross-language wordplay presenting the emerging community as citizens of both worlds, through a nisba formation from ʿālam and rabbinic Hebrew ʿōlām. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The impersonal formula al-hamd lillah does not bind praise to a particular speaker, unlike I or we praise God, and presents God as universally praised. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Saying I praise God reports the speaker's act but does not establish that its object deserves praise; al-hamd lillah states God's entitlement without a specific speaker. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The verb form is time-bound to the speaker's present or future, while al-hamd lillah leaves praise ongoing without a temporal limit. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah declares praise to be God's due by His very essence and abundant favors, unlike a report that one person praises Him. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamdu lillah is a nominal and definite expression, unlike hamdan lillah; al can indicate known praise or all praise, while indefiniteness lacks this scope. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Most scholars treat al-hamd lillah as a report that praise is established for God; others call it performative praise, a transferred report, or a report that entails performance, and the author accepts both report and exaltation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah is better than lillah al-hamd here: fronting lillah suggests exclusivity or rebuttal of doubt, neither of which fits this address to believers. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Using God's proper name rather than an attribute avoids implying that He deserves praise only for one quality; the name points to His essence and entitlement across all attributes. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The verse's wording is preferable to the alternatives madḥ, shukr, I/we praise, an imperative, accusative praise, an indefinite praise, inna, fronted lillah, or a divine attribute. [94 notes: TABRISI(ابن جني,الأخفش وأبو علي الفارسي,سيبويه); IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(بعض المفسرين); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو نهيك,أهل النظر,ابن السميفع,الجمهور); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI(ابن أبي عبلة); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن أبي عبلة,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI; ABUHAYYAN(الجمهور); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة,الحسن وزيد بن علي); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR(ابن أبي عبلة,ابن جرير); NASHR(إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة,الحسن); BIQAI-FULL; DURR(ابن عباس,رسول الله,سفيان الثوري,منصور بن إبراهيم); DURR-FULL(الحكم بن عمير,الله تعالى,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,جعفر بن محمد,علي بن أبي طالب,كعب); ALUSI(الأكثر,الحسن البصري وزيد بن علي,بعضهم); ALUSI-FULL(الأكثر,الملا خسرو,النواس بن سمعان); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو هريرة,أبي بن كعب,أنس بن مالك,البيضاوي,النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم,سيبويه,صاحب الكشاف,عائشة,عبد الله بن مغفل); CORPUSCORANICUM; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT; ABUHAYYAN+; IBNASHUR-FULL+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+; SAMIN-DURR+]
- 1:2/q06/p2 The lām means for God's sake. — TABRISI, d. 548 (سيبويه): The lām normally has fatḥa because it cannot begin with sukun; lām of possession and emphasis are distinguished, and attached pronouns restore its original opening vowel. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-hamdu lillah is better than ash-shukru lillah because it praises God for every benefit to the worlds, even one not received by the speaker; shukr is personal gratitude. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-hamdu lillah also means praise and thanks are God’s due and property, because His abundant favors make Him inherently deserving of them. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): They further argue that if God is praised intrinsically, no act causes that entitlement, so no duty, compensation, or reward is owed; if it is not intrinsic, God would depend on another. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (طائفة من العلماء): One group prefers alhamdulillah because it combines praise with the monotheism expressed by “There is no god but God.” BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Readings assimilate dāl and lām in opposite directions, treating the letters as one word because they are used together. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Two recitations assimilate the dāl and lām in either direction, treating them as one word because the terms are used together. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الحسن وزيد بن علي): Al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn ʿAlī assimilate the dāl's kasra to the lām's kasra; the author calls this stranger because a case vowel follows a non-case vowel. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The lām can express possession, transfer of ownership, entitlement, relation, cause, communication, exclamation, specification, result, location, endpoint, and superiority; Qur'anic and Arabic examples illustrate these functions. | mentions 16:72, 4:105, 28:8, 21:47, 17:78, 7:57, 17:107 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The lām in lillāh has numerous possible relations, including ownership, entitlement, relation, cause, communication, specification, outcome, time, endpoint, and elevation. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Although al-ʿālamīn is an irregular plural, Abū Ḥayyān favors the responsible creatures because a cited verse and Ḥafṣ's lām-kasra reading support that scope. | mentions 29:44 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The lām in li-llah expresses entitlement here; other senses include ownership, transfer, kinship, cause, address, oath exclamation, clarification, result, location, endpoint, elevation, and augmentation. | mentions 16:72, 4:105, 12:23, 28:8, 21:47, 17:78, 35:13, 17:109, 12:43, 11:107, 43:29, 16:1, 27:72, 8:9 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir says ʿālam derives from ʿalāmah, a sign, because every created thing points to its Maker, Creator, and oneness; he cites a poem by Ibn al-Mu'tazz. DURR, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad tells how he vowed to praise God with approved praises if his mule returned, then said al-hamdulillah alone because it encompasses all praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some prefer Ibrāhīm’s reading because following the first sound is easier than the reverse and because the dāl’s inflection is stronger than the lām’s fixed vowel. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): A particularly unusual parsing makes the lām causal, so that praise is established for God’s sake. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The thing praised for is the cause of the praise: its excellence becomes manifest through the praised person’s possession of it; most scholars include both acts and enduring qualities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن الهمام): Another interpretation parses al-ḥamd as a passive verbal noun meaning being praised; its form permits other readings, and some even take the lām as causal. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن الهمام): Some interpreters propose forty-two possibilities for the phrase; a rare proposal makes its lām mean because of God. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The question how servants’ created praise is “for” God is answered by distinguishing attachment from inherence, like knowledge attaching to its objects; another parses the noun as passive “being praised,” while a rare view makes the lām causal. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God alone has real ownership, which necessarily entails governance because whatever depends on Him for existence also depends on Him in its effects. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah is better than inna al-hamd lillah because believers do not doubt the claim, and the unmarked formula can report praise while enacting exaltation; inna makes it mere report. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): الحمد لله also declares praise to be God's rightful property because of His abundant favors; a personal act of praising does not assert this entitlement. [23 notes: TABRISI(سيبويه); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(طائفة من العلماء); BAYDAWI; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN(الحسن وزيد بن علي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR; DURR(جعفر بن محمد); ALUSI(بعضهم); ALUSI-FULL(ابن الهمام); TABATABAI; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+]
- 1:2/q06/p3 The lām marks God's possession of praise. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The lām in lillāh can signify fitting اختصاص, ownership, or power and dominion; each makes all praise belong to God. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): God's lordship and mercy make Him worthy of praise; lordship as mastery or ownership is followed fittingly by mercy, which sustains servants' hope after failure. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says that if someone possessed the entire world and then said al-hamdulillah, the praise would be better than it all. ALUSI, d. 1270 (معظم العلماء): Most scholars consider الحمد لله informative: otherwise God’s possession of the praised qualities would begin only when the speaker utters the praise. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The lām in lillah denotes entitlement, particularity, and ownership; the whole genus and every form of hamd belongs to God. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The genus article with the possessive l am implies all praise belongs to God: if any instance belonged to another, exclusive possession of the genus would fail; this is hyperbolic restriction. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The lam in li-llah may mean figurative exclusive possession or strengthen the verbal noun's relation as a weak, derived construction; exclusivity is also conveyed by the two definite parts. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The claim that Arabs reserved رب for God is false: they used it for human rulers and masters, idols, and particular possessions; الربّة was a name for al-ʿUzza and أرباب is plural. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): رب may be a verbal noun or an adjective from upbringing, meaning one who nurtures and directs; tarbiya gradually brings something to its perfection. It may also derive from ownership, with an intensive or rare adjectival form. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Rabb means an owner who manages what is owned; human ownership is a conventional social relation, unlike real ownership grounded in dependence of existence. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): Rabb combines rightful possession and authority with rearing, sustaining, and fostering something from its beginning to completion. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The visible domain is called the realm of dominion and witness, while immaterial existence is the unseen and angelic realm; God alone owns the latter, though worldly ownership can appear to belong to others. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Used alone, Rabb refers only to God and names His ownership and nurturing; in a construct such as master of a house, it can refer to others. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah implies that God is living, possesses perfect qualities, and does beautiful deeds and favors; madḥ lillah would not convey all this. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God is praiseworthy in His essence, lordship, rightly placed mercy, and ownership of Judgment Day; the sequence spans all times from eternity through the endless afterlife. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Lord of all worlds establishes God’s oneness and ownership of all creation, with no other lord or deity. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The sequence praises God for His essence, lordship, properly placed mercy, and ownership and kingship on the day of recompense. [17 notes: RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; DURR(رسول الله); ALUSI(معظم العلماء); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; ASAD-NOTES; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+]
- 1:2/q06/p4 The lām is explanatory. — ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): In the accusative reading the lām is explanatory, not a strengthening of transitivity, since the verbal noun cannot govern this phrase as its object. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): With the accusative, lillāh is an explanatory lām; it cannot be governed by the verbal noun, as the Arabic construction saqyan li-Zayd shows. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): On the accusative reading, li-llah is attached to an omitted explanatory verb such as I mean; source nouns do not normally govern this lām phrase, though it may strengthen a derivative verb. | mentions 47:8 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The lām may be explanatory, resultative, locative meaning in, at, or after, terminative, or superposition; the source gives examples for each. | mentions 12:23, 28:8, 21:47, 17:78, 35:13, 17:109 [4 notes: ABUHAYYAN; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR]

## 1:2/q07 What does the name Allāh mean and why use it here?  [لِلَّهِ · meaning]
turns on: Whether the divine name is derived from al-ilāh, another root, Syriac, or coined directly.
- 1:2/q07/p1 Allāh is God's proper name and names His essence. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas likewise uses Rabb for being ruled when discussing ʿAbd Allah ibn al-Zubayr and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwan. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The formula says al-hamdu lillah rather than ahmadu Allah so it declares God worthy of praise before any creature praises Him, from eternity to eternity. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The declarative al-ḥamdu lillāh is preferable to ahmadu Allāh: it establishes praise as God’s right instead of reporting one speaker’s act. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The sequence assigns praise to God, then names Him Lord of the worlds: Allah signifies necessary perfection, while Lord of the worlds shows that all else flows from His generosity, making Him beyond completeness. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Allah is nobler than Lord, although supplicants commonly call God ‘Lord’; al-Rāzī refers readers to his earlier discussion of the divine names. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb means owner; al-Ṣiḥāḥ says it is a divine name used of others only in an annexed construction, though pre-Islamic Arabs also used it for a king. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Abu ʿAbd Allah explains that the person first receives the world and then the enduring word of praise, which is better because the world perishes while the word remains among the righteous deeds. | mentions 18:46 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Rabb means owner; lexically it is a divine name, used of others only in a possessive construction, though pre-Islamic Arabs also used it for a king. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو عبد الله): Abu ʿAbd Allah explains that the enduring word is better because the world perishes, while praise remains among the righteous deeds; both world and praise ultimately come from God. | mentions 18:46 NASAFI, d. 710 (author): For Nasafi, the definite article in al-hamd is comprehensive, against the Mu‘tazila; its attachment to Allah, the name of the divine essence, gathers all perfections and reflects the doctrine of created acts. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The definite article in al-hamd is exhaustive according to the author's school, against the Mu'tazila; joining it to Allah's proper name gathers every perfection and rests on the doctrine of created acts. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading makes rabb genitive as an adjective or appositive to Allah; accusative allows an implied I praise, a detached praise, or vocative, with vocative weakest because it separates adjective from noun; nominative is detached predicate huwa rabb. NASHR, d. 833 (الحسن): Al-Hasan also reads the lām in Allāh with fatḥa, matching the preceding accusative; the source calls this a dialect of some Qays speakers. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali says the Fatiha is the Qur'an's mother: its first three verses encompass the divine names and attributes, while its last three cover the creature's way to God, mercy or failure. | mentions 2:281, 75:17, 33:50, 33:52 DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): Abd Allah ibn Amr assigns distinct meanings to the formulas: tasbih is the creatures' prayer, al-hamd is gratitude, tahlil is sincerity, takbir fills heaven and earth, and “no power” expresses surrender. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن أبي جمرة): Explaining the scale of commentary, Ibn Abi Jamra says the opening requires explaining praise, the divine name and transcendence, and the worlds, their nature, types, number, and extent. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The Sufi hierarchy distinguishes Rabb, Rahmān, and Allāh: Rahmān encompasses more divine attributes, while Allāh names the highest, all-encompassing station of divinity. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): In Ibn ʿArabī’s account the spirit names God its Rabb; God gives it divine names and attributes to know through existence, while veiling the manner of that gift because the creature cannot bear it. | mentions 50:16 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Praise precedes the divine name because the speaker is about to perform the act of praise; in 30:18 the order is reversed for a different purpose. | mentions 30:18 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Mentioning the divine proper name avoids implying that entitlement to praise belongs only to one attribute; the entitlement belongs to the essence without isolating it from its attributes. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Allah signifies the all-encompassing divine essence and highest level; below it are oneness, mercifulness, lordship, and sovereignty, while absolute divine uniqueness has no manifestation of created qualities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ساداتنا الصوفية): Sufi masters say the appropriate divine name in prayer changes with the seeker's spiritual state and whether the experience is one of differentiation or union. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A Sufi anthropology maps the five divine names to the human's angelic intellect, devilish, predatory, animal, and dense bodily dimensions, with cited verses illustrating the correspondences. | mentions 13:28, 23:97, 25:26, 5:4, 55:31 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The final rank manifests the divine names Hamid, Mahmud, and Hamid together; the Prophet also bears the related names Ahmed and Muhammad. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Allah is the proper name of the necessarily existent essence, worthy of every praise. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Proper-name formation can alter a word: Ibn Jinni's example Shums ibn Malik changes the usual vowel, and Zamakhshari cites fixed-case Abdullah; Allah's naming has no parallel among personal names. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Calling Allah with ya while retaining the definite article shows that speakers treat the article as part of the name, unlike ordinary nouns entered by al. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari says Arabs pronounce Allah's lam emphatically except where the preceding sound requires kasra, treating inherited consensus as evidence against speculative explanations. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (جمع من العلماء): A group of scholars, cited by al-Tilimsani, says discussion of Allah's name should be avoided out of reverence and because it requires scriptural permission. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Rabb is an attribute of Allah: after praise is attached to His essence to show inherent entitlement, the attribute adds entitlement through His qualities; this is clearer than saying 'praise belongs to Lord of the worlds.' | mentions 83:6 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The four descriptions—Lord of the worlds, Merciful, Compassionate, King of Judgment Day—show each divine name's meaning merits praise and distinguishes God from alleged deities. | mentions 1:3, 1:4 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (البيضاوي): Al-Baydawi explains pairing God's proper name with al-Rahman and al-Rahim as invoking His entitlement to assistance and mercy: al-Rahman signals the gifts involved in righteous action, with al-Rahim explained later. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أمية ابن أبي الصلت): Umayya ibn Abi al-Salt's praise of Abd Allah ibn Jud'an says the addressee's modesty and praise can make a request unnecessary. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He says these effects cannot be achieved throug h a purely performative wording such as hamdan lillah or ahmadu Allah hamdan. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Allah is the proper name of the necessarily existent divine essence, the one deserving every praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The divine name became a unique proper form whose article is treated as part of the word, as the vocative يا الله shows; its formation has no parallel among ordinary names. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A poet's use of لاه and fixed expressions such as لله أبوك support treating لاه as a shortened form of Allah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (التلمساني): Al-Tilimsani reports a group of scholars who refrain from explaining the divine name out of reverence and because discussion requires scriptural permission; Ibn Ashur calls this an astonishing opinion. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Using God's proper name rather than an attribute avoids implying that He deserves praise only for one quality; the name points to His essence and entitlement across all attributes. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (روح المعاني): The proper name makes God's essence, rather than one particular quality, the ground of His right to praise. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Even attributes such as Living or Knowing can describe others, and even unique descriptions may not explicitly identify God, so Allah is the clearest name. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Using God's proper name means He deserves praise for His essence, not merely for one attribute such as power or creation. [42 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI; QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عبد الله); NASAFI; NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; NASHR(الحسن); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص); ITQAN(ابن أبي جمرة); ALUSI(الشيخ الأكبر); ALUSI-FULL(ساداتنا الصوفية); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR(جمع من العلماء,صاحب الكشاف); IBNASHUR-FULL(أمية ابن أبي الصلت,البيضاوي,التلمساني); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(روح المعاني); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; RAZI-FULL+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+]
- 1:2/q07/p2 Allāh derives from al-ilāh, the deity. — QURTUBI, d. 671 (طائفة): Another group considers la ilaha illa Allah better because it repels unbelief and associating partners, and people fight on its basis; they cite the Prophet’s command to fight until people say it. QURTUBI, d. 671 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAtiyya chooses la ilaha illa Allah as superior, citing the report that it is the best thing the Prophet and earlier prophets said. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says the best remembrance is lā ilāha illā Allāh and the best supplication is al-ḥamdu lillāh; al-Tirmidhi grades this report ḥasan gharīb. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (طائفة): A group holds that al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn is better than lā ilāha illā Allāh because it combines praise with monotheism. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (آخرون): Others hold that lā ilāha illā Allāh is superior because it distinguishes faith from unbelief and is the formula people must profess; reports also call it the best remembrance. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says the best remembrance is “la ilaha illa Allah” and the best supplication is “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn Umar reports that al-hamd fills the balance and there is no barrier between “la ilaha illa Allah” and God. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The name derives from al-ilah, definite form of ilah, a generic word for deity; ilah is linked to worship or states entailing humble reverence and follows a passive fa'al pattern like kitab. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur thinks the name was established before Arab idolatry and originally signified the unique deity; it became a proper name through exclusive reference, not common usage. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Arabs do not appear to have used definite singular al-ilah for an idol: they used a possessive construction, another title, or the plural alihah. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Frequent use led speakers to drop the hamza from al-ilah, as in al-nas from al-unas; Ba'ith ibn Hurayth preserves al-ilah and Ubayd ibn al-Abras preserves al-unas with hamza. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari uses al-Ba'ith's 'ma'adh al-ilah' as evidence that Allah originated from al-ilah; Ibn Ashur says this is persuasive because ma'adh conventionally takes Allah as its complement. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He conjectures that the name was established before Arab idolatry as the unique true deity, becoming a proper name by exclusive reference rather than predominance; it then marked the Creator as uniquely deserving worship. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur finds no evidence that Arabs used the definite singular al-ilah for an idol; they used a possessive phrase, often rabb, or a plural, and a conquest report speaks of idols in the Kaaba. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He says frequent use led Arabs to drop the hamza in al-ilah when referring to God, as in al-nas from al-unas, though they retained it in some speech. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-Kashshaf's derivation of Allah from al-ilah is well supported: the poet's fixed expression معاذ الإلاه shows speakers treated al-ilah as the name's source, with variation only in the form's letters. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Allah suits the following worship verse: on the prevalent etymology it derives from ilah, the worshipped, and the Qur'an often pairs this name with worship. | mentions 39:66, 13:36, 39:64 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The name Allah suits the move to worship because it denotes the deity to be worshipped, and the author says it is paired with worship more than fifty times in the Quran. [18 notes: QURTUBI(ابن عطية,طائفة); IBNKATHIR(آخرون,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,طائفة); DURR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); IBNASHUR(صاحب الكشاف); IBNASHUR-FULL; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; IBNASHUR-FULL+; IBNASHUR+]
- 1:2/q07/p3 Allāh is adapted from a Syriac divine name. — IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A further etymology borrows Syriac laaha as a divine name, Arabized by removing its final alif and adding the article. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A further proposal takes the divine name from Syriac لاها, adapted by dropping its final alif and adding Arabic ال. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn corresponds to Jewish liturgical ribbon ʿōlām, an eschatological title spanning this world and the next; a Syriac parallel itself lacks that connotation. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): Syriac ʿâlam ʿâlmīn could nonetheless evoke the Greek liturgical formula eis tous aiōnas tōn aiōnōn, meaning eternally. [4 notes: IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL; CORPUSCORANICUM]
- 1:2/q07/p4 Allāh derives from a root of hiddenness. — IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (الجوهري عن سيبويه): Another proposed derivation takes Allah from laah, a verbal noun meaning to be hidden; the article was added to suggest the original sense, as in fadl and majd used as names. [1 notes: IBNASHUR(الجوهري عن سيبويه)]
- 1:2/q07/p5 Allāh derives from a root of bewilderment. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur derives Allah from al-ilah, a common noun for deity; its root may mean worship or states such as bewilderment, stillness, fear, or ardor, all associated with submission and reverence. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 1:2/q07/p6 Allāh was coined directly as God's name. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj, with the view also attributed to al-Khalil and Sibawayh, holds that Allah was coined directly as God's name rather than derived from أله. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(الزجاج)]

## 1:2/q08 What does rabb mean and imply?  [رَبِّ · meaning]
turns on: Whether rabb primarily denotes ownership, mastery, nurturing, or another established Arabic sense.
- 1:2/q08/p1 God's lordship embraces all creation. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): The phrase adīm marbūb means repaired leather, showing Rabb as the one who sets something right; al-Farazdaq supplies poetic evidence. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas likewise uses Rabb for being ruled when discussing ʿAbd Allah ibn al-Zubayr and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwan. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (بعضهم): Some read Rabb in the accusative as a form of praise. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (بعضهم): Others take accusative Rabb as direct address, parallel to the implied address in “You alone.” TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn may be accusative as praise or as an implied command to remember the Lord; a nominative praise reading is also possible, as poetry shows. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): In ordinary usage the worlds can mean all created things, supported by Pharaoh's question about the Lord of the worlds and God's answer. | mentions 26:23, 26:24 TABRISI, d. 548 (زيد بن علي): Zayd ibn Ali's fatḥa on the bā of rabb is understood as demonstrating that the form is possible, not as a recitation. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أبو منصور اللغوي): Abū Manṣūr says rabba ṣanīʿatahu means to complete and improve one's craft; he cites poetry for the verb rabb. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الجمهور): Most readers put rabb in the genitive; Abū al-Ghāliya, Ibn al-Sumayfa, and ʿĪsā ibn ʿUmar read it in the accusative; several others use nominative. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أبو رزين العقيلي والربيع بن خيثم وأبو عمران الجوني): These readers pronounce the bāʾ of rabb in the nominative. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi sketches the sūra’s sequence: seek refuge from sins, begin good works in God’s name, praise and recognize his lordship and mercy, heed judgment, worship and seek help, ask guidance, and avoid ignorance and error. RAZI, d. 606 (author): One cannot know God as Lord of the worlds without first understanding what “Lord” and “the worlds” mean. RAZI, d. 606 (author): “Lord” is in a genitive construction with “the worlds” as its genitive complement; understanding this relation requires knowing both terms. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A fourth reason is that the sura gathers theology, religious duties, and spiritual purification: praise and lordship establish divine existence and attributes, worship presents duties, and guidance seeks unveiling. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The phrase al-ḥamdu lillāh raises questions about God’s existence and right to praise; Rabb al-ʿālamīn answers the first, while al-Raḥmān, al-Raḥīm, and Mālik yawm al-dīn answer the second. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Rāzī prefers Rabb al-ʿālamīn to Creator of the worlds because sustaining creatures after their beginning is disputed, and the phrase stresses their dependence on God at every moment. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi reads Lord of the worlds as an argument for the ancient God's existence; the created lights, darkness, and angels are kinds within that all-encompassing world-order. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because worlds include everything besides God, including space and time, their Lord created them and existed before them; this proves His transcendence beyond place, direction, and spatial extension. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The same lordship excludes indwelling: the creator precedes every created locus and therefore never depends on a place, against Christians and adherents of indwelling. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Anʿām’s praise for creating the heavens, earth, darkness, and light is one subset of Rabb al-ʿālamīn; dependence on God for preservation entails dependence for origination. | mentions 6:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Saba’s statement that everything in the heavens and earth belongs to God is another subset within the universal lordship named in the Fātiḥa. | mentions 34:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Fāṭir describes bringing the heavens and earth into existence, distinct from creation as determination yet also included within Rabb al-ʿālamīn. | mentions 35:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): All worlds share the contingency, need, and change that make a person depend on God; their common cause of need establishes God's lordship over every creature and heaven. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The construction rabb al-ʿālamīn is an iḍāfa, whose relation cannot be known without first knowing both correlated terms. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The same group says praise is useless to a God complete in Himself and that punishing people eternally for omitting a useless act would not befit the generous Lord. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (القدرية): The Qadariyya argue that God would not truly be Lord if He created a disbeliever’s unbelief and then punished him, or commanded faith while preventing it. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الجبرية): The Jabriyya answer that faith is the greatest blessing, so if it comes from God He is the Lord and benefactor of all. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Allah is nobler than Lord, although supplicants commonly call God ‘Lord’; al-Rāzī refers readers to his earlier discussion of the divine names. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The seven Fatiha verses remove seven vices: praise dispels desire, Lord of the worlds greed, Judgment Day anger, worship and aid pride and vanity, guidance caprice, the favored path unbelief and doubt, and the final phrase innovation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Prayer seeks remembrance of the heart, and this concise surah gathers lordship and servitude and is equated with the mighty Qur'an; nothing else can replace it. | mentions 20:14, 15:87 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Calling the Fatiha “prayer” shows that religious law serves human interests: people most need knowledge of God's lordship and servitude, which the surah's two halves provide to fulfill the covenant. | mentions 17:7, 51:56, 76:2, 2:40 QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): The poet’s use of muḥammad for a person rich in praiseworthy qualities explains the name Muhammad; another verse says the Throne’s Lord is Maḥmūd and the Prophet is Muhammad. QURTUBI, d. 671 (النحاس): Al-Naḥḥās records the intensive forms rabbāhu, rabbabahu, and rabbatahu; al-Ṣiḥāḥ says rabbahu, rabbabahu, and tarabbabahu all mean he raised him, and marbūb means the one raised. QURTUBI, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some scholars call Rabb the greatest name of God because callers frequently invoke Him by it and it conveys the bond of care, mercy, and need between Lord and servant. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الجنيد): A man praised God before al-Junayd but stopped before saying Lord of the worlds; al-Junayd urged him to complete the verse, explaining that created things vanish when paired with the Eternal. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb may be read in the nominative by syntactic disconnection, meaning He is Lord of the worlds, or in the accusative as a word of praise. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): A hadith asks whether someone has a blessing he tends for another, using rabbaha for tending and improving it. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The verb has intensive forms and can mean raising a child; al-Nahhas records rabbahu, rabbabahu, and rabbatahu as forms. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some consider Rabb God’s greatest name because supplicants frequently call upon Him by it and the name conveys the relation of care, mercy, and dependence. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Rabb is unqualified for God alone, though it can be qualified for others, as in “return to your lord.” | mentions 12:50 BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): The accusative rabb al-ʿālamīn is analyzed as praise, a vocative, or an implied verb indicated by “praise.” BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Rabb is used of others only in a qualified phrase, whereas al-ʿālam names what makes something known and came to mean everything besides God. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Rabb is one of God’s greatest names. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Rabb is used without qualification only for God; human use requires a restriction, as in the cited accounts of Joseph. | mentions 12:23, 12:50 NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (الواسطى): Al-Wasiti explains Rabb as creator at the beginning, sustainer through nourishment, and forgiver at the end, and calls it God's greatest name. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): Praise in action and in the tongue of each thing’s state is the appearance of its perfections and attainment of its ends, a fitting opening tribute to its Lord. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الأهوازي): Al-Ahwāzī reports Zayd ibn ʿAlī reading rabb, raḥmān, and raḥīm all in the accusative, avoiding the mismatch in case. | mentions 1:3 ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects parsing accusative rabb as an implied verb of praise or as apposition: one rests on imagined syntax, the other is interrupted by raḥmān raḥīm. | mentions 1:3 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects deriving the accusative rabb from a prior implied verb such as We praise God, since this analogy to ellipsis in coordination is not productive. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (زيد بن علي وطائفة): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and a group read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative as praise, though lowering the following epithets weakens the construction. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الأهوازي): Al-Ahwāzī records Zayd ibn ʿAlī reading rabb, raḥmān, and raḥīm all in the accusative, which removes the syntactic weakness. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Rabb is a verbal noun used adjectivally in one analysis, or an active participle with its alif omitted, like barr from bārr. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Rabb is used absolutely for God, but for others only in a construct phrase such as the lord of the house. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Parsing rabb as apposition is weak because raḥmān and raḥīm intervene. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading makes rabb genitive as an adjective or appositive to Allah; accusative allows an implied I praise, a detached praise, or vocative, with vocative weakest because it separates adjective from noun; nominative is detached predicate huwa rabb. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): One view treats rabb as an adjective, either fa'l or fa'il from rāb with a shortened long vowel; the author objects that bār and barr may simply be separate forms. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report attributed to the Prophet says that whoever says al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn has thanked God, who then increases him. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Companion al-Aswad ibn Sari asks to recite praises of his Lord, and the Prophet replies that his Lord loves praise. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (طائفة): A group holds that al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn is better than lā ilāha illā Allāh because it combines praise with monotheism. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (القرطبي): Al-Qurtubi prefers a comprehensive meaning encompassing all worlds, citing Pharaoh's question and God's identification of the Lord of the worlds as Lord of heavens, earth, and what is between them. | mentions 26:23 BURHAN, d. 794 (author): The successive praises intensify the servant's attention: God's exclusive praise, universal lordship, gifts of mercy, and command of judgment prepare the worshipper to approach and seek help. NASHR, d. 833 (author): Stopping after these phrases is good because their sense is understood, but starting with attached phrases such as rabb al-ʿālamīn, ṣirāṭ alladhīna, or ghayr al-maghḍūb ʿalayhim is poor, except at verse ends per the Sunnah. NASHR, d. 833 (author): A pause on an incomplete fragment such as “bi-smi,” “al-ḥamdu,” or “rabbi” leaves the utterance unfinished and without an intelligible meaning. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Lordship does not yield wellbeing without mercy, so Rahman and Rahim follow al-hamd and encourage people to persist in praising God. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (مسعود التفتازاني): Al-Taftazani maps the Fatiha's blessings to two creations and t wo preservations: creation in Rabb al-alamin, preservation in mercy, second creation in judgment, and afterlife preservation in worship onward. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): He offers two readings of the merciful names: universal mercy permits created benefits while specific mercy protects from harm; he says the nearer reading is general lordly beneficence and a special divine mercy directed to felicity. DURR, d. 911 (الله): The Lord of the worlds is Lord of humans, jinn, angels, devils, all creation, and everything. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): When al-Aswad ibn Sariʿ offers the Prophet praises he composed for God, the Prophet replies that his Lord loves praise; al-Hakim authenticates the report. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas describes an exchange at sneezing: the sneezer says al-hamdulillah, an angel answers Lord of the worlds, and after the sneezer repeats it the angel asks God to show mercy. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A weak report from the Companion al-Hakam ibn Umayr says that saying Lord of the worlds is gratitude to God, bringing an increase. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas also explains the Lord of the worlds as Lord of all creation: all the heavens and their inhabitants, all earths and their inhabitants, and everything between them, known or unknown. DURR, d. 911 (قتادة): Qatada says the Lord of the worlds describes what God has made. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (مجاهد): Mujāhid says the revelation of al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabb al-ʿālamīn greatly distressed Iblīs, who groaned and snorted; those who groan or snort are accursed. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الله تعالى): The report explains “Lord of the worlds” as lord of humans, jinn, angels, devils, all creation, and everything. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): When someone sneezes and says al-hamd, an angel responds “Lord of the worlds”; when the sneezer repeats it, the angel says “may God have mercy on you.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي بن أبي طالب): A report attributed to Ali says that saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” at each heard sneeze prevents toothache and earache. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas, Mujahid, and Sa'id ibn Jubayr interpret “Lord of the worlds” as Lord of jinn and humans. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas also explains the Lord of the worlds as God of all creation, the heavens and their inhabitants, the earths and their inhabitants, and all between them, known or unknown. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (قتادة): Qatada says “Lord of the worlds” encompasses every class of created beings. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses Lord of the worlds as the whole of creation. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn is read genitive as an adjective, nominative with an implicit subject, or accusative with an implicit verb or as a vocative. ITQAN, d. 911 (الخويي): Al-Khuyi says al-Fatiha fittingly gives the broadest divine attribute, Lord of all creation, while other surahs open with particular acts or attributes. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): For the gnostics, the station of lordship comprises names that call for objects, such as knowing and power, including shared names and creative names directed toward creatures. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The Sufi hierarchy distinguishes Rabb, Rahmān, and Allāh: Rahmān encompasses more divine attributes, while Allāh names the highest, all-encompassing station of divinity. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): Ibn ʿArabī’s account says the universal spirit sought its source without knowing that the sought reality was already within it, then recognized God in the station of lordship. | mentions 50:16 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): In Ibn ʿArabī’s account the spirit names God its Rabb; God gives it divine names and attributes to know through existence, while veiling the manner of that gift because the creature cannot bear it. | mentions 50:16 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Opening the sūra’s descriptions with Rabb al-ʿālamīn suits the Prophet’s mission and asks for compassion toward all creatures, since God is their Lord. ALUSI, d. 1270 (زيد بن علي): Zayd b. ʿAlī is reported to read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative; the author’s preferred parse is an accusative of detachment with an implied verb such as أمدح. ALUSI, d. 1270 (أنس): Al-Ālūsī cites a supporting report from Anas in which God tells Abraham that praise is the key to gratitude and gratitude ascends to the Lord of the Throne. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Rabb is explained as an intensive verbal noun, a form of exaggerated agent noun, or—according to Abū Ḥayyān—an agent noun with a deleted alif; one grammatical account is disputed. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الطيبي): Al-Ṭībī finds it preferable to intend both meanings of rabb and their shared sense, as a figure of speech can preserve the direct and implied meaning together. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): In ordinary usage rabb is not freely applied to anyone but God unless restricted by an addition or other qualifier; Ibn Ḥilaza’s unqualified poetic usage is rare. ALUSI, d. 1270 (ابن حلزة): Ibn Ḥilaza’s poem calls al-Mundhir rabb, an unusual unqualified application of the title to a human being. ALUSI, d. 1270 (النابغة): Al-Nābigha’s verse uses rabb without a qualifier, illustrating a disputed poetic license. ALUSI, d. 1270 (السيوطي): Al-Suyūṭī interprets the restriction as a legal rather than purely linguistic ban on calling someone other than God rabb. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The definite plural makes the scope of divine lordship explicit across every created kind; singular al-ʿālam might be mistaken for the familiar perceptible world alone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Other rationales say praise is a fitting beginning for the Lord and that praise exceeds thanks because it need not arise from a benefit received by the speaker. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الطيبي): Al-Ṭībī favors simultaneously applying rabb to both meanings through their shared core, comparing it to an allusive expression that does not exclude its explicit sense. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Unqualified rabb is not widely used linguistically for anyone but God; in a construct or with a qualifier it can express limited lordship, though unrestricted human uses occur rarely. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The human uses “return to your lord” and “he is my lord,” and Joseph’s brothers prostrating to him, are explained as licenses specific to that historical period. | mentions 12:50, 12:23, 12:100 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The plural al-ʿālamīn guarantees inclusion of every created kind: a singular definite noun could be heard as the familiar perceptible world, whereas the plural distributes lordship across all named kinds and their individuals. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rabb names the station of divine lordship that calls for the created objects required by attributes such as knowledge, power, will, and hearing. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Names beneath Rabb include attributes shared in some respect between Creator and creation and active names exclusive to God; the latter belong under Malik. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rahman is more comprehensive than Rabb because it gathers every exalted divine attribute, whether exclusive to God or related to creation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The primordial spirits first heard and affirmed God's lordship, so they call on Him by the first name through which they recognized and pledged themselves to Him. | mentions 7:172 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): Ibn ʿArabi describes the universal Spirit, initially unaware of its origin, as journeying inward until God showed it its secrets and taught it to recognize Him as Lord, while reserving divinity to Himself. | mentions 50:16 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (زيد بن علي): A reading attributed to Zayd ibn ʿAli puts Rabb al-ʿalamin in the accusative; al-Alusi finds the first grammatical explanation, an implied praise or mention verb, most apparent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Other proposed parses take Rabb as a vocative or a past-tense verb, but al-Alusi finds the vocative unclear and the verbal reading syntactically remote. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The descriptions close by pointing to resurrection, just as Lord of the worlds opened with origination; they explain the prior praise and prepare for the prayer that follows. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The relation between the basmala and Fatiha unfolds the primordial covenant between the eternal Lord and c reation, inscribing a relation already present in human nature. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Fatiha proceeds as instruction; the command to read in the name of the Lord is a key contextual sign of this teaching purpose. | mentions 96:1 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sound plural al-alamin foregrounds rational beings; a concise Turkish rendering is difficult, but it means God alone is Lord of all realms and especially their rational inhabitants. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sound rational plural and the definite article extend the meaning across all realms and foreground rational beings; understanding the Lord of the worlds requires conceiving rational agents. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Every ordered community of things, from inner awareness to external objects, is named a realm because it serves as a sign and means of knowing God’s creation, lordship, and perfection. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): He renders the verse as praise belonging to the Lord of the worlds. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Islam therefore concentrated on freeing creed from every ambiguity through pure, comprehensive monotheism and an unambiguous account of God's absolute lordship. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): The phrase Lord of the worlds states a core creed: God's complete lordship extends across all creation. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله): Abu Hurayra reports in Sahih Muslim that God calls al-Fatiha a prayer divided between Himself and His servant: praise, mercy, and glorification are the Lord's; worship and aid are shared; guidance and the servant's request belong to the servant, who receives what he asks. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Rabb is an attribute of Allah: after praise is attached to His essence to show inherent entitlement, the attribute adds entitlement through His qualities; this is clearer than saying 'praise belongs to Lord of the worlds.' | mentions 83:6 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (الحارث بن حلزة): Al-Harith ibn Hilliza calls Amr ibn Hind al-rabb in a poem, evidence that Arabs applied the title to a human ruler. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (النابغة): Al-Nabigha calls al-Nu'man ibn al-Harith rabb and uses rabb again of al-Nu'man when describing God's favor to him. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف ومن تابعه): Al-Zamakhshari and followers claim rabb was used for others only with a qualifier; Ibn Ashur rejects this on unqualified poetic usage and the plural أرباب. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The Quran uses rabb for the Egyptian official in 12:23 and for multiple false gods in 12:39, confirming that Arabic applies the term to beings other than God. | mentions 12:23, 12:39 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Exhausting all genera entails their individuals, since lordship over abstract essences makes no sense; the plural with the definite article explicitly signals comprehensive scope. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Rabb al-alamin encompasses God's acts and creative attributes for those who affirm such attributes. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): With the definite article, rabb is used in the Qur'an exclusively for God, sole sustainer of all creation and ultimate authority. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): To preserve the eschatological dimension, translate rabb al-ʿālamīn as Lord of the worlds, with people implied; translate it simply as people where that reference is explicit. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): The doxology was already used in the early Meccan period; in middle Meccan texts it is joined to the epithet rabb al-ʿālamīn, itself attested early. | mentions 52:48, 37:182, 39:75, 83:6, 81:27, 81:29, 69:43, 68:52, 56:80 CORPUSCORANICUM (Paret): Paret takes ʿālamīn primarily as people, translating the epithet as Lord of people throughout the world or Lord of the world’s inhabitants. | mentions 26:165, 29:6, 2:47, 2:122 CORPUSCORANICUM (author): Paret’s rendering may be factually right but omits the eschatological force; rabb an-nās would have expressed simply Lord of people. | mentions 114:1 CORPUSCORANICUM (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn corresponds to Jewish liturgical ribbon ʿōlām, an eschatological title spanning this world and the next; a Syriac parallel itself lacks that connotation. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): The form may be a cross-language wordplay presenting the emerging community as citizens of both worlds, through a nisba formation from ʿālam and rabbinic Hebrew ʿōlām. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Word order varies with context: al-Jathiya foregrounds God as deserving praise because it confronts disbelievers' false beliefs and denials of His lordship. | mentions 45:10, 45:23, 45:24 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya answers denials of God's creative and beneficent lordship by showing Him as sole creator and provider, so He alone deserves praise; al-Fatiha addresses believers who seek steadfast guidance. | mentions 45:5, 45:12, 45:13, 45:22, 45:26 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Although some restrict worlds to accountable beings, Pharaoh's question is answered by describing the Lord of the worlds, not by defining the word worlds. | mentions 3:108, 26:23, 26:24 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Moses' reply lists qualities of the Lord rather than defining al-alamin, just as calling a house's lord a merchant does not define the house. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): This plural suits al-Fatiha because its worship, aid, guidance, and moral categories address accountable creatures; Rabb al-alamin may mean their Lord or all creation with rational beings predominant. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb al-alamin also answers Jews who claim God is Lord only of Israel: He is Lord of all humanity and accountable creatures. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Fatiha's concise Rabb al-alamin fits its focus on accountable creatures, whereas al-Jathiya explicitly names heaven and earth after repeatedly detailing their signs and God's rule over them. | mentions 45:3, 45:4, 45:13, 45:22, 45:27, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya details God's lordship over humans and other living beings through creation, subjection, sustenance, and resurrection. | mentions 45:4, 45:12, 45:13, 45:26 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God's mercy toward believers and the laws sent to humanity also display His lordship; al-Fatiha names moral classes while al-Jathiya details the wider cosmos. | mentions 45:30, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The differing formulations of Rabb al-alamin and Lord of heaven, earth, and worlds each fit the focus of their surah. | mentions 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Rabb highlights that creation depends on God not only when it begins but also while it continues, a dependence disputed for things in their state of persistence. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): The next four opening praise formulas describe particular aspects contained in al-Fatiha's general Rabb al-alamin. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Anam's creation of heaven and earth, darkness, and light is one part of the wider lordship over all worlds in al-Fatiha. | mentions 6:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Saba's statement that all in heaven and earth belongs to God is one aspect of al-Fatiha's general praise of the Lord of the worlds. | mentions 34:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Fatir's origination differs from Anam's creation by measure, yet both are aspects of Rabb al-alamin; Anam then mentions darkness and light, while Fatir mentions angels as messengers. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Razi summarizes praise as God’s existence and choice, lordship as unity, mercy as compassion in both worlds, and Judgment Day as wisdom and mercy. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Jathiya repeatedly discusses God's lordship over the heavens, earth, creation, and worlds; its closing praise gathers these themes, unlike al-Fatiha's focus on the worlds and their accountable groups. | mentions 45:4, 45:27, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-alamin may refer to all accountable generations or a subset, as in Lot's people; it fits al-Fatiha's concern with guidance and human moral groups and answers Jews who claimed God only as their Lord. | mentions 15:70 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Rabb also fits the request for guidance because education is a core sense of lordship and guidance frequently follows the name Rabb in scripture. | mentions 20 :49, 20:50, 20:122, 87:1, 87:2, 87:3, 6:161, 18:24, 26:62, 37:99, 28:22 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The guidance request ties the chapter together: Rabb guides, Rahman and Rahim show mercy, worship and reliance require the path, and the favored oppose the astray. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Fatiha contains faith and good works, affirming God, the Last Day, angels, messengers, and scriptures, and joins lordship's unity to the unity of worship; it is therefore the Mother of the Book. STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet says that praising God, Lord of the worlds, constitutes thanks and brings increased bounty.
