- 1:2/q08/p2 Rabb means owner. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): Rabb can mean the worshipped deity, master or owner, the one who manages and repairs affairs, or king; its absolute, unrestricted sense as Lord of lords belongs to God alone. TABRISI, d. 548 (النبي): The Prophet uses rabb to mean owner, asking a man whether he owns sheep or camels. TABRISI, d. 548 (أبو ذؤيب): Abu Dhu'ayb uses rabb in the sense of companion or owner of the dogs. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Rabb has three uses: owner, one who sets something right, and an obeyed master; the last is illustrated by Joseph 12:41. | mentions 12:41 IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Rabb means owner; for a created being the title is used only in a construct phrase such as owner of the house or servant. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The lām in lillāh can signify fitting اختصاص, ownership, or power and dominion; each makes all praise belong to God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb means owner; al-Ṣiḥāḥ says it is a divine name used of others only in an annexed construction, though pre-Islamic Arabs also used it for a king. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): With the definite article, Rabb is specific to God; without it, the term can apply to God or a servant, but God alone owns owners and owned things, creates and provides, and cannot lose His dominion as human owners can. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): With the definite article, Rabb belongs exclusively to God; without it, people may be called a household’s lord. God alone creates and provides, owns both owners and owned things, and cannot lose His dominion. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Rabb means owner; lexically it is a divine name, used of others only in a possessive construction, though pre-Islamic Arabs also used it for a king. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Rabb is used absolutely only of God, while for human masters it is restricted by a possessive phrase, as in Joseph’s story; al-Wasiti explains God as creator at the beginning, sustainer, and forgiver at the end. | mentions 12:23, 12:50 NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Rabb means owner; the cited exchange has Safwan tell Abu Sufyan that being owned by a Qurayshi is preferable to being owned by a Hawazini. Rabb can also be a verbal noun used intensively as an adjective. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Rabb means owner or master; the source cites Safwan telling Abu Sufyan that being ruled by a Quraysh man is preferable to being ruled by a Hawazin man, and allows “rabb” as an intensive verbal noun. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): God is owner at the final return and recompense; only the worshipped one to whom sovereignty returns can reward enduring blessing for perishable life, as servants shed acts and attributes and receive true existence after annihilation. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Rabb may be a verbal noun used adjectivally or an active participle with deleted alif; as a title it is exclusive to God, while others require a possessive complement. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The lām can express possession, transfer of ownership, entitlement, relation, cause, communication, exclamation, specification, result, location, endpoint, and superiority; Qur'anic and Arabic examples illustrate these functions. | mentions 16:72, 4:105, 28:8, 21:47, 17:78, 7:57, 17:107 ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Rabb can mean master, owner, constant one, deity, or reformer; some add “companion,” supported by a poem about the master of the dogs. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The lām in lillāh has numerous possible relations, including ownership, entitlement, relation, cause, communication, specification, outcome, time, endpoint, and elevation. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Rabb may mean master, owner, abiding one, worshipped one, or reformer; some also understand companion or creator. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): God's lordship and mercy make Him worthy of praise; lordship as mastery or ownership is followed fittingly by mercy, which sustains servants' hope after failure. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The lām in li-llah expresses entitlement here; other senses include ownership, transfer, kinship, cause, address, oath exclamation, clarification, result, location, endpoint, elevation, and augmentation. | mentions 16:72, 4:105, 12:23, 28:8, 21:47, 17:78, 35:13, 17:109, 12:43, 11:107, 43:29, 16:1, 27:72, 8:9 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Rabb can mean lord, owner, enduring, worshipped, or reformer; some add companion, supported by two poems, but here the author prefers owner and says no extra sense is needed; creator is another proposal. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Rabb means owner and disposer, and in Arabic also master or one who manages something toward its good; each meaning applies to God. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): The unqualified title al-Rabb is used only for God; for other possessors it must be annexed, and some say al-Rabb is the greatest name. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): Al-ḥamd lillāh establishes that God deserves every praise by His essence and all perfections; Rabb adds that He deserves praise as Lord, Owner, and Benefactor. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (السيوطي): Al-Suyūṭī takes the prohibition on calling another “lord” as religious rather than linguistic; an old poem and al-Jawharī support him, while al-Shihāb allows non-owner senses with contextual indication. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The lām in lillah denotes entitlement, particularity, and ownership; the whole genus and every form of hamd belongs to God. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari limits rabb to 'master and owner,' its frequent Arabic sense, while allowing verbal-noun or adjective analysis; Ibn Ashur says context can select a less frequent established sense. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur finds no evidence that Arabs used the definite singular al-ilah for an idol; they used a possessive phrase, often rabb, or a plural, and a conquest report speaks of idols in the Kaaba. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The claim that Arabs reserved رب for God is false: they used it for human rulers and masters, idols, and particular possessions; الربّة was a name for al-ʿUzza and أرباب is plural. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The hadith discouraging a person from calling a human master ربي is an etiquette rule to prevent suspected association; possessive phrases such as رب الدار remain permissible. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Rabb means an owner who manages what is owned; human ownership is a conventional social relation, unlike real ownership grounded in dependence of existence. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God alone has real ownership, which necessarily entails governance because whatever depends on Him for existence also depends on Him in its effects. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The visible domain is called the realm of dominion and witness, while immaterial existence is the unseen and angelic realm; God alone owns the latter, though worldly ownership can appear to belong to others. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb can mean owner, master, educator, guardian, and benefactor; the Lord of the worlds embodies these roles. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): An owner, master, and beneficent educator has a stronger claim to praise, and the Lord can direct His subjects as He wills. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Other opening praises name God as creator, originator, owner of all things, or sender of scripture; each fits its surah and the openings complement one another. | mentions 6:1, 35:1, 34:1, 18:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): These openings jointly describe God as originator, shaper, owner, sustainer, and guide through revealed books; Rabb al-alamin encompasses all these qualities and worlds. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God is praiseworthy in His essence, lordship, rightly placed mercy, and ownership of Judgment Day; the sequence spans all times from eternity through the endless afterlife. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Lord of all worlds establishes God’s oneness and ownership of all creation, with no other lord or deity. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Rabb al-alamin is the apt continuation after praise: Rabb means owner, master, educator, benefactor, and caretaker, and encompasses God's attributes. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The sequence praises God for His essence, lordship, properly placed mercy, and ownership and kingship on the day of recompense. STUDYQURAN (author): Rabb denotes master, arranger, and possessor; of God it means peerless Master who orders affairs and owns creation. KASHSHAF: Rabb is owner, an intensive epithet, unqualified only for God; Qurʾanic examples and Zayd's accusative are recorded. KASHSHAF: Rabb is owner, an intensive epithet, unqualified only for God; Qurʾanic examples and Zayd's accusative are recorded. KASHSHAF: Rabb is owner, an intensive epithet, unqualified only for God; Qurʾanic examples and Zayd's accusative are recorded. KASHSHAF: Rabb is owner, an intensive epithet, unqualified only for God; Qurʾanic examples and Zayd's accusative are recorded. KASHSHAF: Rabb is owner, an intensive epithet, unqualified only for God; Qurʾanic examples and Zayd's accusative are recorded. KASHSHAF-FULL: The text also treats rabb as intensive and reserves unqualified use for God. [49 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI(أبو ذؤيب,النبي); IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI; QURTUBI-FULL; NASAFI; NASAFI-FULL; QASHANI; ABUHAYYAN; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR; BIQAI; ALUSI-FULL(السيوطي); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR(صاحب الكشاف); IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; STUDYQURAN; KASHSHAF(زيد بن علي); KASHSHAF-FULL; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT+]
- 1:2/q08/p3 Rabb nurtures creatures toward completion. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): All beautiful praise belongs to God, who deserves worship as the source of original blessings and the creator, nurturer, and reformer of creation; the verse teaches gratitude and praise. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Rabb also means caretaker, cultivator, or reformer; it can be restricted in other compounds, though used absolutely it is reserved for God. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (قيل): A second etymology derives rabb from nurturing or cultivation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Knowledge of God’s existence is theoretical, not immediate; the world of heavens, earth, seas, minerals, plants, and animals needs a governing, originating, nurturing, and sustaining cause. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Kahf’s praise for the revealed Book concerns spiritual nurture through knowledge and unveiling, one kind of the Fātiḥa’s universal nurturing. | mentions 18:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī argues that gratitude is due at all times, even before revelation: the verse makes praise God’s right without qualification and grounds it in His nurturing and mercy. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Created nurturers seek their own profit, such as reward or praise, while God nurtures for the creatures’ benefit rather than His own. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): God’s nurturing does not diminish His store; unlike other givers, He gives from inexhaustible provision. | mentions 15:21 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): God gives before being asked, nurturing a person as a fetus and protecting him before he can think or pray. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): One derivation traces Rabb to tarbiya, since God manages and nurtures creation; the stepdaughter is called rabība because her mother’s husband raises her. In this sense it is an attribute of action, while owner or master denotes an attribute of essence. | mentions 4:23 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Rabb may derive from nurture: God manages and raises creation; stepdaughters are called rabāʾib because a husband raises them, making Rabb an attribute of action, while owner or master is an attribute of essence. | mentions 4:23 BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Rabb originally means tarbiya, bringing something to perfection little by little, then became an intensive description like ṣawm and ʿadl. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Another derivation makes rabb a descriptive form from rabba-yarubbuhu; it then means owner because an owner preserves and nurtures what is owned. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Rabb originally means gradual nurturing toward completion, then became an intensive description; another derivation treats it as a noun for the owner who preserves and develops what he owns. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The five divine attributes unfold a sequence: God creates, nurtures with favors, conceals sin in mercy, forgives repentance, and finally judges. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Every possible being remains under God’s nurture: without the continuing influx of divine support and the removal of every impediment, it would return to nonexistence. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Because contingent beings need a cause both to begin and to persist, continual divine preservation is itself tarbiya; keeping even indefinitely many possible obstacles absent sustains a thing. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض العارفين): A mystic observes that God has other servants, but each person has no lord but Him, while God nurtures each as though He had no servant but that person. ALUSI, d. 1270 (أرباب الظاهر): The outward scholars explain why petitioners say yā Rabb: a petitioner seeks what benefits and nurtures him, so calling on the nurturer is especially apt. | mentions 17:110, 7:180 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Rabb originally denotes tarbiya: gradually bringing a thing to the fullness suited to its primordial capacity, from the growth of a young child. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Rabb is also used for creator, master, king, benefactor, reformer, worshipped one, and companion; tarbiya is the primary sense, with the others best treated as figurative extensions. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī interprets rabb here as owner; al-Ālūsī prefers tarbiya because owner would repeat the sense of Mālik yawm al-dīn and gives no clear reason for placing al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm between them. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rabb originally denotes nurturing: bringing a thing gradually to the perfection suited to its primordial capacity; the word’s derivation and grammatical form are debated. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rabb is also used for creator, master, king, benefactor, reformer, worshiped one, and possessor; most commonly it means nurturing, which some treat as the literal sense because it comes first to mind. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers derivation from nurturing and takes the other uses as metaphors, since they all involve care and Arabic principles favor metaphor over homonymy; al-Zamakhsharī instead chooses “owner.” ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He rejects “owner” here because “Owner of the Day of Judgment” would repeat the meaning, with no evident reason for placing al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm between them; nurturing better fits the context and signals divine beneficence, power, and wisdom. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Alusi prefers the universal sense because the worlds are innumerable and God's nurturing encompasses every existing thing, both celestial and earthly. | mentions 16:8, 74:31 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Every contingent being depends on God's continual nurturing for existence and persistence; sustaining it also means keeping all its possible impediments absent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أرباب الظاهر): Outward scholars explain frequent supplication with Rabb as fitting because a petitioner seeks what seems beneficial for personal nurture and care. | mentions 17:110, 7:180 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Opening the Fatiha's descriptions with Lord of the worlds suits the Prophet's mission as a profound act of nurturing humanity and hints at compassion for all people. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Rab is a verbal noun for nurturing, applied emphatically to the nurturer; rububiyyah names this relation. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Rab entails complete power to nurture, govern, guide, command, reward, and discipline, and also means owner; without a qualifying relation it is reserved for God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Nurturing is gradual movement toward perfection, visibly at work throughout the universe; contemplating the realms reveals the law of divine nurturing, while the realms themselves are known through their relation to God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Some misuse evolutionary language by treating nature’s law as an uncaused absolute, displacing divine nurturing and making humanity the absolute summit of existence. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Those who deny divine nurturing contradict themselves by investing effort in education and child-rearing, which presuppose that development can be guided. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Without God’s perfect lordship and nurturing, neither existence nor development could be real; the world’s real share in these qualities reveals God as Lord of all realms. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): In Arabic, rabb can mean master or the one who manages, reforms, and nurtures; God continuously tends every creature rather than leaving the universe unattended. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Rabb may be a verbal noun or adjective on fa'l from rabba-yarubbu, 'to nurture and govern,' or from 'to own'; a verbal noun is intensive. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Tarbiya means gradually bringing something to completion; the adjective pattern fa'l from fa'ala yaf'ulu is rare, as in namma-yunimmu, nammun. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur prefers 'nurturer and governor' over 'owner' because it fits God's management of creation toward perfection and avoids redundancy with 'King of Judgment Day'; the latter can specify the afterlife. | mentions 1:4 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur prefers deriving رب from nurturing and directing: it suits God's governance and gradual completion of creatures, and avoids redundancy with ملك يوم الدين; one could answer that the worlds here mean only this world. | mentions 1:4 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): رب may be a verbal noun or an adjective from upbringing, meaning one who nurtures and directs; tarbiya gradually brings something to its perfection. It may also derive from ownership, with an intensive or rare adjectival form. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): Rabb combines rightful possession and authority with rearing, sustaining, and fostering something from its beginning to completion. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Used alone, Rabb refers only to God and names His ownership and nurturing; in a construct such as master of a house, it can refer to others. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Kahf's revelation nurtures souls with knowledge, a particular form of the general nurture expressed by Rabb al-alamin. | mentions 18:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb al-alamin suits the request for guidance to the straight path because a nurturer's primary task is guidance, a frequent Qur'anic association. | mentions 20:49, 20:50, 20:122, 87:1, 87:2, 87:3, 6:161, 3:8, 18:24, 26:62, 37:99, 28:22 STUDYQURAN (author): Some relate rabb to cultivation: God is caretaker and trainer of things and of human souls, hearts, and spirits. WAHIDI-BASIT: Al-Wāḥidī rejects the abiding root as unattested to linguists, though arrabba may mean remain. Al-Ghazālī, as reported by al-Biqāʿī, takes the Fātiḥa’s repeated mercy as pointing to perfect creation and provision for each world, alongside mercy in the afterlife. [49 notes: TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(قيل); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI; QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI; BAYDAWI-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI(أرباب الظاهر,الزمخشري,بعض العارفين); ALUSI-FULL(أرباب الظاهر); ELMALILI; QUTB-ZILAL; IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL; ASAD-NOTES; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي); STUDYQURAN; WAHIDI-BASIT; BIQAI-FULL(الغزالي); ALUSI-FULL+; ALUSI+; IBNASHUR-FULL+; IBNASHUR+; RAZI-FULL+]
- 1:2/q08/p4 Rabb means master or king. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (صفوان بن أمية): Safwan ibn Umayya's saying about being governed by a Qurayshi rather than a Hawazini uses the verb rabbani for rule or mastery. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): Al-ʿAlqama's line shows Rabb as the master to whom another's household or property has passed. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (لبيد): Labid's line uses Rabb for a master or chief who oversees affairs. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (النابغة): Al-Nabigha uses Rabb in the sense of king in his line about reaching al-Nuʿman. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Rabb can mean an obeyed master, as Labid's verse calls the chief of Kinda its rabb. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A third reason is that all knowledge concerns either divine lordship or human servitude: the opening praises God’s mastery, while the remainder shows the servant’s dependence on His aid and guidance. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The sequence Lord of the worlds, Merciful, Compassionate, Master of Judgment is, in Razi's view, a rationally unsurpassable ordering of God's attributes. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): In the divided-prayer report, God calls the basmala glorification, al-Hamd praise, the two mercy names extolling, the Master of Judgment entrustment, and the remaining response a sharing and a request granted. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The four motives for honoring someone map onto the verse: intrinsic perfection, beneficence, hoped-for future good, and fear correspond respectively to God, Lord, Merciful, and Master of Judgment. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The sequence assigns praise to God, then names Him Lord of the worlds: Allah signifies necessary perfection, while Lord of the worlds shows that all else flows from His generosity, making Him beyond completeness. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb also means master: Qurtubi cites the command to remember Joseph with his master and the report that a slave woman gives birth to her mistress. | mentions 12:42 QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Qurtubi prefers the first view, that the worlds include every creature and existent thing, supported by Pharaoh asking about the Lord of the worlds and Moses answering that He is Lord of the heavens, earth, and all between them. | mentions 26:23, 26:24 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Rabb can mean master, as in Joseph’s “your lord,” and the hadith that a slave woman gives birth to her mistress. | mentions 12:42 ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (بعضهم): Some lexicographers allow rabb to mean “companion,” adducing a poem in which a man approaches as master of the dogs. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The lām in “to God” restricts all praise to Him, and the annexation “Master of the Day of Judgment” underscores His sole dominion when other dominions vanish. | mentions 40:16 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Rabb is normally reserved for God unless annexed to a noun, though pre-Islamic Arabs used it without qualification for a human king, which the author calls unbelief. | mentions 12:50 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الحارث بن حلزة): Al-Harith ibn Hilliza's line calls a human king al-rabb without qualification, exemplifying pre-Islamic usage. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Al-hamd lillah establishes that God deserves praise for His essence and perfection; Rabb al-alamin adds His lordship, sovereignty and beneficence, while the worlds point to creation's beginning and the inference to resurrection. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الحكم بن عمير): In a weak report, saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” counts as thanking God, after which He increases the speaker. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عائشة): During a drought prayer, the Prophet invoked God as Lord of the worlds, the Merciful, and Master of Judgment, asking for rain that would sustain them; Abu Dawud calls the report rare with a good chain, and says it supports the Medinan no-alif reading. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers an exhaustive reading here: the sūra’s encompassing attributes of lordship, mercy, and sovereignty support all praise belonging to God, and the shift to إياك نعبد marks a new address rather than an explanation. ALUSI, d. 1270 (أبو هريرة): Abū Hurayra reports the Prophet forbidding a person to say feed or perform ablution for your rabb and instructing him to say master or patron; a note in the source calls this report abrogated. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Joseph’s use of rabb for his master, and the prostration before Joseph, are explained as permissions specific to that earlier time. | mentions 12:50, 12:23, 12:100 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī argues that comprehensiveness fits this passage’s total lordship, mercy, and continuing sovereignty; he contrasts it with another surah whose relevant details are absent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufi masters say “praise belongs to God” is like “might belongs to God”: God is both praiser and praised, and all things are His affairs, as the verse says creation and command belong to Him. | mentions 7:54 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو هريرة): Al-Bukhārī and Muslim report from Abū Hurayra that one should not say “feed your lord” or “give your lord ablution”; instead one should say “my master” or “my patron.” ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Allah signifies the all-encompassing divine essence and highest level; below it are oneness, mercifulness, lordship, and sovereignty, while absolute divine uniqueness has no manifestation of created qualities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ساداتنا الصوفية): Sufi masters say the appropriate divine name in prayer changes with the seeker's spiritual state and whether the experience is one of differentiation or union. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): After invoking the Merciful God, the worshipper turns to praise Him and name His unrestricted lordship over all worlds. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Making God Lord of all worlds points away from a chaotic crowd of competing lords toward one sovereign, bringing order, certainty, and confidence in His constant care. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The four descriptions—Lord of the worlds, Merciful, Compassionate, King of Judgment Day—show each divine name's meaning merits praise and distinguishes God from alleged deities. | mentions 1:3, 1:4 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A hadith discourages calling a human master 'my rabb' as etiquette to prevent implication of worship; 'master' is preferred, compounds for a house or animal are allowed, and unrestricted use for a king is more disliked. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Kashshaf limits رب here to master or king, allowing either a verbal noun or adjective, because the word most commonly carries those meanings. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Quranic expressions about Joseph's master and the rival lords show that رب can refer to someone besides God; the term remained available in Arabic usage. | mentions 12:23, 12:39 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (التفسير القيم): The names following praise show God is praiseworthy in divinity, lordship, mercy, and kingship. STUDYQURAN (author): Some say the worlds are all existent things other than God; al-Rāzī adds that God can actualize infinitely many unknown universes, making Him Lord of all existence and space. | mentions 17:44 [36 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(النابغة,صفوان بن أمية,لبيد); TABRISI; RAZI-FULL(أبو هريرة عن النبي); QURTUBI; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN(بعضهم); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(الحارث بن حلزة); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(الحكم بن عمير,عائشة); ALUSI(أبو هريرة); ALUSI-FULL(أبو هريرة,بعض ساداتنا الصوفية,ساداتنا الصوفية); QUTB-ZILAL; IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL(صاحب الكشاف); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(التفسير القيم); STUDYQURAN; ALUSI+; QURTUBI+]
- 1:2/q08/p5 Rabb manages and sets affairs right. — TABRISI, d. 548 (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash says rabb al-ʿālamīn is genitive as an adjective, with the adjectival relation itself governing the adjective, as shown by adjectives whose case differs from their head. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الهروي وغيره): Al-Harawī and others say rabb can mean one who rectifies, manages, mends, or tends something to completion; they connect rabbāniyyūn with tending the books. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الهروي): Rabb also means one who repairs, manages, restores, or takes care of something; rabbani scholars are named for tending to scripture. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): God is Lord of the worlds as origin, preserver, and manager of all; worlds means everything known to God, and the sound masculine plural reflects encompassing knowledge or predominance. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Others treat rabb as the verbal noun rabba yarubbu, meaning owned or governed, functioning as an agent noun like a just or fasting man. NASHR, d. 833 (أبو زيد سعيد بن أوس الأنصاري): Abu Zayd reports rabb al-ʿālamīn in both nominative and accusative; successive adjectives may differ, with some governed by an implied verb and others by an implied subject, but not revert to genitive. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Other proposed governors are the expressed الحمد, a vocative, or a past-tense reading of rabb; al-Ālūsī objects to the first for its syntax and to the latter parses as awkward. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Recognizing God's unrestricted lordship separates clear monotheism from beliefs that combine one creator with multiple powers governing life. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb al-alamin subsumes many divine qualities, since each name or attribute governs what it describes. TUSTARI: Al-Tustarī calls God creation's master, nurturer, caretaker, reformer and governor by prior knowledge, will, command and decree. [10 notes: TABRISI(الأخفش); QURTUBI(الهروي وغيره); QURTUBI-FULL(الهروي); QASHANI; SAMIN-DURR; NASHR(أبو زيد سعيد بن أوس الأنصاري); ALUSI(بعضهم); QUTB-ZILAL; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT; TUSTARI]
- 1:2/q08/p6 Rabb means the one worshipped. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (غاوي بن عبد العزى): Ghawy ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzza's line uses Rabb for the one worshipped; Rabb of all in the unrestricted sense is God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since possible beings need God to bring them into existence and preserve them, He is their deity as creator and their Lord as sustainer. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): “Lord of the worlds” signifies that God is one, owns all worlds, and is the only deity and object of worship. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb can also mean the deity, as shown by a poet’s line about a lord upon whose head a fox urinates. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Rabb can also mean the one worshipped; the cited poet mocks a fox worshipped after it soils an idol’s head. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Some polytheists claimed they worshipped lesser patrons only to bring them nearer to God, while some People of the Book treated their rabbis and monks as lords. | mentions 39:3, 9:31 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (غاوي بن ظالم أو عباس بن مرداس): A pre-Islamic poet applies rabb to an idol, ridiculing a deity with a fox urinating on its head; al-Uzza was also called al-Rabba and the plural is ارباب. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God’s lordship over all entails that He alone is Lord, while exclusive worship and reliance affirm that there is no deity but Him. [8 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(غاوي بن عبد العزى); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI; QURTUBI-FULL; QUTB-ZILAL; IBNASHUR(غاوي بن ظالم أو عباس بن مرداس); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT]
- 1:2/q08/p7 Rabb denotes abiding. — ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (زيد بن علي وطائفة): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and a group read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative as praise; the reading weakens if the following adjectives remain genitive. WAHIDI-BASIT: Theologians derive eternal lordship from remaining in place. [2 notes: ABUHAYYAN(زيد بن علي وطائفة); WAHIDI-BASIT(theologians)]

## 1:2/q09 How is rabb read and parsed?  [رَبِّ · readings]
turns on: Whether rabb follows Allāh in the genitive or detaches in the accusative or nominative.
- 1:2/q09/p1 Rabb is read accusative as detached praise. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (بعضهم): Some read Rabb in the accusative as a form of praise. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (بعضهم): Others take accusative Rabb as direct address, parallel to the implied address in “You alone.” TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn may be accusative as praise or as an implied command to remember the Lord; a nominative praise reading is also possible, as poetry shows. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الجمهور): Most readers put rabb in the genitive; Abū al-Ghāliya, Ibn al-Sumayfa, and ʿĪsā ibn ʿUmar read it in the accusative; several others use nominative. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb may be read in the nominative by syntactic disconnection, meaning He is Lord of the worlds, or in the accusative as a word of praise. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (زيد بن علي وطائفة): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and a group read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative as praise; the reading weakens if the following adjectives remain genitive. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الأهوازي): Al-Ahwāzī reports Zayd ibn ʿAlī reading rabb, raḥmān, and raḥīm all in the accusative, avoiding the mismatch in case. | mentions 1:3 ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects parsing accusative rabb as an implied verb of praise or as apposition: one rests on imagined syntax, the other is interrupted by raḥmān raḥīm. | mentions 1:3 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects deriving the accusative rabb from a prior implied verb such as We praise God, since this analogy to ellipsis in coordination is not productive. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (زيد بن علي وطائفة): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and a group read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative as praise, though lowering the following epithets weakens the construction. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الأهوازي): Al-Ahwāzī records Zayd ibn ʿAlī reading rabb, raḥmān, and raḥīm all in the accusative, which removes the syntactic weakness. NASHR, d. 833 (أبو زيد سعيد بن أوس الأنصاري): Abu Zayd reports rabb al-ʿālamīn in both nominative and accusative; successive adjectives may differ, with some governed by an implied verb and others by an implied subject, but not revert to genitive. ALUSI, d. 1270 (زيد بن علي): Zayd b. ʿAlī is reported to read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative; the author’s preferred parse is an accusative of detachment with an implied verb such as أمدح. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (زيد بن علي): A reading attributed to Zayd ibn ʿAli puts Rabb al-ʿalamin in the accusative; al-Alusi finds the first grammatical explanation, an implied praise or mention verb, most apparent. NAHHAS: Al-Kisāʾī allows accusative praise, Abū Ḥātim supplies a verb, Abū Isḥāq proposes vocative, Ibn Kaysān rejects vocative, and nominative 'He is Lord' is also possible. NAHHAS: Al-Kisāʾī allows accusative praise, Abū Ḥātim supplies a verb, Abū Isḥāq proposes vocative, Ibn Kaysān rejects vocative, and nominative 'He is Lord' is also possible. NAHHAS: Al-Kisāʾī allows accusative praise, Abū Ḥātim supplies a verb, Abū Isḥāq proposes vocative, Ibn Kaysān rejects vocative, and nominative 'He is Lord' is also possible. NAHHAS: Al-Kisāʾī allows accusative praise, Abū Ḥātim supplies a verb, Abū Isḥāq proposes vocative, Ibn Kaysān rejects vocative, and nominative 'He is Lord' is also possible. NAHHAS: Al-Kisāʾī allows accusative praise, Abū Ḥātim supplies a verb, Abū Isḥāq proposes vocative, Ibn Kaysān rejects vocative, and nominative 'He is Lord' is also possible. THALABI: Zayd reads rabb accusative as praise, while the usual genitive describes creator, originator, owner and manager. THALABI: Zayd reads rabb accusative as praise, while the usual genitive describes creator, originator, owner and manager. THALABI: Abū Saʿīd supplies 'I praise the Lord of the worlds'. KASHSHAF-FULL: Zayd's reading is introduced. IBNATIYYA: Accusative rabb may be praise or vocative aligned with 1:5. IBNATIYYA: Accusative rabb may be praise or vocative aligned with 1:5. IBNATIYYA: Accusative rabb may be praise or vocative aligned with 1:5. Al-Zajjāj allows accusative rabb al-ʿālamīn in ordinary speech as detached praise with an implied ‘I mention the Lord of the worlds’; he cites an unnamed poet to show that accusative epithets may follow a preceding noun or serve as praise, and says grammarians accept these case combinations. [29 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(بعضهم); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الجمهور); QURTUBI; ABUHAYYAN(الأهوازي,زيد بن علي وطائفة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأهوازي,زيد بن علي وطائفة); NASHR(أبو زيد سعيد بن أوس الأنصاري); ALUSI(زيد بن علي); ALUSI-FULL(زيد بن علي); NAHHAS(أبو إسحاق,أبو حاتم,ابن كيسان,الكسائي); THALABI(أبو سعيد ابن أوس الأنصاري,زيد بن علي); KASHSHAF-FULL(زيد بن علي); IBNATIYYA(بعضهم,طائفة); ZAJJAJ]
- 1:2/q09/p2 Rabb is read accusative as direct address. — BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): The accusative rabb al-ʿālamīn is analyzed as praise, a vocative, or an implied verb indicated by “praise.” SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading makes rabb genitive as an adjective or appositive to Allah; accusative allows an implied I praise, a detached praise, or vocative, with vocative weakest because it separates adjective from noun; nominative is detached predicate huwa rabb. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn is read genitive as an adjective, nominative with an implicit subject, or accusative with an implicit verb or as a vocative. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Other proposed governors are the expressed الحمد, a vocative, or a past-tense reading of rabb; al-Ālūsī objects to the first for its syntax and to the latter parses as awkward. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Other proposed parses take Rabb as a vocative or a past-tense verb, but al-Alusi finds the vocative unclear and the verbal reading syntactically remote. [5 notes: BAYDAWI; SAMIN-DURR; ITQAN; ALUSI(بعضهم); ALUSI-FULL(بعضهم)]
- 1:2/q09/p3 Rabb is genitive as a description of Allāh. — TABRISI, d. 548 (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash says rabb al-ʿālamīn is genitive as an adjective, with the adjectival relation itself governing the adjective, as shown by adjectives whose case differs from their head. TABRISI, d. 548 (زيد بن علي): Zayd ibn Ali's fatḥa on the bā of rabb is understood as demonstrating that the form is possible, not as a recitation. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): The doxology was already used in the early Meccan period; in middle Meccan texts it is joined to the epithet rabb al-ʿālamīn, itself attested early. | mentions 52:48, 37:182, 39:75, 83:6, 81:27, 81:29, 69:43, 68:52, 56:80 JISHUMI: Rabb's case is discussed with its senses, while al-ḥamd's article is generic. JISHUMI: Rabb's case is discussed with its senses, while al-ḥamd's article is generic. JISHUMI: Rabb's case is discussed with its senses, while al-ḥamd's article is generic. Al-Zajjāj allows genitive rabb as continuation in ordinary speech and cites an unnamed poet for epithets that follow the preceding noun. He says the Qurʾan chooses the best and most eloquent wording, the genitive sequence rabb al-ʿālamīn al-raḥmān al-raḥīm, despite other case forms being possible in ordinary speech. [9 notes: TABRISI(الأخفش,زيد بن علي); CORPUSCORANICUM; JISHUMI; ZAJJAJ; ZAJJAJ+]
- 1:2/q09/p4 Rabb is read nominative with an implied subject. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أبو رزين العقيلي والربيع بن خيثم وأبو عمران الجوني): These readers pronounce the bāʾ of rabb in the nominative. Al-Zajjāj explains nominative rabb al-ʿālamīn as ‘He is the Lord of the worlds’; his unnamed poetic example illustrates how nominative epithets may state ‘they are the ones,’ among case combinations accepted by grammarians. [3 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو رزين العقيلي والربيع بن خيثم وأبو عمران الجوني); ZAJJAJ]

## 1:2/q10 Who or what are the worlds?  [ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ · referent]
turns on: Whether the plural is restricted to rational or accountable beings, or extends to every created kind.
- 1:2/q10/p1 The worlds include every created thing. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj derives ʿālam from knowledge or a sign because the world points to its maker; ʿālamīn encompasses all beings besides God, including human and jinn realms. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The nominative reading is stronger and more laudatory than an accusative meaning I praise, because it makes praise due to God from all creation, not only the speaker. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): In ordinary usage the worlds can mean all created things, supported by Pharaoh's question about the Lord of the worlds and God's answer. | mentions 26:23, 26:24 IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (بعض المفسرين): Some derive ʿālam from ʿalāma, a sign pointing to its Creator; that derivation supports taking it for all created existence. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أهل العربية): For Arab grammarians, ʿālamīn is the plural of ʿālam and names all created beings from beginning to end; people also call their present age a world. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الضحاك عن ابن عباس): One reported interpretation takes “the worlds” to mean all creation: heavens and earths, what they contain, and what lies between them. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ʿālamīn includes everything besides God, which depends on Him for existence and continuance; every indivisible particle and individual accident proves the wise, powerful God. | mentions 17:44 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because worlds include everything besides God, including space and time, their Lord created them and existed before them; this proves His transcendence beyond place, direction, and spatial extension. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): All worlds share the contingency, need, and change that make a person depend on God; their common cause of need establishes God's lordship over every creature and heaven. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Qurtubi prefers the first view, that the worlds include every creature and existent thing, supported by Pharaoh asking about the Lord of the worlds and Moses answering that He is Lord of the heavens, earth, and all between them. | mentions 26:23, 26:24 QURTUBI, d. 671 (الزجاج والخليل): Al-Zajjāj derives ʿālam from knowledge or a sign: it is everything God created in this life and the next and it points to its maker and planner; al-Khalīl likewise says a sign or marker indicates a thing. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Muslims agree God is praised for all blessings; since faith is a blessing and belongs among all created things, faith is God’s act and creation, against the Qadariyya. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (سيبويه): Sibawayh says the nominative attributes praise to the speaker and all creation, while the accusative attributes it to the speaker alone. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): ʿĀlam means what makes something known, like a signet or mold; it came to denote all besides God, substances and accidents, whose contingency indicates the necessary Creator. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Rabb is used of others only in a qualified phrase, whereas al-ʿālam names what makes something known and came to mean everything besides God. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The worlds comprise all substances and accidents, whose possibility and need for a necessary cause show God's existence; the plural gathers different genera, with rational beings predominant in its form. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Al-‘alamin may mean everything the Creator knows of bodies, substances, and accidents, or every existing thing besides God, named for being evidence of His existence. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): God is Lord of the worlds as origin, preserver, and manager of all; worlds means everything known to God, and the sound masculine plural reflects encompassing knowledge or predominance. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الحسن وقتادة): Al-Ḥasan and Qatāda define al-ʿālamīn as every created thing. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The author prefers al-Raghib's account that the sound plural includes all creation, with human usage prevailing when humans share the label. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Al-ʿālamīn is the plural of ʿālam, encompassing every created being besides God; created kinds in heavens, earth, land, and sea are worlds, as is each generation. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas describes the worlds as all creation belonging to God: heavens, earths, their inhabitants and everything between them, known and unknown. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir says ʿālam derives from ʿalāmah, a sign, because every created thing points to its Maker, Creator, and oneness; he cites a poem by Ibn al-Mu'tazz. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas also explains the Lord of the worlds as Lord of all creation: all the heavens and their inhabitants, all earths and their inhabitants, and everything between them, known or unknown. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas also explains the Lord of the worlds as God of all creation, the heavens and their inhabitants, the earths and their inhabitants, and all between them, known or unknown. ITQAN, d. 911 (الخويي): Al-Khuyi says al-Fatiha fittingly gives the broadest divine attribute, Lord of all creation, while other surahs open with particular acts or attributes. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Although the sound masculine plural often denotes a small number, the form here hints that all created worlds remain few beside God’s greatness; the plural can also denote many. | mentions 39:67 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Opening the sūra’s descriptions with Rabb al-ʿālamīn suits the Prophet’s mission and asks for compassion toward all creatures, since God is their Lord. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): ʿĀlam means what makes something known and is commonly used for all that God created, including substances and accidents, or for a whole class of beings. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The definite plural makes the scope of divine lordship explicit across every created kind; singular al-ʿālam might be mistaken for the familiar perceptible world alone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): ʿĀlam originally means a means of knowing, then came to denote what reveals the Creator: all that exists besides Him, substances and accidents, either as the whole or as each kind. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The plural al-ʿālamīn guarantees inclusion of every created kind: a singular definite noun could be heard as the familiar perceptible world, whereas the plural distributes lordship across all named kinds and their individuals. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): The phrase Lord of the worlds states a core creed: God's complete lordship extends across all creation. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Raising hamd announces praise from the speaker and all creation, whereas the accusative announces only the speaker's praise. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Everything is God's cr eation, and He made every created thing good; creation and goodness therefore coincide in what He creates. | mentions 40:62, 32:7 ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): With the definite article, rabb is used in the Qur'an exclusively for God, sole sustainer of all creation and ultimate authority. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Hamd is preferable to both madḥ and shukr because it covers God's qualities and favors to the speaker and all creation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): This plural suits al-Fatiha because its worship, aid, guidance, and moral categories address accountable creatures; Rabb al-alamin may mean their Lord or all creation with rational beings predominant. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Lord of all worlds establishes God’s oneness and ownership of all creation, with no other lord or deity. STUDYQURAN (author): Some say the worlds are all existent things other than God; al-Rāzī adds that God can actualize infinitely many unknown universes, making Him Lord of all existence and space. | mentions 17:44 TAB: Ibn ʿAbbās lists heavens, earth and everything between, known or unknown, under God's incomparable ownership. TAB-FULL: Ibn ʿAbbās includes heaven, earth and all known or unknown creatures. IBNABIHATIM: Qatāda identifies the worlds with God's described creation.
