IBNATIYYA: Every existent besides God is a world; generations are worlds, and al-Zajjāj derives the term from knowledge or sign. Al-Zajjāj takes al-ʿālamīn to mean everything God created and supports the breadth of ‘Lord of the worlds’ with ‘He is Lord of everything’ (6:164). [81 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(الزجاج); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(بعض المفسرين); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أهل العربية,الضحاك عن ابن عباس); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI(الزجاج والخليل); QURTUBI-FULL(سيبويه); BAYDAWI; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI; QASHANI; ABUHAYYAN(الحسن وقتادة); IBNKATHIR(ابن عباس); DURR(ابن عباس); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); ITQAN(الخويي); ALUSI; ALUSI-FULL; QUTB-ZILAL; IBNASHUR; TABATABAI; ASAD-NOTES; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي); STUDYQURAN; TAB(ابن عباس); TAB-FULL(عبد الله بن عباس); IBNABIHATIM(قتادة); THALABI(أبو عمرو بن العلاء، ابن عباس,الحسن وقتادة ومجاهد,الخضر بن إسماعيل,الفراء وأبو عبيدة,جعفر بن محمد الصادق,سفيان بن عيينة,عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم,عبد العزيز بن يحيى الكناني); MAWARDI(unattributed,أبو إسحاق الزجاج,ابن عباس); MAWARDI-FULL(أبو إسحاق الزجاج,ابن عباس); WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو الهيثم والأزهري,ابن عباس,الحسن ومجاهد وقتادة,الحسين بن الفضل وأبو معاذ,الفراء وأبو عبيدة); JISHUMI(ابن عباس وأبي علي); BAGHAWI(قتادة ومجاهد والحسن); BAGHAWI-FULL(قتادة ومجاهد والحسن); IBNATIYYA(الزجاج); ZAJJAJ; QURTUBI+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT+; SAMIN-DURR+; TABRISI+; WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو إسحاق)+]
- 1:2/q10/p2 Each kind or generation is a world. — TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن عباس وسعيد بن جبير وقتادة): Most commentators take each species and each generation of a species as a world, so al-ʿālamīn is plural across kinds and times. TABRISI, d. 548 (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash says rabb al-ʿālamīn is genitive as an adjective, with the adjectival relation itself governing the adjective, as shown by adjectives whose case differs from their head. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn may be accusative as praise or as an implied command to remember the Lord; a nominative praise reading is also possible, as poetry shows. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): In al-ʿālamīn, yā is both the plural and case marker, while nūn replaces the singular's case vowel and is opened to distinguish it from the dual. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): All beautiful praise belongs to God, who deserves worship as the source of original blessings and the creator, nurturer, and reformer of creation; the verse teaches gratitude and praise. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Al-ʿālamīn has no singular of the same form and may derive from the mark by which creation points to its Maker, or from knowledge as what is known. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (شيخنا): The speculative theologians use ʿālam for the crea ted universe as a whole: the celestial sphere, heavens, earth, and what lies between; al-Zajjāj is said to hold this meaning. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (بعض المفسرين): Another derivation connects ʿālam to knowledge, supporting Ibn ʿAbbās’s interpretation. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أهل النظر): The speculative theologians use ʿālam for the created cosmos as a whole: the celestial sphere, heavens, earth, and what lies between. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أهل النظر): The derivation of ʿālam from ʿalāma, “sign,” supports the speculative theologians' view: the world is named as a sign pointing to its Creator. RAZI, d. 606 (author): One cannot know God as Lord of the worlds without first understanding what “Lord” and “the worlds” mean. RAZI, d. 606 (author): The worlds comprise every existent other than God: spatial bodies, incorporeal entities, and attributes; bodies divide into simple entities and composites. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi says there is no proof that bodies are limited to the familiar cosmic categories: since an infinite void lies beyond the world and God can do all possible things, He can create countless external worlds, each larger than this one and containing analogous heavens, earths and celestial bodies. RAZI, d. 606 (author): “Lord” is in a genitive construction with “the worlds” as its genitive complement; understanding this relation requires knowing both terms. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The phrase al-ḥamdu lillāh raises questions about God’s existence and right to praise; Rabb al-ʿālamīn answers the first, while al-Raḥmān, al-Raḥīm, and Mālik yawm al-dīn answer the second. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Rāzī prefers Rabb al-ʿālamīn to Creator of the worlds because sustaining creatures after their beginning is disputed, and the phrase stresses their dependence on God at every moment. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Anʿām’s praise for creating the heavens, earth, darkness, and light is one subset of Rabb al-ʿālamīn; dependence on God for preservation entails dependence for origination. | mentions 6:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Fāṭir describes bringing the heavens and earth into existence, distinct from creation as determination yet also included within Rabb al-ʿālamīn. | mentions 35:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The sequence Lord of the worlds, Merciful, Compassionate, Master of Judgment is, in Razi's view, a rationally unsurpassable ordering of God's attributes. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The one able to create the immense worlds, Throne, heavens, and stars can destroy them and is independent of them. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī says there is no proof that only the familiar kinds of bodies exist: an infinite void lies beyond this world, and God can create innumerable greater worlds containing counterparts of its cosmic features; philosophers’ arguments for one world rely on weak premises. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The construction rabb al-ʿālamīn is an iḍāfa, whose relation cannot be known without first knowing both correlated terms. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ʿālamīn means every existent other than God; these beings comprise extended bodies, separate entities, and attributes, with bodies divided into simple and composite kinds. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-hamdu lillah is better than ash-shukru lillah because it praises God for every benefit to the worlds, even one not received by the speaker; shukr is personal gratitude. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The sequence assigns praise to God, then names Him Lord of the worlds: Allah signifies necessary perfection, while Lord of the worlds shows that all else flows from His generosity, making Him beyond completeness. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The seven Fatiha verses remove seven vices: praise dispels desire, Lord of the worlds greed, Judgment Day anger, worship and aid pride and vanity, guidance caprice, the favored path unbelief and doubt, and the final phrase innovation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): “Lord of the worlds” signifies that God is one, owns all worlds, and is the only deity and object of worship. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Raising the hands signals leaving both worlds and directing all inner faculties toward God; “God is greatest” means He exceeds all beings, knowledge, comparison, and even the claim that He is simply greater. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): One derivation traces Rabb to tarbiya, since God manages and nurtures creation; the stepdaughter is called rabība because her mother’s husband raises her. In this sense it is an attribute of action, while owner or master denotes an attribute of essence. | mentions 4:23 QURTUBI, d. 671 (قتادة): Qatāda says al-ʿālamīn is the plural of ʿālam and means every existing thing other than God, with no singular on this pattern, like rahṭ or qawm. QURTUBI, d. 671 (وهب بن منبه): Wahb ibn Munabbih says God has eighteen thousand worlds, of which this world is one. QURTUBI, d. 671 (أبو سعيد الخدري): Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī says God has forty thousand worlds, with the inhabited world from east to west counting as one. QURTUBI, d. 671 (مقاتل): Muqātil says there are eighty thousand worlds, forty thousand on land and forty thousand at sea. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الجنيد): A man praised God before al-Junayd but stopped before saying Lord of the worlds; al-Junayd urged him to complete the verse, explaining that created things vanish when paired with the Eternal. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb may be read in the nominative by syntactic disconnection, meaning He is Lord of the worlds, or in the accusative as a word of praise. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Rabb may derive from nurture: God manages and raises creation; stepdaughters are called rabāʾib because a husband raises them, making Rabb an attribute of action, while owner or master is an attribute of essence. | mentions 4:23 BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): The accusative rabb al-ʿālamīn is analyzed as praise, a vocative, or an implied verb indicated by “praise.” NASAFI, d. 710 (author): The plural ending -in is used although normally associated with rational beings or their equivalent because ‘alamin has an adjectival sense, indicating knowledge. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Al-ʿālamīn denotes what makes the Creator known—bodies, substances, and accidents—or every existent apart from God; its sound masculine plural reflects a descriptive quality associated with rational beings. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (زيد بن علي وطائفة): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and a group read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative as praise; the reading weakens if the following adjectives remain genitive. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The article in al-ʿālamīn is exhaustive; ʿālam is an irregular collective and its sound masculine plural is even more irregular because it violates some conditions of that pattern. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān prefers that al-ʿālamīn denote morally responsible beings, supported by the verse about signs for the knowledgeable; Ḥafṣ's kasra on the lām clarifies the sense. | mentions 30:22 ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (ابن عباس): One report from Ibn ʿAbbās defines al-ʿālamīn as every being with a soul. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (زيد بن علي وطائفة): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and a group read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative as praise, though lowering the following epithets weakens the construction. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Although al-ʿālamīn is an irregular plural, Abū Ḥayyān favors the responsible creatures because a cited verse and Ḥafṣ's lām-kasra reading support that scope. | mentions 29:44 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Al-alamin is an annexed sound masculine plural in form but an ism-jam because its singular differs; it cannot be plural of alam if that covers all non-divine beings while al-alamin means rational beings only. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Sibawayh similarly says a'rab is not plural of arab because the former is narrower; an objection that al-alamin could mean only rational alam is answered by comparison with the unavailable form shay'un. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report attributed to the Prophet says that whoever says al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn has thanked God, who then increases him. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (طائفة): A group holds that al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn is better than lā ilāha illā Allāh because it combines praise with monotheism. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (مروان بن محمد بن مروان بن الحكم): Ibn Asakir reports Marwan's claim that God created seventeen thousand worlds, with the inhabitants of the heavens and earth forming one and God alone knowing the rest. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (تبيع الحميري): Tubyay al-Himyari says the worlds comprise one thousand nations, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سعيد بن المسيب): A similar count of one thousand worlds, divided into six hundred sea and four hundred land worlds, is also attributed to Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سعيد بن المسيب): Al-Baghawi reports Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab's view that God has one thousand worlds, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (وهب بن منبه): Wahb ibn Munabbih says God has eighteen thousand worlds and this world is one of them. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (مقاتل): Muqatil says there are eighty thousand worlds. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (كعب الأحبار): Ka'b al-Ahbar says that no one knows the number of worlds except God. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (أبو سعيد الخدري): Al-Qurtubi reports from Abu Sa'id al-Khudri that God has forty thousand worlds and the inhabited east-to-west world is only one. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (القرطبي): Al-Qurtubi prefers a comprehensive meaning encompassing all worlds, citing Pharaoh's question and God's identification of the Lord of the worlds as Lord of heavens, earth, and what is between them. | mentions 26:23 NASHR, d. 833 (author): Stopping after these phrases is good because their sense is understood, but starting with attached phrases such as rabb al-ʿālamīn, ṣirāṭ alladhīna, or ghayr al-maghḍūb ʿalayhim is poor, except at verse ends per the Sunnah. NASHR, d. 833 (أبو زيد سعيد بن أوس الأنصاري): Abu Zayd reports rabb al-ʿālamīn in both nominative and accusative; successive adjectives may differ, with some governed by an implied verb and others by an implied subject, but not revert to genitive. NASHR, d. 833 (قتيبة عن الكسائي، كردم عن نافع، ورش): The author adds unlisted transmissions: Qutayba from al-Kisa'i has imāla in al-ʿālamīn and al-raḥmān; Kurram from Nafi and al-Ahwazi from Warsh lengthen vowels in mālik, naʿbudu, and iyyāka. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): Al-ʿālamīn points to creation's beginning, inviting inference from the made thing to its Maker and from the beginning to resurrection, as the Torah also begins with creation for this reason. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha's purpose is to establish God's exclusive right to all praise and perfection, sovereignty over both worlds, worship and aid, and to ask for the path of the successful rather than the doomed; its center is مراقبة. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Al-hamd lillah establishes that God deserves praise for His essence and perfection; Rabb al-alamin adds His lordship, sovereignty and beneficence, while the worlds point to creation's beginning and the inference to resurrection. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (مسعود التفتازاني): Al-Taftazani maps the Fatiha's blessings to two creations and t wo preservations: creation in Rabb al-alamin, preservation in mercy, second creation in judgment, and afterlife preservation in worship onward. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali says the seven repeated verses are the Qur'an's gathered essence and its contents will be disclosed at creation's completion; earlier scriptures progress from changeable scrolls and lasting books to the Qur'an, which completes religion. | mentions 5:3 DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas describes an exchange at sneezing: the sneezer says al-hamdulillah, an angel answers Lord of the worlds, and after the sneezer repeats it the angel asks God to show mercy. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A weak report from the Companion al-Hakam ibn Umayr says that saying Lord of the worlds is gratitude to God, bringing an increase. DURR, d. 911 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): Ibn ʿAmr assigns distinct functions to remembrances: glorification is creation's prayer, al-hamd gratitude, tawhid sincerity, takbir fills heaven and earth, and the declaration of God's powerlessness-to-act-without-help brings God's command to submit. DURR, d. 911 (تتبع الجهري): Al-Tatabbuʿ al-Jahri says the worlds are a thousand communities, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land. DURR, d. 911 (وهب): Wahb says God has eighteen thousand worlds, of which this world is only one. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas explains al-hamd as gratitude, humble submission to God, and acknowledgment of His blessings, guidance, creation, and other favors. DURR, d. 911 (قتادة): Qatada says the Lord of the worlds describes what God has made. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (مجاهد): Mujāhid says the revelation of al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabb al-ʿālamīn greatly distressed Iblīs, who groaned and snorted; those who groan or snort are accursed. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الحكم بن عمير): In a weak report, saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” counts as thanking God, after which He increases the speaker. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): When someone sneezes and says al-hamd, an angel responds “Lord of the worlds”; when the sneezer repeats it, the angel says “may God have mercy on you.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي بن أبي طالب): A report attributed to Ali says that saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” at each heard sneeze prevents toothache and earache. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (قتادة): Qatada says “Lord of the worlds” encompasses every class of created beings. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (تتبع الجهري): A report counts the worlds as a thousand communities, 600 in the sea and 400 on land. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (وهب): Wahb says God has eighteen thousand worlds, of which this world is only one. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عائشة): During a drought prayer, the Prophet invoked God as Lord of the worlds, the Merciful, and Master of Judgment, asking for rain that would sustain them; Abu Dawud calls the report rare with a good chain, and says it supports the Medinan no-alif reading. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses Lord of the worlds as the whole of creation. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn is read genitive as an adjective, nominative with an implicit subject, or accusative with an implicit verb or as a vocative. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن أبي جمرة): Explaining the scale of commentary, Ibn Abi Jamra says the opening requires explaining praise, the divine name and transcendence, and the worlds, their nature, types, number, and extent. ALUSI, d. 1270 (زيد بن علي): Zayd b. ʿAlī is reported to read rabb al-ʿālamīn in the accusative; the author’s preferred parse is an accusative of detachment with an implied verb such as أمدح. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawwānī explains al-Sharīf’s requirement of inward and outward exaltation as intending the performative creation of esteem, not belief in a proposition. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufis understand the praise as belonging to God Himself, like sovereignty and creation-command: He is both praiser and praised, and all things are His manifestations. | mentions 7:54 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): When the distinction between Creator and creation is considered together, all praise comes from God and returns to Him; there is no praiser or praised one apart from Him. | mentions 53:42 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Although ʿālamīn is a sound masculine plural, it can cover nonrational beings if the word is restricted to rational creatures, if rational beings predominate, or if the others are treated as signs of knowledge. | mentions 41:11, 12:4 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The suggestion that ʿālamīn is a unique collective noun on the pattern of sound plurals does not settle its form; the author distinguishes ordinary plurals, collective generics, and collective nouns. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The following divine attributes imply that God deserves praise for them; since their scope is comprehensive, the praise should likewise be comprehensive, with God encompassing all names and worlds and mercy extending universally. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufi masters say “praise belongs to God” is like “might belongs to God”: God is both praiser and praised, and all things are His affairs, as the verse says creation and command belong to Him. | mentions 7:54 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): A mystical account divides praise into praise originating from God and from creation: God’s praise concerns His essence, manifestations, attributes, and effects, while creatures praise His essence, names, actions, secrets, and wisdom according to their understanding. | mentions 6:91, 20:110, 37:180, 53:42 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Another debate asks whether al-ʿālamīn is a regular plural, a collective noun, or a name for a totality; al-Ālūsī concludes it is a true plural because each member is itself called an ʿālam. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Some say al-ʿālamīn is a plural restricted to rational beings; replies derive it from rational ʿālam after restriction, from grammatical predominance, or by treating knowledgeable signs as rational. | mentions 41:11, 12:4 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Although common usage applies ʿālam to the totality or to a kind, not ordinarily to an individual, its original sense can apply to every part, since every contingent thing witnesses its need for the necessary Creator. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الإمام الأسيوطي): Al-Suyuti derives the term from knowledge when the worlds are understood generally, and from a sign when the term is taken in its broader sense. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Alusi prefers the universal sense because the worlds are innumerable and God's nurturing encompasses every existing thing, both celestial and earthly. | mentions 16:8, 74:31 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (كعب الأحبار): Kaʿb al-Aḥbār says only God can count the number of worlds. | mentions 16:8, 74:31 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The masculine sound plural can denote paucity despite the many worlds, underscoring that all are insignificant beside God's majesty; Arabic usage can also allow either number. | mentions 39:67 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Names beneath Rabb include attributes shared in some respect between Creator and creation and active names exclusive to God; the latter belong under Malik. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rahman is more comprehensive than Rabb because it gathers every exalted divine attribute, whether exclusive to God or related to creation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (زيد بن علي): A reading attributed to Zayd ibn ʿAli puts Rabb al-ʿalamin in the accusative; al-Alusi finds the first grammatical explanation, an implied praise or mention verb, most apparent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The descriptions close by pointing to resurrection, just as Lord of the worlds opened with origination; they explain the prior praise and prepare for the prayer that follows. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الإمام): An argument for rational gratitude before revelation says the verse establishes God's praiseworthiness through creation, mercy, and future judgment; al-Alusi replies that this proof itself is scriptural, not rational. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The community that reserves worship and appeal to God reflects a delegated divine trust and receives a share in creation; this supports communal consensus as a legally probative source. | mentions 35:39, 21:105 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sound plural al-alamin foregrounds rational beings; a concise Turkish rendering is difficult, but it means God alone is Lord of all realms and especially their rational inhabitants. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sign in al-alamin, later clarified by al-Baqara’s description of the Qur’an as guidance for the Godfearing, signals the wisdom of revelation. | mentions 2:2 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sound rational plural and the definite article extend the meaning across all realms and foreground rational beings; understanding the Lord of the worlds requires conceiving rational agents. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Every ordered community of things, from inner awareness to external objects, is named a realm because it serves as a sign and means of knowing God’s creation, lordship, and perfection. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): He renders the verse as praise belonging to the Lord of the worlds. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): After invoking the Merciful God, the worshipper turns to praise Him and name His unrestricted lordship over all worlds. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Making God Lord of all worlds points away from a chaotic crowd of competing lords toward one sovereign, bringing order, certainty, and confidence in His constant care. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Rabb is an attribute of Allah: after praise is attached to His essence to show inherent entitlement, the attribute adds entitlement through His qualities; this is clearer than saying 'praise belongs to Lord of the worlds.' | mentions 83:6 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The four descriptions—Lord of the worlds, Merciful, Compassionate, King of Judgment Day—show each divine name's meaning merits praise and distinguishes God from alleged deities. | mentions 1:3, 1:4 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur prefers 'nurturer and governor' over 'owner' because it fits God's management of creation toward perfection and avoids redundancy with 'King of Judgment Day'; the latter can specify the afterlife. | mentions 1:4 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Al-alamin is the plural of alam; few nouns on fa'il take this plural, with yasm (jasmine) the other example, whose plurals occur in al-A'sha's verse. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The fa'il pattern usually marks an instrument; worlds are thus imaginatively instruments for knowing the Maker or truths, a subtle coinage by the Arabs. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The plural uses the rational masculine form even though some worlds are nonrational, by giving precedence to rational beings. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Rabb al-alamin encompasses God's acts and creative attributes for those who affirm such attributes. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Praise precedes God's name because this occasion is the beginning of Qur'anic revelation, the great blessing that brings success in both worlds and evokes the Revealer's beautiful attributes. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur prefers deriving رب from nurturing and directing: it suits God's governance and gradual completion of creatures, and avoids redundancy with ملك يوم الدين; one could answer that the worlds here mean only this world. | mentions 1:4 CORPUSCORANICUM (author): The doxology was already used in the early Meccan period; in middle Meccan texts it is joined to the epithet rabb al-ʿālamīn, itself attested early. | mentions 52:48, 37:182, 39:75, 83:6, 81:27, 81:29, 69:43, 68:52, 56:80 CORPUSCORANICUM (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn corresponds to Jewish liturgical ribbon ʿōlām, an eschatological title spanning this world and the next; a Syriac parallel itself lacks that connotation. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): The form may be a cross-language wordplay presenting the emerging community as citizens of both worlds, through a nisba formation from ʿālam and rabbinic Hebrew ʿōlām. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Revelation compares the visible realm to a drop in the sea and a grain of sand beside the unseen; knowledge of cosmic scale points both to the limits of reason and the immensity of divine creation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb can mean owner, master, educator, guardian, and benefactor; the Lord of the worlds embodies these roles. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb al-alamin subsumes many divine qualities, since each name or attribute governs what it describes. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (بعضهم): Some limit al-alamin to knowledgeable or morally responsible creatures, citing its use for those who receive warning and recognize signs. | mentions 25:1, 30:22 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Although some restrict worlds to accountable beings, Pharaoh's question is answered by describing the Lord of the worlds, not by defining the word worlds. | mentions 3:108, 26:23, 26:24 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Moses' reply lists qualities of the Lord rather than defining al-alamin, just as calling a house's lord a merchant does not define the house. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-awalim can include all beings, but al-alamin applies to rational beings or groups where rational creatures predominate, not to nonrational creatures alone. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Fatiha's concise Rabb al-alamin fits its focus on accountable creatures, whereas al-Jathiya explicitly names heaven and earth after repeatedly detailing their signs and God's rule over them. | mentions 45:3, 45:4, 45:13, 45:22, 45:27, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The differing formulations of Rabb al-alamin and Lord of heaven, earth, and worlds each fit the focus of their surah. | mentions 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God originates heaven and earth from nothing, creates by measuring and shaping, owns them and their contents, sustains and repairs them, and sends scripture to guide creation. | mentions 3:49 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): These openings jointly describe God as originator, shaper, owner, sustainer, and guide through revealed books; Rabb al-alamin encompasses all these qualities and worlds. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Rabb highlights that creation depends on God not only when it begins but also while it continues, a dependence disputed for things in their state of persistence. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): The next four opening praise formulas describe particular aspects contained in al-Fatiha's general Rabb al-alamin. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Anam's creation of heaven and earth, darkness, and light is one part of the wider lordship over all worlds in al-Fatiha. | mentions 6:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Kahf's revelation nurtures souls with knowledge, a particular form of the general nurture expressed by Rabb al-alamin. | mentions 18:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Saba's statement that all in heaven and earth belongs to God is one aspect of al-Fatiha's general praise of the Lord of the worlds. | mentions 34:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb al-alamin suits the request for guidance to the straight path because a nurturer's primary task is guidance, a frequent Qur'anic association. | mentions 20:49, 20:50, 20:122, 87:1, 87:2, 87:3, 6:161, 3:8, 18:24, 26:62, 37:99, 28:22 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Razi summarizes praise as God’s existence and choice, lordship as unity, mercy as compassion in both worlds, and Judgment Day as wisdom and mercy. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Jathiya repeatedly discusses God's lordship over the heavens, earth, creation, and worlds; its closing praise gathers these themes, unlike al-Fatiha's focus on the worlds and their accountable groups. | mentions 45:4, 45:27, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Using God's proper name means He deserves praise for His essence, not merely for one attribute such as power or creation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Rabb al-alamin is the apt continuation after praise: Rabb means owner, master, educator, benefactor, and caretaker, and encompasses God's attributes. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The author says al-alamin is suited to accountable and rational beings, unlike awalim, which can name classes of animals, insects, or inanimate things. | mentions 25:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The opening attributes span all time: praise before creation, at creation, through continuing mercy, and through the unending recompense of the afterlife, as 28:70 says. | mentions 28:70 STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet says that praising God, Lord of the worlds, constitutes thanks and brings increased bounty. STUDYQURAN (author): Rabb denotes master, arranger, and possessor; of God it means peerless Master who orders affairs and owns creation. STUDYQURAN (author): The point beneath bāʾ can symbolize the Divine Pen’s first drop of ink, which inscribed all archetypes on the Preserved Tablet before creation; thus basmalah marks both Quranic and cosmic beginnings. | mentions 68:1, 96:4, 85:22 TAB: Qatāda says every created kind is a world. TAB-FULL: Qatāda calls each class or kind a world. THALABI: A poetic usage of al-ʿAjjāj is noted without an explicit gloss. [154 notes: TABRISI(ابن عباس وسعيد بن جبير وقتادة,الأخفش); IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(بعض المفسرين,شيخنا); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أهل النظر); RAZI; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI(أبو سعيد الخدري,الجنيد,قتادة,مقاتل,وهب بن منبه); QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI; NASAFI; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN(ابن عباس,زيد بن علي وطائفة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(زيد بن علي وطائفة); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR(أبو سعيد الخدري,القرطبي,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,تبيع الحميري,سعيد بن المسيب,طائفة,كعب الأحبار,مروان بن محمد بن مروان بن الحكم,مقاتل,وهب بن منبه); NASHR(أبو زيد سعيد بن أوس الأنصاري,قتيبة عن الكسائي، كردم عن نافع، ورش); BIQAI; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي,مسعود التفتازاني); DURR(ابن عباس,تتبع الجهري,رسول الله,عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص,قتادة,وهب); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,الحكم بن عمير,تتبع الجهري,عائشة,علي بن أبي طالب,قتادة,مجاهد,وهب); ITQAN(ابن أبي جمرة,ابن عباس); ALUSI(الدواني,بعض ساداتنا الصوفية,زيد بن علي); ALUSI-FULL(الإمام,الإمام الأسيوطي,بعض ساداتنا الصوفية,زيد بن علي,كعب الأحبار); ELMALILI; QUTB-ZILAL; IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL; CORPUSCORANICUM; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي,بعضهم); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; STUDYQURAN(Prophet Muhammad); TAB(قتادة); TAB-FULL(قتادة); THALABI(العجاج); ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; ABUHAYYAN+; ALUSI-FULL+; IBNASHUR-FULL+; IBNASHUR+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT+]
- 1:2/q10/p3 The worlds are humans and jinn. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Another view limits the worlds to jinn and humans, based on the warning to the worlds. | mentions 25:1 IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن عباس ومجاهد ومقاتل): A third interpretation identifies the worlds as jinn and humans; it is reported from Ibn ʿAbbās and held by Mujāhid and Muqātil. QURTUBI, d. 671 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says the worlds are jinn and humans, citing the Qur’an’s description of the Prophet as a warner to all worlds, since he was not sent as a warner to animals. | mentions 25:1 QURTUBI, d. 671 (الربيع بن أنس عن أبي العالية): Al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas transmits from Abū al-ʿĀliya that jinn and humans are worlds and that each of earth’s four corners contains 1,500 worlds created to worship God. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya says al-ʿālamīn means the two classes of moral beings, humans and jinn. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya restricts al-ʿālamīn to the two responsible kinds, humans and jinn. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن عباس وسعيد بن جبير وعكرمة): Ibn Abbas, through Sa'id ibn Jubayr and Ikrimah, and other early authorities interpret the worlds as jinn and humans; Ibn Abi Hatim's chain is said to be unreliable. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (القرطبي): Al-Qurtubi supports the interpretation of the worlds as jinn and humans by citing the description of the Qur'an as a warning to the worlds. | mentions 25:1 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliyah says humans and jinn are worlds and reports uncertainty between fourteen and eighteen thousand other angelic worlds on earth, in groups worshipping God. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas identifies the worlds as jinn and humans. DURR, d. 911 (مجاهد): Mujahid likewise identifies the worlds as jinn and humans. DURR, d. 911 (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿid ibn Jubayr gives the same identification of the worlds as jinn and humans. DURR, d. 911 (أبو العالية): Abu al-ʿAliya counts humans and jinn as two worlds and says there are another eighteen thousand worlds of angels; each of earth's four corners has 3,500 worlds created to worship God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas, Mujahid, and Sa'id ibn Jubayr interpret “Lord of the worlds” as Lord of jinn and humans. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliya says humans and jinn are two worlds and there are eighteen thousand other worlds of angels, with 3,500 worshipping worlds at each of the earth's four corners. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Other reported views limit the worlds to jinn and humans, or to humans alone, citing the Qurʾānic uses in 25:1 and 26:165. | mentions 25:1, 26:165 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some commentators restrict the worlds to beings with knowledge: angels and the two accountable species, humans and jinn. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): One reported view limits the worlds to jinn and humans, appealing to the description of the Messenger as a warner to all worlds. | mentions 25:1 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Alamin can mean all beings, kinds, or communities; because judgment concerns humans and jinn, the context most suitably means their communities. | mentions 3:42, 25:1, 7:80 STUDYQURAN (author): Others restrict the worlds to humans and jinn because only these communities need a warner, as in 25:1. | mentions 25:1 MUQATIL: Muqātil reads the phrase as humans and jinn, citing the warning to all worlds in 25:1. TAB: Reports from Ibn ʿAbbās, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr and Mujāhid identify the worlds with humans and jinn, with communities as distinct worlds. TAB-FULL: Ibn ʿAbbās, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr and Mujāhid give the narrower gloss. TAB-FULL: Ibn Jurayj gives this gloss. IBNABIHATIM: Ibn ʿAbbās and Mujāhid give this gloss; a similar attribution to ʿAlī has an unreliable chain. - 1:2/q10/p4 The worlds are human beings. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): A further view limits the worlds to humans, based on the rebuke about approaching males among the worlds. | mentions 26:165 IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (author): In ordinary usage, calling someone the best of the worlds can mean the people of his own era; lines by Labīd and al-Ḥuṭayʾa illustrate this usage. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الحطيئة): Al-Ḥuṭayʾa's line uses al-ʿālamīn for people of the present age, as evidence for that usage. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Anatomists have identified nearly five thousand benefits and interests that God wisely arranged in the creation of the human body. RAZI, d. 606 (author): The same inquiry includes the wisdom in minerals, plants, animals, their kinds and states; God has made most of creation serve human benefit. | mentions 45:13 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī illustrates the scale of the inquiry through the human body: anatomists found nearly five thousand benefits, and known examples are only a drop beside the unknown; understanding divine wisdom in human creation entails ten thousand or more questions. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Considering divine wisdom in the throne, heavens, luminaries, elements, minerals, plants, and animals adds vast further questions; most of creation is made for human benefit, and al-hamd lillah thus encompasses roughly a million issues. | mentions 45:13 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The mind cannot contemplate God's essence: one cannot conceive His true nature, and the Prophet instead directs people to contemplate creation. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Muslims agree that God is praised for all blessings, including faith; since faith belongs to the created worlds, Qurtubi infers that faith is God’s act and creation, contrary to the Qadari claim that humans create it. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الحسين بن الفضل): Al-Ḥusayn ibn al-Faḍl says the worlds are the people of each era, citing the address about men among the worlds; al-ʿAjjāj and Jarīr also use ʿālam for human communities. | mentions 26:165 ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (أبو معاذ): Abū Muʿādh restricts al-ʿālamīn to the children of Adam. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الصادق): Al-Ṣādiq defines al-ʿālamīn as the people of Paradise and the Fire. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (البجلي): Al-Bajalī says al-ʿālamīn means people. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (البجلي): Al-Bajalī says al-ʿālamīn means people. ALUSI, d. 1270 (جعفر الصادق): Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq is reported to identify the worlds as humans; al-Ālūsī says the broader reading suits the context, while human beings are the focal recipients of law and prophecy. | mentions 25:1, 26:165 ALUSI, d. 1270 (كعب الأحبار): Kaʿb al-Aḥbār says no one can enumerate the worlds except God, who creates things people do not know and alone knows His hosts. | mentions 16:8, 74:31 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (جعفر الصادق): A view transmitted from Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq limits the worlds to humans; al-Alusi suggests humans are the central object of religious duties and messengers. | mentions 26:165 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Opening the Fatiha's descriptions with Lord of the worlds suits the Prophet's mission as a profound act of nurturing humanity and hints at compassion for all people. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Al-Fatiha begins in gratitude, places humans in the worlds under a hidden divine relation, renews the manifestation of mercy, and trains consciousness through truth and instruction until people recognize God and themselves. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Human conscience could not settle questions of the cosmos, the self, and life before it reached clear certainty about God, His attributes, and His relation to creation. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): To preserve the eschatological dimension, translate rabb al-ʿālamīn as Lord of the worlds, with people implied; translate it simply as people where that reference is explicit. CORPUSCORANICUM (Paret): Paret takes ʿālamīn primarily as people, translating the epithet as Lord of people throughout the world or Lord of the world’s inhabitants. | mentions 26:165, 29:6, 2:47, 2:122 CORPUSCORANICUM (author): Although ʿālamīn’s sound plural cannot mean worlds morphologically, it can designate a collective of people. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): This theological lexicon recasts the negatively charged early Meccan human as homo eschatologicus, belonging to both worlds under God’s rule. | mentions 103:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (بعضهم): Some say al-alamin means humans, supported by Qur'anic references to men among the worlds and Israel receiving a distinction over them. | mentions 26:165, 5:20 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Qur'anic uses show al-alamin can mean a generation or people of a particular time, including Israel, Mary, and the men addressed by Lot. | mentions 2:47, 3:42, 26:165, 15:70, 7:80 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb al-alamin also answers Jews who claim God is Lord only of Israel: He is Lord of all humanity and accountable creatures. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya details God's lordship over humans and other living beings through creation, subjection, sustenance, and resurrection. | mentions 45:4, 45:12, 45:13, 45:26 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-alamin may refer to all accountable generations or a subset, as in Lot's people; it fits al-Fatiha's concern with guidance and human moral groups and answers Jews who claimed God only as their Lord. | mentions 15:70 THALABI: Abū Muʿādh and al-Ḥusayn ibn al-Faḍl say humans, while Abū Haytham and Ibn ʿAbbās say jinn and humans, citing 25:1 and 26:165. THALABI: Abū Muʿādh and al-Ḥusayn ibn al-Faḍl say humans, while Abū Haytham and Ibn ʿAbbās say jinn and humans, citing 25:1 and 26:165. - 1:2/q10/p5 The worlds are humans, jinn, and angels. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): One view limits the worlds to rational beings: angels, jinn, and humans. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās first defines the worlds as all rational beings among humans, jinn, and angels. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Another view restricts al-ʿālamīn to rational beings—angels and the human and jinn races—with others included secondarily. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (أبو عبيدة والفراء): Abū ʿUbayda and al-Farrāʾ include humans, jinn, angels, and devils among al-ʿālamīn. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās is cited for two scopes of al-ʿālamīn: every living being, or humans, jinn, and angels. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو عبيدة والفراء): Abū ʿUbayda and al-Farrāʾ include humans, jinn, angels, and devils in al-ʿālamīn. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الراغب): Al-Raghib transmits Ibn Abbas's explanation that al-alamin means angels, jinn, and humans, and says the word was formed as an instrument of knowing the Creator. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الفراء وأبو عبيدة): Al-Farra and Abu Ubayda restrict the term worlds to rational beings—humans, jinn, angels, and devils—and exclude animals. DURR, d. 911 (الله): The Lord of the worlds is Lord of humans, jinn, angels, devils, all creation, and everything. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الله تعالى): The report explains “Lord of the worlds” as lord of humans, jinn, angels, devils, all creation, and everything. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some restrict the worlds to rational beings among angels, humans, and jinn; al-Suyūṭī associates this reading with ʿilm, while universal scope fits ʿalāma, sign. STUDYQURAN (author): Some take the worlds as four communities: humans, jinn, angels, and satans. KASHSHAF: The text distinguishes knowledgeable beings from a reported all-creatures view including bodies and accidents; plural embraces every genus. KASHSHAF: The text distinguishes knowledgeable beings from a reported all-creatures view including bodies and accidents; plural embraces every genus. KASHSHAF-FULL: The text gives accusative rabb and knowledgeable worlds versus all created bodies and accidents. KASHSHAF-FULL: The text gives accusative rabb and knowledgeable worlds versus all created bodies and accidents. - 1:2/q10/p6 The worlds are living or spiritual beings. — IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (ابن عباس): In another report, Ibn ʿAbbās says the worlds are every living being. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أبو صالح عن ابن عباس): Another report interprets the worlds as every living creature that moves on the surface of the earth. QURTUBI, d. 671 (زيد بن أسلم): Zayd ibn Aslam says the worlds are the beings who receive sustenance. QURTUBI, d. 671 (أبو عمرو بن العلاء): Abū ʿAmr ibn al-ʿAlāʾ says the worlds are spiritual beings; a similar Ibn ʿAbbās gloss includes every living creature moving on earth. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (عبد الرحمن بن زيد): ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd says al-ʿālamīn means those who receive provision. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (بعضهم): Another reported interpretation restricts al-ʿālamīn to spiritual beings. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (زيد بن أسلم وأبو عمرو بن العلاء): Zayd ibn Aslam and Abu Amr ibn al-Ala define worlds as everything with a spirit that receives provision. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): “Worlds” covers every category of physical and spiritual existence. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): All created things in the material and spiritual, visible and unseen worlds, in this life and the hereafter, are included in the worlds. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The pair of openings complement each other: Anam follows creation with light and darkness, while Fatir follows origination with the creation of spiritual messengers. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 [10 notes: IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(ابن عباس); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو صالح عن ابن عباس); QURTUBI(أبو عمرو بن العلاء,زيد بن أسلم); ABUHAYYAN(بعضهم,عبد الرحمن بن زيد); IBNKATHIR(زيد بن أسلم وأبو عمرو بن العلاء); ASAD-NOTES; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT]
- 1:2/q10/p7 The worlds are angels. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن عباس): A fifth interpretation, also transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās, says the worlds here are the angels. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi reads Lord of the worlds as an argument for the ancient God's existence; the created lights, darkness, and angels are kinds within that all-encompassing world-order. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise frames creation: the angels speak of it before Adam is created, and the inhabitants of Paradise end their invocation with it. | mentions 2:30, 10:10 DURR, d. 911 (أبي بن كعب): Ubayy ibn Kaʿb identifies the worlds as angels, numbering eighteen thousand; four regions each have four or five hundred angels, each with innumerable helpers. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبي كعب): Ubayy ibn Kab identifies the worlds as angels, numbering eighteen thousand; he describes groups in four regions with countless attendants. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Fatir's origination differs from Anam's creation by measure, yet both are aspects of Rabb al-alamin; Anam then mentions darkness and light, while Fatir mentions angels as messengers. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 THALABI: Ubayy's report enumerates angelic groups and hosts. [7 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس); RAZI-FULL; DURR(أبي بن كعب); DURR-FULL(أبي كعب); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي); THALABI(أبي بن كعب)]
- 1:2/q10/p8 The worlds are four rational communities. — BAGHAWI: Abū ʿUbayd excludes beasts. BAGHAWI-FULL: Abū ʿUbayda excludes beasts. [2 notes: BAGHAWI(أبو عبيد); BAGHAWI-FULL(أبو عبيدة)]

## 1:2/q11 How is al-ʿālamīn formed and derived?  [ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ · grammar]
turns on: Whether ʿālam is a sign, a means of knowledge, or a collective; how its sound plural is justified.
- 1:2/q11/p1 ʿĀlam derives from a sign pointing to the Creator. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj derives ʿālam from knowledge or a sign because the world points to its maker; ʿālamīn encompasses all beings besides God, including human and jinn realms. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): In al-ʿālamīn, yā is both the plural and case marker, while nūn replaces the singular's case vowel and is opened to distinguish it from the dual. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Al-ʿālamīn has no singular of the same form and may derive from the mark by which creation points to its Maker, or from knowledge as what is known. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (بعض المفسرين): Some derive ʿālam from ʿalāma, a sign pointing to its Creator; that derivation supports taking it for all created existence. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أهل النظر): The derivation of ʿālam from ʿalāma, “sign,” supports the speculative theologians' view: the world is named as a sign pointing to its Creator. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The sequence assigns praise to God, then names Him Lord of the worlds: Allah signifies necessary perfection, while Lord of the worlds shows that all else flows from His generosity, making Him beyond completeness. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): “Lord of the worlds” signifies that God is one, owns all worlds, and is the only deity and object of worship. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Raising the hands signals leaving both worlds and directing all inner faculties toward God; “God is greatest” means He exceeds all beings, knowledge, comparison, and even the claim that He is simply greater. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الزجاج والخليل): Al-Zajjāj derives ʿālam from knowledge or a sign: it is everything God created in this life and the next and it points to its maker and planner; al-Khalīl likewise says a sign or marker indicates a thing. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): ʿĀlam means what makes something known, like a signet or mold; it came to denote all besides God, substances and accidents, whose contingency indicates the necessary Creator. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān prefers that al-ʿālamīn denote morally responsible beings, supported by the verse about signs for the knowledgeable; Ḥafṣ's kasra on the lām clarifies the sense. | mentions 30:22 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir says ʿālam derives from ʿalāmah, a sign, because every created thing points to its Maker, Creator, and oneness; he cites a poem by Ibn al-Mu'tazz. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some restrict the worlds to rational beings among angels, humans, and jinn; al-Suyūṭī associates this reading with ʿilm, while universal scope fits ʿalāma, sign. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Although ʿālamīn is a sound masculine plural, it can cover nonrational beings if the word is restricted to rational creatures, if rational beings predominate, or if the others are treated as signs of knowledge. | mentions 41:11, 12:4 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Some say al-ʿālamīn is a plural restricted to rational beings; replies derive it from rational ʿālam after restriction, from grammatical predominance, or by treating knowledgeable signs as rational. | mentions 41:11, 12:4 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الإمام الأسيوطي): Al-Suyuti derives the term from knowledge when the worlds are understood generally, and from a sign when the term is taken in its broader sense. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The masculine sound plural can denote paucity despite the many worlds, underscoring that all are insignificant beside God's majesty; Arabic usage can also allow either number. | mentions 39:67 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sign in al-alamin, later clarified by al-Baqara’s description of the Qur’an as guidance for the Godfearing, signals the wisdom of revelation. | mentions 2:2 CORPUSCORANICUM (author): Although ʿālamīn’s sound plural cannot mean worlds morphologically, it can designate a collective of people. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (بعضهم): Some limit al-alamin to knowledgeable or morally responsible creatures, citing its use for those who receive warning and recognize signs. | mentions 25:1, 30:22 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Fatiha's concise Rabb al-alamin fits its focus on accountable creatures, whereas al-Jathiya explicitly names heaven and earth after repeatedly detailing their signs and God's rule over them. | mentions 45:3, 45:4, 45:13, 45:22, 45:27, 45:36 [21 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(الزجاج); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(بعض المفسرين); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أهل النظر); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI(الزجاج والخليل); BAYDAWI; IBNKATHIR; ALUSI(بعضهم); ALUSI-FULL(الإمام الأسيوطي); ELMALILI; CORPUSCORANICUM; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(بعضهم); ABUHAYYAN+]
- 1:2/q11/p2 The plural endings mark case and number. — TABRISI, d. 548 (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash says rabb al-ʿālamīn is genitive as an adjective, with the adjectival relation itself governing the adjective, as shown by adjectives whose case differs from their head. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The phrase al-ḥamdu lillāh raises questions about God’s existence and right to praise; Rabb al-ʿālamīn answers the first, while al-Raḥmān, al-Raḥīm, and Mālik yawm al-dīn answer the second. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الربيع بن أنس عن أبي العالية): Al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas transmits from Abū al-ʿĀliya that jinn and humans are worlds and that each of earth’s four corners contains 1,500 worlds created to worship God. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya says al-ʿālamīn means the two classes of moral beings, humans and jinn. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya restricts al-ʿālamīn to the two responsible kinds, humans and jinn. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Although al-ʿālamīn is an irregular plural, Abū Ḥayyān favors the responsible creatures because a cited verse and Ḥafṣ's lām-kasra reading support that scope. | mentions 29:44 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliyah says humans and jinn are worlds and reports uncertainty between fourteen and eighteen thousand other angelic worlds on earth, in groups worshipping God. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (تبيع الحميري): Tubyay al-Himyari says the worlds comprise one thousand nations, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سعيد بن المسيب): A similar count of one thousand worlds, divided into six hundred sea and four hundred land worlds, is also attributed to Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سعيد بن المسيب): Al-Baghawi reports Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab's view that God has one thousand worlds, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land. NASHR, d. 833 (قتيبة عن الكسائي، كردم عن نافع، ورش): The author adds unlisted transmissions: Qutayba from al-Kisa'i has imāla in al-ʿālamīn and al-raḥmān; Kurram from Nafi and al-Ahwazi from Warsh lengthen vowels in mālik, naʿbudu, and iyyāka. DURR, d. 911 (أبو العالية): Abu al-ʿAliya counts humans and jinn as two worlds and says there are another eighteen thousand worlds of angels; each of earth's four corners has 3,500 worlds created to worship God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliya says humans and jinn are two worlds and there are eighteen thousand other worlds of angels, with 3,500 worshipping worlds at each of the earth's four corners. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Al-alamin is the plural of alam; few nouns on fa'il take this plural, with yasm (jasmine) the other example, whose plurals occur in al-A'sha's verse. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Jathiya repeatedly discusses God's lordship over the heavens, earth, creation, and worlds; its closing praise gathers these themes, unlike al-Fatiha's focus on the worlds and their accountable groups. | mentions 45:4, 45:27, 45:36 AKHFASH: Al-Akhfash explains wāw/yāʾ cases, the added open nūn and its loss in annexation; he contrasts the dual and broken plurals and cites 5:23 and 36:14. AKHFASH: Al-Akhfash explains wāw/yāʾ cases, the added open nūn and its loss in annexation; he contrasts the dual and broken plurals and cites 5:23 and 36:14. AKHFASH: Al-Akhfash explains wāw/yāʾ cases, the added open nūn and its loss in annexation; he contrasts the dual and broken plurals and cites 5:23 and 36:14. AKHFASH: Al-Akhfash explains wāw/yāʾ cases, the added open nūn and its loss in annexation; he contrasts the dual and broken plurals and cites 5:23 and 36:14. AKHFASH: Al-Akhfash explains wāw/yāʾ cases, the added open nūn and its loss in annexation; he contrasts the dual and broken plurals and cites 5:23 and 36:14. NAHHAS: Sībawayh, Abū al-ʿAbbās and Abū Isḥāq disagree whether the nūn replaces case vowel, tanwīn, or both; the fatḥa distinguishes dual. NAHHAS: Sībawayh, Abū al-ʿAbbās and Abū Isḥāq disagree whether the nūn replaces case vowel, tanwīn, or both; the fatḥa distinguishes dual. NAHHAS: Sībawayh, Abū al-ʿAbbās and Abū Isḥāq disagree whether the nūn replaces case vowel, tanwīn, or both; the fatḥa distinguishes dual. NAHHAS: Sībawayh, Abū al-ʿAbbās and Abū Isḥāq disagree whether the nūn replaces case vowel, tanwīn, or both; the fatḥa distinguishes dual. NAHHAS: Sībawayh, Abū al-ʿAbbās and Abū Isḥāq disagree whether the nūn replaces case vowel, tanwīn, or both; the fatḥa distinguishes dual. Al-Zajjāj reports Sibawayh’s explanation that the plural nūn has fatḥa to distinguish it from the dual nūn with kasra. Al-Zajjāj adds that fatḥa also resolves two adjacent still letters, since kasra after wāw or yāʾ is heavy, and cites sawfa and ayna Zayd as analogies. [28 notes: TABRISI(الأخفش); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI(الربيع بن أنس عن أبي العالية); ABUHAYYAN(ابن عطية); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عطية); IBNKATHIR(أبو العالية,تبيع الحميري,سعيد بن المسيب); NASHR(قتيبة عن الكسائي، كردم عن نافع، ورش); DURR(أبو العالية); DURR-FULL(أبو العالية); IBNASHUR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; AKHFASH; NAHHAS(أبو إسحاق,أبو العباس,سيبويه); ZAJJAJ(سيبويه); ABUHAYYAN-FULL+]
- 1:2/q11/p3 ʿĀlam relates to knowledge. — IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (بعض المفسرين): Another derivation connects ʿālam to knowledge, supporting Ibn ʿAbbās’s interpretation. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The worlds comprise all substances and accidents, whose possibility and need for a necessary cause show God's existence; the plural gathers different genera, with rational beings predominant in its form. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): The plural ending -in is used although normally associated with rational beings or their equivalent because ‘alamin has an adjectival sense, indicating knowledge. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Al-ʿālamīn denotes what makes the Creator known—bodies, substances, and accidents—or every existent apart from God; its sound masculine plural reflects a descriptive quality associated with rational beings. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): God is Lord of the worlds as origin, preserver, and manager of all; worlds means everything known to God, and the sound masculine plural reflects encompassing knowledge or predominance. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Al-alamin is an annexed sound masculine plural in form but an ism-jam because its singular differs; it cannot be plural of alam if that covers all non-divine beings while al-alamin means rational beings only. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Sibawayh similarly says a'rab is not plural of arab because the former is narrower; an objection that al-alamin could mean only rational alam is answered by comparison with the unavailable form shay'un. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some commentators restrict the worlds to beings with knowledge: angels and the two accountable species, humans and jinn. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sound plural al-alamin foregrounds rational beings; a concise Turkish rendering is difficult, but it means God alone is Lord of all realms and especially their rational inhabitants. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sound rational plural and the definite article extend the meaning across all realms and foreground rational beings; understanding the Lord of the worlds requires conceiving rational agents. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The plural uses the rational masculine form even though some worlds are nonrational, by giving precedence to rational beings. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): This plural suits al-Fatiha because its worship, aid, guidance, and moral categories address accountable creatures; Rabb al-alamin may mean their Lord or all creation with rational beings predominant. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Kahf's revelation nurtures souls with knowledge, a particular form of the general nurture expressed by Rabb al-alamin. | mentions 18:1 [13 notes: IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(بعض المفسرين); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI; NASAFI-FULL; QASHANI; SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL(بعضهم); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي)]
- 1:2/q11/p4 ʿĀlamīn is a collective plural. — QURTUBI, d. 671 (قتادة): Qatāda says al-ʿālamīn is the plural of ʿālam and means every existing thing other than God, with no singular on this pattern, like rahṭ or qawm. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The article in al-ʿālamīn is exhaustive; ʿālam is an irregular collective and its sound masculine plural is even more irregular because it violates some conditions of that pattern. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The suggestion that ʿālamīn is a unique collective noun on the pattern of sound plurals does not settle its form; the author distinguishes ordinary plurals, collective generics, and collective nouns. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Another debate asks whether al-ʿālamīn is a regular plural, a collective noun, or a name for a totality; al-Ālūsī concludes it is a true plural because each member is itself called an ʿālam. TAB: Al-Ṭabarī calls ʿālam a collective class or generation, comparing anām, rahṭ and jaysh and citing al-ʿAjjāj. TAB: Al-Ṭabarī calls ʿālam a collective class or generation, comparing anām, rahṭ and jaysh and citing al-ʿAjjāj. TAB-FULL: Each class and generation forms a world; al-ʿAjjāj's line illustrates. TAB-FULL: Each class and generation forms a world; al-ʿAjjāj's line illustrates. THALABI: It has no singular of the same form. MAWARDI-FULL: The text compares rahṭ and qawm and cites al-ʿAjjāj. MAWARDI-FULL: The text compares rahṭ and qawm and cites al-ʿAjjāj. Al-Zajjāj says al-ʿālamīn has no singular of its own form because it gathers unlike kinds, whereas a single ʿālam gathers members of one kind. [12 notes: QURTUBI(قتادة); ABUHAYYAN; ALUSI; ALUSI-FULL; TAB; TAB-FULL; THALABI; MAWARDI-FULL(العجاج); ZAJJAJ]
- 1:2/q11/p5 ʿĀlamīn is the plural of ʿālam. — TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن عباس وسعيد بن جبير وقتادة): Most commentators take each species and each generation of a species as a world, so al-ʿālamīn is plural across kinds and times. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أهل العربية): For Arab grammarians, ʿālamīn is the plural of ʿālam and names all created beings from beginning to end; people also call their present age a world. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Al-ʿālamīn is the plural of ʿālam, encompassing every created being besides God; created kinds in heavens, earth, land, and sea are worlds, as is each generation. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Although the sound masculine plural often denotes a small number, the form here hints that all created worlds remain few beside God’s greatness; the plural can also denote many. | mentions 39:67 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The definite plural makes the scope of divine lordship explicit across every created kind; singular al-ʿālam might be mistaken for the familiar perceptible world alone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The plural al-ʿālamīn guarantees inclusion of every created kind: a singular definite noun could be heard as the familiar perceptible world, whereas the plural distributes lordship across all named kinds and their individuals. MAJAZ: Abū ʿUbayda takes the worlds as created beings and cites Labīd and al-ʿAjjāj for the singular. MAJAZ: Abū ʿUbayda takes the worlds as created beings and cites Labīd and al-ʿAjjāj for the singular. Al-Zajjāj explicitly calls al-ʿālamīn the plural of ʿālam, distinguishing its gathered kinds from the members grouped in one ʿālam. [9 notes: TABRISI(ابن عباس وسعيد بن جبير وقتادة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أهل العربية); IBNKATHIR; ALUSI; ALUSI-FULL; MAJAZ; ZAJJAJ]

## 1:2/q12 How many worlds are reported?  [ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ · reports]
turns on: The reports give distinct counts and some hold that only God knows the number.
- 1:2/q12/p1 A report counts eighteen thousand worlds. — QURTUBI, d. 671 (وهب بن منبه): Wahb ibn Munabbih says God has eighteen thousand worlds, of which this world is one. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (وهب بن منبه): Wahb ibn Munabbih says God has eighteen thousand worlds and this world is one of them. DURR, d. 911 (أبو العالية): Abu al-ʿAliya counts humans and jinn as two worlds and says there are another eighteen thousand worlds of angels; each of earth's four corners has 3,500 worlds created to worship God. DURR, d. 911 (وهب): Wahb says God has eighteen thousand worlds, of which this world is only one. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliya says humans and jinn are two worlds and there are eighteen thousand other worlds of angels, with 3,500 worshipping worlds at each of the earth's four corners. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (وهب): Wahb says God has eighteen thousand worlds, of which this world is only one. DURR, d. 911 (أبي بن كعب): Ubayy ibn Kaʿb identifies the worlds as angels, numbering eighteen thousand; four regions each have four or five hundred angels, each with innumerable helpers. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبي كعب): Ubayy ibn Kab identifies the worlds as angels, numbering eighteen thousand; he describes groups in four regions with countless attendants. THALABI: Wahb likens the inhabited portion to a tent in a desert. BAGHAWI: Wahb says the known world is only one. BAGHAWI-FULL: Wahb's tent-in-desert image emphasizes the known world's smallness. [11 notes: QURTUBI(وهب بن منبه); IBNKATHIR(وهب بن منبه); DURR(أبو العالية,أبي بن كعب,وهب); DURR-FULL(أبو العالية,أبي كعب,وهب); THALABI(وهب); BAGHAWI(وهب); BAGHAWI-FULL(وهب)]
- 1:2/q12/p2 A report counts one thousand worlds or communities. — IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (تبيع الحميري): Tubyay al-Himyari says the worlds comprise one thousand nations, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سعيد بن المسيب): A similar count of one thousand worlds, divided into six hundred sea and four hundred land worlds, is also attributed to Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سعيد بن المسيب): Al-Baghawi reports Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab's view that God has one thousand worlds, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A weak report from Jabir recounts Umar's search for locusts and a prophetic saying that God made a thousand communities, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land; locusts are the first to perish, after which the others follow like a broken necklace. DURR, d. 911 (تتبع الجهري): Al-Tatabbuʿ al-Jahri says the worlds are a thousand communities, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A weak report says God created a thousand communities, 600 in the sea and 400 on land; locusts are the first to perish, followed by the rest like beads when their string breaks. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (تتبع الجهري): A report counts the worlds as a thousand communities, 600 in the sea and 400 on land. IBNABIHATIM: Tabīʿ divides the worlds into six hundred sea and four hundred land communities. THALABI: Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib gives six hundred sea and four hundred land worlds. BAGHAWI: Six hundred are sea and four hundred land. BAGHAWI-FULL: Saʿīd gives 600 sea and 400 land. [11 notes: IBNKATHIR(تبيع الحميري,سعيد بن المسيب); DURR(تتبع الجهري,رسول الله); DURR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,تتبع الجهري); IBNABIHATIM(تبيع); THALABI(سعيد بن المسيب); BAGHAWI(سعيد بن المسيب); BAGHAWI-FULL(سعيد بن المسيب)]
- 1:2/q12/p3 Only God can know or count the worlds. — RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi says there is no proof that bodies are limited to the familiar cosmic categories: since an infinite void lies beyond the world and God can do all possible things, He can create countless external worlds, each larger than this one and containing analogous heavens, earths and celestial bodies. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī says there is no proof that only the familiar kinds of bodies exist: an infinite void lies beyond this world, and God can create innumerable greater worlds containing counterparts of its cosmic features; philosophers’ arguments for one world rely on weak premises. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Alusi prefers the universal sense because the worlds are innumerable and God's nurturing encompasses every existing thing, both celestial and earthly. | mentions 16:8, 74:31 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (كعب الأحبار): Kaʿb al-Aḥbār says only God can count the number of worlds. | mentions 16:8, 74:31 THALABI: Muqātil says fully explaining the worlds would require a thousand volumes of a thousand pages. THALABI: Kaʿb says only God can count the worlds, citing 74:31. BAGHAWI: Kaʿb says only God knows the worlds' number, citing 74:31. BAGHAWI-FULL: Kaʿb says only God knows the number of worlds and His hosts. [8 notes: RAZI; RAZI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(كعب الأحبار); THALABI(كعب الأحبار,مقاتل بن سليمان); BAGHAWI(كعب الأحبار); BAGHAWI-FULL(كعب الأحبار); ALUSI-FULL+]
- 1:2/q12/p4 A report is uncertain between fourteen and eighteen thousand angelic worlds. — IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliyah says humans and jinn are worlds and reports uncertainty between fourteen and eighteen thousand other angelic worlds on earth, in groups worshipping God. TAB: Abū al-ʿĀliya is unsure of the count and locates angelic worshippers at earth's corners. TAB-FULL: Abū al-ʿĀliya is uncertain between the numbers. IBNABIHATIM: Abū al-ʿĀliya gives an uncertain count of angelic worlds on earth. [4 notes: IBNKATHIR(أبو العالية); TAB(أبو العالية); TAB-FULL(أبو العالية); IBNABIHATIM(أبو العالية); IBNKATHIR-]
- 1:2/q12/p5 A report counts eighty thousand worlds. — IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (مقاتل): Muqatil says there are eighty thousand worlds. THALABI: Muqātil ibn Ḥayyān divides them equally between land and sea. BAGHAWI: Muqātil divides them evenly between sea and land. BAGHAWI-FULL: Muqātil splits sea and land equally. [4 notes: IBNKATHIR(مقاتل); THALABI(مقاتل بن حيان); BAGHAWI(مقاتل بن حيان); BAGHAWI-FULL(مقاتل بن حيان)]
- 1:2/q12/p6 A report counts forty thousand worlds. — QURTUBI, d. 671 (أبو سعيد الخدري): Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī says God has forty thousand worlds, with the inhabited world from east to west counting as one. QURTUBI, d. 671 (مقاتل): Muqātil says there are eighty thousand worlds, forty thousand on land and forty thousand at sea. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (أبو سعيد الخدري): Al-Qurtubi reports from Abu Sa'id al-Khudri that God has forty thousand worlds and the inhabited east-to-west world is only one. THALABI: Abū Saʿīd says the known world is only one. [4 notes: QURTUBI(أبو سعيد الخدري,مقاتل); IBNKATHIR(أبو سعيد الخدري); THALABI(أبو سعيد الخدري)]
- 1:2/q12/p7 A report counts seventeen thousand worlds. — IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (مروان بن محمد بن مروان بن الحكم): Ibn Asakir reports Marwan's claim that God created seventeen thousand worlds, with the inhabitants of the heavens and earth forming one and God alone knowing the rest. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR(مروان بن محمد بن مروان بن الحكم)]
- 1:2/q12/p8 A report counts 420 worlds. — THALABI: Al-Ḍaḥḥāk divides them into 360 unclothed and sixty clothed worlds. [1 notes: THALABI(الضحاك)]

## 1:2/q13 What does the verse teach about God and human acts?  [* · theology]
turns on: The notes contest created acts, the reach of divine sustaining, and the ground of gratitude.
- 1:2/q13/p1 All created things depend on God. — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj derives ʿālam from knowledge or a sign because the world points to its maker; ʿālamīn encompasses all beings besides God, including human and jinn realms. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): Rabb can mean the worshipped deity, master or owner, the one who manages and repairs affairs, or king; its absolute, unrestricted sense as Lord of lords belongs to God alone. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (غاوي بن عبد العزى): Ghawy ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzza's line uses Rabb for the one worshipped; Rabb of all in the unrestricted sense is God. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (بعض الناس): Some distinguish thanks as praise for God's acts and favors, and praise as praise of his attributes; the author finds this more accurate than synonymy. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): Al-Tabari's evidence “praise to God, in thanks” actually limits praise to praise for a favor and therefore argues against synonymy. TABRISI, d. 548 (الأخفش وأبو علي الفارسي): They parse al-ḥamdu as a nominative subject with an omitted predication such as is due or is established for God; the phrase lillah supplies its meaning. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The nominative reading is stronger and more laudatory than an accusative meaning I praise, because it makes praise due to God from all creation, not only the speaker. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The transmitted broken and rounded vowel combinations arise by assimilation between the two words; many grammarians reject the assimilation because case vowels are not fixed. TABRISI, d. 548 (سيبويه): The lām normally has fatḥa because it cannot begin with sukun; lām of possession and emphasis are distinguished, and attached pronouns restore its original opening vowel. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn may be accusative as praise or as an implied command to remember the Lord; a nominative praise reading is also possible, as poetry shows. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Another account says the plural nūn is opened because kasra after waw or yā is heavy, like in sawfa and ayna. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): All beautiful praise belongs to God, who deserves worship as the source of original blessings and the creator, nurturer, and reformer of creation; the verse teaches gratitude and praise. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Rabb also means caretaker, cultivator, or reformer; it can be restricted in other compounds, though used absolutely it is reserved for God. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Al-ʿālamīn has no singular of the same form and may derive from the mark by which creation points to its Maker, or from knowledge as what is known. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): In ordinary usage the worlds can mean all created things, supported by Pharaoh's question about the Lord of the worlds and God's answer. | mentions 26:23, 26:24 IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (شيخنا): The speculative theologians use ʿālam for the crea ted universe as a whole: the celestial sphere, heavens, earth, and what lies between; al-Zajjāj is said to hold this meaning. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (بعض المفسرين): Some derive ʿālam from ʿalāma, a sign pointing to its Creator; that derivation supports taking it for all created existence. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (بعض المفسرين): One of six Qur’anic senses is every living being, as in the opening praise of God. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Al-ḥamd is nominative as the subject and lillāh its predicate: praise is established and belongs to God. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أهل العربية): For Arab grammarians, ʿālamīn is the plural of ʿālam and names all created beings from beginning to end; people also call their present age a world. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أهل النظر): The speculative theologians use ʿālam for the created cosmos as a whole: the celestial sphere, heavens, earth, and what lies between. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (قيل): Another grammatical account takes the wording as a declarative form whose intended force is a command: say, “Praise belongs to God.” IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Rabb means owner; for a created being the title is used only in a construct phrase such as owner of the house or servant. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أهل النظر): The derivation of ʿālam from ʿalāma, “sign,” supports the speculative theologians' view: the world is named as a sign pointing to its Creator. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الضحاك عن ابن عباس): One reported interpretation takes “the worlds” to mean all creation: heavens and earths, what they contain, and what lies between them. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi sketches the sūra’s sequence: seek refuge from sins, begin good works in God’s name, praise and recognize his lordship and mercy, heed judgment, worship and seek help, ask guidance, and avoid ignorance and error. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Praise is praise for a blessing, and praise for a blessing requires knowing it; God’s blessings cannot be counted, as the Qur’an says. | mentions 14:34, 16:18 RAZI, d. 606 (author): One cannot know God as Lord of the worlds without first understanding what “Lord” and “the worlds” mean. RAZI, d. 606 (author): The worlds comprise every existent other than God: spatial bodies, incorporeal entities, and attributes; bodies divide into simple entities and composites. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi says there is no proof that bodies are limited to the familiar cosmic categories: since an infinite void lies beyond the world and God can do all possible things, He can create countless external worlds, each larger than this one and containing analogous heavens, earths and celestial bodies. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi rejects philosophers’ arguments that there is only one world as weak and based on unsound premises; he cites Abū al-ʿAlāʾ al-Maʿarrī’s verses about God’s many heavens and the smallness of our past and future. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi links the inexhaustibility of created wonders and divine wisdom to the Qur’anic image of earthly trees as pens and seas as ink, which would not exhaust God’s words. | mentions 31:27 RAZI, d. 606 (author): Anatomists have identified nearly five thousand benefits and interests that God wisely arranged in the creation of the human body. RAZI, d. 606 (author): Adding the wisdom evident in the Throne, Footstool, heavens, fixed and moving stars, and the varied measures and colors of created things expands the inquiry to roughly a million questions. RAZI, d. 606 (author): The same inquiry includes the wisdom in minerals, plants, animals, their kinds and states; God has made most of creation serve human benefit. | mentions 45:13 RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi links the statement that God subjected what is in the heavens and earth for people to the vast created benefits contained in praise. | mentions 45:13 RAZI, d. 606 (author): Because all these innumerable blessings and their wisdom fall under praise to God, the phrase “Praise belongs to God” encompasses about a million questions, more or less. RAZI, d. 606 (author): “Lord” is in a genitive construction with “the worlds” as its genitive complement; understanding this relation requires knowing both terms. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Many names indicate the honor of the named; Fātiḥat al-Kitāb is so called because it opens codices, teaching, recitation, and prayer, or because praise opens speech, or because it was the first sura sent down. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): It is called Sūrat al-Ḥamd because its opening word is praise. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): It is called the Mother of the Qur’an because its verses encompass the Qur’an’s four central aims: theology, resurrection, prophethood, and divine decree. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A third reason is that all knowledge concerns either divine lordship or human servitude: the opening praises God’s mastery, while the remainder shows the servant’s dependence on His aid and guidance. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A fourth reason is that the sura gathers theology, religious duties, and spiritual purification: praise and lordship establish divine existence and attributes, worship presents duties, and guidance seeks unveiling. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The phrase al-ḥamdu lillāh raises questions about God’s existence and right to praise; Rabb al-ʿālamīn answers the first, while al-Raḥmān, al-Raḥīm, and Mālik yawm al-dīn answer the second. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi reads Lord of the worlds as an argument for the ancient God's existence; the created lights, darkness, and angels are kinds within that all-encompassing world-order. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Deserving praise shows God acts by choice, not by necessity: fire and ice are not praised for natural effects, while changing effects prove a voluntary, powerful agent. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): As the Mother of the Qur’an, the Fātiḥa is the source from which other suras branch; every being besides God is evidence of His divinity. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Saba’s statement that everything in the heavens and earth belongs to God is another subset within the universal lordship named in the Fātiḥa. | mentions 34:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Fāṭir describes bringing the heavens and earth into existence, distinct from creation as determination yet also included within Rabb al-ʿālamīn. | mentions 35:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The sequence Lord of the worlds, Merciful, Compassionate, Master of Judgment is, in Razi's view, a rationally unsurpassable ordering of God's attributes.
