RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): All worlds share the contingency, need, and change that make a person depend on God; their common cause of need establishes God's lordship over every creature and heaven. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The one able to create the immense worlds, Throne, heavens, and stars can destroy them and is independent of them. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Changing states arise after nonexistence and therefore point to a capable creator; a necessary-by-nature cause would produce effects eternally without change. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since beneficial ease and safety predominate, the agent is merciful and beneficent and deserves praise; these familiar facts make the reason for praising God apparent to every rational person. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because worldly benefits and protection from harm usually require visible causes, people love those causes, and repeated action and imitation make this attachment deeply rooted. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Recognizing the true cause detaches the heart from apparent means: every benefit comes from God and every harm is attributed to Him, so praise and requests belong to God alone. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Reflection scales from the small world to the greater one: no condition in either world is ordered except by God's decree. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The prayer's two halves arise through the verses of al-Fatiha; since prayer entails this division and it entails this surah, al-Fatiha is necessary for valid prayer. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If the hadith must be figurative, invalidity is nearer than mere incompleteness because a nonexistent act resembles an invalid act more than a valid but imperfect one; presumption, prohibition, and caution reinforce this reading. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because the established form of the Prophet's prayer included al-Fatiha, the command to establish prayer requires it; the command recurs over a hundred times in the Quran. | mentions 2:43 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet identifies al-Fatiha as a surah unmatched in the Torah, Gospel, or Psalms; the Companions' answer about what they recite in prayer indicates a known universal practice. [RAZI-FULL:v1p158#2/ r2] RAZI-FULL, d. 606 · author | holds | Razi argues that Quran 73:20 requires al-Fatiha: the options are al-Fatiha, another surah, or a choice; the latter two fail because another is not universally required and al-Fatiha is agreed better and more complete. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Fatiha is the recitation made accessible to all because every Muslim has it memorized, whereas other surahs may not be memorized by each person. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi compares Abu Hanifa's ruling that an Eid fast cannot make up Ramadan because it is incomplete and asks why the same reasoning does not invalidate prayer without al-Fatiha. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Although belief in obligation may itself leave theoretical doubt, actual recitation creates no danger while omission creates fear of failing the duty; therefore action is the safer course. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If prayers with and without al-Fatiha were both valid, habitual recitation would make other surahs abandoned; because the community agrees al-Fatiha is preferable, Razi concludes omission is invalid. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): In the divided-prayer report, God calls the basmala glorification, al-Hamd praise, the two mercy names extolling, the Master of Judgment entrustment, and the remaining response a sharing and a request granted. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī says there is no proof that only the familiar kinds of bodies exist: an infinite void lies beyond this world, and God can create innumerable greater worlds containing counterparts of its cosmic features; philosophers’ arguments for one world rely on weak premises. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise is praise for blessing, and praising a blessing presupposes knowing it; God’s blessings cannot be counted, as the cited verse says. | mentions 14:34 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī illustrates the scale of the inquiry through the human body: anatomists found nearly five thousand benefits, and known examples are only a drop beside the unknown; understanding divine wisdom in human creation entails ten thousand or more questions. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Considering divine wisdom in the throne, heavens, luminaries, elements, minerals, plants, and animals adds vast further questions; most of creation is made for human benefit, and al-hamd lillah thus encompasses roughly a million issues. | mentions 45:13 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ʿālamīn means every existent other than God; these beings comprise extended bodies, separate entities, and attributes, with bodies divided into simple and composite kinds. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Saying al-hamdu lillah rather than al-madhu lillah acknowledges God as a freely choosing agent, not a necessary emanation as philosophers claim. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-hamdu lillah is better than ash-shukru lillah because it praises God for every benefit to the worlds, even one not received by the speaker; shukr is personal gratitude. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (المخالف): One reported distinction says hamd thanks God for warding off calamity, while shukr thanks Him for gifts; an objection asks why the lesser benefit is named first. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The formula says al-hamdu lillah rather than ahmadu Allah so it declares God worthy of praise before any creature praises Him, from eternity to eternity. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-hamdu lillah also means praise and thanks are God’s due and property, because His abundant favors make Him inherently deserving of them. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The declarative al-ḥamdu lillāh is preferable to ahmadu Allāh: it establishes praise as God’s right instead of reporting one speaker’s act. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The lām in lillāh can signify fitting اختصاص, ownership, or power and dominion; each makes all praise belong to God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): On either grammatical analysis, the phrase denies that anyone besides God is independently entitled to praise. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (Prophet): The Prophet is cited as saying that whoever does not thank people does not thank God; al-Rāzī answers that God is the true source of every human benefit. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because full gratitude is beyond human capacity, the verse states that all praise belongs to God rather than commanding people to praise Him. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A human benefactor acts only because God creates the motive, the blessing, and the recipient’s capacity to benefit, so God is the true benefactor. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A giver who seeks reward, praise, repayment, or release from stinginess seeks compensation and is not the true benefactor; God gives from intrinsic fullness. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Every blessing is a possible existent brought into being by God, directly or through means; therefore praise for blessing belongs to God. | mentions 16:53 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A complete blessing requires benefit, freedom from harm, and security from loss, and only God supplies all three conditions. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): People cannot fully thank God because they cannot enumerate His countless blessings, as the Qur’an says. | mentions 14:34 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Human conceptions of perfection never reach God’s greater perfection, so people cannot praise Him as He deserves. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الواسطي): Al-Wāsiṭī calls gratitude shirk when a person imagines that his thanks can equal God’s blessings. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Existence is better than nonexistence, and every possible existent receives its existence from God; hence every creature receives His blessing and mercy. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (داود): David asks how he can thank God when even the ability to thank depends on God’s gift; God says recognizing that incapacity is gratitude according to one’s capacity. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (Prophet): A report says that when a blessed servant says al-ḥamdu lillāh, God tells the angels that the servant returned something priceless for an unvalued gift. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī rejects supplying an unspoken command ‘say’: the phrase is already a complete assertion that praise belongs to God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The assertion makes God intrinsically deserving of praise through His essence and acts, whether people praise Him or not; what is essential exceeds what depends on another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Stating that praise is due, rather than directly commanding it, lets the obedient praise God while leaving the disobedient with a lesser offense than rejecting an order. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise thanks God for past blessings and brings future increase, as promised in the Qur’an; it closes barriers and opens the way to knowledge of God, hence the name al-Fātiḥa. | mentions 14:7 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (السري السقطي): Al-Sarī al-Saqaṭī spent thirty years seeking forgiveness for thanking God that his shop survived Baghdad’s fire while others’ homes and shops burned, since his happiness showed disregard for others. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): They further argue that if God is praised intrinsically, no act causes that entitlement, so no duty, compensation, or reward is owed; if it is not intrinsic, God would depend on another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (المعتزلة): They argue that if a servant deserves God’s praise, the servant must be capable of action, contrary to determinism; if God praises Himself, that is also invalid, so the phrase only works on their doctrine. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): One group says God cannot command praise: if it repays a blessing, the command makes generosity transactional; if not, it is needless toil imposed on another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The same group says praise is useless to a God complete in Himself and that punishing people eternally for omitting a useless act would not befit the generous Lord. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Only God is necessary in Himself; everything else is possible and is called the world because its existence points to God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Created nurturers seek their own profit, such as reward or praise, while God nurtures for the creatures’ benefit rather than His own. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): God’s nurturing does not diminish His store; unlike other givers, He gives from inexhaustible provision. | mentions 15:21 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (Prophet): The Prophet is cited as saying that God loves those who persistently supplicate; no grading is stated. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): God gives before being asked, nurturing a person as a fetus and protecting him before he can think or pray. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Unlike human benefaction, God’s care never ends and reaches everyone. | mentions 7:156 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The four motives for honoring someone map onto the verse: intrinsic perfection, beneficence, hoped-for future good, and fear correspond respectively to God, Lord, Merciful, and Master of Judgment. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Embryonic development forms distinct bones, cartilage, ligaments, vessels, and senses, displaying God’s care; He gives hearing through bone, sight through fat, and speech through flesh. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): God arranged night for rest and day for work, the sun and moon for timekeeping, stars for navigation, and earth and mountains for habitation; reflecting on nature reveals His providence. | mentions 10:5, 6:97, 78:6 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The sequence assigns praise to God, then names Him Lord of the worlds: Allah signifies necessary perfection, while Lord of the worlds shows that all else flows from His generosity, making Him beyond completeness. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A Prophetic saying calls the human being God’s structure and curses whoever demolishes it; God guards people by night and day despite their sins. | mentions 21:42 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Allah is nobler than Lord, although supplicants commonly call God ‘Lord’; al-Rāzī refers readers to his earlier discussion of the divine names. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The seven Fatiha verses remove seven vices: praise dispels desire, Lord of the worlds greed, Judgment Day anger, worship and aid pride and vanity, guidance caprice, the favored path unbelief and doubt, and the final phrase innovation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): “Lord of the worlds” signifies that God is one, owns all worlds, and is the only deity and object of worship. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi supports obligatory Fatiha recitation because the Prophet consistently recited it and commands require following him. | mentions 6:153 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The command to recite what is easy from the Qur'an is obligatory; because reciting other passages is not, the obligatory recitation must be the Fatiha. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Reciting the Fatiha is the safer course, following the prophetic instruction to leave what causes doubt for what does not. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الله تعالى): In a divine report transmitted by the Prophet, God responds to each Fatiha phrase: the opening remembers Him, praise praises Him, the mercy names magnify Him, Judgment Day glorifies Him or is entrusted to Him, worship belongs to Him, aid relies on Him, and guidance is the servant's request. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Calling the Fatiha “prayer” shows that religious law serves human interests: people most need knowledge of God's lordship and servitude, which the surah's two halves provide to fulfill the covenant. | mentions 17:7, 51:56, 76:2, 2:40 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since God names the Fatiha “prayer,” prayer without its recitation does not occur; Razi takes this to establish that reciting it is a pillar, as his school holds. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The report that God says “my servant praised Me” shows that praise is higher than remembrance. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise frames creation: the angels speak of it before Adam is created, and the inhabitants of Paradise end their invocation with it. | mentions 2:30, 10:10 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The mind cannot contemplate God's essence: one cannot conceive His true nature, and the Prophet instead directs people to contemplate creation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Contemplating creatures reveals more mercy, favor, and beneficence than evil, because good is essential and evil incidental; therefore praise and thanks predominate. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise corresponds to bowing: gratitude looks both to God and His gift, a middle state between turning away and absorption, as bowing lies between standing and prostration; abundant gifts also weigh the back down. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The seven Fatiha verses are the soul of the seven prayer acts, themselves the soul of seven stages of human formation; God is the soul of souls and light of lights. | mentions 23:12, 23:13, 23:14, 53:42 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (النبي): A prophetic report says God has seventy veils of light; if unveiled, the splendor of His face would burn everything perceived by sight, and only prophetic light reveals the number. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Prayer was given to Muhammad as a gift for his community because it joins bodily ascent through actions with spiritual ascent through remembrances. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Raising the hands signals leaving both worlds and directing all inner faculties toward God; “God is greatest” means He exceeds all beings, knowledge, comparison, and even the claim that He is simply greater. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): “Exalted is Your majesty” signifies that God's knowledge and greatness exceed every stated measure. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): “There is no god but You” means every attribute of majesty and perfection belongs only to God; ultimately reason, speech, understanding, and imagination fail before Him. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The spiritual ascent rises through human spirits, the spirits of the heavens, angels, throne-bearers, and pure spirits detached from bodies, whose worship and love of God exceed human comprehension. | mentions 39:75, 69:17, 21:20, 12:76 QURTUBI, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report from Abu Hurayra and Abu Saʿid says that when a servant praises God, God affirms that the servant has spoken truly and that praise belongs to Him. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Malik al-Ashʿari reports from the Prophet that purification is half of faith, praise fills the balance, and glorification with prai se fills or fills the space between heaven and earth; Qurtubi cites Muslim. QURTUBI, d. 671 (طائفة): One group considers praise of God better than the declaration of unity because praise includes both tawhid and praise, while the declaration includes tawhid alone. QURTUBI, d. 671 (طائفة): Another group considers la ilaha illa Allah better because it repels unbelief and associating partners, and people fight on its basis; they cite the Prophet’s command to fight until people say it. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): In Arabic, al-ḥamd is complete praise; the definite article encompasses every kind of praise, all of which God deserves because He has the most beautiful names and highest attributes. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): The poet’s use of muḥammad for a person rich in praiseworthy qualities explains the name Muhammad; another verse says the Throne’s Lord is Maḥmūd and the Prophet is Muhammad. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports that God is pleased when a servant eats or drinks and praises Him for it; Qurtubi cites this from Muslim. QURTUBI, d. 671 (ابن عطاء): Al-Sulami transmits from Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq and Ibn ʿAṭāʾ the synonymy view; Ibn ʿAṭāʾ explains praise as gratitude for God enabling people to learn and praise Him. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الطبري): Al-Ṭabarī argues the terms are synonymous because Arabic permits the phrase al-ḥamdu lillāhi shukran, praise to God in gratitude. QURTUBI, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some scholars say gratitude is broader because it can be expressed by tongue, limbs, and heart, while praise is verbal alone. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Qurtubi’s preferred distinction is that praise commends attributes without requiring prior kindness, whereas gratitude praises a benefactor for a favor; praise is broader because it includes commendation as well as thanks. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الحسن): Al-Hasan says that no blessing exists for which praise of God is not better than the blessing itself. QURTUBI, d. 671 (جعفر الصادق): Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq gives a symbolic reading of the letters: ḥāʾ signifies unity, mīm dominion, and dāl permanence; knowing these is knowing God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (شقيق بن إبراهيم): Shafīq ibn Ibrāhīm says gratitude has three conditions: recognizing the giver, being content with what was given, and not disobeying God while one retains strength. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): God praises Himself and opens His Book with praise, while people are forbidden to claim purity for themselves; Qurtubi cites the command not to praise yourselves and the report to throw dust in flatterers’ faces. | mentions 53:32 QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): God’s self-praise precedes any creature’s praise and is without cause, while created praise is mixed with ulterior causes. QURTUBI, d. 671 (قيل): One explanation says God praised Himself eternally because His servants cannot fulfill the duty of praising Him; even the Messenger declared that he could not enumerate praise to God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet’s confession that he cannot enumerate praise to God illustrates the limits of human praise. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): A poet says that when people praise God rightly, He is as they praise Him and greater than their praise can express. QURTUBI, d. 671 (قيل): Another explanation says God praised Himself eternally because He knew His blessings would overwhelm servants’ ability to repay them, thereby easing the burden of indebtedness and making the blessing more enjoyable. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports from the Prophet that when God gives a servant a blessing and the servant praises Him, the praise is better than what was received. QURTUBI, d. 671 (سيبويه): Sībawayh says nominative al-ḥamdu lillāh conveys that praise from the speaker and all creatures belongs to God, while the accusative reports only the speaker’s own praise. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الطبري): Al-Ṭabarī says God’s praise of Himself implicitly commands His servants to praise Him, as though the wording said, Say praise belongs to God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report in Nawādir al-uṣūl from Anas says that if someone possessed the whole world and then praised God, the praise would be better than it. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb can also mean the deity, as shown by a poet’s line about a lord upon whose head a fox urinates. QURTUBI, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some scholars call Rabb the greatest name of God because callers frequently invoke Him by it and it conveys the bond of care, mercy, and need between Lord and servant. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): One derivation traces Rabb to tarbiya, since God manages and nurtures creation; the stepdaughter is called rabība because her mother’s husband raises her. In this sense it is an attribute of action, while owner or master denotes an attribute of essence. | mentions 4:23 QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): With the definite article, Rabb is specific to God; without it, the term can apply to God or a servant, but God alone owns owners and owned things, creates and provides, and cannot lose His dominion as human owners can. QURTUBI, d. 671 (قتادة): Qatāda says al-ʿālamīn is the plural of ʿālam and means every existing thing other than God, with no singular on this pattern, like rahṭ or qawm. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الفراء وأبو عبيدة): Al-Farrāʾ and Abū ʿUbayda restrict the term to rational beings: humans, jinn, angels, and devils, excluding animals because the plural form is for rational beings; al-Aʿshā’s verse is cited. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Abu ʿAbd Allah explains that the person first receives the world and then the enduring word of praise, which is better because the world perishes while the word remains among the righteous deeds. | mentions 18:46 QURTUBI, d. 671 (وهب بن منبه): Wahb ibn Munabbih says God has eighteen thousand worlds, of which this world is one. QURTUBI, d. 671 (أبو سعيد الخدري): Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī says God has forty thousand worlds, with the inhabited world from east to west counting as one. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الربيع بن أنس عن أبي العالية): Al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas transmits from Abū al-ʿĀliya that jinn and humans are worlds and that each of earth’s four corners contains 1,500 worlds created to worship God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Qurtubi prefers the first view, that the worlds include every creature and existent thing, supported by Pharaoh asking about the Lord of the worlds and Moses answering that He is Lord of the heavens, earth, and all between them. | mentions 26:23, 26:24 QURTUBI, d. 671 (الزجاج والخليل): Al-Zajjāj derives ʿālam from knowledge or a sign: it is everything God created in this life and the next and it points to its maker and planner; al-Khalīl likewise says a sign or marker indicates a thing. QURTUBI, d. 671 (الجنيد): A man praised God before al-Junayd but stopped before saying Lord of the worlds; al-Junayd urged him to complete the verse, explaining that created things vanish when paired with the Eternal. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Rabb may be read in the nominative by syntactic disconnection, meaning He is Lord of the worlds, or in the accusative as a word of praise. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Another wording says that what the servant gave was more than what he received; this frames praise as the servant’s gift and the world as God’s gift, though both ultimately come from God. QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn ʿUmar reports that angels could not record a servant’s praise fitting God’s majesty, so God told them to write it as the servant had said until He rewards him. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو جعفر الطبري): Al-Tabari and al-Mubarrad treat hamd and shukr as synonymous; al-Sulami also attributes this position to Jaʿfar al-Sadiq and Ibn ʿAtaʾ, who says praise is thanks for God teaching it. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAtiyya says “praise to God, in thanks” restricts praise to a blessing and therefore argues against equating praise and gratitude. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some say gratitude is broader because it uses tongue, limbs, and heart, whereas praise is only verbal. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Malik al-Ashʿari reports that purification is half of faith, praise fills the balance, and glorification with praise fills the space between heaven and earth. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (طائفة من العلماء): One group prefers alhamdulillah because it combines praise with the monotheism expressed by “There is no god but God.” QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (طائفة من العلماء): Another group prefers “There is no god but God” because it repels unbelief and shirk and is the profession for which people are fought. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Hamd means complete praise; the definite article encompasses every kind of praise, all of which God deserves for His beautiful names and exalted attributes. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet admitted that he could not enumerate praise of God, illustrating the creature’s inability to fulfill God’s due praise. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Other explanations say God praised Himself because servants cannot fully praise Him, or because abundant blessings exceed their capacity; divine praise removes the burden of gratitude. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (جعفر الصادق): Jaʿfar al-Sadiq offers an esoteric reading of the letters of hamd: unity, dominion, and permanence; knowing these attributes is the reality of praising God. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): God praises Himself and opens His Book with praise, while scripture and the Prophet forbid human self-praise; human praise is compromised by motives, unlike God’s eternal self-praise. | mentions 53:32 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (سيبويه): Sibawayh says the nominative attributes praise to the speaker and all creation, while the accusative attributes it to the speaker alone. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الطبري): Al-Tabari says God’s praise of Himself implicitly commands people to praise Him; Arabic can omit the understood imperative, as in the cited poem. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Rabb may derive from nurture: God manages and raises creation; stepdaughters are called rabāʾib because a husband raises them, making Rabb an attribute of action, while owner or master is an attribute of essence. | mentions 4:23 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): With the definite article, Rabb belongs exclusively to God; without it, people may be called a household’s lord. God alone creates and provides, owns both owners and owned things, and cannot lose His dominion. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Rabb can mean master, as in Joseph’s “your lord,” and the hadith that a slave woman gives birth to her mistress. | mentions 12:42 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some consider Rabb God’s greatest name because supplicants frequently call upon Him by it and the name conveys the relation of care, mercy, and dependence. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Hurayra and Abu Saʿid report that when a servant says “Praise belongs to God,” God affirms the servant and says the praise belongs to Him. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports that God is pleased when a servant praises Him after eating a meal or drinking. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الحسن): Hasan says that no blessing is better than saying “Praise belongs to God.” QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports that praising God for a blessing is met with a gift greater than what the servant received. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو عبد الله): Abu ʿAbd Allah explains that the enduring word is better because the world perishes, while praise remains among the righteous deeds; both world and praise ultimately come from God. | mentions 18:46 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): A variant says the gift was greater than what was received; this treats praise as the servant’s gift and the world as God’s, though both are from God, who enriches with the world and honors with praise in the afterlife. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn ʿUmar reports that angels could not record a servant’s praise “as befits” God’s majesty, so God told them to write it as spoken and reward him when they meet. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): If the article is exhaustive, all praise belongs to God because He bestows every good directly or indirectly; this implies He is living, powerful, willing, and knowing. | mentions 16:53 BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Readings assimilate dāl and lām in opposite directions, treating the letters as one word because they are used together. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Rabb is unqualified for God alone, though it can be qualified for others, as in “return to your lord.” | mentions 12:50 BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): ʿĀlam means what makes something known, like a signet or mold; it came to denote all besides God, substances and accidents, whose contingency indicates the necessary Creator. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): The plural encompasses diverse classes and uses the masculine rational plural because rational beings predominate among them. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (النبي): The Prophet is quoted saying praise is the head of gratitude and that whoever does not praise God has not thanked Him. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Al-ḥamdu is nominative as subject with “to God” as predicate, though an accusative reading is also reported. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): The nominative conveys universal and permanent praise belonging to God rather than newly arising praise. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Rabb is used of others only in a qualified phrase, whereas al-ʿālam names what makes something known and came to mean everything besides God. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The worlds comprise all substances and accidents, whose possibility and need for a necessary cause show God's existence; the plural gathers different genera, with rational beings predominant in its form. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Possible things need God not only to originate them but also to keep them in existence. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Gratitude is broader than praise and commendation in one respect but narrower in another; praise is its chief expression because it displays blessings and makes gratitude audible. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report calls praise the head of gratitude and says one who does not praise God has not thanked Him. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The definite article can mark the genus or all praise: in reality every good comes from God, directly or through an intermediary, as 16:53 says. | mentions 16:53 BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The entitlement to praise implies that God is living, powerful, willing, and knowing. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Two recitations assimilate the dāl and lām in either direction, treating them as one word because the terms are used together. NASAFI, d. 710 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): Abu Hurayra reports God’s dialogue dividing the Fatiha between Himself and the servant: praise, glorification, and exaltation belong to God; the worshipper’s request for guidance and the response to it belong to the servant, while worship and aid are shared. NASAFI, d. 710 (الواسطي): Al-Wasiti describes God as creator at the beginning, nourisher through provision, and forgiver at the end. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Rabb is one of God’s greatest names. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Al-‘alamin may mean everything the Creator knows of bodies, substances, and accidents, or every existing thing besides God, named for being evidence of His existence. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): The plural ending -in is used although normally associated with rational beings or their equivalent because ‘alamin has an adjectival sense, indicating knowledge. NASAFI, d. 710 (النبي): A Prophetic report calls praise the head of gratitude and says a servant has not thanked God without praising Him; praise leads because the tongue makes gratitude more public than the hidden heart or ambiguous bodily acts. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The report begins God's response when the servant says “Praise belongs to God,” which indicates the basmala is not part of al-Fatiha; if it is outside al-Fatiha, consensus excludes it from other surahs too. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Rabb is used without qualification only for God; human use requires a restriction, as in the cited accounts of Joseph. | mentions 12:23, 12:50 NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Al-ʿālamīn denotes what makes the Creator known—bodies, substances, and accidents—or every existent apart from God; its sound masculine plural reflects a descriptive quality associated with rational beings. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): A report calls praise the head of gratitude and says a servant has not thanked God without praising Him; verbal mention spreads notice of a favor more than inward belief or ambiguous bodily acts. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): Praise in action and in the tongue of each thing’s state is the appearance of its perfections and attainment of its ends, a fitting opening tribute to its Lord. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): All beings praise and glorify God through their particular properties, orientation toward ends, and actualization of potential; their dependence on Him alone denies partners and deficiency and manifests His majesty and beauty. The Qur’an says every thing glorifies Him. | mentions 17:44 QASHANI, d. 736 (author): God is owner at the final return and recompense; only the worshipped one to whom sovereignty returns can reward enduring blessing for perishable life, as servants shed acts and attributes and receive true existence after annihilation. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): All praise belongs to God eternally according to His due, at beginning, end, and everything between; He is both praiser and praised, worshipper and worshipped, in detail and in unity. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): Seeing God’s attributes in His speech leads servants to address Him by worship and ask His aid, since they see no other object of worship or independent power; every motion becomes worship and love seeks His beauty everywhere. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الحسن وقتادة): Al-Ḥasan and Qatāda define al-ʿālamīn as every created thing. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (الحسن وزيد بن علي): Al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn ʿAlī assimilate the dāl's kasra to the lām's kasra; the author calls this stranger because a case vowel follows a non-case vowel. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (ابن الأنباري): Ibn al-Anbārī treats ḥamd as an inversion of madḥ; Abū Ḥayyān rejects this because a jewel may be praised but not thanked. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (القراء السبعة): The nominative reading is stronger and agreed upon by the seven canonical readers: it states that praise is established and enduringly belongs to God, including praise by God and others. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): With nominative al-ḥamdu the lām marks entitlement; with accusative al-ḥamda a verb such as “I praise God” must be supplied, conveying renewed action. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): For the accusative reading, Abū Ḥayyān prefers an omitted verb directly indicated by the wording, such as “I praise God,” over “I say” or “adhere to praise.” ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Rabb may be a verbal noun used adjectivally or an active participle with deleted alif; as a title it is exclusive to God, while others require a possessive complement. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Views say ḥamd and shukr are synonymous, ḥamd is broader, or shukr praises God's acts while ḥamd praises His attributes; Abū Ḥayyān prefers ḥamd as broader. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The article in al-ʿālamīn is exhaustive; ʿālam is an irregular collective and its sound masculine plural is even more irregular because it violates some conditions of that pattern. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The lām can express possession, transfer of ownership, entitlement, relation, cause, communication, exclamation, specification, result, location, endpoint, and superiority; Qur'anic and Arabic examples illustrate these functions. | mentions 16:72, 4:105, 28:8, 21:47, 17:78, 7:57, 17:107 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Other reported scopes include Adam's children, the people of Paradise and Hell, all recipients of provision, every made thing, and spiritual beings; Abū Ḥayyān leaves the correct count to God. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The lām in lillāh has numerous possible relations, including ownership, entitlement, relation, cause, communication, specification, outcome, time, endpoint, and elevation. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects deriving the accusative rabb from a prior implied verb such as We praise God, since this analogy to ellipsis in coordination is not productive. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The nominative reading is semantically stronger and unanimously adopted by the Seven because it states that praise is established for God. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The accusative reading needs an implied governor such as I praise God or I praised God; it presents praise as newly occurring and names its agent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Rabb is used absolutely for God, but for others only in a construct phrase such as the lord of the house. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Although al-ʿālamīn is an irregular plural, Abū Ḥayyān favors the responsible creatures because a cited verse and Ḥafṣ's lām-kasra reading support that scope. | mentions 29:44 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Parsing rabb as apposition is weak because raḥmān and raḥīm intervene. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Some rhetoricians call “Praise be to God” a statement whose intended force is command, like “no doubt in it” meaning “do not doubt.” | mentions 2:2 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The lām in “to God” restricts all praise to Him, and the annexation “Master of the Day of Judgment” underscores His sole dominion when other dominions vanish. | mentions 40:16 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The author prefers a nominal estimate because it is mandatory in fa-idha and amma clauses; the relative clause al-ladhi fi al-dar instead requires a verb but is not structurally comparable. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The accusative may alternatively be a direct object of recite or read, but the verbal-noun analysis is better because the wording itself supports it. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The lām in li-llah expresses entitlement here; other senses include ownership, transfer, kinship, cause, address, oath exclamation, clarification, result, location, endpoint, elevation, and augmentation. | mentions 16:72, 4:105, 12:23, 28:8, 21:47, 17:78, 35:13, 17:109, 12:43, 11:107, 43:29, 16:1, 27:72, 8:9 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading makes rabb genitive as an adjective or appositive to Allah; accusative allows an implied I praise, a detached praise, or vocative, with vocative weakest because it separates adjective from noun; nominative is detached predicate huwa rabb. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Rabb can mean lord, owner, enduring, worshipped, or reformer; some add companion, supported by two poems, but here the author prefers owner and says no extra sense is needed; creator is another proposal. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Rabb is normally reserved for God unless annexed to a noun, though pre-Islamic Arabs used it without qualification for a human king, which the author calls unbelief. | mentions 12:50 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Al-alamin is an annexed sound masculine plural in form but an ism-jam because its singular differs; it cannot be plural of alam if that covers all non-divine beings while al-alamin means rational beings only. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Sibawayh similarly says a'rab is not plural of arab because the former is narrower; an objection that al-alamin could mean only rational alam is answered by comparison with the unavailable form shay'un. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The author prefers al-Raghib's account that the sound plural includes all creation, with human usage prevailing when humans share the label. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Tha'lab's derivation of hamd as a reversal of madh is unsound: both forms are equally common, and hamd applies to God where madh does not; a possible prohibition is acknowledged. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): In the divine hadith, “prayer” means recitation, as in 17:110; because the Fatiha details the division, its recitation is a major pillar of prayer. | mentions 17:110 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir questions Ibn Jarir's equation: praise is verbal commendation for necessary or bestowed qualities, while thanks applies only to bestowed qualities and may be inward, spoken, or enacted. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir distinguishes praise from eulogy: eulogy can concern living or dead people and even objects, precede or follow a favor, and address necessary or bestowed qualities. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (علي بن أبي طالب): When Umar asks what al-ḥamdu lillāh means after learning other formulas of glorification, Ali answers that it is a phrase God approves for Himself. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says al-ḥamdu lillāh is a word of thanks; when a servant says it, God says that His servant has thanked Him. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas explains praise as thanks, humble submission, and acknowledgment of God's favors, guidance, and origination. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (كعب الأحبار): Ka'b al-Ahbar says al-ḥamdu lillāh is God's praise. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report attributed to the Prophet says that whoever says al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn has thanked God, who then increases him. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Companion al-Aswad ibn Sari asks to recite praises of his Lord, and the Prophet replies that his Lord loves praise. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Anas reports that when a servant praises God for a blessing, the reward for that praise is better than the blessing received. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (القرطبي): Al-Qurtubi transmits a report that praising God would be better than possessing the whole world; he explains that the enduring reward for praise exceeds transient worldly enjoyment. | mentions 18:46 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn Umar reports that angels cannot calculate the full reward for a servant's praise fitting God's majesty, so God tells them to record it as said until He rewards him. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (طائفة): A group holds that al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn is better than lā ilāha illā Allāh because it combines praise with monotheism. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (آخرون): Others hold that lā ilāha illā Allāh is superior because it distinguishes faith from unbelief and is the formula people must profess; reports also call it the best remembrance. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): The definite article in al-ḥamdu encompasses every kind and form of praise as belonging to God, as expressed in a transmitted prayer. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Rabb means owner and disposer, and in Arabic also master or one who manages something toward its good; each meaning applies to God. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): The unqualified title al-Rabb is used only for God; for other possessors it must be annexed, and some say al-Rabb is the greatest name. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Al-ʿālamīn is the plural of ʿālam, encompassing every created being besides God; created kinds in heavens, earth, land, and sea are worlds, as is each generation. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas describes the worlds as all creation belonging to God: heavens, earths, their inhabitants and everything between them, known and unknown. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (مروان بن محمد بن مروان بن الحكم): Ibn Asakir reports Marwan's claim that God created seventeen thousand worlds, with the inhabitants of the heavens and earth forming one and God alone knowing the rest. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliyah says humans and jinn are worlds and reports uncertainty between fourteen and eighteen thousand other angelic worlds on earth, in groups worshipping God. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سعيد بن المسيب): Al-Baghawi reports Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab's view that God has one thousand worlds, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (وهب بن منبه): Wahb ibn Munabbih says God has eighteen thousand worlds and this world is one of them. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (كعب الأحبار): Ka'b al-Ahbar says that no one knows the number of worlds except God. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (أبو سعيد الخدري): Al-Qurtubi reports from Abu Sa'id al-Khudri that God has forty thousand worlds and the inhabited east-to-west world is only one. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj defines a world as everything God created in this life and the next. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir explains praise as exclusive thanks to God for countless blessings, including faculties for worship, worldly provision, and guidance toward lasting bliss despite no prior entitlement. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (القرطبي): Al-Qurtubi prefers a comprehensive meaning encompassing all worlds, citing Pharaoh's question and God's identification of the Lord of the worlds as Lord of heavens, earth, and what is between them. | mentions 26:23 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir says ʿālam derives from ʿalāmah, a sign, because every created thing points to its Maker, Creator, and oneness; he cites a poem by Ibn al-Mu'tazz. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir says the phrase is God's praise of Himself and implicitly commands His servants to say it too. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir reports a distinction that praise names God's beautiful attributes while thanks praises His favors, then argues Arabic speakers use both words interchangeably. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الله تعالى): In a divine report, God divides the prayer between Himself and His servant, responds to each recited section, calls 1:5 shared between them, and grants the servant's request. BURHAN, d. 794 (ابن حبان): Ibn Hibban reads the report comparing al-Fatiha with the Torah and Gospel as a difference in reward granted by God’s favor, not as intrinsic superiority within the Quran. BURHAN, d. 794 (author): The successive praises intensify the servant's attention: God's exclusive praise, universal lordship, gifts of mercy, and command of judgment prepare the worshipper to approach and seek help. BURHAN, d. 794 (قيل): One explanation says praise uses third person because it ranks below worship, while direct address elevates the worshipper's encounter; similarly, blessing is explicitly attributed to God but anger is phrased obliquely as a matter of etiquette. | mentions 6:101 BURHAN, d. 794 (author): These three senses can coincide in praise of God: He deserves praise, has authority over it, and is the one to whom it belongs. NASHR, d. 833 (author): Stopping after these phrases is good because their sense is understood, but starting with attached phrases such as rabb al-ʿālamīn, ṣirāṭ alladhīna, or ghayr al-maghḍūb ʿalayhim is poor, except at verse ends per the Sunnah. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): Because the basmala is a kind of praise, it is naturally followed by the comprehensive name al-ḥamd; this implicitly calls people to praise God as the one deserving every praise. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): People should make this form of praise open their affairs, since it awakens hope and fear of God and leads to steadfastness on guidance. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): Al-ḥamd lillāh establishes that God deserves every praise by His essence and all perfections; Rabb adds that He deserves praise as Lord, Owner, and Benefactor. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): Al-ʿālamīn points to creation's beginning, inviting inference from the made thing to its Maker and from the beginning to resurrection, as the Torah also begins with creation for this reason. BIQAI, d. 885 (أبو الحسن الحرالي): Al-Ḥarālī defines ḥamd as complete praise encompassing every action and attribute: all are from God and all are praise untouched by blame. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha's purpose is to establish God's exclusive right to all praise and perfection, sovereignty over both worlds, worship and aid, and to ask for the path of the successful rather than the doomed; its center is مراقبة. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Since the basmala is a kind of praise, it naturally leads to comprehensive al-hamd: begin affairs by praising God as the one deserving every praise, with hope and awe guiding the way. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Al-hamd lillah establishes that God deserves praise for His essence and perfection; Rabb al-alamin adds His lordship, sovereignty and beneficence, while the worlds point to creation's beginning and the inference to resurrection. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali defines al-hamd as complete praise covering all actions and attributes, all from God and free of blame; its definite form signals praise reaching its full extent. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Lordship does not yield wellbeing without mercy, so Rahman and Rahim follow al-hamd and encourage people to persist in praising God. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali says all Qur'an is God's speech, but some verses voice what creatures at different levels ought to say yet cannot express; God teaches them words that fulfill their duty. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali explains the sound report that God divided prayer between Himself and His servant: the first three verses are God's praise, which He accepts from the reciting servant as praise and glorification. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali says the seven repeated verses are the Qur'an's gathered essence and its contents will be disclosed at creation's completion; earlier scriptures progress from changeable scrolls and lasting books to the Qur'an, which completes religion. | mentions 5:3 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali says the Fatiha is the Qur'an's mother: its first three verses encompass the divine names and attributes, while its last three cover the creature's way to God, mercy or failure. | mentions 2:281, 75:17, 33:50, 33:52 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (أبو هريرة): In the authentic report in Muslim and the four Sunan, God says the Fatiha is divided between Himself and His servant: He answers praise, glorification and worship, and grants the servant's final requests. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The cow story makes the surah's title apt because resurrection occurs through an apparently slight human cause; it is a clearer sign of divine power than Abraham's story or earlier Israelite resurrections. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The following comparison of hearts and stones shows the guided and astray: hearts become hard and wretched, while stones display fear and yield benefit that reaches God's servants. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): He offers two readings of the merciful names: universal mercy permits created benefits while specific mercy protects from harm; he says the nearer reading is general lordly beneficence and a special divine mercy directed to felicity. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Because praise is the seventh inclusive letter, the Fatiha begins with it and gathers the seven universals diffused through the Qur'an, as the Qur'an gathers earlier scriptures. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Alternatively, the surah's purpose is solely to describe the Book; it establishes the truth of its meaning, exposes disbelief and hypocrisy to show that inward faith matters, then proves its inimitable form and authority for teaching law. | mentions 2:21 DURR, d. 911 (الله): The report glosses al-Hamd as gratitude and praise to God. DURR, d. 911 (الله): The Lord of the worlds is Lord of humans, jinn, angels, devils, all creation, and everything. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet calls al-hamd the head of gratitude: a servant has not thanked God unless he praises Him. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says the best remembrance is there is no god but God, while the best supplication is al-hamdulillah; al-Tirmidhi grades the report hasan. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): Al-Hasan transmits that every blessing praised by the servant is surpassed by praise of God, whatever the blessing may be. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says purification is half of faith and al-hamd fills the balance; other phrases fill the space between heaven and earth, while prayer, charity, patience, and the Quran serve as signs or testimony. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says glorification is half the balance and al-hamd fills it; there is no barrier between God and the declaration of divine unity. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): When al-Aswad ibn Sariʿ offers the Prophet praises he composed for God, the Prophet replies that his Lord loves praise; al-Hakim authenticates the report. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says nothing is more beloved to God than praise, so God praised Himself by saying al-hamdulillah. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): In a weakly chained report, the Prophet treats saying al-hamdulillah after his stolen camel returned as fulfilling his vow to thank God. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet contrasts divine forbearance with satanic haste and says nothing is more beloved to God than praise. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says every important matter begun without praise of God is deficient. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas describes an exchange at sneezing: the sneezer says al-hamdulillah, an angel answers Lord of the worlds, and after the sneezer repeats it the angel asks God to show mercy. DURR, d. 911 (موسى بن طلحة): Musa ibn Talha reports God's instruction to Solomon to remember Him if anyone sneezes, even beyond seven seas. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): Ali reports that the Prophet vowed to thank God properly if his family expedition returned safely; on their return he said al-hamdulillah for God's abundant favors and counted that as fulfilling the vow. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A weak report from the Companion al-Hakam ibn Umayr says that saying Lord of the worlds is gratitude to God, bringing an increase. DURR, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad tells how he vowed to praise God with approved praises if his mule returned, then said al-hamdulillah alone because it encompasses all praise. DURR, d. 911 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): Ibn ʿAmr assigns distinct functions to remembrances: glorification is creation's prayer, al-hamd gratitude, tawhid sincerity, takbir fills heaven and earth, and the declaration of God's powerlessness-to-act-without-help brings God's command to submit. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas also explains the Lord of the worlds as Lord of all creation: all the heavens and their inhabitants, all earths and their inhabitants, and everything between them, known or unknown. DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): A weak report from Jabir recounts Umar's search for locusts and a prophetic saying that God made a thousand communities, six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land; locusts are the first to perish, after which the others follow like a broken necklace. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas calls al-hamd the word of gratitude; when a servant says it, God says that His servant has thanked Him. DURR, d. 911 (أبو العالية): Abu al-ʿAliya counts humans and jinn as two worlds and says there are another eighteen thousand worlds of angels; each of earth's four corners has 3,500 worlds created to worship God. DURR, d. 911 (وهب): Wahb says God has eighteen thousand worlds, of which this world is only one. DURR, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas explains al-hamd as gratitude, humble submission to God, and acknowledgment of His blessings, guidance, creation, and other favors. DURR, d. 911 (علي): When Umar asks what al-hamd is, Ali answers that it is a phrase God chose for Himself and loves people to say. DURR, d. 911 (كعب): Kaʿb defines al-hamd as praise of God. DURR, d. 911 (أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي): Abu ʿAbd al-Rahman al-Jubbaʾi says prayer, fasting, and every good deed done for God count as gratitude, with al-hamd the best gratitude. DURR, d. 911 (قتادة): Qatada says the Lord of the worlds describes what God has made. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (رجاء الغنوي): Rajāʾ al-Ghanawī attributes to the Prophet a healing practice using God's own praise of Himself, naming al-ḥamdu lillāh and al-Ikhlāṣ; whoever is not healed by the Qur'an is not healed by God. | mentions 112:1 DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الله تعالى): When the servant says “al-hamd lillah,” God says the servant has thanked and praised Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الله تعالى): The report explains “Lord of the worlds” as lord of humans, jinn, angels, devils, all creation, and everything. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): A prophetic report calls al-hamd the head of gratitude: a servant has not thanked God until praising Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الحكم بن عمير): In a weak report, saying “al-hamd lillah rabb al-alamin” counts as thanking God, after which He increases the speaker. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says that when the servant says al-hamd, God says the servant has thanked Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas defines praise as gratitude, praise of God, and acknowledgment of His blessings, guidance, and originating bounty. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي): Ali answers Umar that al-hamd is a phrase God approved for Himself and loves to be spoken. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (كعب): Kab says “al-hamd lillah” is praise of God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي): Abu Abd al-Rahman al-Jubba'i says prayer, fasting, and every good deed for God are gratitude, with al-hamd the best form. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says purification is half of faith, al-hamd fills the balance, and glorification fills the space between heaven and earth, among other deeds' spiritual benefits. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn Umar reports that al-hamd fills the balance and there is no barrier between “la ilaha illa Allah” and God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الأسود بن سريع): Al-Aswad ibn Sari asked to recite praises he had composed, and the Prophet replied that God loves praise. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says nothing is more beloved to God than praise, so God praised Himself with “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أنس): Anas reports that deliberation is from God, haste from Satan, God is most ready to excuse, and praise is most beloved to Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو هريرة): Abu Hurayra reports that every significant matter begun without praise of God is deficient. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): When someone sneezes and says al-hamd, an angel responds “Lord of the worlds”; when the sneezer repeats it, the angel says “may God have mercy on you.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (واثلة بن الأسقع): Wathila reports that praising God when greeting a sneezer prevents abdominal illness; another report says God told Solomon to remember Him when someone sneezes e ven seven seas away. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): After an expedition returned safely, the Prophet fulfilled his pledge to thank God by saying “al-hamd lillah.” DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report about an Ansar expedition says the Prophet pledged thanks if they returned safe with spoils, then said he had fulfilled that pledge and praised God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): When Jaafar ibn Muhammad's mule returned, he thanked God with only “al-hamd lillah,” saying this gathered all praise. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas, Mujahid, and Sa'id ibn Jubayr interpret “Lord of the worlds” as Lord of jinn and humans. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas also explains the Lord of the worlds as God of all creation, the heavens and their inhabitants, the earths and their inhabitants, and all between them, known or unknown. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A weak report says God created a thousand communities, 600 in the sea and 400 on land; locusts are the first to perish, followed by the rest like beads when their string breaks. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (قتادة): Qatada says “Lord of the worlds” encompasses every class of created beings. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (وهب): Wahb says God has eighteen thousand worlds, of which this world is only one. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses praise to God as thanks to God. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses Lord of the worlds as the whole of creation. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): The Fatiha points to all major Qur’anic disciplines: knowledge of God and attributes, prophecy, resurrection, worship, ethical conduct and the straight path, and stories teaching the outcomes of obedience and disobedience. | mentions 1:2, 1:3, 1:4, 1:5, 1:6, 1:7 ITQAN, d. 911 (الخويي): Al-Khuyi says al-Fatiha fittingly gives the broadest divine attribute, Lord of all creation, while other surahs open with particular acts or attributes. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 ITQAN, d. 911 (الإمام فخر الدين): Fakhr al-Din maps the Qur’an’s four aims—God, resurrection, prophethood, and divine decree—onto the Fatiha: praise, Judgment Day, worship and reliance, and guidance through the surah’s end; this supports its name Umm al-Qur’an. | mentions 1:2, 1:4, 1:6, 1:7 ITQAN, d. 911 (الطيبي): Al-Tibi divides the Fatiha’s religious knowledge into foundations (God and attributes, prophethood, resurrection), branches (worship), ethical perfection and seeking the path, and accounts of earlier peoples with promise and threat. | mentions 1:2, 1:4, 1:6, 1:7 ITQAN, d. 911 (الغزالي): Al-Ghazali lists three essential Qur’anic aims—knowing God, the straight path, and the return in the Hereafter—and three supplementary aims: the obedient, deniers’ speech, and the stations of the path, all signaled by the Fatiha. | mentions 1:4, 1:5, 1:6, 1:7 ITQAN, d. 911 (ناصر الدين بن الميلق): A second explanation counts three kinds of rights—God’s claim on servants, servants’ claim on God, and people’s claims on one another—and says the Fatiha explicitly covers the first two, hence two-thirds. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): There is no conflict between hadiths calling the Fatiha the greatest surah and al-Baqara the greatest: al-Baqara is greatest among surahs other than the Fatiha because of its detailed laws, parables, and arguments. | mentions 2:1 ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن العربي): Ibn al-Arabi quotes a teacher saying the Fatiha contains a thousand commands, prohibitions, rulings, and reports; Ibn Umar spent eight years teaching it because of its jurisprudential depth. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): It is called the seven oft-repeated because it has seven verses, each can stand for seven Qur'anic portions, and reciting them is said to earn the whole Qur'an's reward or close seven gates of Hell. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Other names include the Prayer, Healing, Cure, Foundation, Sufficient, Complete, Praise, Supplication, Thanks, and Invocation surahs because their meanings occur in it. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): It is called the Treasure surah because a report says the Opening is a treasure from God's Throne. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The surah is Fatiha because it opens the codex, instruction, Qur'an recitation, prayer, speech, revelation, the aims of this life, and the gates of Paradise. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Fatiha is the Mother of the Qur'an because it gathers the Qur'an's purposes: affirming divinity and prophethood, and establishing decree and predestination. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The opening praise and names of the Merciful indicate God's divinity. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The five divine attributes unfold a sequence: God creates, nurtures with favors, conceals sin in mercy, forgives repentance, and finally judges. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Bursevi relays the Najmiyya interpretation that the self has four faculties, matched by four divine attributes that draw the servant toward God. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض الأخبار): A report says God created one hundred thousand lamps hung from the Throne, with the heavens, earth, and all they contain in a single lamp; only God knows the rest. | mentions 31:27 ALUSI, d. 1270 (كعب الأحبار): Kaʿb al-Aḥbār says no one can enumerate the worlds except God, who creates things people do not know and alone knows His hosts. | mentions 16:8, 74:31 ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض العارفين): A mystic observes that God has other servants, but each person has no lord but Him, while God nurtures each as though He had no servant but that person. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Praising God for essential attributes raises a problem if ḥamd requires voluntary acts, since those attributes may be identical with or additional to the divine essence. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Although the sound masculine plural often denotes a small number, the form here hints that all created worlds remain few beside God’s greatness; the plural can also denote many. | mentions 39:67 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): For the gnostics, the station of lordship comprises names that call for objects, such as knowing and power, including shared names and creative names directed toward creatures. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī’s own Sufi reading is that souls invoke God by the first name through which they acknowledged Him in the primordial covenant. | mentions 7:172 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): Ibn ʿArabī’s account says the universal spirit sought its source without knowing that the sought reality was already within it, then recognized God in the station of lordship. | mentions 50:16 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): In Ibn ʿArabī’s account the spirit names God its Rabb; God gives it divine names and attributes to know through existence, while veiling the manner of that gift because the creature cannot bear it. | mentions 50:16 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Opening the sūra’s descriptions with Rabb al-ʿālamīn suits the Prophet’s mission and asks for compassion toward all creatures, since God is their Lord. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): One solution treats praise of divine attributes as praise for what is effectively voluntary because God’s essence suffices for those attributes. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): A further view says divine attributes issue by choice in the sense that God would bring them about if He willed and would not if He did not, without making them temporally originated. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الفلاسفة): Philosophers appeal to the distinction between a necessary conditional and the actuality of its antecedent, but al-Ālūsī argues that necessity leaves divine volition nominal or makes the world’s origin incompatible with their account. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawwānī explains al-Sharīf’s requirement of inward and outward exaltation as intending the performative creation of esteem, not belief in a proposition. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The fifth element is an expression that manifests the praised one’s praiseworthiness; speech is conventionally singled out because the tongue is the usual instrument of speaking. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The Qurʾānic statement that every thing glorifies God with praise shows that praise is not confined to utterance by a human tongue. | mentions 17:44 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الأكثر): Most scholars explain God’s self-praise as declaring His entitlement to praise, commanding praise, quoting the words of servants, or figuratively displaying His perfections; al-Sayyid favors the last account. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawwānī considers figurative divine praise unnecessary because the people of truth affirm that God truly speaks; al-Ālūsī reads the tongue restriction as relative or customary. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Ḥamd carries greater reverence and grandeur than madḥ, applies more particularly to rational beings and eminent persons, and is more often used of God. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Ḥamd is generally commanded, as in the saying that whoever does not thank people does not thank God, whereas madḥ is not universally praised and flatterers are rebuked. ALUSI, d. 1270 (النواس بن سمعان): Al-Ṭabarānī reports from al-Nawwās b. Samʿān that the Prophet vowed to thank God if his she-camel returned; when it did, he said al-ḥamdu lillāh, and the Companions expected further prayer or fasting. ALUSI, d. 1270 (صاحب التجريد): The author of al-Tajrīd adds a fourth form of gratitude: thanking God by God, since no one but God can give Him His due thanks. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): People generally recognize three modes of shukr; it overlaps with ḥamd in some respects, but ḥamd is its strongest branch because it openly discloses the blessing. ALUSI, d. 1270 (ابن عمر): A report from Ibn ʿUmar calls ḥamd the head of gratitude and says a servant has not thanked God unless he praises Him; al-Ālūsī notes its chain is interrupted. ALUSI, d. 1270 (أنس): Al-Ālūsī cites a supporting report from Anas in which God tells Abraham that praise is the key to gratitude and gratitude ascends to the Lord of the Throne. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Hamd is more elevated as worship than shukr because it need not be prompted by a benefit received, unlike gratitude for a gift. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Although tasbīḥ commonly precedes taḥmīd, the sūra omits it because praise includes declaring God free of defects while the reverse is not always true; tasbīḥ elsewhere serves a different purpose. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Tasbīḥ signals that God is free of defects, while ḥamd also points to His positive beneficence and the knowledge, power, and sufficiency that make it possible. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Alternatively, every tasbīḥ is a kind of praise, but ḥamd is broader because it includes both negative and positive attributes while tasbīḥ addresses negative attributes. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī paraphrases the phrase as we praise God with a praise, then treats إياك نعبد as an answer explaining how that praise is performed. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers an exhaustive reading here: the sūra’s encompassing attributes of lordship, mercy, and sovereignty support all praise belonging to God, and the shift to إياك نعبد marks a new address rather than an explanation. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Zamakhsharī’s statement in 64:1 that sovereignty and praise belong exclusively to God supports a comprehensive reading, though the present context gives further reason to include every instance of praise. | mentions 64:1 ALUSI, d. 1270 (أبو العباس المرسي): Al-Mursī says the article is covenantal because God praised Himself eternally on behalf of creatures unable to encompass His praise, supported by the prophetic supplication that He is as He praised Himself. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufis understand the praise as belonging to God Himself, like sovereignty and creation-command: He is both praiser and praised, and all things are His manifestations. | mentions 7:54 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الماتريدي): Al-Māturīdī says God praised Himself in order to teach creatures, and this is fitting because He inherently deserves praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): This cross-word assimilation is permissible because the two frequently paired words are treated as if they were one word. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some prefer Ibrāhīm’s reading because following the first sound is easier than the reverse and because the dāl’s inflection is stronger than the lām’s fixed vowel. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Others prefer al-Ḥasan’s reading because the second sound more commonly follows the first and the uninflected sound should follow the inflected one. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The nominative reading is strongest because the nominal sentence, in this context, conveys settled and enduring praise rather than a renewed act of praising. ALUSI, d. 1270 (معظم العلماء): Most scholars consider الحمد لله informative: otherwise God’s possession of the praised qualities would begin only when the speaker utters the praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (ابن الهمام): Ibn al-Humām argues that ḥamd is performative because it manifests qualities rather than establishing them, so praise need not be informative. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī sides with the majority: the sentence informs that God deserves praise, while a believe r who says it intending reverent description is rightly called a praiser. | mentions 28:70 ALUSI, d. 1270 (داود): In a report, David asks how to thank God when gratitude itself is among His gifts; God answers that recognizing inability to thank Him is itself gratitude. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The servants’ contingent praise relates to God without inhering in Him, just as knowledge relates to its objects without becoming part of them. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some take the verbal noun in a passive sense, meaning that God is the one praised; because its form allows several construals, early commentators counted forty-two possibilities. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): A particularly unusual parsing makes the lām causal, so that praise is established for God’s sake. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Naming God by His essential name avoids suggesting that He deserves praise only through one particular attribute; entitlement by essence still means entitlement for divine excellence. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The thing praised for is the cause of the praise: its excellence becomes manifest through the praised person’s possession of it; most scholars include both acts and enduring qualities. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا): A Sufi account divides praise from God into praise of His essence, unity, manifestations, qualities, effects, and acts, while creaturely praise varies with knowledge and capacity. | mentions 6:91, 20:110, 37:180, 53:42 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): When the distinction between Creator and creation is considered together, all praise comes from God and returns to Him; there is no praiser or praised one apart from Him. | mentions 53:42 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Praise precedes the divine name because the speaker is about to perform the act of praise; in 30:18 the order is reversed for a different purpose. | mentions 30:18 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī interprets rabb here as owner; al-Ālūsī prefers tarbiya because owner would repeat the sense of Mālik yawm al-dīn and gives no clear reason for placing al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm between them. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): In ordinary usage rabb is not freely applied to anyone but God unless restricted by an addition or other qualifier; Ibn Ḥilaza’s unqualified poetic usage is rare. ALUSI, d. 1270 (السيوطي): Al-Suyūṭī interprets the restriction as a legal rather than purely linguistic ban on calling someone other than God rabb. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): ʿĀlam means what makes something known and is commonly used for all that God created, including substances and accidents, or for a whole class of beings. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Each possible thing, great or small, points to the Creator because all depend on the Necessary Being for existence. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The definite plural makes the scope of divine lordship explicit across every created kind; singular al-ʿālam might be mistaken for the familiar perceptible world alone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Because the book opens with the basmala, a kind of praise, God follows it with comprehensive praise at the highest degree of perfection. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Several answers address praise for God’s essential attributes: treat them as voluntary because the divine essence suffices for them, define choice as willing-agent attribution, or say they occur by choice in the sense that God acts if He wills. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawānī objects that treating God’s praise as figurative conflicts with the orthodox affirmation of real divine speech, since saying and speech are equivalent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Hamd is also said to be both report and performative through love and reverence, unlike madḥ as bare report; one report says whoever does not praise people does not praise God, while another condemns flatterers. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī holds that madḥ can praise involuntary qualities, unlike hamd; that distinction explains why God says al-hamdu lillāh, since God is a freely acting agent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He rejects two replies—that the speaker believes the attributed quality, or that the praise is figurative—because the first contradicts plain experience and the second often contradicts the facts. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Tongue is the usual instrument, not an absolute condition: lip sounds without a tongue, or speech produced through another organ, still count as praise; the Qur’an says everything glorifies God’s praise. | mentions 17:44 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الأكثر): Most authorities interpret God praising Himself as reporting His entitlement to praise, commanding praise, speaking through servants, or figuratively displaying His perfect attributes; al-Sayyid favors the last explanation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (النواس بن سمعان): Al-Ṭabarānī reports that after the Prophet’s she-camel was stolen, he vowed to thank God if it returned; when it did, he said alhamdulillah, and the Companions awaited fasting or prayer, showing they understood gratitude as action. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (صاحب التجريد): Some add a fourth form, God thanking God, since only God can thank Him fully; a cited poem contrasts gratitude to people by heart, word, and deed with gratitude to God through His own thanking. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Hamd and gratitude overlap in part, but hamd is stronger because it openly discloses blessing while ingratitude conceals it; verbal praise is clearer than hidden belief or deeds that may be habitual. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Calling the state more eloquent than speech is a rhetorical claim: worshipful actions may be indistinguishable from ordinary habits, whereas verbal praise explicitly names God’s glory. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Another defense treats genus in rhetorical context as the complete instance; a critic replies that comprehensive scope can likewise treat everything else as negligible, so neither reading avoids the theological difficulty unaided. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He prefers genus because its exclusivity follows directly from the wording and logically entails exclusivity of every instance, making the proof stronger than separately asserting total scope. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar transmits the saying that praise is gratitude’s head and that no servant thanks God without praising Him; al-Ālūsī notes its interrupted chain but says a supporting report strengthens it. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Other rationales say praise is a fitting beginning for the Lord and that praise exceeds thanks because it need not arise from a benefit received by the speaker. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Praise has a narrower occasion but a broader object than gratitude; its inclusion may signal a sacred allusion, and tasbīḥ is omitted because praise already includes both negative and positive perfections. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī says treating genus as perfection and treating comprehensive praise as exhaustive both require interpretation; making everything else negligible adds an unnecessary step. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The sounder reading makes the first clause praise of God in the third person and the next a speaker’s self-report; the missing conjunction marks a change of state, not an explanation. | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The following divine attributes imply that God deserves praise for them; since their scope is comprehensive, the praise should likewise be comprehensive, with God encompassing all names and worlds and mercy extending universally.
