ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): In al-Taghābun, al-Zamakhsharī says the fronted phrases “His is the dominion” and “His is praise” signal their exclusivity; praise of others recognizes that God’s favor reached them through those people. | mentions 64:1 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Some read al-ḥamdu as true, complete praise alone belonging to God rather than all instances; al-Ālūsī says this meaning can be apt but rejects using it to evade comprehensive scope here. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He rejects limiting the expression to “true complete praise” as an ad hoc escape from comprehensiveness, even when supported by a leading commentator, because rhetorical discourse often speaks through persuasive address. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The claim that exclusive genus praise is more comprehensive than exclusive individuals is reversible: if every individual is exclusively God’s, no instance remains outside the exclusive genus. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rhetorical address is a divinely used mode even when a proof is available, because it affects hearts and benefits ordinary hearers; al-Ālūsī points to the command to invite with wisdom and good exhortation. | mentions 16:125 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو العباس المرسي): Al-Fākihī reports that Abū al-ʿAbbās al-Mursī told Ibn al-Naḥḥās the article is referential: God praised Himself eternally on creatures’ behalf because they could not praise Him adequately. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (النبي ﷺ): Al-Mursī appeals to the Prophet’s authentic supplication that people cannot enumerate God’s praise and that He is as He praised Himself. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufi masters say “praise belongs to God” is like “might belongs to God”: God is both praiser and praised, and all things are His affairs, as the verse says creation and command belong to Him. | mentions 7:54 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الماتريدي): Al-Māturīdī says God praised Himself to teach creatures; this is fitting because He deserves praise by His own essence and perfection. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Some grammarians say verbal nouns are originally accusative because events require predication; the underlying verbal relation is normally expressed by a verb, making the noun-plus-verb construction seem redundant. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī opens a further question about whether “Praise belongs to God” is declarative or performative; most scholars call it declarative, following the apparent form. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): A mystical account divides praise into praise originating from God and from creation: God’s praise concerns His essence, manifestations, attributes, and effects, while creatures praise His essence, names, actions, secrets, and wisdom according to their understanding. | mentions 6:91, 20:110, 37:180, 53:42 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): When all praise is considered together, it begins and ends in God: there is no praiser or praised one besides Him, and human understanding cannot encompass Him. | mentions 53:42 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Praise precedes the name “God” because the speaker is about to utter its meaning; the contextual need can outweigh the name’s greater intrinsic importance, unlike another verse whose arrangement has another purpose. | mentions 28:70 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن الهمام): Some interpreters propose forty-two possibilities for the phrase; a rare proposal makes its lām mean because of God. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Naming God by His essence avoids suggesting that entitlement to praise belongs only to one attribute; essential entitlement means no particular attribute is specified, not that praise lacks a beautiful ground. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الملا خسرو): Entitlement by essence means entitlement without specifying a particular attribute; it does not mean God deserves praise apart from beautiful qualities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Mystically, praise originating from God ranges across His hidden essence, absolute existence, manifestations, attributes, effects, and knowledge in general or detail. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Mystical praise arising from creatures may concern God's essence, attributes, names, actions, mysteries, subtle workmanship, or hidden wisdom, according to each person's knowledge and understanding. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The question how servants’ created praise is “for” God is answered by distinguishing attachment from inherence, like knowledge attaching to its objects; another parses the noun as passive “being praised,” while a rare view makes the lām causal. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rabb is also used for creator, master, king, benefactor, reformer, worshiped one, and possessor; most commonly it means nurturing, which some treat as the literal sense because it comes first to mind. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Another debate asks whether al-ʿālamīn is a regular plural, a collective noun, or a name for a totality; al-Ālūsī concludes it is a true plural because each member is itself called an ʿālam. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): He rejects “owner” here because “Owner of the Day of Judgment” would repeat the meaning, with no evident reason for placing al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm between them; nurturing better fits the context and signals divine beneficence, power, and wisdom. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Unqualified rabb is not widely used linguistically for anyone but God; in a construct or with a qualifier it can express limited lordship, though unrestricted human uses occur rarely. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (السيوطي): Al-Suyūṭī takes the prohibition on calling another “lord” as religious rather than linguistic; an old poem and al-Jawharī support him, while al-Shihāb allows non-owner senses with contextual indication. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو هريرة): Al-Bukhārī and Muslim report from Abū Hurayra that one should not say “feed your lord” or “give your lord ablution”; instead one should say “my master” or “my patron.” ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The human uses “return to your lord” and “he is my lord,” and Joseph’s brothers prostrating to him, are explained as licenses specific to that historical period. | mentions 12:50, 12:23, 12:100 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Although common usage applies ʿālam to the totality or to a kind, not ordinarily to an individual, its original sense can apply to every part, since every contingent thing witnesses its need for the necessary Creator. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Every created thing, great or small, present or absent, is a clear sign of the Creator and a path to tawḥīd; the author quotes a poem saying each thing has a sign of God’s oneness. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The plural al-ʿālamīn guarantees inclusion of every created kind: a singular definite noun could be heard as the familiar perceptible world, whereas the plural distributes lordship across all named kinds and their individuals. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The claim that a definite singular is more comprehensive does not undermine this wording; the plural avoids the familiar-world reading and includes all created genera. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Humanity is a concentrated summary of created things, and reflection on the self reveals the Creator's greatness; the apt reading here remains universal. | mentions 51:20, 51:21 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Alusi prefers the universal sense because the worlds are innumerable and God's nurturing encompasses every existing thing, both celestial and earthly. | mentions 16:8, 74:31 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض الأخبار): A report says God created one hundred thousand lamps suspended from the Throne, with the heavens, earth, Paradise, and Hell in one lamp; only God knows the rest. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (كعب الأحبار): Kaʿb al-Aḥbār says only God can count the number of worlds. | mentions 16:8, 74:31 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A mystic's saying contrasts God's care for the servant with the servant's lax service: God tends him as though no other servant existed, though God owns countless others. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The masculine sound plural can denote paucity despite the many worlds, underscoring that all are insignificant beside God's majesty; Arabic usage can also allow either number. | mentions 39:67 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rabb names the station of divine lordship that calls for the created objects required by attributes such as knowledge, power, will, and hearing. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Names beneath Rabb include attributes shared in some respect between Creator and creation and active names exclusive to God; the latter belong under Malik. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rahman is more comprehensive than Rabb because it gathers every exalted divine attribute, whether exclusive to God or related to creation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Allah signifies the all-encompassing divine essence and highest level; below it are oneness, mercifulness, lordship, and sovereignty, while absolute divine uniqueness has no manifestation of created qualities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أرباب الظاهر): Outward scholars explain frequent supplication with Rabb as fitting because a petitioner seeks what seems beneficial for personal nurture and care. | mentions 17:110, 7:180 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The primordial spirits first heard and affirmed God's lordship, so they call on Him by the first name through which they recognized and pledged themselves to Him. | mentions 7:172 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): Ibn ʿArabi describes the universal Spirit, initially unaware of its origin, as journeying inward until God showed it its secrets and taught it to recognize Him as Lord, while reserving divinity to Himself. | mentions 50:16 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Opening the Fatiha's descriptions with Lord of the worlds suits the Prophet's mission as a profound act of nurturing humanity and hints at compassion for all people. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The descriptions close by pointing to resurrection, just as Lord of the worlds opened with origination; they explain the prior praise and prepare for the prayer that follows. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The five names answer four grounds for praising someone: inherent perfection, past beneficence, hoped-for future kindness, and fear of power; God encompasses them all. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The community that reserves worship and appeal to God reflects a delegated divine trust and receives a share in creation; this supports communal consensus as a legally probative source. | mentions 35:39, 21:105 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The relation between the basmala and Fatiha unfolds the primordial covenant between the eternal Lord and c reation, inscribing a relation already present in human nature. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Fatiha proceeds as instruction; the command to read in the name of the Lord is a key contextual sign of this teaching purpose. | mentions 96:1 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The opening three verses speak about God in the unseen; reflection makes God present to the worshipper, who can then turn to direct address and pledge worship and need. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Elmalılı rejects Le Bon’s conclusion: imagination affects people because it can represent reality, so reality remains the true source of its force. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Resûl-i Ekrem): The Prophet calls hamd the head of gratitude and says that whoever fails to praise God has not thanked Him. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Resûl-i Ekrem): The Prophet says that one who does not praise people does not praise God, making hamd generally commendable. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Hazret-i Ömeru’l-Fârûk): Umar’s remark that truth left him no friend conveys how difficult it is to remain faithful to truth. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The lām in lillah denotes entitlement, particularity, and ownership; the whole genus and every form of hamd belongs to God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Hamd’s fullest nature speaks through the Prophet’s Ahmediyyah and Muhammadiyyah; its ultimate reality belongs at its beginning and end to God alone. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The definite article in alhamd can express a known instance, totality, kind, or essence; because no particular hamd is presupposed, it here gathers the entire genus and all its ranks. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sound plural al-alamin foregrounds rational beings; a concise Turkish rendering is difficult, but it means God alone is Lord of all realms and especially their rational inhabitants. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sign in al-alamin, later clarified by al-Baqara’s description of the Qur’an as guidance for the Godfearing, signals the wisdom of revelation. | mentions 2:2 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Rab entails complete power to nurture, govern, guide, command, reward, and discipline, and also means owner; without a qualifying relation it is reserved for God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Nurturing is gradual movement toward perfection, visibly at work throughout the universe; contemplating the realms reveals the law of divine nurturing, while the realms themselves are known through their relation to God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (bir kısım felâsife): Some philosophers say the whole cosmos came to be all at once, with or without causes, rather than through gradual development. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Observation, experience, and reason jointly establish origination and development; science may affirm evolution, but it must recognize God as its complete first cause. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Some misuse evolutionary language by treating nature’s law as an uncaused absolute, displacing divine nurturing and making humanity the absolute summit of existence. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Science depends on causality: what comes into existence after not existing must have a cause, since nothing can arise from nothing. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Cause and effect must be proportionate: an effect cannot exceed its complete cause, and known effects disclose their cause; otherwise nonbeing would produce being. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Consciousness, knowledge, and reason cannot have a blind force or blind nature as their complete cause; natural laws require an intelligent necessary being. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Absolute knowledge is God’s eternal knowledge and self-disclosure; human knowledge is only a relative, contingent disclosure of that truth. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Without God’s perfect lordship and nurturing, neither existence nor development could be real; the world’s real share in these qualities reveals God as Lord of all realms. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The world is the collection of all things other than God that point to Him through causal relations; any part possessing an ordered relation can be called a realm. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The soul cannot itself be the ultimate cause of all truth: it acknowledges its limitations and does not claim to create at will or to have existed eternally. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Even the basic judgment I exist depends on affirming a true relation between self-awareness and being, which rests on God’s encompassing truth; all limited knowledge is thereby God’s gift. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sound rational plural and the definite article extend the meaning across all realms and foreground rational beings; understanding the Lord of the worlds requires conceiving rational agents. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Every ordered community of things, from inner awareness to external objects, is named a realm because it serves as a sign and means of knowing God’s creation, lordship, and perfection. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): For those who truly witness, nothing is seen without God also being seen behind, before, or with it; the world is other-than-God and God lies beyond it. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Signs of God’s unity may be immediate or detailed, sensory or imagined, and can be read through perception, memory, and reason; all require conscious attention. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Judging the self, consciousness, and external objects as truly corresponding already presupposes an encompassing necessary truth that guarantees their relations; this tacit attribution to God grounds our judgments. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The certainty I exist requires consciousness, and the truth of that self-awareness is guaranteed by the necessary testimony of the sovereign God, the beginning and end of every certainty. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The world is a sign of God, while God is its witness and light; the cited verses describe Him as the light of heaven and earth, witness over all, and first and last. | mentions 24:35, 41:53, 57:3 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Faith is assent to this light, and Islam is living out that assent and relation to God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): He renders the verse as praise belonging to the Lord of the worlds. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The basmala names God and His attributes while leaving humanity implicit; saying it establishes the divine relation that lets al-Fatiha bring humans forth with gratitude into speech. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): It then grants people speech to pledge exclusive worship and dependence, binding human will and the right to ask for guidance into an enduring covenant between God and humanity. | mentions 1:5 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The hidden humanity of the basmala becomes a speaking subject in al-Fatiha, while God is present as interlocutor; the surah captures the full relation of life and existence. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): A sacred hadith says al-Fatiha is divided equally between God and servant, with the servant entitled to what is asked; its opening praises and glorifies God, its middle is shared, and its ending is the servant's supplication. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): After invoking the Merciful God, the worshipper turns to praise Him and name His unrestricted lordship over all worlds. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Human conscience could not settle questions of the cosmos, the self, and life before it reached clear certainty about God, His attributes, and His relation to creation. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Islam therefore concentrated on freeing creed from every ambiguity through pure, comprehensive monotheism and an unambiguous account of God's absolute lordship. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Comparing Islam's simple, coherent account of God with the inherited tangle of beliefs reveals this creed as mercy for heart and mind. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Praising God wells up in a believer's heart at His mention: existence itself and ceaseless blessings call forth praise. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Praise begins and ends the Islamic worldview; the Qur'an says God alone is deity and that praise belongs to Him in this world and the next. | mentions 28:70 QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله [ ص ]): Ibn Umar reports that the Prophet described a servant's expansive praise, which two angels could not record; God ordered them to write it as spoken and promised reward when the servant meets Him. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): The phrase Lord of the worlds states a core creed: God's complete lordship extends across all creation. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Recognizing God's unrestricted lordship separates clear monotheism from beliefs that combine one creator with multiple powers governing life. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Some polytheists claimed they worshipped lesser patrons only to bring them nearer to God, while some People of the Book treated their rabbis and monks as lords. | mentions 39:3, 9:31 QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Making God Lord of all worlds points away from a chaotic crowd of competing lords toward one sovereign, bringing order, certainty, and confidence in His constant care. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله): Abu Hurayra reports in Sahih Muslim that God calls al-Fatiha a prayer divided between Himself and His servant: praise, mercy, and glorification are the Lord's; worship and aid are shared; guidance and the servant's request belong to the servant, who receives what he asks. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A fourth rule for a preface is to begin with praise of God. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (أبو هريرة عن النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report from Abu Hurayra, attributed to the Prophet, says an important matter not begun with praise of God is cut off; Ibn Ashur gives no grading. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): To allow praise of God's essential attributes under the majority's definition, scholars offer three replies: the attributes count as voluntary by their perfection, by their effects, or because God acts freely even when the praised attribute is not voluntary. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur answers that nonvoluntariness is a defect in human qualities but perfection in God's eternal, necessary attributes; no analogy to human choice is needed, as with God's having no child. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Hamd is the strongest language available to people for praising God, though it cannot fully convey realities beyond ordinary human experience. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The cited grammar says a definite source such as al-hamdu can be raised as a subject because it is declarative and definite; the sense of the omitted verb remains. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Raising hamd announces praise from the speaker and all creation, whereas the accusative announces only the speaker's praise. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Praise is foregrounded because revelation of the Quran is the immediate great blessing being acknowledged; its contextual importance can take precedence over God's name's intrinsic importance. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The genus article with the possessive l am implies all praise belongs to God: if any instance belonged to another, exclusive possession of the genus would fail; this is hyperbolic restriction. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): One group treats the sentence as a plain report that praise belongs to God, objecting that this leaves the speaker not actually praising Him. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Defenders of a purely declarative analysis answer that reporting praise recognizes God's beautiful attributes, that the speaker is included in the general report, or that inherited practice entails following it. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Dhu al-Rumma's phrase 'we brought into being thanks for its Creator' confirms that Arabs described praise or thanks as something they create, treating it as performative expression. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The name's origin recalls God specifically as deity and signals that He alone deserves worship because He created all. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Zamakhshari uses al-Ba'ith's 'ma'adh al-ilah' as evidence that Allah originated from al-ilah; Ibn Ashur says this is persuasive because ma'adh conventionally takes Allah as its complement. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (الزجاج ونسب إلى الخليل وسيبويه): Another group says the name was coined directly for God without derivation from alaha; one rationale is that Arabs named everything and would not leave God unnamed. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (جمع من العلماء): A group of scholars, cited by al-Tilimsani, says discussion of Allah's name should be avoided out of reverence and because it requires scriptural permission. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Rabb is an attribute of Allah: after praise is attached to His essence to show inherent entitlement, the attribute adds entitlement through His qualities; this is clearer than saying 'praise belongs to Lord of the worlds.' | mentions 83:6 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The four descriptions—Lord of the worlds, Merciful, Compassionate, King of Judgment Day—show each divine name's meaning merits praise and distinguishes God from alleged deities. | mentions 1:3, 1:4 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur prefers 'nurturer and governor' over 'owner' because it fits God's management of creation toward perfection and avoids redundancy with 'King of Judgment Day'; the latter can specify the afterlife. | mentions 1:4 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (النابغة): Al-Nabigha calls al-Nu'man ibn al-Harith rabb and uses rabb again of al-Nu'man when describing God's favor to him. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The Quran uses rabb for the Egyptian official in 12:23 and for multiple false gods in 12:39, confirming that Arabic applies the term to beings other than God. | mentions 12:23, 12:39 IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): An alam is a genus among existing things; the word may derive from knowledge or sign because each genus is distinguishable and serves as a sign by which it is known. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (التفتزاني): Al-Taftazani says alam names knowledgeable beings or each genus through which the Creator is known; singular alam normally needs a qualifying genitive, while 'the entire cosmos' is later theological terminology. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Exhausting all genera entails their individuals, since lordship over abstract essences makes no sense; the plural with the definite article explicitly signals comprehensive scope. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He also supports the sura's comprehensiveness by citing the sound report that al-Ikhlas counts as a third of the Qur'an because all its wording praises God. | mentions 112:1 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The opening praise gathers God's praised attributes, declares His uniqueness in divinity, and affirms resurrection and recompense. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A third explanation of Umm al-Qur'an is that it contains the Qur'an's theoretical wisdom and practical rulings, including theology, prophecy, admonition, worship, transactions, morals, and law. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu lillah implies all attributes of perfection that entitle God to exclusive praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Rabb al-alamin encompasses God's acts and creative attributes for those who affirm such attributes. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur accepts the explanation tied to each rak'ah because al-Fatiha is joined to another sura in every rak'ah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A hadith in both Sahihs from Abu Hurayra has God divide prayer between Himself and the servant, answering each portion of al-Fatiha and granting the servant's request. | mentions 1:2, 1:3, 1:4, 1:5, 1:6, 1:7 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو هريرة): The first proof is Malik's report from Abu Hurayra: God's division of prayer begins with al-Hamdu lillah, without mentioning the basmala; prayer here means recitation in prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (البيضاوي): Al-Baydawi explains pairing God's proper name with al-Rahman and al-Rahim as invoking His entitlement to assistance and mercy: al-Rahman signals the gifts involved in righteous action, with al-Rahim explained later. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-Fatiha models four rules for introductions: keep them brief, foreshadow the subject, use concise comprehensive wording, and begin with praise of God. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A p rologue should also be made of concise, comprehensive expressions and should open by praising God. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Because the Qur'an guides people and reforms both worldly and future life, listeners must prepare by removing stubbornness, error, and low impulses before receiving its wisdom. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أمية ابن أبي الصلت): Umayya ibn Abi al-Salt's praise of Abd Allah ibn Jud'an says the addressee's modesty and praise can make a request unnecessary. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ziyad ibn Abi Sufyan's Basra sermon was nicknamed al-Batra because it did not begin with praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الجمهور): The majority hold that madh is broader than hamd because it can concern voluntary or involuntary qualities. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A difficulty for defining hamd by voluntary qualities is that it would exclude God's essential attributes such as knowledge and power; proponents offer several answers. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الجمهور): One answer treats God's sublime attributes as voluntary because their possessor is independent; another says their voluntary effects make them equivalent to voluntary qualities. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur says the voluntary condition only excludes traits that are not perfections in human beings; God's necessary eternal attributes are perfections, so no workaround is needed. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu is nominative in all transmitted readings; its predicate is lillah, while an underlying analysis treats hamd as an accusative verbal noun replacing an omitted verb meaning “we praise God.” IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Raising hamd creates a nominal sentence for permanence, generic al for comprehensive praise, and fronting for emphasis; these effects are unavailable in the ordinary accusative construction. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Praise precedes God's name because this occasion is the beginning of Qur'anic revelation, the great blessing that brings success in both worlds and evokes the Revealer's beautiful attributes. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Situational importance can warrant foregrounding praise despite God's name being inherently more important; rhetoric follows the occasion and highlights what might otherwise go unnoticed. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Although the generic article is not itself exhaustive, its combination with the lām of divine specification implies that every instance of praise belongs to God; the restriction is rhetorical and emphatic. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): One camp treats the sentence as a plain report: reporting that God has praise acknowledges His beautiful qualities, and some say the speaker is included among those praising Him. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He says frequent use led Arabs to drop the hamza in al-ilah when referring to God, as in al-nas from al-unas, though they retained it in some speech. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الجوهري عن سيبويه): One proposed origin is لاه, a verbal noun meaning that God is hidden, with the definite article added to suggest the original sense. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj, with the view also attributed to al-Khalil and Sibawayh, holds that Allah was coined directly as God's name rather than derived from أله. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verse puts الحمد before لله because praise for revelation and Islam frames the opening recitation; contextual importance can precede God's intrinsic priority, while praising Him still attends to His affairs. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur prefers deriving رب from nurturing and directing: it suits God's governance and gradual completion of creatures, and avoids redundancy with ملك يوم الدين; one could answer that the worlds here mean only this world. | mentions 1:4 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Kashshaf limits رب here to master or king, allowing either a verbal noun or adjective, because the word most commonly carries those meanings. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The claim that Arabs reserved رب for God is false: they used it for human rulers and masters, idols, and particular possessions; الربّة was a name for al-ʿUzza and أرباب is plural. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (التلمساني): Al-Tilimsani reports a group of scholars who refrain from explaining the divine name out of reverence and because discussion requires scriptural permission; Ibn Ashur calls this an astonishing opinion. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): رب العالمين apposes God's name: praise first recognizes His intrinsic worth, then this description adds His descriptive worthiness for praise. | mentions 83:6 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The four descriptions that follow signal that each attribute also merits praise and distinguish God from the idols, objects, and elements other peoples worship. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Quranic expressions about Joseph's master and the rival lords show that رب can refer to someone besides God; the term remained available in Arabic usage. | mentions 12:23, 12:39 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): عالم names a class of beings, perhaps because each class marks itself out or enables knowledge of its maker; the form suggests an instrument, and its rational plural gives rational beings precedence over other classes. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (التفتزاني): Al-Taftazani defines عالم as knowledgeable beings or any species by which the Creator is known, not a singular name for everything besides God; Ibn Ashur endorses this as accurate Arabic usage. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The article in العالمين is exhaustive because the context supplies neither an external nor mental referent; it therefore covers every relevant species and, by implication, its individuals. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Praise is description of God’s beautiful acts; only God’s sincere servants, whom He draws near without intermediary, can properly describe Him, as 37:159 says. | mentions 1:2, 37:159 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God directly relates praise from selected prophets, Muhammad, and the purified inhabitants of Paradise; other creatures' praise is paired with glorification because they cannot encompass divine perfection. | mentions 23:28, 14:39, 17:111, 27:15, 10:10 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God makes the praise and description of His sincerely purified servants His own. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (النبي): A report transmitted by both communities from the Prophet says no one can enumerate praise of God as fully as He praises Himself. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The opening الحمد لله is taught etiquette: a servant could not rightly utter this praise without God first teaching it and speaking on the servant's behalf. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God alone has real ownership, which necessarily entails governance because whatever depends on Him for existence also depends on Him in its effects. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Alamin can mean all beings, kinds, or communities; because judgment concerns humans and jinn, the context most suitably means their communities. | mentions 3:42, 25:1, 7:80 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (الصادق): When al-Sadiq's father recovered a lost mule, he praised God only by saying الحمد لله and explained that every kind of praise is contained in that phrase. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (علي): Ali explains that God teaches servants general gratitude because His blessings cannot all be counted or known in detail; the author connects this to praise taught on the servant's behalf. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Everything is God's cr eation, and He made every created thing good; creation and goodness therefore coincide in what He creates. | mentions 40:62, 32:7 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God creates and acts with knowledge and choice, not under compulsion; therefore every being is His voluntary beautiful act, and all genuine praise belongs to Him. | mentions 39:4, 20:111, 20:8, 7:180 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Since God is beautiful in names and acts and all praised beauty comes from Him, every true act of praise is rightfully His. | mentions 20:8, 7:180 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Every effect and its perfections depend on their cause; since all causes end in God, all genuine beauty, praise, and thanks ultimately belong to Him. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (النبي): The Prophet transmits God's division of al-Fatiha between Himself and the servant: each phrase receives divine acknowledgement and a tailored promise of blessing, mercy, ease of judgment, reward, help, and fulfillment of the servant's requests. ABUDAWUD (الله تعالى): In a hadith qudsi transmitted by Abu Hurayra, God divides al-Fatiha between Himself and the servant: praise and glorification receive divine responses, worship is mutual, and the guidance petition belongs to the servant. CORPUSCORANICUM (Paret): Paret takes ʿālamīn primarily as people, translating the epithet as Lord of people throughout the world or Lord of the world’s inhabitants. | mentions 26:165, 29:6, 2:47, 2:122 CORPUSCORANICUM (author): Paret’s rendering may be factually right but omits the eschatological force; rabb an-nās would have expressed simply Lord of people. | mentions 114:1 CORPUSCORANICUM (author): This theological lexicon recasts the negatively charged early Meccan human as homo eschatologicus, belonging to both worlds under God’s rule. | mentions 103:1 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Hamd in its complete sense belongs only to God: human good is never fully independent of divine will, while unchosen gifts and qualities are directly the Creator’s work. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): All created things in the material and spiritual, visible and unseen worlds, in this life and the hereafter, are included in the worlds. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The visible domain is called the realm of dominion and witness, while immaterial existence is the unseen and angelic realm; God alone owns the latter, though worldly ownership can appear to belong to others. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Revelation compares the visible realm to a drop in the sea and a grain of sand beside the unseen; knowledge of cosmic scale points both to the limits of reason and the immensity of divine creation. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Contemplating the unseen’s immensity through faith leads from awe at God’s greatness to prostration and then to peace, security, love, nearness, and contentment. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Used alone, Rabb refers only to God and names His ownership and nurturing; in a construct such as master of a house, it can refer to others. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Madḥ is broader than hamd because one may praise beautiful pearls or jewels, but cannot give them hamd. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (النبي): The Prophet says that whoever does not praise people has not praised God, recognizing gratitude for people's real contributions. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah implies that God is living, possesses perfect qualities, and does beautiful deeds and favors; madḥ lillah would not convey all this. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Hamd is preferable to both madḥ and shukr because it covers God's qualities and favors to the speaker and all creation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The impersonal formula al-hamd lillah does not bind praise to a particular speaker, unlike I or we praise God, and presents God as universally praised. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Saying I praise God reports the speaker's act but does not establish that its object deserves praise; al-hamd lillah states God's entitlement without a specific speaker. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): The formula means God was praised before any praiser or thanker and remains praised from eternity to eternity. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah declares praise to be God's due by His very essence and abundant favors, unlike a report that one person praises Him. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Hamd is an inward recognition of a benefactor's favor and right to reverence; clai ming I praise God can be false if the heart lacks that recognition, while the formula states a fact. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The contrast resembles the claim of testimony: one may lie about witnessing, as the hypocrites do, but the proposition that God alone is God remains true. | mentions 63:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The author prefers both senses together: familiar praise belongs to God in a way that encompasses every kind and instance. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Most scholars treat al-hamd lillah as a report that praise is established for God; others call it performative praise, a transferred report, or a report that entails performance, and the author accepts both report and exaltation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-hamd lillah is better than inna al-hamd lillah because believers do not doubt the claim, and the unmarked formula can report praise while enacting exaltation; inna makes it mere report. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-hamd may appear to apply to God or another, so naming God clarifies it; worship can only be for God, so fronting You in 1:5 prevents an implication of worshipping another. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Word order varies with context: al-Jathiya foregrounds God as deserving praise because it confronts disbelievers' false beliefs and denials of His lordship. | mentions 45:10, 45:23, 45:24 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya answers denials of God's creative and beneficent lordship by showing Him as sole creator and provider, so He alone deserves praise; al-Fatiha addresses believers who seek steadfast guidance. | mentions 45:5, 45:12, 45:13, 45:22, 45:26 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya repeatedly uses restrictive constructions, matching its context of disbelievers who reject God and setting up its exclusive praise formula. | mentions 45:9, 45:10, 45:11, 45:24 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The many restrictive statements throughout al-Jathiya culminate in exclusive praise and sovereignty for God, so its context calls for fronting Him as the one deserving praise. | mentions 45:12, 45:15, 45:17, 45:24, 45:25, 45:26, 45:27, 45:28, 45:30, 45:32, 45:33, 45:34, 45:35, 45:36, 45:37 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Using God's proper name rather than an attribute avoids implying that He deserves praise only for one quality; the name points to His essence and entitlement across all attributes. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (روح المعاني): The proper name makes God's essence, rather than one particular quality, the ground of His right to praise. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Even attributes such as Living or Knowing can describe others, and even unique descriptions may not explicitly identify God, so Allah is the clearest name. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb can mean owner, master, educator, guardian, and benefactor; the Lord of the worlds embodies these roles. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): An owner, master, and beneficent educator has a stronger claim to praise, and the Lord can direct His subjects as He wills. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Although some restrict worlds to accountable beings, Pharaoh's question is answered by describing the Lord of the worlds, not by defining the word worlds. | mentions 3:108, 26:23, 26:24 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Moses' reply lists qualities of the Lord rather than defining al-alamin, just as calling a house's lord a merchant does not define the house. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): This plural suits al-Fatiha because its worship, aid, guidance, and moral categories address accountable creatures; Rabb al-alamin may mean their Lord or all creation with rational beings predominant. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb al-alamin also answers Jews who claim God is Lord only of Israel: He is Lord of all humanity and accountable creatures. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Fatiha's concise Rabb al-alamin fits its focus on accountable creatures, whereas al-Jathiya explicitly names heaven and earth after repeatedly detailing their signs and God's rule over them. | mentions 45:3, 45:4, 45:13, 45:22, 45:27, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya details God's lordship over humans and other living beings through creation, subjection, sustenance, and resurrection. | mentions 45:4, 45:12, 45:13, 45:26 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God's mercy toward believers and the laws sent to humanity also display His lordship; al-Fatiha names moral classes while al-Jathiya details the wider cosmos. | mentions 45:30, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The differing formulations of Rabb al-alamin and Lord of heaven, earth, and worlds each fit the focus of their surah. | mentions 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya mentions God's true sovereignty because it condemns arrogant mockers, while al-Fatiha has no such context and mentions only praise. | mentions 45:7, 45:8, 45:9, 45:31, 45:33, 45:35, 45:37 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Other opening praises name God as creator, originator, owner of all things, or sender of scripture; each fits its surah and the openings complement one another. | mentions 6:1, 35:1, 34:1, 18:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Anam's creation of heaven and earth, darkness, and light is one part of the wider lordship over all worlds in al-Fatiha. | mentions 6:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Saba's statement that all in heaven and earth belongs to God is one aspect of al-Fatiha's general praise of the Lord of the worlds. | mentions 34:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Fatir's origination differs from Anam's creation by measure, yet both are aspects of Rabb al-alamin; Anam then mentions darkness and light, while Fatir mentions angels as messengers. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The pair of openings complement each other: Anam follows creation with light and darkness, while Fatir follows origination with the creation of spiritual messengers. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Rabb al-alamin suits the request for guidance to the straight path because a nurturer's primary task is guidance, a frequent Qur'anic association. | mentions 20:49, 20:50, 20:122, 87:1, 87:2, 87:3, 6:161, 3:8, 18:24, 26:62, 37:99, 28:22 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (التفسير القيم): The names following praise show God is praiseworthy in divinity, lordship, mercy, and kingship. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The surah gathers the foundations of Islamic belief: God and His perfect attributes, monotheism, the Last Day, divine power, and the messengers and revelation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God’s lordship over all entails that He alone is Lord, while exclusive worship and reliance affirm that there is no deity but Him. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Praise of God implies that a Creator exists, knows, has power, and deserves praise and glorification. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Lord of all worlds establishes God’s oneness and ownership of all creation, with no other lord or deity. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Razi summarizes praise as God’s existence and choice, lordship as unity, mercy as compassion in both worlds, and Judgment Day as wisdom and mercy. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): The surah is truly the Mother of the Book because it contains both doctrinal foundations and the two pillars of action. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Because hamd is a state of the heart, someone saying أحمد الله without reverence may lie, whereas الحمد لله remains true whether or not the speaker is attentive. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The author favors the exhaustive sense, supported by the Prophetic prayer اللهم لك الحمد كله: all known praise belongs to God without exception. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (أكثر النحاة والمفسرين): Most grammarians and exegetes treat الحمد لله as a report that praise belongs to God. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (قسم): Another group treats the expression as performative because it embodies love. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The phrase can function as either report or invocation depending on context, letting the speaker affirm God's praise while expressing appreciation and reverence. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The context contrast explains the order: al-Fatiha has believers confessing worship and asking for help, while al-Jathiya answers unbelievers' claims about life and death by foregrounding God's praise. | mentions 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Jathiya repeatedly discusses God's lordship over the heavens, earth, creation, and worlds; its closing praise gathers these themes, unlike al-Fatiha's focus on the worlds and their accountable groups. | mentions 45:4, 45:27, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Using God's proper name means He deserves praise for His essence, not merely for one attribute such as power or creation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The name Allah suits the move to worship because it denotes the deity to be worshipped, and the author says it is paired with worship more than fifty times in the Quran. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-alamin may refer to all accountable generations or a subset, as in Lot's people; it fits al-Fatiha's concern with guidance and human moral groups and answers Jews who claimed God only as their Lord. | mentions 15:70 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Rabb also fits the request for guidance because education is a core sense of lordship and guidance frequently follows the name Rabb in scripture. | mentions 20 :49, 20:50, 20:122, 87:1, 87:2, 87:3, 6:161, 18:24, 26:62, 37:99, 28:22 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Fatiha contains faith and good works, affirming God, the Last Day, angels, messengers, and scriptures, and joins lordship's unity to the unity of worship; it is therefore the Mother of the Book. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says that whoever fails to thank people has not thanked God. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Hamd is preferable to madḥ and shukr because it covers both God's intrinsic qualities and His benefactions, whereas shukr applies only to favors and madḥ need not reflect real merit. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The wording الحمد لله states that God merits praise for His qualities and deeds, whereas أحمد الله reports only the speaker's own act of praising. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (الرازي): Al-Razi says أحمد الله declares the speaker able to praise, while الحمد لله says God was praised before any created praiser and remains praised eternally. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): الحمد لله also declares praise to be God's rightful property because of His abundant favors; a personal act of praising does not assert this entitlement. STUDYQURAN (author): Al-ḥamd praises God for His qualities and thanks Him for worldly favors and next-world reward. STUDYQURAN (author): Others restrict the worlds to humans and jinn because only these communities need a warner, as in 25:1. | mentions 25:1 STUDYQURAN (author): Some say the worlds are all existent things other than God; al-Rāzī adds that God can actualize infinitely many unknown universes, making Him Lord of all existence and space. | mentions 17:44 STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet says that praising God, Lord of the worlds, constitutes thanks and brings increased bounty. STUDYQURAN (author): Thanks responds to what has already been received, while praise is more universal because it extols qualities prior to any gift. STUDYQURAN (author): The definite form al-ḥamd indicates that all praise and gratitude belong to God. STUDYQURAN (author): God praises Himself in the opening so humans can praise Him in divine speech, which He knows is more adequate than their own words. STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet acknowledges that no one can enumerate praise due to God, who is as He praised Himself. STUDYQURAN (author): Muslims use the basmalah to consecrate actions at their beginning and al-ḥamd to thank God upon completion; etiquette answers any inquiry about one’s condition with praise. STUDYQURAN (author): Rabb denotes master, arranger, and possessor; of God it means peerless Master who orders affairs and owns creation. STUDYQURAN (author): Some relate rabb to cultivation: God is caretaker and trainer of things and of human souls, hearts, and spirits. STUDYQURAN (author): Shiite law permits reciting the Fātiḥah in the third and fourth cycles or an alternative formula praising God. STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet identifies the Fātiḥah as the greatest sūrah, calling it Praise be to God and the seven oft-repeated, and linking it to 15:87. | mentions 15:87 STUDYQURAN (God): In a hadith qudsī, God responds to the servant’s praise, magnification, and glorification during the prayer, which is divided between them. STUDYQURAN (author): The Fātiḥah is a prayer encapsulating metaphysical and eschatological realities and teaches the proper human disposition toward God. STUDYQURAN (author): Its structure moves from God’s nature in the first three verses, through the God-human relationship, to human states in the final three. STUDYQURAN (author): Most scholars reject a Medinan origin because Muslims needed the sūrah to perform obligatory prayers. STUDYQURAN (author): Al-Fātiḥah means The Opening: it opens the Book, each prayer cycle, many everyday acts, and may open the breast to faith. STUDYQURAN (author): It is called the Mother of the Quran because it contains the meaning of the entire Quran. KASHSHAF-FULL: Lordship, ownership, universal beneficence and final authority warrant praise. RAZI; RAZI-FULL(Prophet,أبو هريرة عن النبي,السري السقطي,الله تعالى,المخالف,المعتزلة,النبي,الواسطي,داود); QURTUBI(أبو سعيد الخدري,ابن عطاء,الجنيد,الحسن,الربيع بن أنس عن أبي العالية,الزجاج والخليل,الطبري,الفراء وأبو عبيدة,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,بعض العلماء,جعفر الصادق,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,سيبويه,شقيق بن إبراهيم,طائفة,قتادة,قيل,وهب بن منبه); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو جعفر الطبري,أبو عبد الله,ابن عطية,الحسن,الطبري,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,بعض العلماء,جعفر الصادق,سيبويه,طائفة من العلماء); BAYDAWI(النبي); BAYDAWI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); NASAFI(أبو هريرة عن النبي,النبي,الواسطي); NASAFI-FULL; QASHANI; ABUHAYYAN(ابن الأنباري,الحسن وزيد بن علي,الحسن وقتادة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR(آخرون,أبو العالية,أبو سعيد الخدري,ابن جرير,ابن عباس,الزجاج,القرطبي,الله تعالى,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,سعيد بن المسيب,طائفة,علي بن أبي طالب,كعب الأحبار,مروان بن محمد بن مروان بن الحكم,وهب بن منبه); BURHAN(ابن حبان,قيل); NASHR; BIQAI(أبو الحسن الحرالي); BIQAI-FULL(أبو هريرة,الحرالي); DURR(أبو العالية,أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي,ابن عباس,الله,جعفر بن محمد,رسول الله,عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص,علي,قتادة,كعب,موسى بن طلحة,وهب); DURR-FULL(أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي,أبو هريرة,أنس,ابن عباس,الأسود بن سريع,الحكم بن عمير,الله تعالى,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,جعفر بن محمد,رجاء الغنوي,عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص,علي,قتادة,كعب,واثلة بن الأسقع,وهب); ITQAN(ابن العربي,ابن عباس,الإمام فخر الدين,الخويي,الطيبي,الغزالي,ناصر الدين بن الميلق); BURSEVI; ALUSI(أبو العباس المرسي,أنس,ابن الهمام,ابن عمر,الأكثر,الدواني,الزمخشري,السيوطي,الشيخ الأكبر,الفلاسفة,الماتريدي,النواس بن سمعان,بعض الأخبار,بعض العارفين,بعض ساداتنا,بعض ساداتنا الصوفية,بعضهم,داود,صاحب التجريد,كعب الأحبار,معظم العلماء); ALUSI-FULL(أبو العباس المرسي,أبو هريرة,أرباب الظاهر,ابن الهمام,ابن عمر,الأكثر,الدواني,الزمخشري,السيوطي,الشيخ الأكبر,الماتريدي,الملا خسرو,النبي ﷺ,النواس بن سمعان,بعض الأخبار,بعض ساداتنا الصوفية,صاحب التجريد,كعب الأحبار); ELMALILI(Hazret-i Ömeru’l-Fârûk,Resûl-i Ekrem,bir kısım felâsife); QUTB-ZILAL(رسول الله,رسول الله [ ص ]); IBNASHUR(أبو هريرة عن النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم,التفتزاني,الزجاج ونسب إلى الخليل وسيبويه,النابغة,جمع من العلماء,صاحب الكشاف); IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو هريرة,أمية ابن أبي الصلت,البيضاوي,التفتزاني,التلمساني,الجمهور,الجوهري عن سيبويه,الزجاج,النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم,صاحب الكشاف); TABATABAI(الصادق,النبي,علي); ABUDAWUD(الله تعالى); CORPUSCORANICUM(Paret); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(التفسير القيم,الرازي,النبي,روح المعاني); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT(أكثر النحاة والمفسرين,الرازي,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,قسم); STUDYQURAN(God,Prophet Muhammad); KASHSHAF-FULL; - 1:2/q13/p2 God sustains every contingent being. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Knowledge of God’s existence is theoretical, not immediate; the world of heavens, earth, seas, minerals, plants, and animals needs a governing, originating, nurturing, and sustaining cause. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ʿālamīn includes everything besides God, which depends on Him for existence and continuance; every indivisible particle and individual accident proves the wise, powerful God. | mentions 17:44 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Rāzī prefers Rabb al-ʿālamīn to Creator of the worlds because sustaining creatures after their beginning is disputed, and the phrase stresses their dependence on God at every moment. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Anʿām’s praise for creating the heavens, earth, darkness, and light is one subset of Rabb al-ʿālamīn; dependence on God for preservation entails dependence for origination. | mentions 6:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since possible beings need God to bring them into existence and preserve them, He is their deity as creator and their Lord as sustainer. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The ascent continues through ever-higher spiritual ranks until God, the source of all good and mercy; thus the spirit-world is the unseen and God's presence the unseen of the unseen. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Another derivation makes rabb a descriptive form from rabba-yarubbuhu; it then means owner because an owner preserves and nurtures what is owned. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Contingent things need an originator at coming-to-be and a sustainer for as long as they exist. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Rabb is used absolutely only of God, while for human masters it is restricted by a possessive phrase, as in Joseph’s story; al-Wasiti explains God as creator at the beginning, sustainer, and forgiver at the end. | mentions 12:23, 12:50 NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (الواسطى): Al-Wasiti explains Rabb as creator at the beginning, sustainer through nourishment, and forgiver at the end, and calls it God's greatest name. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): God is Lord of the worlds as origin, preserver, and manager of all; worlds means everything known to God, and the sound masculine plural reflects encompassing knowledge or predominance. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): God's lordship and mercy make Him worthy of praise; lordship as mastery or ownership is followed fittingly by mercy, which sustains servants' hope after failure. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (مسعود التفتازاني): Al-Taftazani maps the Fatiha's blessings to two creations and t wo preservations: creation in Rabb al-alamin, preservation in mercy, second creation in judgment, and afterlife preservation in worship onward. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عائشة): During a drought prayer, the Prophet invoked God as Lord of the worlds, the Merciful, and Master of Judgment, asking for rain that would sustain them; Abu Dawud calls the report rare with a good chain, and says it supports the Medinan no-alif reading. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Every possible being remains under God’s nurture: without the continuing influx of divine support and the removal of every impediment, it would return to nonexistence. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Because contingent beings need a cause both to begin and to persist, continual divine preservation is itself tarbiya; keeping even indefinitely many possible obstacles absent sustains a thing. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Words often become conventionally narrow because one instance is common or other instances are unknown; this can obscure Qur’anic and Prophetic meanings that preserve original lexical breadth. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Praise is like the head because speech makes meaning clearest, and the head is the most evident, elevated, and sustaining member; this may explain why God names praise first. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī argues that comprehensiveness fits this passage’s total lordship, mercy, and continuing sovereignty; he contrasts it with another surah whose relevant details are absent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers the nominative nominal sentence because, in context, it expresses established and continuing praise, whereas a verbal sentence ordinarily presents renewed action; permanence also depends on context, not form alone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Every contingent being depends on God's continual nurturing for existence and persistence; sustaining it also means keeping all its possible impediments absent. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Quṭb contrasts Islam's continuing divine care with Aristotle's alleged view that God creates the cosmos and then ignores it as beneath His attention. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): In Arabic, rabb can mean master or the one who manages, reforms, and nurtures; God continuously tends every creature rather than leaving the universe unattended. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): With the definite article, rabb is used in the Qur'an exclusively for God, sole sustainer of all creation and ultimate authority. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): To preserve the eschatological dimension, translate rabb al-ʿālamīn as Lord of the worlds, with people implied; translate it simply as people where that reference is explicit. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God originates heaven and earth from nothing, creates by measuring and shaping, owns them and their contents, sustains and repairs them, and sends scripture to guide creation. | mentions 3:49 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): These openings jointly describe God as originator, shaper, owner, sustainer, and guide through revealed books; Rabb al-alamin encompasses all these qualities and worlds. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Rabb highlights that creation depends on God not only when it begins but also while it continues, a dependence disputed for things in their state of persistence. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Rabb al-alamin is the apt continuation after praise: Rabb means owner, master, educator, benefactor, and caretaker, and encompasses God's attributes. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The opening attributes span all time: praise before creation, at creation, through continuing mercy, and through the unending recompense of the afterlife, as 28:70 says. | mentions 28:70 STUDYQURAN (author): The point beneath bāʾ can symbolize the Divine Pen’s first drop of ink, which inscribed all archetypes on the Preserved Tablet before creation; thus basmalah marks both Quranic and cosmic beginnings. | mentions 68:1, 96:4, 85:22 [31 notes: RAZI-FULL; BAYDAWI; NASAFI; NASAFI-FULL(الواسطى); QASHANI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(مسعود التفتازاني); DURR-FULL(عائشة); ALUSI; ALUSI-FULL; QUTB-ZILAL; ASAD-NOTES; CORPUSCORANICUM; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; STUDYQURAN; ALUSI-FULL+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT+]
- 1:2/q13/p3 The Lord of all worlds transcends created place. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because worlds include everything besides God, including space and time, their Lord created them and existed before them; this proves His transcendence beyond place, direction, and spatial extension. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The same lordship excludes indwelling: the creator precedes every created locus and therefore never depends on a place, against Christians and adherents of indwelling. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The world's evident order and its fitness for human interests show that its maker knows; praise thus implies God's existence, transcendence from place and indwelling, and utmost power, knowledge, and wisdom. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise precedes tasbīḥ because it includes transcendence and adds God’s beneficence, making Him beyond mere completeness. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Prayer seeks remembrance of the heart, and this concise surah gathers lordship and servitude and is equated with the mighty Qur'an; nothing else can replace it. | mentions 20:14, 15:87 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The opening glorification reveals the lights of divine majesty and moves from declaring God's transcendence to praising Him. QURTUBI, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Another view makes praise broader because it includes both gratitude and commendation, can stand in place of gratitude, and is not itself replaced by gratitude. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Al-Fatiha begins in gratitude, places humans in the worlds under a hidden divine relation, renews the manifestation of mercy, and trains consciousness through truth and instruction until people recognize God and themselves. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Because human power cannot detail every relation, he will sketch them in places and pause especially over al-Fatiha. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (سيبويه): Sibawayhi explains that praise expressions originally use an omitted verb in the absolute accusative, as in 'we praise God with praise'; the verbal noun replaces the verb. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The subject is deliberately fronted because another familiar formula could place the predicate first; the choice itself signals emphasis. | mentions 45:36 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): God placed praise before the supplication, following Arabic eloquence in which requests to great people begin with commendation. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سيبويه): For hamdan or shukran, an understood verb such as “I praise” is omitted because the source itself stands in its place, as in the established supplicatory formulas. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The al in al-hamd is generic: because the verbal noun stands in place of an action, the definite form presents the kind of praise as familiar and makes it prominent, rather than identifying one instance. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Creatures cannot comprehend God's perfection; their praise would limit Him to their understanding, so they must first declare Him transcendent of what they delimit. | mentions 42:5, 17:44, 20:110, 16:74 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God is praiseworthy in His essence, lordship, rightly placed mercy, and ownership of Judgment Day; the sequence spans all times from eternity through the endless afterlife. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The sequence praises God for His essence, lordship, properly placed mercy, and ownership and kingship on the day of recompense. [17 notes: RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI(بعض العلماء); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR(سيبويه); IBNASHUR-FULL(سيبويه); TABATABAI; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 1:2/q13/p4 The verse bears on the source of faith and human acts. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Jabriyya argue that if humans created faith, the human would deserve more praise for this noblest act than God; therefore God creates faith. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Jabriyya argue that Muslims praise God for the blessing of faith; if faith were a human act, praising God for it would wrongly credit Him with another’s act. | mentions 3:188 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Jabriyya argue that obligatory acts only avert blame and cannot earn praise, while voluntary grace would make God gain praise and thus be perfected by another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الجبرية): The Jabriyya infer that faith too comes from God: all praise belongs to Him, praise follows blessing, and faith is the greatest blessing. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الجبرية): They argue that God’s self-praise, though blameworthy among people, shows divine acts cannot be judged by human standards and challenges Muʿtazilī principles. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (المعتزلة): The Muʿtazila say absolute praise is deserved by one whose acts contain no evil, rulings no injustice, and judgments no oppression; they reject the Jabriyya picture of God creating disbelief then punishing it. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (القدرية): The Qadariyya argue that God would not truly be Lord if He created a disbeliever’s unbelief and then punished him, or commanded faith while preventing it. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الجبرية): The Jabriyya answer that faith is the greatest blessing, so if it comes from God He is the Lord and benefactor of all. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): Muslims agree that God is praised for all blessings, including faith; since faith belongs to the created worlds, Qurtubi infers that faith is God’s act and creation, contrary to the Qadari claim that humans create it. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Muslims agree God is praised for all blessings; since faith is a blessing and belongs among all created things, faith is God’s act and creation, against the Qadariyya. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): For Nasafi, the definite article in al-hamd is comprehensive, against the Mu‘tazila; its attachment to Allah, the name of the divine essence, gathers all perfections and reflects the doctrine of created acts. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The definite article in al-hamd is exhaustive according to the author's school, against the Mu'tazila; joining it to Allah's proper name gathers every perfection and rests on the doctrine of created acts. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Some tie the generic reading to the Muʿtazilī account that human actions belong to their agents; al-Ālūsī replies that good acts still depend on God’s enabling and empowering. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): One argument says Muʿtazilīs assign praise for human deeds to humans because they create their acts; the reply is that even good deeds depend on God’s power and enabling, so praise ultimately returns to Him. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Even on the Muʿtazilī account, people’s praise does not displace God’s: He enables their acts and bestows the conditions, while their own agency merits a distinct, lesser praise. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الأكثر): The question is how a contingent human act of praise can be for God without inhering in Him; relation to an object does not entail inherence, as knowledge relates to its objects. [16 notes: RAZI-FULL(الجبرية,القدرية,المعتزلة); QURTUBI; QURTUBI-FULL; NASAFI; NASAFI-FULL; ALUSI; ALUSI-FULL(الأكثر)]
- 1:2/q13/p5 The verse bears on the duty of gratitude. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī argues that gratitude is due at all times, even before revelation: the verse makes praise God’s right without qualification and grounds it in His nurturing and mercy. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الإمام): An argument for rational gratitude before revelation says the verse establishes God's praiseworthiness through creation, mercy, and future judgment; al-Alusi replies that this proof itself is scriptural, not rational. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Alusi says deriving the duty of gratitude from God's own revealed declaration only proves it through revelation, so it cannot establish a pre-revelation rational duty. JISHUMI: Al-Jishumī says praise and gratitude are obligatory within one's capacity for past and future blessings. JISHUMI: Al-Jishumī says praise and gratitude are obligatory within one's capacity for past and future blessings. [5 notes: ALUSI-FULL(الإمام); JISHUMI; RAZI-FULL+]

## 1:2/q14 What does the divided-prayer report say?  [* · reports]
- 1:2/q14/p1 God responds to praise, mercy, worship, and the servant's requests in the divided prayer. — RAZI, d. 606 (author): The worlds comprise every existent other than God: spatial bodies, incorporeal entities, and attributes; bodies divide into simple entities and composites. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ʿālamīn includes everything besides God, which depends on Him for existence and continuance; every indivisible particle and individual accident proves the wise, powerful God. | mentions 17:44 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): In the divided-prayer report, God calls the basmala glorification, al-Hamd praise, the two mercy names extolling, the Master of Judgment entrustment, and the remaining response a sharing and a request granted. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ʿālamīn means every existent other than God; these beings comprise extended bodies, separate entities, and attributes, with bodies divided into simple and composite kinds. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Possible existents divide into spatial bodies, attributes of bodies, and things neither; bodies include celestial spheres and scriptural entities, earthly elements and composites, while attributes comprise about forty genera. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الله تعالى): In a divine report transmitted by the Prophet, God responds to each Fatiha phrase: the opening remembers Him, praise praises Him, the mercy names magnify Him, Judgment Day glorifies Him or is entrusted to Him, worship belongs to Him, aid relies on Him, and guidance is the servant's request. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The report that God says “my servant praised Me” shows that praise is higher than remembrance. NASAFI, d. 710 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): Abu Hurayra reports God’s dialogue dividing the Fatiha between Himself and the servant: praise, glorification, and exaltation belong to God; the worshipper’s request for guidance and the response to it belong to the servant, while worship and aid are shared. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The report begins God's response when the servant says “Praise belongs to God,” which indicates the basmala is not part of al-Fatiha; if it is outside al-Fatiha, consensus excludes it from other surahs too. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (سعيد بن المسيب): A similar count of one thousand worlds, divided into six hundred sea and four hundred land worlds, is also attributed to Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الله تعالى): In a divine report, God divides the prayer between Himself and His servant, responds to each recited section, calls 1:5 shared between them, and grants the servant's request. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali explains the sound report that God divided prayer between Himself and His servant: the first three verses are God's praise, which He accepts from the reciting servant as praise and glorification. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (أبو هريرة): In the authentic report in Muslim and the four Sunan, God says the Fatiha is divided between Himself and His servant: He answers praise, glorification and worship, and grants the servant's final requests. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الله تعالى): When the servant says “al-hamd lillah,” God says the servant has thanked and praised Him. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says that when the servant says al-hamd, God says the servant has thanked Him. ITQAN, d. 911 (الطيبي): Al-Tibi divides the Fatiha’s religious knowledge into foundations (God and attributes, prophethood, resurrection), branches (worship), ethical perfection and seeking the path, and accounts of earlier peoples with promise and threat. | mentions 1:2, 1:4, 1:6, 1:7 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الطيبي): Al-Ṭībī answers that singular exhaustive scope could cover individuals yet fail to make the multiplicity of kinds explicit, so the plural is clearer here. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا): A Sufi account divides praise from God into praise of His essence, unity, manifestations, qualities, effects, and acts, while creaturely praise varies with knowledge and capacity. | mentions 6:91, 20:110, 37:180, 53:42 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The term can denote the aggregate or one or more kinds, but it is not normally applied to a single person; in its basic sense every dependent individual is a sign of its Maker. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The claim that exclusive genus praise is more comprehensive than exclusive individuals is reversible: if every individual is exclusively God’s, no instance remains outside the exclusive genus. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الغزالي): Al-Ghazālī applies the plural-language explanation to the report that congregational prayer exceeds an individual prayer by twenty-seven degrees. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): A mystical account divides praise into praise originating from God and from creation: God’s praise concerns His essence, manifestations, attributes, and effects, while creatures praise His essence, names, actions, secrets, and wisdom according to their understanding. | mentions 6:91, 20:110, 37:180, 53:42 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Although common usage applies ʿālam to the totality or to a kind, not ordinarily to an individual, its original sense can apply to every part, since every contingent thing witnesses its need for the necessary Creator. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The plural al-ʿālamīn guarantees inclusion of every created kind: a singular definite noun could be heard as the familiar perceptible world, whereas the plural distributes lordship across all named kinds and their individuals. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Alam derives from the idea of a means of knowledge and is a collective noun; it is not applied to a wholly isolated individual. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): A sacred hadith says al-Fatiha is divided equally between God and servant, with the servant entitled to what is asked; its opening praises and glorifies God, its middle is shared, and its ending is the servant's supplication. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله): Abu Hurayra reports in Sahih Muslim that God calls al-Fatiha a prayer divided between Himself and His servant: praise, mercy, and glorification are the Lord's; worship and aid are shared; guidance and the servant's request belong to the servant, who receives what he asks. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Exhausting all genera entails their individuals, since lordship over abstract essences makes no sense; the plural with the definite article explicitly signals comprehensive scope. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A hadith in both Sahihs from Abu Hurayra has God divide prayer between Himself and the servant, answering each portion of al-Fatiha and granting the servant's request. | mentions 1:2, 1:3, 1:4, 1:5, 1:6, 1:7 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The article in العالمين is exhaustive because the context supplies neither an external nor mental referent; it therefore covers every relevant species and, by implication, its individuals. ABUDAWUD (الله تعالى): In a hadith qudsi transmitted by Abu Hurayra, God divides al-Fatiha between Himself and the servant: praise and glorification receive divine responses, worship is mutual, and the guidance petition belongs to the servant. STUDYQURAN (God): In a hadith qudsī, God responds to the servant’s praise, magnification, and glorification during the prayer, which is divided between them. MUQATIL: The Abū Hurayra report divides the sūra: praise is thanks, mercy names praise, kingship glorification, and the rest the servant's share. TAB: Abū Hurayra and Jābir versions describe God's responses and the division of prayer, one Abū Hurayra version lacking Prophetic attribution. TAB: Abū Hurayra and Jābir versions describe God's responses and the division of prayer, one Abū Hurayra version lacking Prophetic attribution. TAB: Abū Hurayra and Jābir versions describe God's responses and the division of prayer, one Abū Hurayra version lacking Prophetic attribution. TAB-FULL: Jābir's prophetic report divides the prayer. TAB-FULL: Abū Hurayra versions give God's answer to each portion; one lacks attribution to the Prophet. TAB-FULL: Abū Hurayra versions give God's answer to each portion; one lacks attribution to the Prophet. IBNABIHATIM: A sacred report has God acknowledge the worshipper's praise and extolling. THALABI: The divine report divides prayer, counting basmala as glorification and assigning each following portion its response. BAGHAWI: Al-Baghawī calls the divided-prayer report ṣaḥīḥ. [42 notes: RAZI; RAZI-FULL(أبو هريرة عن النبي,الله تعالى); NASAFI(أبو هريرة عن النبي); IBNKATHIR(الله تعالى,سعيد بن المسيب); BIQAI-FULL(أبو هريرة,الحرالي); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,الله تعالى); ITQAN(الطيبي); ALUSI(الطيبي,بعض ساداتنا); ALUSI-FULL(الغزالي,بعض ساداتنا الصوفية); ELMALILI; QUTB-ZILAL(رسول الله); IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL(النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم); ABUDAWUD(الله تعالى); STUDYQURAN(God); MUQATIL(أبو هريرة عن النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); TAB(أبو هريرة,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); TAB-FULL(أبو هريرة,أبو هريرة عن رسول الله,جابر بن عبد الله الأنصاري عن رسول الله); IBNABIHATIM(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); THALABI(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); BAGHAWI(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); NASAFI-FULL+]

## 1:2/q15 What is said about the Fātiḥa's revelation and verse count?  [* · sciences]
turns on: Reports differ on Meccan or Medinan revelation and on whether the basmala is counted.
- 1:2/q15/p1 The sūra is Meccan and generally counted as seven verses. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Kahf’s praise for the revealed Book concerns spiritual nurture through knowledge and unveiling, one kind of the Fātiḥa’s universal nurturing. | mentions 18:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Scholars disagree whether gratitude is owed by reason or only by revealed law; the latter cite the rule that punishment awaits a messenger and the removal of excuses by messengers. | mentions 17:15, 4:165 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): God arranged night for rest and day for work, the sun and moon for timekeeping, stars for navigation, and earth and mountains for habitation; reflecting on nature reveals His providence. | mentions 10:5, 6:97, 78:6 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Contemplating creatures reveals more mercy, favor, and beneficence than evil, because good is essential and evil incidental; therefore praise and thanks predominate. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (النبي): A prophetic report says God has seventy veils of light; if unveiled, the splendor of His face would burn everything perceived by sight, and only prophetic light reveals the number.
