RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The opening glorification reveals the lights of divine majesty and moves from declaring God's transcendence to praising Him. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): A further view takes it here to mean people: each person contains counterparts of the cosmos that reveal the Creator, as in the verse about looking within oneself. | mentions 51:21 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The passage names al-Baqara 2:281 as the last revealed verse and says its recitation position between the debt verse and the verse before it shows revelation and recitation order can differ. | mentions 2:281, 75:17 BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): It is called the seven oft-repeated because it has seven verses, each can stand for seven Qur'anic portions, and reciting them is said to earn the whole Qur'an's reward or close seven gates of Hell. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Human beings are described as a synthesis and miniature of the cosmos; reflection on oneself and the earth reveals signs of the Creator. | mentions 51:20, 51:21 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): ʿĀlam originally means a means of knowing, then came to denote what reveals the Creator: all that exists besides Him, substances and accidents, either as the whole or as each kind. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Humanity is a concentrated summary of created things, and reflection on the self reveals the Creator's greatness; the apt reading here remains universal. | mentions 51:20, 51:21 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Alusi says deriving the duty of gratitude from God's own revealed declaration only proves it through revelation, so it cannot establish a pre-revelation rational duty. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Nurturing is gradual movement toward perfection, visibly at work throughout the universe; contemplating the realms reveals the law of divine nurturing, while the realms themselves are known through their relation to God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Without God’s perfect lordship and nurturing, neither existence nor development could be real; the world’s real share in these qualities reveals God as Lord of all realms. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Al-Fatiha is Meccan and has seven verses; Muslims recite it at least seventeen times daily, more with voluntary prayers, and may repeat it without limit. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Comparing Islam's simple, coherent account of God with the inherited tangle of beliefs reveals this creed as mercy for heart and mind. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The claim that al-Fatiha was named for being revealed once in Mecca and again in Medina is very remote: repetition of revelation is not accepted, and the sura is agreed to be Meccan. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The sura has seven verses by agreement among reciters and commentators, with a few dissenting counts. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Praise precedes God's name because this occasion is the beginning of Qur'anic revelation, the great blessing that brings success in both worlds and evokes the Revealer's beautiful attributes. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): The doxology was already used in the early Meccan period; in middle Meccan texts it is joined to the epithet rabb al-ʿālamīn, itself attested early. | mentions 52:48, 37:182, 39:75, 83:6, 81:27, 81:29, 69:43, 68:52, 56:80 CORPUSCORANICUM (author): This theological lexicon recasts the negatively charged early Meccan human as homo eschatologicus, belonging to both worlds under God’s rule. | mentions 103:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): These openings jointly describe God as originator, shaper, owner, sustainer, and guide through revealed books; Rabb al-alamin encompasses all these qualities and worlds. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 STUDYQURAN (author): Most scholars place al-Fātiḥah among the earliest sūrahs revealed in Makkah. STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): A report says nothing like the Fātiḥah was revealed in the Torah, Psalms, Gospel, or Quran. STUDYQURAN (Mujāhid): One attribution says Satan was frightened when the Fātiḥah was revealed, among four events; others attribute the saying to the Prophet. STUDYQURAN (Mujāhid): Mujāhid says al-Fātiḥah was revealed in Madinah. THALABI: Reports include twice revelation in Mecca and Medina, healing from poison, averting punishment by children's recitation, and the sūra's comprehensive knowledge. THALABI: Reports include twice revelation in Mecca and Medina, healing from poison, averting punishment by children's recitation, and the sūra's comprehensive knowledge. THALABI: Reports include twice revelation in Mecca and Medina, healing from poison, averting punishment by children's recitation, and the sūra's comprehensive knowledge. THALABI: Reports include twice revelation in Mecca and Medina, healing from poison, averting punishment by children's recitation, and the sūra's comprehensive knowledge. WAHIDI-ASBAB: Al-Wāḥidī reports an early Meccan origin, the call heard after Waraqa's advice, ʿAlī's Throne-treasure report, Quraysh's response, Mujāhid's Medinan dissent, and 15:87 as evidence. WAHIDI-ASBAB: Al-Wāḥidī reports an early Meccan origin, the call heard after Waraqa's advice, ʿAlī's Throne-treasure report, Quraysh's response, Mujāhid's Medinan dissent, and 15:87 as evidence. WAHIDI-ASBAB: Al-Wāḥidī reports an early Meccan origin, the call heard after Waraqa's advice, ʿAlī's Throne-treasure report, Quraysh's response, Mujāhid's Medinan dissent, and 15:87 as evidence. WAHIDI-ASBAB: Al-Wāḥidī reports an early Meccan origin, the call heard after Waraqa's advice, ʿAlī's Throne-treasure report, Quraysh's response, Mujāhid's Medinan dissent, and 15:87 as evidence. WAHIDI-ASBAB: Al-Wāḥidī reports an early Meccan origin, the call heard after Waraqa's advice, ʿAlī's Throne-treasure report, Quraysh's response, Mujāhid's Medinan dissent, and 15:87 as evidence. WAHIDI-ASBAB: Al-Wāḥidī reports an early Meccan origin, the call heard after Waraqa's advice, ʿAlī's Throne-treasure report, Quraysh's response, Mujāhid's Medinan dissent, and 15:87 as evidence. BAGHAWI-FULL: All agree on seven, but the basmala changes where the final verse begins. [38 notes: RAZI-FULL(النبي); BAYDAWI; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; QUTB-ZILAL; IBNASHUR-FULL; CORPUSCORANICUM; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT; STUDYQURAN(Mujāhid,Prophet Muhammad); THALABI(الحسن,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,بعض العلماء); WAHIDI-ASBAB(أبو ميسرة,ابن عباس,الأكثرون,الواحدي,علي بن أبي طالب,مجاهد); BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 1:2/q15/p2 The basmala is included in the verse count. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أم سلمة عن النبي): Umm Salama reports that the Prophet recited al-Fatiha while counting the basmala as its first verse, followed by the rest of its verses. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): In the divided-prayer report, God calls the basmala glorification, al-Hamd praise, the two mercy names extolling, the Master of Judgment entrustment, and the remaining response a sharing and a request granted. BIQAI, d. 885 (author): Because the basmala is a kind of praise, it is naturally followed by the comprehensive name al-ḥamd; this implicitly calls people to praise God as the one deserving every praise. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): The basmala relates to the Fatiha as the Fatiha relates to the Qur'an: each is a compact synthesis of what the larger whole details. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Since the basmala is a kind of praise, it naturally leads to comprehensive al-hamd: begin affairs by praising God as the one deserving every praise, with hope and awe guiding the way. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أم سلمة): Aisha reports that the Prophet counted the basmala as verse one, al-hamd as two, al-Rahman al-Rahim as three, Malik yawm al-din as four, then indicated five for the next verse. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Because the book opens with the basmala, a kind of praise, God follows it with comprehensive praise at the highest degree of perfection. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The relation between the basmala and Fatiha unfolds the primordial covenant between the eternal Lord and c reation, inscribing a relation already present in human nature. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The basmala is the crown, the Qur’an a perfect body, Fatiha its head, and alhamdu lillah its face; mercy, guidance, worship, and divine unity express its spiritual life. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The basmala names God and His attributes while leaving humanity implicit; saying it establishes the divine relation that lets al-Fatiha bring humans forth with gratitude into speech. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The hidden humanity of the basmala becomes a speaking subject in al-Fatiha, while God is present as interlocutor; the surah captures the full relation of life and existence. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Contextual relation may be backward or forward; if the basmala is part of al-Fatiha, backward context is irrelevant, while its relation to 96:1 belongs to revelation order and forward context. | mentions 96:1 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Nothing lies outside the basmala's attraction, so it relates generally to al-Fatiha as one recites, writes, praises, or begins the Quran in its name. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Specifically, basmala and al-Fatiha are closely linked by cadence, wording, summary and explanation; al-Fatiha's gratitude answers the basmala's attraction of mercy. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Their structures correspond: the basmala has seven actual or implicit words, four independent words and three divine-name words; al-Fatiha has seven verses, four statements, and three complete pauses, with the middle tawhid verse linking two three-verse units. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verse division differs by region: Medinans exclude the basmala and count “those You favored” as a verse; Meccans and Kufans include the basmala and combine that phrase with its verse; Hasan counts both separately. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur says sound Sunna contains no evidence that the basmala is a verse at the beginnings of Qur'anic suras, and presents six proofs. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو هريرة): The first proof is Malik's report from Abu Hurayra: God's division of prayer begins with al-Hamdu lillah, without mentioning the basmala; prayer here means recitation in prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبي بن كعب): In the second proof, Ubayy ibn Ka'b recites the celebrated sura from al-Hamdu lillah to its end when asked how he opens prayer, without reciting the basmala. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports in Muslim and other collections that the Prophet, Abu Bakr, and Umar began recitation with al-Hamdu lillah and did not mention the basmala at either end. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عبد الله بن مغفل): Ibn Mughaffal says he prayed with the Prophet and the first three caliphs but never heard them say the basmala, and directs worshippers to recite al-Hamdu lillah. STUDYQURAN (author): Muslims use the basmalah to consecrate actions at their beginning and al-ḥamd to thank God upon completion; etiquette answers any inquiry about one’s condition with praise. STUDYQURAN (ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib): A saying attributed to ʿAlī compresses the Quran into the Fātiḥah, the basmalah, its opening bāʾ, and the diacritical point beneath it. STUDYQURAN (author): The point beneath bāʾ can symbolize the Divine Pen’s first drop of ink, which inscribed all archetypes on the Preserved Tablet before creation; thus basmalah marks both Quranic and cosmic beginnings. | mentions 68:1, 96:4, 85:22 ʿAlī counts the basmala as a verse when challenged that al-Ḥamd has only six verses; Abū Hurayra reports a seven-verse count that expressly includes the basmala. [26 notes: RAZI-FULL(أبو هريرة عن النبي,أم سلمة عن النبي); BIQAI; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(أبو هريرة,أم سلمة,علي); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو هريرة,أبي بن كعب,أنس بن مالك,عبد الله بن مغفل); STUDYQURAN(ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib)]
- 1:2/q15/p3 The basmala is outside the verse count. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The report begins God's response when the servant says “Praise belongs to God,” which indicates the basmala is not part of al-Fatiha; if it is outside al-Fatiha, consensus excludes it from other surahs too. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The decisive sixth proof is Medina's continuous practice: across generations, its mosque did not recite the basmala aloud in congregational prayers, making the practice mutawatir evidence that it is not part of the sura. [2 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL; NASAFI-FULL+]
- 1:2/q15/p4 The sūra was revealed in Medina. — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عائشة): During a drought prayer, the Prophet invoked God as Lord of the worlds, the Merciful, and Master of Judgment, asking for rain that would sustain them; Abu Dawud calls the report rare with a good chain, and says it supports the Medinan no-alif reading. STUDYQURAN (author): Most scholars reject a Medinan origin because Muslims needed the sūrah to perform obligatory prayers. [2 notes: DURR-FULL(عائشة); STUDYQURAN]
- 1:2/q15/p5 The sūra has eight verses. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الحسن البصري): Hasan al-Basri counts the sura as eight verses. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(الحسن البصري)]
- 1:2/q15/p6 The sūra has six verses. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الحسين الجعفي): Al-Husayn al-Ju'fi counts it as six verses; an unspecified view counts nine, which requires excluding the basmala, while counting it in seven merges two verses. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(الحسين الجعفي)]

## 1:2/q16 Why is the Fātiḥa given its names?  [* · sciences]
turns on: The titles are explained by different functions and contents of the sūra.
- 1:2/q16/p1 It is the Opening of the Book and prayer. — TABRISI, d. 548 (سيبويه): The lām normally has fatḥa because it cannot begin with sukun; lām of possession and emphasis are distinguished, and attached pronouns restore its original opening vowel. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Rabb means owner; for a created being the title is used only in a construct phrase such as owner of the house or servant. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أهل النظر): The derivation of ʿālam from ʿalāma, “sign,” supports the speculative theologians' view: the world is named as a sign pointing to its Creator. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Saba’s statement that everything in the heavens and earth belongs to God is another subset within the universal lordship named in the Fātiḥa. | mentions 34:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Only God is necessary in Himself; everything else is possible and is called the world because its existence points to God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The opening glorification reveals the lights of divine majesty and moves from declaring God's transcendence to praising Him. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Rabb may derive from nurture: God manages and raises creation; stepdaughters are called rabāʾib because a husband raises them, making Rabb an attribute of action, while owner or master is an attribute of essence. | mentions 4:23 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): With the definite article, Rabb belongs exclusively to God; without it, people may be called a household’s lord. God alone creates and provides, owns both owners and owned things, and cannot lose His dominion. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الهروي): Rabb also means one who repairs, manages, restores, or takes care of something; rabbani scholars are named for tending to scripture. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Al-‘alamin may mean everything the Creator knows of bodies, substances, and accidents, or every existing thing besides God, named for being evidence of His existence. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): Rabb may be a verbal noun used adjectivally or an active participle with deleted alif; as a title it is exclusive to God, while others require a possessive complement. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): The lām can express possession, transfer of ownership, entitlement, relation, cause, communication, exclamation, specification, result, location, endpoint, and superiority; Qur'anic and Arabic examples illustrate these functions. | mentions 16:72, 4:105, 28:8, 21:47, 17:78, 7:57, 17:107 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The lām in lillāh has numerous possible relations, including ownership, entitlement, relation, cause, communication, specification, outcome, time, endpoint, and elevation. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The lām in li-llah expresses entitlement here; other senses include ownership, transfer, kinship, cause, address, oath exclamation, clarification, result, location, endpoint, elevation, and augmentation. | mentions 16:72, 4:105, 12:23, 28:8, 21:47, 17:78, 35:13, 17:109, 12:43, 11:107, 43:29, 16:1, 27:72, 8:9 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): The unqualified title al-Rabb is used only for God; for other possessors it must be annexed, and some say al-Rabb is the greatest name. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A Prophetic report says he was sent to complete noble character; the author links this completion with the Fatiha's comprehensive opening and the Qur'an's culmination. | mentions 5:3 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The cow story makes the surah's title apt because resurrection occurs through an apparently slight human cause; it is a clearer sign of divine power than Abraham's story or earlier Israelite resurrections. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Al-Baqara is also called the Radiant and the Hump: it illuminates guidance and sufficiency, brightens believers' faces at judgment, and exalts belief in the afterlife above every other good after monotheism. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): By signaling all Qur’anic aims, the Fatiha achieves an exemplary opening and combines beautiful words, pleasing cadence, and rhetorical forms. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): It is called the Treasure surah because a report says the Opening is a treasure from God's Throne. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The opening of al-Fātiḥa is also the final wording of the supplications in 10:10, giving the sūra a return-to-the-beginning frame. | mentions 10:10 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Opening the sūra’s descriptions with Rabb al-ʿālamīn suits the Prophet’s mission and asks for compassion toward all creatures, since God is their Lord. ALUSI, d. 1270 (ابن حلزة): Ibn Ḥilaza’s poem calls al-Mundhir rabb, an unusual unqualified application of the title to a human being. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (السعد): Al-Ālūsī says treating You alone we worship as an explanation rests on the missing conjunction, but that reverses the proper relation between the opening and its continuation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Another debate asks whether al-ʿālamīn is a regular plural, a collective noun, or a name for a totality; al-Ālūsī concludes it is a true plural because each member is itself called an ʿālam. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The plural al-ʿālamīn guarantees inclusion of every created kind: a singular definite noun could be heard as the familiar perceptible world, whereas the plural distributes lordship across all named kinds and their individuals. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Opening the Fatiha's descriptions with Lord of the worlds suits the Prophet's mission as a profound act of nurturing humanity and hints at compassion for all people. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The opening three verses speak about God in the unseen; reflection makes God present to the worshipper, who can then turn to direct address and pledge worship and need. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The world is the collection of all things other than God that point to Him through causal relations; any part possessing an ordered relation can be called a realm. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Every ordered community of things, from inner awareness to external objects, is named a realm because it serves as a sign and means of knowing God’s creation, lordship, and perfection. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A good opening hints at its subject, preparing receptive hearers and allowing advanced hearers to assess or complete the argument; this displays the speaker's command of it. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Arabs do not appear to have used definite singular al-ilah for an idol: they used a possessive construction, another title, or the plural alihah. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (الزجاج ونسب إلى الخليل وسيبويه): Another group says the name was coined directly for God without derivation from alaha; one rationale is that Arabs named everything and would not leave God unnamed. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (الحارث بن حلزة): Al-Harith ibn Hilliza calls Amr ibn Hind al-rabb in a poem, evidence that Arabs applied the title to a human ruler. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (غاوي بن ظالم أو عباس بن مرداس): A pre-Islamic poet applies rabb to an idol, ridiculing a deity with a fox urinating on its head; al-Uzza was also called al-Rabba and the plural is ارباب. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He reads the sura's annexation to Fatihat al-Kitab as descriptive: it means a sura characterized as the Book's opening, with no separate title needed. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): By contrast, Fatihat al-Kitab is a true annexation: al-Kitab here means the remainder of the Book apart from the sura called the Opening. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The claim that al-Fatiha was named for being revealed once in Mecca and again in Medina is very remote: repetition of revelation is not accepted, and the sura is agreed to be Meccan. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (البيضاوي): Al-Baydawi explains pairing God's proper name with al-Rahman and al-Rahim as invoking His entitlement to assistance and mercy: al-Rahman signals the gifts involved in righteous action, with al-Rahim explained later. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): As a Qur'anic preface, al-Fatiha models an Arabic literary opening that improves understanding and attentive reception. CORPUSCORANICUM (author): Rabb al-ʿālamīn corresponds to Jewish liturgical ribbon ʿōlām, an eschatological title spanning this world and the next; a Syriac parallel itself lacks that connotation. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The visible domain is called the realm of dominion and witness, while immaterial existence is the unseen and angelic realm; God alone owns the latter, though worldly ownership can appear to belong to others. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (قيل): One view takes the plural to include every kind, since humans, animals, insects, and each species may each be called a world. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): These openings jointly describe God as originator, shaper, owner, sustainer, and guide through revealed books; Rabb al-alamin encompasses all these qualities and worlds. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The pair of openings complement each other: Anam follows creation with light and darkness, while Fatir follows origination with the creation of spiritual messengers. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 STUDYQURAN (author): Muslims recite the Fātiḥah at diverse life events; funeral services are called fātiḥah in some lands as an opening from one life to another. STUDYQURAN (ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib): A saying attributed to ʿAlī compresses the Quran into the Fātiḥah, the basmalah, its opening bāʾ, and the diacritical point beneath it. STUDYQURAN (author): The title The Foundation reflects the Fātiḥah’s role as a foundation for the whole Quran. WAHIDI-ASBAB: The Prophet calls the sūra unmatched in earlier scriptures and the seven oft-repeated. Abū Hurayra reports the title Fātiḥat al-Kitāb among the names of the seven-verse sūra. [50 notes: TABRISI(سيبويه); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أهل النظر); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(الهروي); NASAFI; ABUHAYYAN; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR; BIQAI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); ITQAN; BURSEVI; ALUSI(ابن حلزة); ALUSI-FULL(السعد); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR(الحارث بن حلزة,الزجاج ونسب إلى الخليل وسيبويه,غاوي بن ظالم أو عباس بن مرداس); IBNASHUR-FULL(البيضاوي); CORPUSCORANICUM; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(قيل); STUDYQURAN(ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib); WAHIDI-ASBAB(أبو هريرة); DURR-FULL(أبو هريرة)]
- 1:2/q16/p2 It is named Praise after its opening. — IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (بعض المفسرين): One of six Qur’anic senses is every living being, as in the opening praise of God. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): It is called Sūrat al-Ḥamd because its opening word is praise. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A third reason is that all knowledge concerns either divine lordship or human servitude: the opening praises God’s mastery, while the remainder shows the servant’s dependence on His aid and guidance. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (المخالف): One reported distinction says hamd thanks God for warding off calamity, while shukr thanks Him for gifts; an objection asks why the lesser benefit is named first. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): On either grammatical analysis, the phrase denies that anyone besides God is independently entitled to praise. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): They further argue that if God is praised intrinsically, no act causes that entitlement, so no duty, compensation, or reward is owed; if it is not intrinsic, God would depend on another. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الله تعالى): In a divine report transmitted by the Prophet, God responds to each Fatiha phrase: the opening remembers Him, praise praises Him, the mercy names magnify Him, Judgment Day glorifies Him or is entrusted to Him, worship belongs to Him, aid relies on Him, and guidance is the servant's request. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The entitlement to praise implies that God is living, powerful, willing, and knowing. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): Praise in action and in the tongue of each thing’s state is the appearance of its perfections and attainment of its ends, a fitting opening tribute to its Lord. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (author): With nominative al-ḥamdu the lām marks entitlement; with accusative al-ḥamda a verb such as “I praise God” must be supplied, conveying renewed action. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir explains praise as exclusive thanks to God for countless blessings, including faculties for worship, worldly provision, and guidance toward lasting bliss despite no prior entitlement. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali maps al-Fatiha's verses to the seven letters: praise, lawful and forbidden, command and prohibition, clear and ambiguous, then the corresponding petitions; its opening gathers all seven. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): A good stop may be made, but starting with the words that follow is not sound; the opening praise is the example. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن أبي جمرة): Explaining the scale of commentary, Ibn Abi Jamra says the opening requires explaining praise, the divine name and transcendence, and the worlds, their nature, types, number, and extent. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The opening praise and names of the Merciful indicate God's divinity. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الجنيد): Junayd was asked about the spiritual end and answered that it is returning to the beginning, an apt pattern for the sūra’s opening praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الأكثر): Most scholars explain God’s self-praise as declaring His entitlement to praise, commanding praise, quoting the words of servants, or figuratively displaying His perfections; al-Sayyid favors the last account. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī sides with the majority: the sentence informs that God deserves praise, while a believe r who says it intending reverent description is rightly called a praiser. | mentions 28:70 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Naming God by His essential name avoids suggesting that He deserves praise only through one particular attribute; entitlement by essence still means entitlement for divine excellence. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الأكثر): Most authorities interpret God praising Himself as reporting His entitlement to praise, commanding praise, speaking through servants, or figuratively displaying His perfect attributes; al-Sayyid favors the last explanation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Naming God by His essence avoids suggesting that entitlement to praise belongs only to one attribute; essential entitlement means no particular attribute is specified, not that praise lacks a beautiful ground. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الملا خسرو): Entitlement by essence means entitlement without specifying a particular attribute; it does not mean God deserves praise apart from beautiful qualities. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Mentioning the divine proper name avoids implying that entitlement to praise belongs only to one attribute; the entitlement belongs to the essence without isolating it from its attributes. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Fatiha’s successive clauses unfold the opening praise and return to alhamdu lillah; the surah is the root and comprehensive representative of the other Qur’anic surahs. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The lām in lillah denotes entitlement, particularity, and ownership; the whole genus and every form of hamd belongs to God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): A sacred hadith says al-Fatiha is divided equally between God and servant, with the servant entitled to what is asked; its opening praises and glorifies God, its middle is shared, and its ending is the servant's supplication. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Muslims consequently called valuable speech lacking opening praise abtar; Ziyad ibn Abi Sufyan's Basran sermon was nicknamed al-Batra for this omission. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Rabb is an attribute of Allah: after praise is attached to His essence to show inherent entitlement, the attribute adds entitlement through His qualities; this is clearer than saying 'praise belongs to Lord of the worlds.' | mentions 83:6 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The surah begins with praise so the second half of the Qur'an opens with الحمد لله, echoing the opening of the first half in al-Fātiḥa and the approximate start of the fourth quarter in Q 35:1. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The opening praise gathers God's praised attributes, declares His uniqueness in divinity, and affirms resurrection and recompense. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-hamdu lillah implies all attributes of perfection that entitle God to exclusive praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The sura also teaches gratitude by crowning supplication with praise of the giver of intellect and guidance; opening important speech with praise is therefore Qur'anic practice. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Opening weighty speech with praise became Qur'anic practice; Muslims call an important speech without such an opening “cut off,” following a hadith from Abu Hurayra. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ziyad ibn Abi Sufyan's Basra sermon was nicknamed al-Batra because it did not begin with praise. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verse puts الحمد before لله because praise for revelation and Islam frames the opening recitation; contextual importance can precede God's intrinsic priority, while praising Him still attends to His affairs. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The opening الحمد لله is taught etiquette: a servant could not rightly utter this praise without God first teaching it and speaking on the servant's behalf. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Saying I praise God reports the speaker's act but does not establish that its object deserves praise; al-hamd lillah states God's entitlement without a specific speaker. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Using God's proper name rather than an attribute avoids implying that He deserves praise only for one quality; the name points to His essence and entitlement across all attributes. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Other opening praises name God as creator, originator, owner of all things, or sender of scripture; each fits its surah and the openings complement one another. | mentions 6:1, 35:1, 34:1, 18:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): The next four opening praise formulas describe particular aspects contained in al-Fatiha's general Rabb al-alamin. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The opening attributes span all time: praise before creation, at creation, through continuing mercy, and through the unending recompense of the afterlife, as 28:70 says. | mentions 28:70 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): الحمد لله also declares praise to be God's rightful property because of His abundant favors; a personal act of praising does not assert this entitlement. STUDYQURAN (author): God praises Himself in the opening so humans can praise Him in divine speech, which He knows is more adequate than their own words. ʿAlī calls the seven oft-repeated al-Ḥamd, identifying the sūra by its opening praise. [44 notes: IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(بعض المفسرين); RAZI-FULL(الله تعالى,المخالف); BAYDAWI-FULL; QASHANI; ABUHAYYAN; IBNKATHIR(ابن جرير); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); ITQAN(ابن أبي جمرة); BURSEVI; ALUSI(الأكثر,الجنيد); ALUSI-FULL(الأكثر,الملا خسرو); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; STUDYQURAN; DURR-FULL(علي); ALUSI+]
- 1:2/q16/p3 It is the Mother of the Qurʾān because it gathers its aims. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): It is called the Mother of the Qur’an because its verses encompass the Qur’an’s four central aims: theology, resurrection, prophethood, and divine decree. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A second reason for calling it the Mother of the Book is that all revelation comprises praise, worshipful service, and requests for unveiling; the sura includes each in that order. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): As the Mother of the Qur’an, the Fātiḥa is the source from which other suras branch; every being besides God is evidence of His divinity. QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): One derivation traces Rabb to tarbiya, since God manages and nurtures creation; the stepdaughter is called rabība because her mother’s husband raises her. In this sense it is an attribute of action, while owner or master denotes an attribute of essence. | mentions 4:23 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): A surah's name summarizes its purpose, as a title signals the details within; Adam demonstrated this naming principle when he named things before the angels. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha's many titles converge on مراقبة: it is the Mother of the Book, foundation, oft-repeated, treasure, healing, sufficing, complete, protecting, incantation, praise, thanks, supplication and prayer. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali says the Qur'an's sevenfold gathering in Muhammad's heart relates to the Mother of Qur'an's universals as the Qur'an's gathering in his heart relates to the Mother. ITQAN, d. 911 (الإمام فخر الدين): Fakhr al-Din maps the Qur’an’s four aims—God, resurrection, prophethood, and divine decree—onto the Fatiha: praise, Judgment Day, worship and reliance, and guidance through the surah’s end; this supports its name Umm al-Qur’an. | mentions 1:2, 1:4, 1:6, 1:7 ITQAN, d. 911 (علي): Ali said, as transmitted by Ibn Abi Jamra, that he could load seventy camels with commentary on the Umm al-Qur’an; the source explains the remark by showing how much each phrase requires. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Fatiha is the Mother of the Qur'an because it gathers the Qur'an's purposes: affirming divinity and prophethood, and establishing decree and predestination. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The surah's preparation rejects atheism and materialism, polytheism, stubbornness, and errors corrupting sound knowledge or true law; this is why it is called the Mother of the Quran. | mentions 1:4, 1:5, 1:6, 1:7 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The names Umm al-Qur'an and Umm al-Kitab are established in the Sunna; “mother” denotes a thing's origin and source. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو سعيد الخدري): A report in Sahih al-Bukhari says Abu Sa'id al-Khudri recited Umm al-Qur'an over a person suffering from a sting as a healing prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A sound hadith calls a prayer without Umm al-Qur'an deficient. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): One explanation of Umm al-Qur'an is that it begins and opens the Qur'an, making it like its origin. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A third explanation of Umm al-Qur'an is that it contains the Qur'an's theoretical wisdom and practical rulings, including theology, prophecy, admonition, worship, transactions, morals, and law. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): These aims help explain why al-Fatiha is called Umm al-Qur'an. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The praise covers time from eternity to eternity, echoed by praise in the first and last life and at the end of judgment; this fullness makes al-Fatiha worthy to be the Mother of the Book. | mentions 28:70, 39:75, 10:10 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): The surah is truly the Mother of the Book because it contains both doctrinal foundations and the two pillars of action. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Fatiha contains faith and good works, affirming God, the Last Day, angels, messengers, and scriptures, and joins lordship's unity to the unity of worship; it is therefore the Mother of the Book. STUDYQURAN (author): The sūrah is seen as a synthesis and the Quran’s most important chapter, hence Umm al-kitāb; the title also refers to 3:7 and the celestial archetype of scripture. | mentions 3:7, 13:39, 43:4 STUDYQURAN (author): It is called the Mother of the Quran because it contains the meaning of the entire Quran. Abū Hurayra reports the Prophet calling ‘Praise belongs to God, Lord of the worlds’ the Mother of the Qurʾan and the Mother of the Book; another report from Abū Hurayra includes Mother of the Qurʾan among the Fātiḥa’s titles. [24 notes: RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); ITQAN(الإمام فخر الدين,علي); BURSEVI; IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو سعيد الخدري,النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT; STUDYQURAN; DURR-FULL(أبو هريرة)]
- 1:2/q16/p4 It is named for its role in prayer. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Many names indicate the honor of the named; Fātiḥat al-Kitāb is so called because it opens codices, teaching, recitation, and prayer, or because praise opens speech, or because it was the first sura sent down. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The prophetic statement “no prayer without the Opening of the Book” directly supports the requirement. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله [ ص ]): The Prophet says prayer is invalid without reciting the Opening of the Book; the report is in both Sahih collections. STUDYQURAN (author): Al-Fātiḥah means The Opening: it opens the Book, each prayer cycle, many everyday acts, and may open the breast to faith. [4 notes: RAZI-FULL; QUTB-ZILAL(رسول الله [ ص ]); STUDYQURAN]
- 1:2/q16/p5 It is the Seven Oft-Repeated through its verses and repeated recitation. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): It is called the seven oft-repeated because it has seven verses, each can stand for seven Qur'anic portions, and reciting them is said to earn the whole Qur'an's reward or close seven gates of Hell. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The name al-mathani is explained by repetition in prayer or each rak'a, intercession in each rak'a, or revelation twice, in Mecca and Medina. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report in Sahih al-Bukhari from Abu Sa'id ibn al-Mu'alla says the Prophet identified al-Hamdu lillah as the seven oft-repeated verses and the great Qur'an given to him. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): On this reading, mathani in al-Fatiha resembles the repeated narratives and themes in the Qur'an called mathani in al-Zumar. | mentions 39:23 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Mathani is a plural of muthanna, with alternative vocalized or lightened forms; al-Jurjani notes that each can also be feminine. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A further view takes mathani to mean repeated in prayer, citing Arabic use of the dual for repeated action. STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet identifies the Fātiḥah as the greatest sūrah, calling it Praise be to God and the seven oft-repeated, and linking it to 15:87. | mentions 15:87 STUDYQURAN (author): Another title, The Seven Oft-Repeated, is linked to 15:87. | mentions 15:87 Abū Hurayra reports the Prophet calling the opening praise the seven oft-repeated; ʿAlī identifies them as al-Ḥamd and counts the basmala to make seven; another report from Abū Hurayra gives the title alongside a seven-verse count that includes the basmala. [11 notes: BURSEVI; IBNASHUR-FULL(النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم); STUDYQURAN(Prophet Muhammad); DURR-FULL(أبو هريرة,علي)]
- 1:2/q16/p6 It is named for healing. — STUDYQURAN (author): The title The Cure reflects its reputed healing power for body and soul. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]

## 1:2/q17 Must the Fātiḥa be recited in prayer?  [* · law]
turns on: The jurists differ over whether this particular sūra is necessary for valid prayer.
- 1:2/q17/p1 The Fātiḥa is required in prayer. — TABRISI, d. 548 (زيد بن علي): Zayd ibn Ali's fatḥa on the bā of rabb is understood as demonstrating that the form is possible, not as a recitation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Many names indicate the honor of the named; Fātiḥat al-Kitāb is so called because it opens codices, teaching, recitation, and prayer, or because praise opens speech, or because it was the first sura sent down. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The command to perform the dawn recitation in Quran 17:78 is a command to read and therefore makes recitation obligatory. | mentions 17:78 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو الدرداء عن النبي): A man asked the Prophet whether prayer includes recitation; when the man concluded that it was obligatory, the Prophet approved him. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن مسعود عن النبي): In another report, the Prophet answers a question about reciting in prayer by asking whether there can be prayer without recitation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الأصم): Al-Asamm argues that the Prophet described prayer as things seen, whereas recitation is not visible and must therefore fall outside prayer; Razi answers that seeing with two objects means knowing. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The prayer's two halves arise through the verses of al-Fatiha; since prayer entails this division and it entails this surah, al-Fatiha is necessary for valid prayer. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If al-Fatiha is a constituent of prayer, omitting it means the prayer's very essence does not exist, so the hadith's negation can remain literal. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because the established form of the Prophet's prayer included al-Fatiha, the command to establish prayer requires it; the command recurs over a hundred times in the Quran. | mentions 2:43 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The rightly guided caliphs consistently recited al-Fatiha; reports in both Sahihs say the Prophet, Abu Bakr, and Umar began recitation with al-Hamd, so their practice should be followed. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Muslims everywhere recite al-Fatiha in prayer; even if some regard it as recommended rather than obligatory, abandoning the unanimous practice leaves the believers' path and incurs Quran 4:115's warning. | mentions 4:115 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet identifies al-Fatiha as a surah unmatched in the Torah, Gospel, or Psalms; the Companions' answer about what they recite in prayer indicates a known universal practice. [RAZI-FULL:v1p158#2/ r2] RAZI-FULL, d. 606 · author | holds | Razi argues that Quran 73:20 requires al-Fatiha: the options are al-Fatiha, another surah, or a choice; the latter two fail because another is not universally required and al-Fatiha is agreed better and more complete. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Fatiha is the recitation made accessible to all because every Muslim has it memorized, whereas other surahs may not be memorized by each person. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The original duty to pray remains until evidence shows discharge; al-Fatiha is superior and makes prayer more complete, so omitting it does not establish discharge. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): The Prophet says a prayer without al-Fatiha is deficient and incomplete; Razi argues that a deficient performance does not discharge the continuing obligation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi compares Abu Hanifa's ruling that an Eid fast cannot make up Ramadan because it is incomplete and asks why the same reasoning does not invalidate prayer without al-Fatiha. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن المنذر عن أبي هريرة عن النبي): Ibn al-Mundhir transmits from Abu Huraira that the Prophet said a prayer without al-Fatiha does not suffice. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (رفاعة بن مالك عن النبي): A man who asks the Prophet to teach him prayer is told to face the qibla, say takbir, and recite al-Fatiha; Razi treats this imperative as obligation and recitation as part of prayer. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Although belief in obligation may itself leave theoretical doubt, actual recitation creates no danger while omission creates fear of failing the duty; therefore action is the safer course. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since the Prophet recited al-Fatiha throughout his life, replacing it with another recitation is innovation and abandonment of the required prophetic example. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Prayer without al-Fatiha loses an agreed extra virtue without compensation, which Razi regards as improper and therefore impermissible. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): Abu Huraira reports that the Prophet ordered him to announce that there is no prayer without recitation, even if by al-Fatiha. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi counters the opposing report with Abu Huraira's version no prayer except by al-Fatiha; if reports conflict, the stronger and safer view is his. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi reports disagreement over the minimum recitation for Hanafis: Abu Hanifa accepts one verse, even isolated letters, while Abu Yusuf and Muhammad require three short verses or one long verse. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): In the divided-prayer report, God calls the basmala glorification, al-Hamd praise, the two mercy names extolling, the Master of Judgment entrustment, and the remaining response a sharing and a request granted. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi supports obligatory Fatiha recitation because the Prophet consistently recited it and commands require following him. | mentions 6:153 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The transmitted report itself says that prayer is not established without the Fatiha. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Muslims everywhere recite it in prayer, and the Qur'an commands following the path of believers. | mentions 4:115 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The prophetic statement “no prayer without the Opening of the Book” directly supports the requirement. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The command to recite what is easy from the Qur'an is obligatory; because reciting other passages is not, the obligatory recitation must be the Fatiha. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet's constant practice makes abandoning his command forbidden, and Muslims agree that the Fatiha is better and more complete than another recitation. | mentions 24:63 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If the superior prayer with the Fatiha discharges the obligation, it does not follow that the inferior prayer with another passage does; the obligation remains due. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Prayer seeks remembrance of the heart, and this concise surah gathers lordship and servitude and is equated with the mighty Qur'an; nothing else can replace it. | mentions 20:14, 15:87 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Calling the Fatiha “prayer” shows that religious law serves human interests: people most need knowledge of God's lordship and servitude, which the surah's two halves provide to fulfill the covenant. | mentions 17:7, 51:56, 76:2, 2:40 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since God names the Fatiha “prayer,” prayer without its recitation does not occur; Razi takes this to establish that reciting it is a pillar, as his school holds. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The seven Fatiha verses correspond to the seven bodily prayer acts—standing, bowing, rising, first prostration, rising between prostrations, second prostration, and sitting—as soul to body. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The seven Fatiha verses are the soul of the seven prayer acts, themselves the soul of seven stages of human formation; God is the soul of souls and light of lights. | mentions 23:12, 23:13, 23:14, 53:42 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Prayer was given to Muhammad as a gift for his community because it joins bodily ascent through actions with spiritual ascent through remembrances. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Two recitations assimilate the dāl and lām in either direction, treating them as one word because the terms are used together. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): In the divine hadith, “prayer” means recitation, as in 17:110; because the Fatiha details the division, its recitation is a major pillar of prayer. | mentions 17:110 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): “Qur'an of dawn” in 17:78 means the dawn prayer, as the sound reports say angels of night and day witness it; scholars agree prayer requires recitation. | mentions 17:78 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): The definite article in al-ḥamdu encompasses every kind and form of praise as belonging to God, as expressed in a transmitted prayer. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (الله تعالى): In a divine report, God divides the prayer between Himself and His servant, responds to each recited section, calls 1:5 shared between them, and grants the servant's request. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha's many titles converge on مراقبة: it is the Mother of the Book, foundation, oft-repeated, treasure, healing, sufficing, complete, protecting, incantation, praise, thanks, supplication and prayer. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha is a treasure, cure, sufficiency, fulfillment, protection and incantation; it gathers praise, gratitude and supplication, with prayer as their greatest assembly. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali explains the sound report that God divided prayer between Himself and His servant: the first three verses are God's praise, which He accepts from the reciting servant as praise and glorification. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Qur'an's arrangement is miraculous, so the Fatiha must retain its required order in prayer; rearrangement or insertion of foreign material invalidates it. Al-Isfahani locates its clearest inimitability in order. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The passage names al-Baqara 2:281 as the last revealed verse and says its recitation position between the debt verse and the verse before it shows revelation and recitation order can differ. | mentions 2:281, 75:17 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Recitation order can differ from revelation chronology; the verse allowing the Prophet to marry certain women was placed before a later restriction. | mentions 33:50, 33:52 DURR, d. 911 (رسول الله): The Prophet says purification is half of faith and al-hamd fills the balance; other phrases fill the space between heaven and earth, while prayer, charity, patience, and the Quran serve as signs or testimony. DURR, d. 911 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): Ibn ʿAmr assigns distinct functions to remembrances: glorification is creation's prayer, al-hamd gratitude, tawhid sincerity, takbir fills heaven and earth, and the declaration of God's powerlessness-to-act-without-help brings God's command to submit. DURR, d. 911 (أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي): Abu ʿAbd al-Rahman al-Jubbaʾi says prayer, fasting, and every good deed done for God count as gratitude, with al-hamd the best gratitude. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي): Abu Abd al-Rahman al-Jubba'i says prayer, fasting, and every good deed for God are gratitude, with al-hamd the best form. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): Abd Allah ibn Amr assigns distinct meanings to the formulas: tasbih is the creatures' prayer, al-hamd is gratitude, tahlil is sincerity, takbir fills heaven and earth, and “no power” expresses surrender. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عائشة): During a drought prayer, the Prophet invoked God as Lord of the worlds, the Merciful, and Master of Judgment, asking for rain that would sustain them; Abu Dawud calls the report rare with a good chain, and says it supports the Medinan no-alif reading. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The name al-mathani is explained by repetition in prayer or each rak'a, intercession in each rak'a, or revelation twice, in Mecca and Medina. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Other names include the Prayer, Healing, Cure, Foundation, Sufficient, Complete, Praise, Supplication, Thanks, and Invocation surahs because their meanings occur in it. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The surah is Fatiha because it opens the codex, instruction, Qur'an recitation, prayer, speech, revelation, the aims of this life, and the gates of Paradise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (النواس بن سمعان): Al-Ṭabarānī reports from al-Nawwās b. Samʿān that the Prophet vowed to thank God if his she-camel returned; when it did, he said al-ḥamdu lillāh, and the Companions expected further prayer or fasting. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الغزالي): Al-Ghazālī interprets the congregational prayer report as the worshippers speaking collectively in the first-person plural. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The phrase opens the Fātiḥa and also closes the final supplications, as in the verse describing the last prayer of Paradise’s inhabitants. | mentions 10:10 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (النواس بن سمعان): Al-Ṭabarānī reports that after the Prophet’s she-camel was stolen, he vowed to thank God if it returned; when it did, he said alhamdulillah, and the Companions awaited fasting or prayer, showing they understood gratitude as action. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الغزالي): Al-Ghazālī applies the plural-language explanation to the report that congregational prayer exceeds an individual prayer by twenty-seven degrees. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ساداتنا الصوفية): Sufi masters say the appropriate divine name in prayer changes with the seeker's spiritual state and whether the experience is one of differentiation or union. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The descriptions close by pointing to resurrection, just as Lord of the worlds opened with origination; they explain the prior praise and prepare for the prayer that follows. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Al-Fatiha is Meccan and has seven verses; Muslims recite it at least seventeen times daily, more with voluntary prayers, and may repeat it without limit. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله [ ص ]): The Prophet says prayer is invalid without reciting the Opening of the Book; the report is in both Sahih collections. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله): Abu Hurayra reports in Sahih Muslim that God calls al-Fatiha a prayer divided between Himself and His servant: praise, mercy, and glorification are the Lord's; worship and aid are shared; guidance and the servant's request belong to the servant, who receives what he asks. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The prayer teaches believers to val ue blessings properly by thanking their giver, praising the giver of reason and success. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو سعيد الخدري): A report in Sahih al-Bukhari says Abu Sa'id al-Khudri recited Umm al-Qur'an over a person suffering from a sting as a healing prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A sound hadith calls a prayer without Umm al-Qur'an deficient. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Requiring al-Fatiha in every rak'ah preserves recollection of the Qur'an's purposes contained within it. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-Kashshaf explains the name as referring to verses repeated in each rak'ah; this is also said to be transmitted from Umar. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur accepts the explanation tied to each rak'ah because al-Fatiha is joined to another sura in every rak'ah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Another account connects the name to the early form of prayer: prayers were first prescribed as two rak'ahs, travel prayer remained so, and residence prayer was lengthened; an opposite account is also mentioned. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A further view takes mathani to mean repeated in prayer, citing Arabic use of the dual for repeated action. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A hadith in both Sahihs from Abu Hurayra has God divide prayer between Himself and the servant, answering each portion of al-Fatiha and granting the servant's request. | mentions 1:2, 1:3, 1:4, 1:5, 1:6, 1:7 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو هريرة): The first proof is Malik's report from Abu Hurayra: God's division of prayer begins with al-Hamdu lillah, without mentioning the basmala; prayer here means recitation in prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبي بن كعب): In the second proof, Ubayy ibn Ka'b recites the celebrated sura from al-Hamdu lillah to its end when asked how he opens prayer, without reciting the basmala. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports in Muslim and other collections that the Prophet, Abu Bakr, and Umar began recitation with al-Hamdu lillah and did not mention the basmala at either end. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عائشة): Aisha reports that the Prophet opened prayer with the takbir and began recitation with al-Hamdu lillah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The decisive sixth proof is Medina's continuous practice: across generations, its mosque did not recite the basmala aloud in congregational prayers, making the practice mutawatir evidence that it is not part of the sura. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verse puts الحمد before لله because praise for revelation and Islam frames the opening recitation; contextual importance can precede God's intrinsic priority, while praising Him still attends to His affairs. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The author favors the exhaustive sense, supported by the Prophetic prayer اللهم لك الحمد كله: all known praise belongs to God without exception. STUDYQURAN (author): As Sūrat al-Ḥamd and Sūrat al-Ṣalāh, the Fātiḥah begins each prayer cycle for Sunnis and many Shiites. STUDYQURAN (God): In a hadith qudsī, God responds to the servant’s praise, magnification, and glorification during the prayer, which is divided between them. STUDYQURAN (author): The Fātiḥah is a prayer encapsulating metaphysical and eschatological realities and teaches the proper human disposition toward God. STUDYQURAN (author): Most scholars reject a Medinan origin because Muslims needed the sūrah to perform obligatory prayers. STUDYQURAN (author): Al-Fātiḥah means The Opening: it opens the Book, each prayer cycle, many everyday acts, and may open the breast to faith. Al-Rāzī argues that 73:20 requires the Fātiḥa: another sūra is not universally required and the Fātiḥa is agreed to be better and more complete. [90 notes: TABRISI(زيد بن علي); RAZI-FULL(أبو الدرداء عن النبي,أبو هريرة عن النبي,ابن المنذر عن أبي هريرة عن النبي,ابن مسعود عن النبي,الأصم,رفاعة بن مالك عن النبي); BAYDAWI-FULL; IBNKATHIR(الله تعالى); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR(أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي,رسول الله,عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص); DURR-FULL(أبو عبد الرحمن الجبائي,عائشة,عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص); BURSEVI; ALUSI(الغزالي,النواس بن سمعان); ALUSI-FULL(الغزالي,النواس بن سمعان,ساداتنا الصوفية); QUTB-ZILAL(رسول الله,رسول الله [ ص ]); IBNASHUR; IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو سعيد الخدري,أبو هريرة,أبي بن كعب,أنس بن مالك,النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم,عائشة); STUDYQURAN(God); IBNASHUR-FULL+; RAZI-FULL+; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT+]
- 1:2/q17/p2 The Fātiḥa is not required in prayer. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Most scholars require recitation in prayer, while al-Asamm and al-Hasan b. Salih deny that recitation is obligatory. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Shafi'i and most scholars require al-Fatiha itself; omitting even one letter knowingly invalidates prayer, whereas Abu Hanifa says it is not required. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Against the objection that the negation in no prayer except with al-Fatiha must mean incompleteness rather than invalidity, Razi cites a version explicitly denying prayer's occurrence for one who omits it. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If prayers with and without al-Fatiha were both valid, habitual recitation would make other surahs abandoned; because the community agrees al-Fatiha is preferable, Razi concludes omission is invalid. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): The short surah gathers core Islamic creed, worldview, feelings, and guidance, explaining its repetition in every rakʿa and why prayer without it is invalid. [5 notes: RAZI-FULL; QUTB-ZILAL]

## 1:2/q18 How does this verse relate to the sūra and other Qurʾanic passages?  [* · links]
turns on: The sources connect the opening with guidance, subsequent divine names, or other praise openings.
- 1:2/q18/p1 The sūra moves from praise to worship and petition. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Many names indicate the honor of the named; Fātiḥat al-Kitāb is so called because it opens codices, teaching, recitation, and prayer, or because praise opens speech, or because it was the first sura sent down. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): It is called Sūrat al-Ḥamd because its opening word is praise. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): As the Mother of the Qur’an, the Fātiḥa is the source from which other suras branch; every being besides God is evidence of His divinity. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Shafi'i and most scholars require al-Fatiha itself; omitting even one letter knowingly invalidates prayer, whereas Abu Hanifa says it is not required. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet's lifelong practice of reciting al-Fatiha obligates imitation, supported by Quranic commands to follow him. | mentions 7:158, 24:63, 3:31 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi charges Abu Hanifa with inconsistency for requiring forehead wiping from one report while denying al-Fatiha's requirement despite its mass-transmitted practice. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The prayer's two halves arise through the verses of al-Fatiha; since prayer entails this division and it entails this surah, al-Fatiha is necessary for valid prayer. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Against the objection that the negation in no prayer except with al-Fatiha must mean incompleteness rather than invalidity, Razi cites a version explicitly denying prayer's occurrence for one who omits it. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If al-Fatiha is a constituent of prayer, omitting it means the prayer's very essence does not exist, so the hadith's negation can remain literal. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Because the established form of the Prophet's prayer included al-Fatiha, the command to establish prayer requires it; the command recurs over a hundred times in the Quran. | mentions 2:43 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The rightly guided caliphs consistently recited al-Fatiha; reports in both Sahihs say the Prophet, Abu Bakr, and Umar began recitation with al-Hamd, so their practice should be followed. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi criticizes Abu Hanifa for relying on Uthman's report in the fugitive-divorce issue despite contrary Companions and Quran, but not following all Companions' practice on al-Fatiha. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Muslims everywhere recite al-Fatiha in prayer; even if some regard it as recommended rather than obligatory, abandoning the unanimous practice leaves the believers' path and incurs Quran 4:115's warning. | mentions 4:115 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet identifies al-Fatiha as a surah unmatched in the Torah, Gospel, or Psalms; the Companions' answer about what they recite in prayer indicates a known universal practice. [RAZI-FULL:v1p158#2/ r2] RAZI-FULL, d. 606 · author | holds | Razi argues that Quran 73:20 requires al-Fatiha: the options are al-Fatiha, another surah, or a choice; the latter two fail because another is not universally required and al-Fatiha is agreed better and more complete. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Fatiha is the recitation made accessible to all because every Muslim has it memorized, whereas other surahs may not be memorized by each person. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The original duty to pray remains until evidence shows discharge; al-Fatiha is superior and makes prayer more complete, so omitting it does not establish discharge. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): The Prophet says a prayer without al-Fatiha is deficient and incomplete; Razi argues that a deficient performance does not discharge the continuing obligation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi compares Abu Hanifa's ruling that an Eid fast cannot make up Ramadan because it is incomplete and asks why the same reasoning does not invalidate prayer without al-Fatiha. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن المنذر عن أبي هريرة عن النبي): Ibn al-Mundhir transmits from Abu Huraira that the Prophet said a prayer without al-Fatiha does not suffice. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (رفاعة بن مالك عن النبي): A man who asks the Prophet to teach him prayer is told to face the qibla, say takbir, and recite al-Fatiha; Razi treats this imperative as obligation and recitation as part of prayer. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If prayers with and without al-Fatiha were both valid, habitual recitation would make other surahs abandoned; because the community agrees al-Fatiha is preferable, Razi concludes omission is invalid. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Just as bowing and prostration cannot be replaced, al-Fatiha cannot be replaced by another reading, with caution as the common rationale. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The duty to pray persists; neither Quran 73:20 nor analogy proves discharge without al-Fatiha, and ritual worship is a domain where analogy should be set aside. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since the Prophet recited al-Fatiha throughout his life, replacing it with another recitation is innovation and abandonment of the required prophetic example. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Prayer without al-Fatiha loses an agreed extra virtue without compensation, which Razi regards as improper and therefore impermissible. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): Abu Huraira reports that the Prophet ordered him to announce that there is no prayer without recitation, even if by al-Fatiha. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi answers Quran 73:20 by the same three options: al-Fatiha itself, another passage, or a choice; the latter two fail, and al-Fatiha is accessible to all. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi counters the opposing report with Abu Huraira's version no prayer except by al-Fatiha; if reports conflict, the stronger and safer view is his. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أم سلمة عن النبي): Umm Salama reports that the Prophet recited al-Fatiha while counting the basmala as its first verse, followed by the rest of its verses. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Praise thanks God for past blessings and brings future increase, as promised in the Qur’an; it closes barriers and opens the way to knowledge of God, hence the name al-Fātiḥa. | mentions 14:7 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi supports obligatory Fatiha recitation because the Prophet consistently recited it and commands require following him. | mentions 6:153 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The transmitted report itself says that prayer is not established without the Fatiha. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The command to recite what is easy from the Qur'an is obligatory; because reciting other passages is not, the obligatory recitation must be the Fatiha. | mentions 73:20 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Reciting the Fatiha is the safer course, following the prophetic instruction to leave what causes doubt for what does not. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The Prophet's constant practice makes abandoning his command forbidden, and Muslims agree that the Fatiha is better and more complete than another recitation. | mentions 24:63 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If the superior prayer with the Fatiha discharges the obligation, it does not follow that the inferior prayer with another passage does; the obligation remains due. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Prayer seeks remembrance of the heart, and this concise surah gathers lordship and servitude and is equated with the mighty Qur'an; nothing else can replace it. | mentions 20:14, 15:87 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Calling the Fatiha “prayer” shows that religious law serves human interests: people most need knowledge of God's lordship and servitude, which the surah's two halves provide to fulfill the covenant. | mentions 17:7, 51:56, 76:2, 2:40 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Since God names the Fatiha “prayer,” prayer without its recitation does not occur; Razi takes this to establish that reciting it is a pillar, as his school holds. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The seven Fatiha verses correspond to the seven bodily prayer acts—standing, bowing, rising, first prostration, rising between prostrations, second prostration, and sitting—as soul to body. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The seven Fatiha verses are the soul of the seven prayer acts, themselves the soul of seven stages of human formation; God is the soul of souls and light of lights. | mentions 23:12, 23:13, 23:14, 53:42 NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The report begins God's response when the servant says “Praise belongs to God,” which indicates the basmala is not part of al-Fatiha; if it is outside al-Fatiha, consensus excludes it from other surahs too. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): In the divine hadith, “prayer” means recitation, as in 17:110; because the Fatiha details the division, its recitation is a major pillar of prayer. | mentions 17:110 BURHAN, d. 794 (ابن حبان): Ibn Hibban reads the report comparing al-Fatiha with the Torah and Gospel as a difference in reward granted by God’s favor, not as intrinsic superiority within the Quran. BURHAN, d. 794 (ابن حبان): Ibn Hibban says calling al-Fatiha the greatest surah means greatest in reward, not that one part of the Quran is intrinsically superior. BURHAN, d. 794 (author): Abu Said reports the Prophet calling al-Fatiha the greatest surah in a Bukhari narration; al-Zarkashi says this does not conflict with Ayat al-Kursi’s rank among verses, since the comparisons concern surahs and verses respectively. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (أبو الفضل محمد المشدالي المغربي): Al-Mashdali's method for Qur'anic coherence is to identify a surah's purpose, its prerequisites and their distance from the goal, then answer expectations raised as the discourse unfolds. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): A surah's name summarizes its purpose, as a title signals the details within; Adam demonstrated this naming principle when he named things before the angels. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha's many titles converge on مراقبة: it is the Mother of the Book, foundation, oft-repeated, treasure, healing, sufficing, complete, protecting, incantation, praise, thanks, supplication and prayer. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha is a treasure, cure, sufficiency, fulfillment, protection and incantation; it gathers praise, gratitude and supplication, with prayer as their greatest assembly. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Fatiha's purpose is to establish God's exclusive right to all praise and perfection, sovereignty over both worlds, worship and aid, and to ask for the path of the successful rather than the doomed; its center is مراقبة. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): The basmala relates to the Fatiha as the Fatiha relates to the Qur'an: each is a compact synthesis of what the larger whole details. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Qur'an's arrangement is miraculous, so the Fatiha must retain its required order in prayer; rearrangement or insertion of foreign material invalidates it. Al-Isfahani locates its clearest inimitability in order. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (مسعود التفتازاني): Al-Taftazani maps the Fatiha's blessings to two creations and t wo preservations: creation in Rabb al-alamin, preservation in mercy, second creation in judgment, and afterlife preservation in worship onward. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A Prophetic report says he was sent to complete noble character; the author links this completion with the Fatiha's comprehensive opening and the Qur'an's culmination. | mentions 5:3 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali says the Fatiha is the Qur'an's mother: its first three verses encompass the divine names and attributes, while its last three cover the creature's way to God, mercy or failure. | mentions 2:281, 75:17, 33:50, 33:52 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (أبو هريرة): In the authentic report in Muslim and the four Sunan, God says the Fatiha is divided between Himself and His servant: He answers praise, glorification and worship, and grants the servant's final requests. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The cow story makes the surah's title apt because resurrection occurs through an apparently slight human cause; it is a clearer sign of divine power than Abraham's story or earlier Israelite resurrections. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali maps al-Fatiha's verses to the seven letters: praise, lawful and forbidden, command and prohibition, clear and ambiguous, then the corresponding petitions; its opening gathers all seven. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Because praise is the seventh inclusive letter, the Fatiha begins with it and gathers the seven universals diffused through the Qur'an, as the Qur'an gathers earlier scriptures. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Alternatively, the surah's purpose is solely to describe the Book; it establishes the truth of its meaning, exposes disbelief and hypocrisy to show that inward faith matters, then proves its inimitable form and authority for teaching law. | mentions 2:21 ITQAN, d. 911 (author): The Fatiha points to all major Qur’anic disciplines: knowledge of God and attributes, prophecy, resurrection, worship, ethical conduct and the straight path, and stories teaching the outcomes of obedience and disobedience. | mentions 1:2, 1:3, 1:4, 1:5, 1:6, 1:7 ITQAN, d. 911 (author): By signaling all Qur’anic aims, the Fatiha achieves an exemplary opening and combines beautiful words, pleasing cadence, and rhetorical forms. ITQAN, d. 911 (الخويي): Al-Khuyi says al-Fatiha fittingly gives the broadest divine attribute, Lord of all creation, while other surahs open with particular acts or attributes. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 ITQAN, d. 911 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari says the Fatiha encompasses praise, worship through commands and prohibitions, and promise and warning; every Qur’anic verse falls under one of these topics. ITQAN, d. 911 (البيضاوي): Al-Baydawi says the Fatiha contains theoretical wisdom, practical rulings in walking the straight path, and knowledge of the ranks of the blessed and abodes of the wretched. ITQAN, d. 911 (الطيبي): Al-Tibi divides the Fatiha’s religious knowledge into foundations (God and attributes, prophethood, resurrection), branches (worship), ethical perfection and seeking the path, and accounts of earlier peoples with promise and threat. | mentions 1:2, 1:4, 1:6, 1:7 ITQAN, d. 911 (الغزالي): Al-Ghazali lists three essential Qur’anic aims—knowing God, the straight path, and the return in the Hereafter—and three supplementary aims: the obedient, deniers’ speech, and the stations of the path, all signaled by the Fatiha. | mentions 1:4, 1:5, 1:6, 1:7 ITQAN, d. 911 (الزركشي وناصر الدين بن الميلق): They reconcile the hadith calling the Fatiha two-thirds of the Qur’an by saying it indicates all Qur’anic aims through inclusion and implication, but not direct correspondence; those are two of three modes of indication. ITQAN, d. 911 (ناصر الدين بن الميلق): A second explanation counts three kinds of rights—God’s claim on servants, servants’ claim on God, and people’s claims on one another—and says the Fatiha explicitly covers the first two, hence two-thirds. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن العربي): Ibn al-Arabi quotes a teacher saying the Fatiha contains a thousand commands, prohibitions, rulings, and reports; Ibn Umar spent eight years teaching it because of its jurisprudential depth. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Other names include the Prayer, Healing, Cure, Foundation, Sufficient, Complete, Praise, Supplication, Thanks, and Invocation surahs because their meanings occur in it. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): It is called the Treasure surah because a report says the Opening is a treasure from God's Throne. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The surah is Fatiha because it opens the codex, instruction, Qur'an recitation, prayer, speech, revelation, the aims of this life, and the gates of Paradise. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Fatiha is the Mother of the Qur'an because it gathers the Qur'an's purposes: affirming divinity and prophethood, and establishing decree and predestination. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The opening of al-Fātiḥa is also the final wording of the supplications in 10:10, giving the sūra a return-to-the-beginning frame. | mentions 10:10 ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Opening the sūra’s descriptions with Rabb al-ʿālamīn suits the Prophet’s mission and asks for compassion toward all creatures, since God is their Lord. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الجنيد): Junayd was asked about the spiritual end and answered that it is returning to the beginning, an apt pattern for the sūra’s opening praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Calling ḥamd the head of gratitude suits its status as the most visible and principal form; this also explains why the Qurʾān opens with praise and begins the precious sūra with what is most precious. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Although tasbīḥ commonly precedes taḥmīd, the sūra omits it because praise includes declaring God free of defects while the reverse is not always true; tasbīḥ elsewhere serves a different purpose. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The phrase opens the Fātiḥa and also closes the final supplications, as in the verse describing the last prayer of Paradise’s inhabitants. | mentions 10:10 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī argues that comprehensiveness fits this passage’s total lordship, mercy, and continuing sovereignty; he contrasts it with another surah whose relevant details are absent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Opening the Fatiha's descriptions with Lord of the worlds suits the Prophet's mission as a profound act of nurturing humanity and hints at compassion for all people. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The relation between the basmala and Fatiha unfolds the primordial covenant between the eternal Lord and c reation, inscribing a relation already present in human nature. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Fatiha proceeds as instruction; the command to read in the name of the Lord is a key contextual sign of this teaching purpose. | mentions 96:1 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (bazı müfessirîn): Some exegetes posit an implicit command meaning say this at the start of Fatiha. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Fatiha’s successive clauses unfold the opening praise and return to alhamdu lillah; the surah is the root and comprehensive representative of the other Qur’anic surahs. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Alhamdu lillah informs believers, implies reverent praise, and uses a timeless nominal sentence; the four following clauses explain it, making Fatiha one sentence in substance. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The basmala names God and His attributes while leaving humanity implicit; saying it establishes the divine relation that lets al-Fatiha bring humans forth with gratitude into speech. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Al-Fatiha begins in gratitude, places humans in the worlds under a hidden divine relation, renews the manifestation of mercy, and trains consciousness through truth and instruction until people recognize God and themselves. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The hidden humanity of the basmala becomes a speaking subject in al-Fatiha, while God is present as interlocutor; the surah captures the full relation of life and existence. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): A sacred hadith says al-Fatiha is divided equally between God and servant, with the servant entitled to what is asked; its opening praises and glorifies God, its middle is shared, and its ending is the servant's supplication. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The Quran has an exhaustive, subtle order among surahs, stories, verses, words, and letters; analyzing it involves innumerable sciences, beginning with grammar and rhetoric. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Because human power cannot detail every relation, he will sketch them in places and pause especially over al-Fatiha. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Contextual relation may be backward or forward; if the basmala is part of al-Fatiha, backward context is irrelevant, while its relation to 96:1 belongs to revelation order and forward context. | mentions 96:1 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Nothing lies outside the basmala's attraction, so it relates generally to al-Fatiha as one recites, writes, praises, or begins the Quran in its name.
