ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Specifically, basmala and al-Fatiha are closely linked by cadence, wording, summary and explanation; al-Fatiha's gratitude answers the basmala's attraction of mercy. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Their structures correspond: the basmala has seven actual or implicit words, four independent words and three divine-name words; al-Fatiha has seven verses, four statements, and three complete pauses, with the middle tawhid verse linking two three-verse units. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Al-Fatiha is Meccan and has seven verses; Muslims recite it at least seventeen times daily, more with voluntary prayers, and may repeat it without limit. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Al-Fatiha serves as the Quran's proem or a sermon introduction; this literary arrangement aids understanding and attentive reception. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A preface should be concise so listeners are not tired by waiting; the shortness of Al-Fatiha explains its placement before the longer surahs. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The surah's preparation rejects atheism and materialism, polytheism, stubbornness, and errors corrupting sound knowledge or true law; this is why it is called the Mother of the Quran. | mentions 1:4, 1:5, 1:6, 1:7 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The surah begins with praise so the second half of the Qur'an opens with الحمد لله, echoing the opening of the first half in al-Fātiḥa and the approximate start of the fourth quarter in Q 35:1. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He reads the sura's annexation to Fatihat al-Kitab as descriptive: it means a sura characterized as the Book's opening, with no separate title needed. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): By contrast, Fatihat al-Kitab is a true annexation: al-Kitab here means the remainder of the Book apart from the sura called the Opening. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Requiring al-Fatiha in every rak'ah preserves recollection of the Qur'an's purposes contained within it. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): On this reading, mathani in al-Fatiha resembles the repeated narratives and themes in the Qur'an called mathani in al-Zumar. | mentions 39:23 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The claim that al-Fatiha was named for being revealed once in Mecca and again in Medina is very remote: repetition of revelation is not accepted, and the sura is agreed to be Meccan. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur accepts the explanation tied to each rak'ah because al-Fatiha is joined to another sura in every rak'ah. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبيء صلى الله عليه وسلم): A hadith in both Sahihs from Abu Hurayra has God divide prayer between Himself and the servant, answering each portion of al-Fatiha and granting the servant's request. | mentions 1:2, 1:3, 1:4, 1:5, 1:6, 1:7 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): As a Qur'anic preface, al-Fatiha models an Arabic literary opening that improves understanding and attentive reception. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-Fatiha models four rules for introductions: keep them brief, foreshadow the subject, use concise comprehensive wording, and begin with praise of God. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): These aims help explain why al-Fatiha is called Umm al-Qur'an. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (النبي): The Prophet transmits God's division of al-Fatiha between Himself and the servant: each phrase receives divine acknowledgement and a tailored promise of blessing, mercy, ease of judgment, reward, help, and fulfillment of the servant's requests. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (بعض النصارى): He also quotes a Christian's parody of al-Fatiha as another failed counter-imitation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The author opens the book with al-Fatiha for blessing. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The differing formulations of Rabb al-alamin and Lord of heaven, earth, and worlds each fit the focus of their surah. | mentions 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Other opening praises name God as creator, originator, owner of all things, or sender of scripture; each fits its surah and the openings complement one another. | mentions 6:1, 35:1, 34:1, 18:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): The next four opening praise formulas describe particular aspects contained in al-Fatiha's general Rabb al-alamin. | mentions 6:1, 18:1, 34:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Anam's creation of heaven and earth, darkness, and light is one part of the wider lordship over all worlds in al-Fatiha. | mentions 6:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The praise covers time from eternity to eternity, echoed by praise in the first and last life and at the end of judgment; this fullness makes al-Fatiha worthy to be the Mother of the Book. | mentions 28:70, 39:75, 10:10 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The surah gathers the foundations of Islamic belief: God and His perfect attributes, monotheism, the Last Day, divine power, and the messengers and revelation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): The surah is truly the Mother of the Book because it contains both doctrinal foundations and the two pillars of action. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Fronting لله in لله الحمد normally marks restriction, but al-Fatiha's context is not one of exclusive praise: worldly praise can also be directed to people, unlike exclusive worship. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Fatiha contains faith and good works, affirming God, the Last Day, angels, messengers, and scriptures, and joins lordship's unity to the unity of worship; it is therefore the Mother of the Book. STUDYQURAN (author): Most scholars place al-Fātiḥah among the earliest sūrahs revealed in Makkah. STUDYQURAN (author): As Sūrat al-Ḥamd and Sūrat al-Ṣalāh, the Fātiḥah begins each prayer cycle for Sunnis and many Shiites. STUDYQURAN (author): Shiite law permits reciting the Fātiḥah in the third and fourth cycles or an alternative formula praising God. STUDYQURAN (author): Muslims recite the Fātiḥah at diverse life events; funeral services are called fātiḥah in some lands as an opening from one life to another. STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): The Prophet identifies the Fātiḥah as the greatest sūrah, calling it Praise be to God and the seven oft-repeated, and linking it to 15:87. | mentions 15:87 STUDYQURAN (author): The Fātiḥah is a prayer encapsulating metaphysical and eschatological realities and teaches the proper human disposition toward God. STUDYQURAN (Prophet Muhammad): A report says nothing like the Fātiḥah was revealed in the Torah, Psalms, Gospel, or Quran. STUDYQURAN (Mujāhid): One attribution says Satan was frightened when the Fātiḥah was revealed, among four events; others attribute the saying to the Prophet. STUDYQURAN (author): The Fātiḥah’s exalted status generated many independent commentaries, often showing how the Quran as a whole is contained in it. STUDYQURAN (Mujāhid): Mujāhid says al-Fātiḥah was revealed in Madinah. STUDYQURAN (ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib): A saying attributed to ʿAlī compresses the Quran into the Fātiḥah, the basmalah, its opening bāʾ, and the diacritical point beneath it. STUDYQURAN (author): Most scholars reject a Medinan origin because Muslims needed the sūrah to perform obligatory prayers. STUDYQURAN (author): Al-Fātiḥah means The Opening: it opens the Book, each prayer cycle, many everyday acts, and may open the breast to faith. STUDYQURAN (author): The sūrah is seen as a synthesis and the Quran’s most important chapter, hence Umm al-kitāb; the title also refers to 3:7 and the celestial archetype of scripture. | mentions 3:7, 13:39, 43:4 STUDYQURAN (author): The title The Foundation reflects the Fātiḥah’s role as a foundation for the whole Quran. - 1:2/q18/p2 The opening praise prepares the request for guidance. — RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi sketches the sūra’s sequence: seek refuge from sins, begin good works in God’s name, praise and recognize his lordship and mercy, heed judgment, worship and seek help, ask guidance, and avoid ignorance and error. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The seven Fatiha verses remove seven vices: praise dispels desire, Lord of the worlds greed, Judgment Day anger, worship and aid pride and vanity, guidance caprice, the favored path unbelief and doubt, and the final phrase innovation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الله تعالى): In a divine report transmitted by the Prophet, God responds to each Fatiha phrase: the opening remembers Him, praise praises Him, the mercy names magnify Him, Judgment Day glorifies Him or is entrusted to Him, worship belongs to Him, aid relies on Him, and guidance is the servant's request. NASAFI, d. 710 (أبو هريرة عن النبي): Abu Hurayra reports God’s dialogue dividing the Fatiha between Himself and the servant: praise, glorification, and exaltation belong to God; the worshipper’s request for guidance and the response to it belong to the servant, while worship and aid are shared. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali contrasts the Qur'an's detailed, dispersed sequence with the Fatiha's seven complete universals, and makes the Fatiha a mirror of the servant's move from misguidance to guidance. | mentions 93:7 ITQAN, d. 911 (الإمام فخر الدين): Fakhr al-Din maps the Qur’an’s four aims—God, resurrection, prophethood, and divine decree—onto the Fatiha: praise, Judgment Day, worship and reliance, and guidance through the surah’s end; this supports its name Umm al-Qur’an. | mentions 1:2, 1:4, 1:6, 1:7 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The basmala is the crown, the Qur’an a perfect body, Fatiha its head, and alhamdu lillah its face; mercy, guidance, worship, and divine unity express its spiritual life. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): The short surah gathers core Islamic creed, worldview, feelings, and guidance, explaining its repetition in every rakʿa and why prayer without it is invalid. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (رسول الله): Abu Hurayra reports in Sahih Muslim that God calls al-Fatiha a prayer divided between Himself and His servant: praise, mercy, and glorification are the Lord's; worship and aid are shared; guidance and the servant's request belong to the servant, who receives what he asks. ABUDAWUD (الله تعالى): In a hadith qudsi transmitted by Abu Hurayra, God divides al-Fatiha between Himself and the servant: praise and glorification receive divine responses, worship is mutual, and the guidance petition belongs to the servant. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): This plural suits al-Fatiha because its worship, aid, guidance, and moral categories address accountable creatures; Rabb al-alamin may mean their Lord or all creation with rational beings predominant. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-alamin may refer to all accountable generations or a subset, as in Lot's people; it fits al-Fatiha's concern with guidance and human moral groups and answers Jews who claimed God only as their Lord. | mentions 15:70 [12 notes: RAZI; RAZI-FULL(الله تعالى); NASAFI(أبو هريرة عن النبي); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); ITQAN(الإمام فخر الدين); ELMALILI; QUTB-ZILAL(رسول الله); ABUDAWUD(الله تعالى); SAMARRAI-LAMASAT; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 1:2/q18/p3 Al-Jāthiya orders praise differently for its context. — BURHAN, d. 794 (author): The Fātiḥa has the usual order الحمد لله; in al-Jāthiya فلله الحمد answers the implied question of who deserves praise, like the question about today's dominion and its answer. | mentions 40:16 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Word order varies with context: al-Jathiya foregrounds God as deserving praise because it confronts disbelievers' false beliefs and denials of His lordship. | mentions 45:10, 45:23, 45:24 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya answers denials of God's creative and beneficent lordship by showing Him as sole creator and provider, so He alone deserves praise; al-Fatiha addresses believers who seek steadfast guidance. | mentions 45:5, 45:12, 45:13, 45:22, 45:26 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya repeatedly uses restrictive constructions, matching its context of disbelievers who reject God and setting up its exclusive praise formula. | mentions 45:9, 45:10, 45:11, 45:24 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The many restrictive statements throughout al-Jathiya culminate in exclusive praise and sovereignty for God, so its context calls for fronting Him as the one deserving praise. | mentions 45:12, 45:15, 45:17, 45:24, 45:25, 45:26, 45:27, 45:28, 45:30, 45:32, 45:33, 45:34, 45:35, 45:36, 45:37 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Fatiha's concise Rabb al-alamin fits its focus on accountable creatures, whereas al-Jathiya explicitly names heaven and earth after repeatedly detailing their signs and God's rule over them. | mentions 45:3, 45:4, 45:13, 45:22, 45:27, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya details God's lordship over humans and other living beings through creation, subjection, sustenance, and resurrection. | mentions 45:4, 45:12, 45:13, 45:26 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): God's mercy toward believers and the laws sent to humanity also display His lordship; al-Fatiha names moral classes while al-Jathiya details the wider cosmos. | mentions 45:30, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Al-Jathiya mentions God's true sovereignty because it condemns arrogant mockers, while al-Fatiha has no such context and mentions only praise. | mentions 45:7, 45:8, 45:9, 45:31, 45:33, 45:35, 45:37 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The context contrast explains the order: al-Fatiha has believers confessing worship and asking for help, while al-Jathiya answers unbelievers' claims about life and death by foregrounding God's praise. | mentions 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Jathiya repeatedly discusses God's lordship over the heavens, earth, creation, and worlds; its closing praise gathers these themes, unlike al-Fatiha's focus on the worlds and their accountable groups. | mentions 45:4, 45:27, 45:36 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Jathiya's mention of divine majesty in heaven and earth answers the arrogant rejecters described in 45:7–9; al-Fatiha has no corresponding context of arrogance. | mentions 45:7, 45:8, 45:9, 45:37 [12 notes: BURHAN; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 1:2/q18/p4 Al-Baqara answers the Fātiḥa's plea for guidance. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The passage names al-Baqara 2:281 as the last revealed verse and says its recitation position between the debt verse and the verse before it shows revelation and recitation order can differ. | mentions 2:281, 75:17 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Al-Baqara aims to prove the Book is guidance to follow; belief in the unseen, especially resurrection and the afterlife, is its greatest guidance and organizing theme. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Al-Baqara is also called the Radiant and the Hump: it illuminates guidance and sufficiency, brightens believers' faces at judgment, and exalts belief in the afterlife above every other good after monotheism. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Baqara answers the Fatiha's plea for guidance by directing worshippers to the Book, describing the guided and denied groups as patterns to follow or avoid; its pious are the straight path and its others the path of ruin. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): There is no conflict between hadiths calling the Fatiha the greatest surah and al-Baqara the greatest: al-Baqara is greatest among surahs other than the Fatiha because of its detailed laws, parables, and arguments. | mentions 2:1 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The sign in al-alamin, later clarified by al-Baqara’s description of the Qur’an as guidance for the Godfearing, signals the wisdom of revelation. | mentions 2:2 [6 notes: BIQAI-FULL; ITQAN; ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q18/p5 Other opening praises detail aspects of this verse's lordship. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Anʿām’s praise for creating the heavens, earth, darkness, and light is one subset of Rabb al-ʿālamīn; dependence on God for preservation entails dependence for origination. | mentions 6:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Kahf’s praise for the revealed Book concerns spiritual nurture through knowledge and unveiling, one kind of the Fātiḥa’s universal nurturing. | mentions 18:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Saba’s statement that everything in the heavens and earth belongs to God is another subset within the universal lordship named in the Fātiḥa. | mentions 34:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Fāṭir describes bringing the heavens and earth into existence, distinct from creation as determination yet also included within Rabb al-ʿālamīn. | mentions 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Al-Kahf's revelation nurtures souls with knowledge, a particular form of the general nurture expressed by Rabb al-alamin. | mentions 18:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Saba's statement that all in heaven and earth belongs to God is one aspect of al-Fatiha's general praise of the Lord of the worlds. | mentions 34:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (الرازي): Fatir's origination differs from Anam's creation by measure, yet both are aspects of Rabb al-alamin; Anam then mentions darkness and light, while Fatir mentions angels as messengers. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The pair of openings complement each other: Anam follows creation with light and darkness, while Fatir follows origination with the creation of spiritual messengers. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 [8 notes: RAZI-FULL; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT(الرازي)]
- 1:2/q18/p6 The opening praise leads to direct address of God. — ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers an exhaustive reading here: the sūra’s encompassing attributes of lordship, mercy, and sovereignty support all praise belonging to God, and the shift to إياك نعبد marks a new address rather than an explanation. [1 notes: ALUSI+]
- 1:2/q18/p7 The Fātiḥa’s repeated mercy links provision for every world to mercy in the afterlife. — Al-Ghazālī, as reported by al-Biqāʿī, reads its repeated mercy as signaling perfect creation and provision for each world together with mercy in the afterlife. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(الغزالي)]

## 1:2/q19 What inward readings are given for this verse?  [* · inward]
turns on: The inward readings differ by which state or divine relation is foregrounded.
- 1:2/q19/p1 All praise arises in God and returns to Him. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Gratitude is acknowledging a blessing with reverence, rooted in the heart and also expressed by the tongue to deny a charge of ingratitude. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): In the mystical reading, guidance brings light to the heart, the blessed become polished mirrors reflecting sanctity, and the final plea seeks protection from desire and doubt. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Kahf’s praise for the revealed Book concerns spiritual nurture through knowledge and unveiling, one kind of the Fātiḥa’s universal nurturing. | mentions 18:1 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Recognizing the true cause detaches the heart from apparent means: every benefit comes from God and every harm is attributed to Him, so praise and requests belong to God alone. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ḥamdu lillāh has eight letters corresponding to Paradise’s eight gates; saying it with a pure heart merits those gates. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): True praise is any act expressing reverence for a benefactor: inward belief in perfection, spoken words of praise, or bodily deeds that express it; saying al-ḥamdu lillāh merely reports praise. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The third class is spirits: lower spirits include good jinn and evil demons; higher ones are attached to celestial bodies or are holy, disembodied spirits. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Prayer was given to Muhammad as a gift for his community because it joins bodily ascent through actions with spiritual ascent through remembrances. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Muhammad's ascent has a bodily journey from Mecca to Jerusalem and then the high kingdom, and a spiritual journey from the visible to the unseen and from the unseen to the unseen of the unseen. | mentions 53:9 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The spiritual ascent rises through human spirits, the spirits of the heavens, angels, throne-bearers, and pure spirits detached from bodies, whose worship and love of God exceed human comprehension. | mentions 39:75, 69:17, 21:20, 12:76 QURTUBI, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some scholars say gratitude is broader because it can be expressed by tongue, limbs, and heart, while praise is verbal alone. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some say gratitude is broader because it uses tongue, limbs, and heart, whereas praise is only verbal. BAYDAWI, d. 685 (author): Shukr responds to a blessing through speech, action, and belief; a poem names hand, tongue, and hidden heart as the three responses. NASAFI, d. 710 (author): Unlike hamd, shukr is only for a blessing and can be expressed by heart, tongue, and limbs; the cited poet names hand, tongue, and the veiled heart as its three forms. NASAFI, d. 710 (النبي): A Prophetic report calls praise the head of gratitude and says a servant has not thanked God without praising Him; praise leads because the tongue makes gratitude more public than the hidden heart or ambiguous bodily acts. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Hamd and madḥ may both mean praising what is beautiful, whether a favor or an inherent quality; shukr is specifically for a favor and uses heart, tongue, and limbs, whereas hamd is praise by tongue alone. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The cited poet says favors elicited three forms of gratitude: hand, tongue, and the veiled heart. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): A report calls praise the head of gratitude and says a servant has not thanked God without praising Him; verbal mention spreads notice of a favor more than inward belief or ambiguous bodily acts. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): Divine provision includes general and particular good: outward blessings such as health and livelihood, and inward blessings such as knowledge and recognition. QASHANI, d. 736 (author): God is owner at the final return and recompense; only the worshipped one to whom sovereignty returns can reward enduring blessing for perishable life, as servants shed acts and attributes and receive true existence after annihilation. ABUHAYYAN, d. 745 (بعضهم): Another reported interpretation restricts al-ʿālamīn to spiritual beings. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Other reported scopes include Adam's children, the people of Paradise and Hell, all recipients of provision, every made thing, and spiritual beings; Abū Ḥayyān leaves the correct count to God. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Hamd praises beauty whether or not a favor was bestowed and is expressed only by the tongue; shukr is for a received favor and may be by heart, tongue, or limbs. | mentions 34:13 IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir questions Ibn Jarir's equation: praise is verbal commendation for necessary or bestowed qualities, while thanks applies only to bestowed qualities and may be inward, spoken, or enacted. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (جعفر الصادق وابن عطاء): The Sufi authorities Ja'far al-Sadiq and Ibn Ata are reported to hold that praise and thanks are equivalent. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali says the Qur'an's sevenfold gathering in Muhammad's heart relates to the Mother of Qur'an's universals as the Qur'an's gathering in his heart relates to the Mother. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The following comparison of hearts and stones shows the guided and astray: hearts become hard and wretched, while stones display fear and yield benefit that reaches God's servants. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Alternatively, the surah's purpose is solely to describe the Book; it establishes the truth of its meaning, exposes disbelief and hypocrisy to show that inward faith matters, then proves its inimitable form and authority for teaching law. | mentions 2:21 DURR-FULL, d. 911 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says purification is half of faith, al-hamd fills the balance, and glorification fills the space between heaven and earth, among other deeds' spiritual benefits. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī’s own Sufi reading is that souls invoke God by the first name through which they acknowledged Him in the primordial covenant. | mentions 7:172 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الصدر): The praiser must exalt the praised one through praise both outwardly and inwardly. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawwānī explains al-Sharīf’s requirement of inward and outward exaltation as intending the performative creation of esteem, not belief in a proposition. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الجنيد): Junayd was asked about the spiritual end and answered that it is returning to the beginning, an apt pattern for the sūra’s opening praise. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Other definitions reserve shukr for apparent blessings or, alternatively, for inward blessings. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الراغب): Al-Rāghib points to a definition of shukr as verbal expression for a blessing, while the familiar account includes grateful speech, service by the limbs, and belief and love in the heart. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الطيبي): Al-Ṭībī says the technical usage of the legal theorists means worship, which is complete only through speech, bodily acts, and the heart. ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufis understand the praise as belonging to God Himself, like sovereignty and creation-command: He is both praiser and praised, and all things are His manifestations. | mentions 7:54 ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا): A Sufi account divides praise from God into praise of His essence, unity, manifestations, qualities, effects, and acts, while creaturely praise varies with knowledge and capacity. | mentions 6:91, 20:110, 37:180, 53:42 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The third element is the praiser, who must exalt the praised in outward expression and inwardly; the next segment debates whether inward belief must match the praise. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The tongue restriction is either relative to heart and limbs or merely usual; hamd originally means displaying perfection, with speech its most familiar form and action potentially fuller. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الدواني): Al-Dawānī explains that praise without belief matching its content would become mockery, taking belief to mean the act of inward exaltation rather than propositional assent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Definitions distinguish gratitude as bodily deed versus hamd as speech, outward versus inward blessings, or tongue-only thanks for blessings; al-Rāghib’s account limits it to blessing. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الطيبي): Al-Ṭībī calls gratitude by speech, deeds, and heart the technical usage of legal theorists, who mean the obligatory worship of the benefactor; al-Ālūsī rejects the claim that linguistic gratitude is only verbal. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (صاحب التجريد): Some add a fourth form, God thanking God, since only God can thank Him fully; a cited poem contrasts gratitude to people by heart, word, and deed with gratitude to God through His own thanking. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الزمخشري): One account answers how the speakers enact praise by joining verbal praise to bodily and inward worship and seeking aid. | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A further account makes worship the explanation of praise by combining verbal praise with bodily and inward devotion; al-Ālūsī says this does not require choosing genus over comprehensive scope. | mentions 1:5 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Rhetorical address is a divinely used mode even when a proof is available, because it affects hearts and benefits ordinary hearers; al-Ālūsī points to the command to invite with wisdom and good exhortation. | mentions 16:125 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): Some Sufi masters say “praise belongs to God” is like “might belongs to God”: God is both praiser and praised, and all things are His affairs, as the verse says creation and command belong to Him. | mentions 7:54 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض ساداتنا الصوفية): A mystical account divides praise into praise originating from God and from creation: God’s praise concerns His essence, manifestations, attributes, and effects, while creatures praise His essence, names, actions, secrets, and wisdom according to their understanding. | mentions 6:91, 20:110, 37:180, 53:42 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Mystically, praise originating from God ranges across His hidden essence, absolute existence, manifestations, attributes, effects, and knowledge in general or detail. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Mystical praise arising from creatures may concern God's essence, attributes, names, actions, mysteries, subtle workmanship, or hidden wisdom, according to each person's knowledge and understanding. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A mystic's saying contrasts God's care for the servant with the servant's lax service: God tends him as though no other servant existed, though God owns countless others. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ساداتنا الصوفية): Sufi masters say the appropriate divine name in prayer changes with the seeker's spiritual state and whether the experience is one of differentiation or union. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A Sufi anthropology maps the five divine names to the human's angelic intellect, devilish, predatory, animal, and dense bodily dimensions, with cited verses illustrating the correspondences. | mentions 13:28, 23:97, 25:26, 5:4, 55:31 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The basmala is the crown, the Qur’an a perfect body, Fatiha its head, and alhamdu lillah its face; mercy, guidance, worship, and divine unity express its spiritual life. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Shukr responds in speech, action, or heart to a blessing actually received; spoken gratitude is also hamd and medh, while inward or bodily thanks need not be either. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Comparing Islam's simple, coherent account of God with the inherited tangle of beliefs reveals this creed as mercy for heart and mind. QUTB-ZILAL, d. 1386 (author): Praising God wells up in a believer's heart at His mention: existence itself and ceaseless blessings call forth praise. IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): The many requirements for successful guidance and reception gather around these six foundations of spiritual preparation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): Hamd is an inward recognition of a benefactor's favor and right to reverence; clai ming I praise God can be false if the heart lacks that recognition, while the formula states a fact. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT (author): The pair of openings complement each other: Anam follows creation with light and darkness, while Fatir follows origination with the creation of spiritual messengers. | mentions 6:1, 35:1 SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Because hamd is a state of the heart, someone saying أحمد الله without reverence may lie, whereas الحمد لله remains true whether or not the speaker is attentive. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Hamd is heartfelt praise of a living, rational being's beautiful qualities or benefactions, with love and reverence. TUSTARI: Al-Tustarī links gratitude to obedience, guardianship and disavowal of all besides God, citing 5:55. SULAMI: Al-Sulamī transmits Ibn ʿAṭāʾ, al-Wāsiṭī, Jaʿfar, Junayd and others on divine self-praise, gratitude, attributes, stages of praise and letters of ḥamd; praise extends through ease and hardship. - 1:2/q19/p2 God nurtures souls and hearts at distinct spiritual ranks. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A fourth reason is that the sura gathers theology, religious duties, and spiritual purification: praise and lordship establish divine existence and attributes, worship presents duties, and guidance seeks unveiling. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Prayer seeks remembrance of the heart, and this concise surah gathers lordship and servitude and is equated with the mighty Qur'an; nothing else can replace it. | mentions 20:14, 15:87 ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعض العارفين): A mystic observes that God has other servants, but each person has no lord but Him, while God nurtures each as though He had no servant but that person. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): For the gnostics, the station of lordship comprises names that call for objects, such as knowing and power, including shared names and creative names directed toward creatures. ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): The Sufi hierarchy distinguishes Rabb, Rahmān, and Allāh: Rahmān encompasses more divine attributes, while Allāh names the highest, all-encompassing station of divinity. ALUSI, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): Ibn ʿArabī’s account says the universal spirit sought its source without knowing that the sought reality was already within it, then recognized God in the station of lordship. | mentions 50:16 ALUSI, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): In Ibn ʿArabī’s account the spirit names God its Rabb; God gives it divine names and attributes to know through existence, while veiling the manner of that gift because the creature cannot bear it. | mentions 50:16 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The primordial spirits first heard and affirmed God's lordship, so they call on Him by the first name through which they recognized and pledged themselves to Him. | mentions 7:172 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشيخ الأكبر): Ibn ʿArabi describes the universal Spirit, initially unaware of its origin, as journeying inward until God showed it its secrets and taught it to recognize Him as Lord, while reserving divinity to Himself. | mentions 50:16 STUDYQURAN (author): Some relate rabb to cultivation: God is caretaker and trainer of things and of human souls, hearts, and spirits. QUSHAYRI: The inward commentary maps praise and lordship through gifts, eternal attributes, unity, ownership, guidance and ancient care. QUSHAYRI: The inward commentary maps praise and lordship through gifts, eternal attributes, unity, ownership, guidance and ancient care. - 1:2/q19/p3 The worlds disclose God's signs to inward vision. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The ascent continues through ever-higher spiritual ranks until God, the source of all good and mercy; thus the spirit-world is the unseen and God's presence the unseen of the unseen. QURTUBI, d. 671 (أبو عمرو بن العلاء): Abū ʿAmr ibn al-ʿAlāʾ says the worlds are spiritual beings; a similar Ibn ʿAbbās gloss includes every living creature moving on earth. IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (زيد بن أسلم وأبو عمرو بن العلاء): Zayd ibn Aslam and Abu Amr ibn al-Ala define worlds as everything with a spirit that receives provision. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): “Worlds” covers every category of physical and spiritual existence. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): All created things in the material and spiritual, visible and unseen worlds, in this life and the hereafter, are included in the worlds. [5 notes: RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI(أبو عمرو بن العلاء); IBNKATHIR(زيد بن أسلم وأبو عمرو بن العلاء); ASAD-NOTES; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 1:2/q20 What else do the notes say about this verse or its sūra?  [* · interpretation]
- 1:2/q20/p1 razi lists spheres, stars and elemental “mothers” among simple bodies, and the three generated kinds—minerals, plants and animals—among composites. — RAZI, d. 606 (author): Razi lists spheres, stars and elemental “mothers” among simple bodies, and the three generated kinds—minerals, plants and animals—among composites. [1 notes: RAZI]
- 1:2/q20/p2 even a lifetime devoted to mineral wonders, plants’ flowers and fruits, or the varieties of beasts, wild animals, birds and insects would not reach the depths of these topics. — RAZI, d. 606 (author): Even a lifetime devoted to mineral wonders, plants’ flowers and fruits, or the varieties of beasts, wild animals, birds and insects would not reach the depths of these topics. [1 notes: RAZI]
- 1:2/q20/p3 a rational person should examine the self, composed of soul and body; the body is the less excellent and less beneficial of the two parts. — RAZI, d. 606 (author): A rational person should examine the self, composed of soul and body; the body is the less excellent and less beneficial of the two parts. [1 notes: RAZI]
- 1:2/q20/p4 although the world contains evil, good predominates: health over illness, satiety over hunger, and ease and joy over painful states. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Although the world contains evil, good predominates: health over illness, satiety over hunger, and ease and joy over painful states. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p5 razi frames the needed reflection as asking whether a ruler's worldly sovereignty came from his own strength and wisdom. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Razi frames the needed reflection as asking whether a ruler's worldly sovereignty came from his own strength and wisdom. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p6 a ruler may be weak and unintelligent, so his authority comes from the allotment of a wise determiner, not his own strength or wisdom. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A ruler may be weak and unintelligent, so his authority comes from the allotment of a wise determiner, not his own strength or wisdom. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p7 rationally, beneficence requires knowing every need, power to meet it, and freedom from need; divine beneficence therefore presupposes perfection without deficiency. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Rationally, beneficence requires knowing every need, power to meet it, and freedom from need; divine beneficence therefore presupposes perfection without deficiency. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p8 a seed’s shoot and roots develop in opposite directions, then produce branches, flowers, fruit, food, oils, and medicines; fine roots draw nourishment even through hard soil. | mentions 80:25 — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A seed’s shoot and roots develop in opposite directions, then produce branches, flowers, fruit, food, oils, and medicines; fine roots draw nourishment even through hard soil. | mentions 80:25 [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p9 “blessed is your name” unveils pre-eternity and eternity, since blessing here indicates abiding beyond extinction and nonexistence. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): “Blessed is Your name” unveils pre-eternity and eternity, since blessing here indicates abiding beyond extinction and nonexistence. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p10 a prophetic question about a blessing one tends means taking responsibility for it and putting it right. — QURTUBI, d. 671 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A prophetic question about a blessing one tends means taking responsibility for it and putting it right. [1 notes: QURTUBI(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 1:2/q20/p11 the language note explains that aʿdala means a matter became difficult and intractable; muʿaddalat are hardships, and the verb also describes obstructed childbirth. — QURTUBI, d. 671 (author): The language note explains that aʿḍala means a matter became difficult and intractable; muʿaḍḍalāt are hardships, and the verb also describes obstructed childbirth. [1 notes: QURTUBI]
- 1:2/q20/p12 another view means human beings here: each person contains counterparts of the greater world's substances and accidents, through which the creator is known, as 51:21 invites people to observe themselves. | mentions 51:21 — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Another view means human beings here: each person contains counterparts of the greater world's substances and accidents, through which the Creator is known, as 51:21 invites people to observe themselves. | mentions 51:21 [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p13 with several such adjectives one may follow all, detach all, or mix them, but must follow first and detach later to avoid splitting adjective from noun. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): With several such adjectives one may follow all, detach all, or mix them, but must follow first and detach later to avoid splitting adjective from noun. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 1:2/q20/p14 qatadah says each kind or species is a world. — IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (قتادة): Qatadah says each kind or species is a world. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR(قتادة)]
- 1:2/q20/p15 ibn kathir calls abu al-aliyah's extraordinary numerical report strange and says such a claim requires sound evidence. — IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir calls Abu al-Aliyah's extraordinary numerical report strange and says such a claim requires sound evidence. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR]
- 1:2/q20/p16 a report through jabir tells how locusts became scarce under umar, then attributes to the prophet a saying that there are one thousand nations and locusts are the first to perish; ibn kathir says muhammad — IBNKATHIR, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report through Jabir tells how locusts became scarce under Umar, then attributes to the Prophet a saying that there are one thousand nations and locusts are the first to perish; Ibn Kathir says Muhammad ibn Isa is weak. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 1:2/q20/p17 the first preservation is signaled by the merciful, the compassionate, whose great and subtle gifts sustain life. — BIQAI, d. 885 (العلامة سعد الدين مسعود ابن عمرو التفتازاني الشافعي): The first preservation is signaled by the Merciful, the Compassionate, whose great and subtle gifts sustain life. [1 notes: BIQAI(العلامة سعد الدين مسعود ابن عمرو التفتازاني الشافعي)]
- 1:2/q20/p18 al-harali describes the qur'an's majesty through rulings spanning immediate and deferred realities, witnessed experience, and reports of the past and patterns that recur in time. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali describes the Qur'an's majesty through rulings spanning immediate and deferred realities, witnessed experience, and reports of the past and patterns that recur in time. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي)]
- 1:2/q20/p19 al-harali distinguishes gradual descent that moves understanding between states and rulings from descent from high to low, which may occur with or without intermediaries. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali distinguishes gradual descent that moves understanding between states and rulings from descent from high to low, which may occur with or without intermediaries. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي)]
- 1:2/q20/p20 al-harali says the qur'an reached the prophet on his tongue over the span of the prophetic mission, after its descent to the lowest heaven. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Al-Harali says the Qur'an reached the Prophet on his tongue over the span of the prophetic mission, after its descent to the lowest heaven. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي)]
- 1:2/q20/p21 aisha reports that muhammad's character was the qur'an, making his conduct the visible form of its inner reality. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (عائشة): Aisha reports that Muhammad's character was the Qur'an, making his conduct the visible form of its inner reality. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(عائشة)]
- 1:2/q20/p22 the book functions like a caliph from the resolute messengers, guiding every matter to the right exit: rejecting it fails, hesitation nearly perishes, and accepting it brings piety and excellence. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The Book functions like a caliph from the resolute messengers, guiding every matter to the right exit: rejecting it fails, hesitation nearly perishes, and accepting it brings piety and excellence. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p23 alternatively, and the author says this is likelier, rahmaniyyah universally grants blessings and removes harms whether for felicity or misery, while rahimiyyah specifically gives the blessings and protection that lead to felicity — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الحرالي): Alternatively, and the author says this is likelier, Rahmaniyyah universally grants blessings and removes harms whether for felicity or misery, while Rahimiyyah specifically gives the blessings and protection that lead to felicity. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي)]
- 1:2/q20/p24 qatada says each kind or class is a world. — DURR, d. 911 (قتادة): Qatada says each kind or class is a world. [1 notes: DURR(قتادة)]
- 1:2/q20/p25 it is also a key to the qur'an's treasures: knowledge of its meanings unlocks ambiguous passages and illuminates its verses. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): It is also a key to the Qur'an's treasures: knowledge of its meanings unlocks ambiguous passages and illuminates its verses. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 1:2/q20/p26 in this hierarchy, the essence-only manifestation of ahadiyya is not disclosed to creatures, whereas uluhiyya manifests the divine in relation to others. — ALUSI, d. 1270 (author): In this hierarchy, the essence-only manifestation of aḥadiyya is not disclosed to creatures, whereas ulūhiyya manifests the divine in relation to others. [1 notes: ALUSI]
- 1:2/q20/p27 another solution defines voluntary action as action attributable to a free agent, whether or not that agent chose it in this instance. — ALUSI, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Another solution defines voluntary action as action attributable to a free agent, whether or not that agent chose it in this instance. [1 notes: ALUSI(بعضهم)]
- 1:2/q20/p28 al-alusi challenges the philosophers’ appeal to conditional willing: if the contrary can occur, their account conflicts with their claim that the world necessarily proceeds eternally; if it cannot, will is only a name. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī challenges the philosophers’ appeal to conditional willing: if the contrary can occur, their account conflicts with their claim that the world necessarily proceeds eternally; if it cannot, will is only a name. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p29 making divine attributes freely produced does not solve the issue: if power itself is produced by choice, it would precede itself, and the remaining proposed answers are unpersuasive. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Making divine attributes freely produced does not solve the issue: if power itself is produced by choice, it would precede itself, and the remaining proposed answers are unpersuasive. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p30 a statement’s not being believed does not prevent its literal or figurative use; deliberate falsehoods can serve emphasis, wit, testing, or imaginative purposes. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A statement’s not being believed does not prevent its literal or figurative use; deliberate falsehoods can serve emphasis, wit, testing, or imaginative purposes. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 1:2/q20/p31 an aphorism attributed to the gate of knowledge says that the human being contains the greater world and carries both remedy and illness within. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (علي بن أبي طالب): An aphorism attributed to the Gate of Knowledge says that the human being contains the greater world and carries both remedy and illness within. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(علي بن أبي طالب)]
- 1:2/q20/p32 the shift from unseen narration to direct address is an eloquent turn of address that compresses extensive meanings and contributes to the qur’an’s inimitability. | mentions 42:51 — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The shift from unseen narration to direct address is an eloquent turn of address that compresses extensive meanings and contributes to the Qur’an’s inimitability. | mentions 42:51 [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q20/p33 le bon and other thinkers conclude that imagination rules people more strongly than reality. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Gustave Le Bon): Le Bon and other thinkers conclude that imagination rules people more strongly than reality. [1 notes: ELMALILI(Gustave Le Bon)]
- 1:2/q20/p34 the plural’s focus on rational consciousness, reinforced by the merciful and sovereign attributes, draws reason from unseen knowledge to witnessed presence and the direct covenant of worship. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The plural’s focus on rational consciousness, reinforced by the merciful and sovereign attributes, draws reason from unseen knowledge to witnessed presence and the direct covenant of worship. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q20/p35 another group denies origination by claiming that the present cosmic order has existed eternally; on this view there is no real progress, striving, or acquisition. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (bir kısım felâsife): Another group denies origination by claiming that the present cosmic order has existed eternally; on this view there is no real progress, striving, or acquisition. [1 notes: ELMALILI(bir kısım felâsife)]
- 1:2/q20/p36 daily observation and inference show that things arise gradually and disappear gradually; examples from geology, plants, animals, and human development contradict those denials. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Daily observation and inference show that things arise gradually and disappear gradually; examples from geology, plants, animals, and human development contradict those denials. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q20/p37 the changing stages of the cosmos and human life still disclose a continuous, powerful reality that binds them and gives the observer awareness of unity. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The changing stages of the cosmos and human life still disclose a continuous, powerful reality that binds them and gives the observer awareness of unity. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q20/p38 the wheat seed’s multiplication illustrates that one cannot explain the many grains by the seed’s isolated capacity alone; each added stage receives perfection from beyond itself. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The wheat seed’s multiplication illustrates that one cannot explain the many grains by the seed’s isolated capacity alone; each added stage receives perfection from beyond itself. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q20/p39 the law of development therefore describes educ ative growth supplied from beyond each stage, not self-sufficient natural evolution; what is deficient cannot produce the complete on its own. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The law of development therefore describes educ ative growth supplied from beyond each stage, not self-sufficient natural evolution; what is deficient cannot produce the complete on its own. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q20/p40 even spencer, treated as a leading formulator of natural evolution, says an absolute must exist beyond finite natural development, though human knowledge cannot encompass it. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Spencer): Even Spencer, treated as a leading formulator of natural evolution, says an Absolute must exist beyond finite natural development, though human knowledge cannot encompass it. [1 notes: ELMALILI(Spencer)]
- 1:2/q20/p41 knowledge begins from a single unpartnered truth and tests every conclusion by relating it to that highest truth; without this relation, nature and evolution cannot be affirmed as real. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Knowledge begins from a single unpartnered Truth and tests every conclusion by relating it to that highest Truth; without this relation, nature and evolution cannot be affirmed as real. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q20/p42 true knowledge is not mere conception but assent to the real relation between what is conceived; the ordered events we perceive become signs through this truth-consciousness. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): True knowledge is not mere conception but assent to the real relation between what is conceived; the ordered events we perceive become signs through this truth-consciousness. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q20/p43 we know things through conscious attention and the representations it produces, yet we also judge external entities to possess objective reality beyond those representations. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): We know things through conscious attention and the representations it produces, yet we also judge external entities to possess objective reality beyond those representations. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q20/p44 some call the creator’s existence self-evident in a clear way, others self-evident but hidden, and others demonstrative; its appearance depends on each person’s strength of self-awareness. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (bazıları; bazıları): Some call the Creator’s existence self-evident in a clear way, others self-evident but hidden, and others demonstrative; its appearance depends on each person’s strength of self-awareness. [1 notes: ELMALILI(bazıları; bazıları)]
- 1:2/q20/p45 these relations combine linguistic convention, rational logic, and natural taste; translation loses much of the first and third and consequently affects logical signification too. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): These relations combine linguistic convention, rational logic, and natural taste; translation loses much of the first and third and consequently affects logical signification too. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 1:2/q20/p46 important unfamiliar teaching should be preceded by preparation that removes resistance and mental obstacles, so hearers can benefit from guidance. — IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): Important unfamiliar teaching should be preceded by preparation that removes resistance and mental obstacles, so hearers can benefit from guidance. [1 notes: IBNASHUR]
- 1:2/q20/p47 a preface should use concise, comprehensive expression, a principle discussed by rhetoricians for places requiring careful composition. — IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (author): A preface should use concise, comprehensive expression, a principle discussed by rhetoricians for places requiring careful composition. [1 notes: IBNASHUR]
- 1:2/q20/p48 ibn al-qatta is associated with the anomalous view that thana can mean mention generally, including neutral mention; ibn ashur says a hadith wording misled him. — IBNASHUR, d. 1393 (ابن القطاع): Ibn al-Qatta is associated with the anomalous view that thanā can mean mention generally, including neutral mention; Ibn Ashur says a hadith wording misled him. - 1:2/q20/p52 natha' with an initial n is the term used for good or bad mention, especially bad mention. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Natha' with an initial n is the term used for good or bad mention, especially bad mention. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]

- 1:2/q20/p55 the form is faʿal with passive meaning, like kitab; arabs applied it to their objects of worship and pluralized it as alihah, easing the second hamza into a long vowel. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The form is faʿal with passive meaning, like kitab; Arabs applied it to their objects of worship and pluralized it as alihah, easing the second hamza into a long vowel. ## 1:2/q21 What recitation follows completion of the Qurʾān in Ibn Kathīr’s practice?  [* · readings]
- 1:2/q21/p1 After the final takbīr, recite the Fātiḥa, the first five verses of al-Baqara, and the completion supplication. — Abū ʿAmr al-Dānī, as reported by Ibn al-Jazarī in al-Nashr, says Ibn Kathīr’s takbīr begins after al-Ḍuḥā and continues at each sūra through al-Nās; after its final takbīr come the Fātiḥa, al-Baqara through 2:5, and the completion supplication. He calls this al-ḥāl al-murtaḥil. [1 notes: NASHR(أبو عمرو الداني)]
